Keynote Speakers
Opening Keynote Lecture
‘Minding the Body:’ Empowering the Self : : : Elevating the Culture
Ketra L. Armstrong, University of Michigan
Disciplines within and related to Kinesiology have the formidable task
and unique opportunity to move the world by motivating people’s
psyche and transporting their minds and bodies to new heights and
dimensions of optimal performance. Although laudable, this task/opportunity is not one that is culture-neutral, given the ever-increasing
cultural diversity of the personhood of Kinesiology constituents and
their culturally infused and culturally informed expectations and aspirations. Markus & Kitayama (1998) noted long ago that a person is a social
and collective construction made possible through participation and
interaction with salient practices and symbolic meanings of a given
culture. Kanagawa et al. (2001) also opined that cultural contexts
inﬂuence the universe of self-conceptions from which a person’s
working self-concept is constituted. How we see ourselves shapes our
lives and is shaped by our culture, because the self is mapped in units of
culture that impacts our ways knowing, interpreting, doing, and behaving – and this includes behaviors in and related to physical activity.
Herein lies the overall focus of my presentation. During this keynote, I
will address theoretical and practical implications of self-culture dynamics on the mind-body movement phenomenon. In doing so, I will
center the concept of a ‘cultured’ self as the seer and that which is seen,
and as the actor and that which is acted upon – while also noting the
implications for motivation, presentation, and optimal performance
during physical activity. Notwithstanding my mindfulness of the sociopolitical challenges and ever-changing ‘rules of the game’ for
addressing culture and the consequent disparities, injustices, and marginalization for which it and its ancillaries are often the subject and
object, I believe that Kinesiology’s motor development, motor learning
and control, and sport and exercise psychology professionals must
‘adapt to the rules’ and ‘stay in the game!’ As such, I will conclude
this presentation with a discussion of the imperative of meeting the
moment at the fore with vigilance and intentionality to: (a) empower the
self of the culturally diverse array of constituents, and (b) elevate the
cultural impact of the profession(s).
Keynote Speaker: Motor Development
Moving beyond borders: What global research is teaching us about
early motor development
Anthony Okely, University of Wollongong, Australia
Motor development research has largely been shaped by studies conducted
in high-income Western countries, yet movement behaviors, motor competence, and their developmental trajectories are profoundly inﬂuenced by
cultural, environmental, and socioeconomic factors. The SUNRISE International Study is the ﬁrst global initiative to examine motor competence,
physical activity, sedentary behavior, and sleep in 3- to 5-year-olds. The
study currently includes 68 countries, 47 of which are low- or middleincome nations. This study challenges traditional assumptions about early
motor development by offering a unique view of how young children move
in diverse settings—from urban pre-schools to rural villages, high-resource
environments to communities where opportunities for structured movement are limited. This keynote will explore three critical questions
emerging from this global work: What do we assume about early motor
competence based on high-income country data? What do the international
data actually tell us? How do these ﬁndings inform research, practice, and
policy? I will present key insights from SUNRISE, focusing on the
variability in motor skill acquisition across cultures, the role of environmental affordances in shaping motor competence, and the unexpected
relationships between motor skill proﬁciency and other movement behaviors such as physical activity and sedentary time. I will examine the
challenges of assessing motor development internationally and the need for
culturally relevant, scalable assessment tools. Beyond the empirical ﬁndings, I will discuss the global policy implications of this work, drawing
from my leadership in WHO’s Global Standards for Early Childhood
Education and Care (ECEC) Settings and the development of international
physical activity guidelines for young children. These initiatives emphasize the importance of early movement opportunities in shaping motor
competence and lifelong health. The session will conclude with a forwardlooking discussion on how international collaboration in motor development research can help close knowledge gaps and inform policy decisions
worldwide—including lessons North America can take from this global
perspective. By broadening our lens beyond high-income settings, we can
build a more inclusive, equitable understanding of early motor development—one that better reﬂects the diverse realities of children around
the world.
Keynote Speaker: Motor Learning and Control
Through the eye to the brain - self-regulating arousal
Nicole Wenderoth, ETH Zürich
There is a well-established link between arousal and performance, as
described by the Yerkes-Dodson law. Arousal has been associated with
activity in the locus coeruleus (LC), a small brainstem nucleus containing
noradrenaline (NA)-releasing neurons. The LC-NA system projects widely
across the brain, brainstem, and spinal cord, modulating various behaviors.
It also sends projections to the eye’s pupil, and previous ﬁndings in mice
suggest that LC-NA activity causally inﬂuences pupil diameter under
constant ambient light conditions. We recently translated this mechanism
into a novel pupil-based neurofeedback approach, demonstrating that
healthy human volunteers can learn to volitionally control their pupil size
through mental strategies. Using fMRI, we found that self-regulating pupil
diameter signiﬁcantly modulates activity in interconnected brainstem
nuclei, including the LC. Additionally, we showed that pupil self-regulation inﬂuences behavioral and physiological responses in an oddball task,
aligning with theoretical predictions of the LC-NA system. The implications of these ﬁndings for basic research and applications in sports will be
discussed.
Keynote Speaker: Sport and Exercise Psychology
The Right Why: How a Simple Change in Frame Transforms
Motivation
Michele Segar, University of Michigan
In 1994, while conducting my Kinesiology master’s thesis, an unanticipated problem arose among our participants: The cancer survivors who had
been motivated to sustain exercise for our research lost their motivation

when the program ended when exercising was just for themselves. This
conundrum suggested that this problem was embedded within our cultural
psyche and ignited my curiosity about how to solve it. This led me to get an
MPH in health-behavior intervention design and evaluation, testing out
and reﬁning my behavior change solutions through real-world delivery of a
theory-based intervention, and eventually getting a PhD in personality
psychology, the original home of motivation scholarship. This psychological perspective brought me back to my original epiphany: the need to help
people free themselves from the cultural expectations that lead to repeated
motivation failures. Using a science-based graphical story, I will explain
what I’ve learned during the past three decades, focused on why our global
promotion of exercise and physical activity has failed and the leading role
that positive affective experiences need to play if we are to ﬁnally succeed
at increasing sustainable motivation and participation among individuals,
communities, and populations.
Senior Lecturers
Motor Learning and Control
Emotion, Movement, and Mayhem: A Predictably Unpredictable
Journey along Bumpy Roads to Nowhere and Winding Motorways
of Discovery
Christopher M. Janelle, University of Florida
Emotions exert both subtle and dramatic inﬂuences on how motor
actions are learned, planned, and executed. For long enough to qualify
as a “Senior Lecturer” (yikes!), I have been privileged to direct the
Performance Psychology Laboratory (PPL) at the University of Florida,
leading a research agenda centered on understanding the motivational,
attentional, and movement mechanisms that underlie variability in the
behavioral manifestations of mind-body interactions that occur under
diverse emotional conditions. Over the past 25 years, the PPL has
housed dedicated teams of students and colleagues who have contributed to research endeavors advancing understanding of how emotions
affect the way people move and how to regulate emotions to optimize
performance. In particular, our work has informed the role of emotions
in planning and controlling motor actions, from simple upper-extremity
tasks to complex whole-body movements and sport-speciﬁc skills. The
winding journey of exploration has included treks down side roads and
occasional dead ends, involving ventures into topics as varied as
knowledge of results, observational learning, body image, visual search,
and expertise, among others. Our expedition eventually settled on an
agenda featuring innovative approaches to explore the impact of emotional reactivity on movement patterns and the importance of considering fundamental emotion-movement mechanisms in improving interventions for individuals as they confront emotion regulation and
movement challenges. A grateful, reﬂective account of this research
journey will be provided, highlighting milestones and scientiﬁc contributions that have been made possible only through building and
sustaining collaborative relationships among talented students and
colleagues.
Motor Development
A career mirrors life, not what you anticipate, but if you remain open
to opportunity, always moving
Lisa M Barnett, Deakin University
If I could cast my mind back to my days as an undergraduate student, I
would never have imagined my career today. The only constant
throughout my life is my drive to question. People say you need passion
for a PhD topic, but for me, my passion has always been in learning and
trying to understand, rather than a particular topic. I ﬁrmly believe you
can develop passion for all manner of topics if you let yourself become
immersed. I started as a social scientist, moved to public health and then
fell into motor development quite by accident. In this senior lecture I will
describe my main research contributions to the ﬁeld of motor development, which are in three areas: i) situating children’s motor development
as important to achieving public health goals, ii) understanding how
perceptions of motor development inﬂuence health behaviours and iii)
developing novel measurement approaches suitable for children and
youth. I will describe my interdisciplinary approaches and the strategies
to forward my research. I will explore what makes a good mentoring
relationship and what has worked in collaborations with researchers
around the world. In recent years I have expanded the frame of motor
development to the concept of physical literacy, a holistic approach
concerning the skills and attributes that assist a person to lead a physically
active lifestyle. I am collaborating with government and industry to bring
physical literacy into practice in school and sport, with the goal to make
an impact on the community we serve.
Sport and Exercise Psychology
Learning to prioritize quality over quantity: Lessons from research
partnerships
Amy E. Latimer-Cheung, Queen’s University
In the early years of my academic career, my research focused on numbers
– determining the strongest predictors of physical activity participation
among persons with disabilities, developing recommendations for the
optimal dose of exercise for persons with disabilities, designing the
clearest message to motivate an increase in rates of physical activity
participation among persons with disabilities. This research emphasizing
quantity of participation contributed empirically – advancing the application of theory to physical activity promotion for persons with disabilities. It also provided a solid foundation for a productive early
research career. However, as I reached mid-career as a female academic
now also juggling a young family, I felt uncertain of where to take my
research next and of how to maintain the “numbers” indicative of
academic productivity. I was stuck. In this presentation, I will share
how I moved through this challenging career stage because of invaluable
partnerships with colleagues and research users. My colleagues helped
me refocus on my personal research mission – conducting research that
makes an empirical contribution but most importantly, makes an impact
in real-world settings. Research users guided me to a new line of research
that is mutually meaningful and is impacting practice. As a senior
scientist, my research now focuses on quality – quality physical activity
participation for persons disabilities and quality academic outputs with
implications for practice. This work has been the most rewarding of my
career.
Early Career Distinguished Scholar Award
Joyful movement as resilience: Stories from a program of research
attending to stress, the body, and physical (in)activity across the life
course
Erica Bennett, University of British Columbia
Working from the understanding that people in non-normative, including
queer, old, fat, disabled, and racialized bodies face unique stressors due to
marginalization, the purpose of my research is to understand the

--- [Página 6] ---
psychosocial complexities of how everyBODY navigates physical activity spaces. Grounded in cultural sport and exercise psychology, taking a
stress, emotion, and coping lens, and adopting interpretive and narrative
methodological approaches, my work has been anchored in the following
questions: What stories do people in non-normative bodies tell about the
physical activity and body-related stressors they face throughout their
lives? How have these stories shaped who they are and who they will
(continue to) become? Taking the position that people assign meaning to
their experiences through storytelling, in this talk, I will tell the story of
the ﬁndings and implications of my scholarly work thus far in four
chapters. I will highlight throughout important characters (mentors, peers,
colleagues), catalyst events (feedback from participants, critical friend
discussions), and turning points (political events) whom and which have
shaped my research contributions. In Chapter 1: The changing body, I
will detail the curiosity as a trainee that drove my pursuit to further
understanding of the interrelated roles of physical activity, masculinities,
and femininities, in shaping how people adapt to changes to their bodies
throughout their lives. In Chapter 2: The emotion(al) turn, I will recount a
catalyst event, a foray into feminist literatures on embodied emotions, that
led me to conduct research showcasing the central role of emotions and
intersecting identities in informing the diverse and (mal)adaptive ways
that people cope with stressors in sport. In Chapter 3: Queer becomings, I
will discuss the affordances yet challenges of my turn to psychological
movement-related research underpinned by social justice, showing how
intertwined experiences of ageism, ableism, and cis-heteronormativity
shape the physical activity experiences of LGTBQ+ older adults. I will
end with an incomplete Chapter 4: Joy and hope amongst the chaos,
discussing my ongoing research pertaining to physical activity-related joy
and resilience as acts of resistance in an increasingly polarized political
landscape.t
The NASPSPA Outstanding Student Paper
Award Recipients
Motor Development
Examining construct validity and reliability of the supine-to-stand and
go task in young adults
Ozgur Altintas, University of South Carolina; Giovanna E. Leone, University of South Carolina; An De Meester, University of South Carolina; A.
Brian, University of South Carolina; Bryan M. Terlizzi, The Citadel; T.
Cade Abrams, Booz Allen Hamilton; Ryan S. Sacko, The Citadel; N.
Hikmet, University of South Carolina; David F. Stodden, University of
South Carolina
There is a need for motor competence (MC) assessments that are
ecologically valid, feasible (i.e., minimal training and cost), and sensitive
to detect changes in MC across the lifespan. The supine-to-stand (STS)
task and the 10-m shuttle run were combined to create a functional
locomotor task, the STS&GO, which measures essential functional
capabilities used across the lifespan. The purpose of this study was to
explore the underlying factor structure of the STS&GO components
(STS, 2-m change of direction speed [2m COD], running speed) and the
reliability of the STS&GO task. Young adults (N = 125, f = 33,
Mage = 22.4 years) completed the STS&GO (2 trials) and the fastest time
(sec) was used. Performance was digitally recorded and individual
components, including STS (sec), 2m COD (sec), and maximum running
speed (m/s) were coded using Dartﬁsh software. Raw scores of the
variables were standardized into z-scores for statistical analysis. Exploratory factor analysis (EFA) was used to identify the underlying component structure of the STS&GO task, and McDonald’s ω was used to
measure internal consistency. Intra-class correlations (ICCs) were used to
assess inter-rater, intra-rater, and inter-trial reliability. Multiple linear
regression was used to assess the utility of the subcomponents in
predicting STS&GO performance. STS&GO subcomponents contributed
signiﬁcantly to a single factor, which together accounted for 59% of the
variance, with strong individual factor loadings (λ = .72 - .80). The
subcomponents had acceptable internal consistency (ω = .81). ICCs for
the STS&GO task showed excellent reliability, with inter-rater and intrarater reliability (ICC = 0.95-0.96, 95% CI: [0.93,0.98], respectively) and
inter-trial reliability (ICC = 0.91, 95% CI: [0.88,0.94]). Lastly, multiple
linear regression results indicated that the subcomponents accounted for
92% of the variance in performance on the STS&GO task (p < .001). EFA
results suggest that the STS&GO assessment has strong psychometric
properties and the potential to measure multiple aspects of MC in a single
assessment. Assessing STS&GO performance across diverse age groups
is a critical next step in demonstrating its developmental validity across
the lifespan.
Motor Learning and Control
Effect of physical, mental, and combined fatigue on military marksmanship performance and perceived workload
Joshua Springer, University of Tennessee, United States Army; Jared
Porter, University of Tennessee
In modern warfare, Soldiers are expected to execute a wide range of
complex tasks under signiﬁcant physical and mental fatigue. To enhance motor performance and future Soldier success, the military must
appraise and integrate performance expertise alongside emerging technological systems to develop training solutions capable of delivering
results on the battleﬁeld. While existing literature provides a promising
future for this line of inquiry, a signiﬁcant gap exists on the impact of
combined fatigue on task performance, learning, and transfer to realworld environments. More speciﬁcally, no study has explored the
complex interplay of performing and learning under combined fatigue
nor the transfer of learned skills to combat-speciﬁc environments. Thus,
this study aimed to examine the effect of combined fatigue on military
marksmanship performance, learning, and transfer to combat-speciﬁc
environments. Participants (N = 28; Mage = 22.04 ± 3.22 years, male =
20) were United States Army and Airforce Reserve Ofﬁcers’ Training
Corps (ROTC) cadets. Volunteers completed a three-day study encompassing a physical, mental, and combined fatigue protocol followed by a
three-part marksmanship task (i.e., precision, discriminatory reaction,
speed, & accuracy). Marksmanship tasks were completed using the
InVeris Squad Advanced Marksmanship Simulator, which leverages
non-immersive virtual reality and wireless weapon technology to replicate the form, ﬁt, and function of live weapons used by the United States
Military. Results from the two-way repeated measures ANOVAs demonstrated a statistically signiﬁcant main effect for condition on marksmanship precision (p < .001), discriminatory reaction time (p = .002),
and efﬁciency (p < .001). Additionally, results revealed a statistically
signiﬁcant main effect for self-reported mental demand (p < .001),
physical
demand
(p < .001),
temporal
demand
(p = .014),
effort
(p < .001), frustration (p < .001), and rate of perceived exertion
(p < .001) when fatigued. The ﬁndings of this study demonstrate the
independent and combined effects of motor and cognitive fatigue on
military marksmanship performance. Furthermore, these ﬁndings highlight the need for comprehensive training programs across disciplines
(i.e., sports, military, aviation, medicine) that address the challenges of
combined fatigue while facilitating motor and cognitive performance,
retention, and transfer of learning to real-world environments.

Sport and Exercise Psychology
How do we get a[head] of the game? The co-creation of a disability
sport-speciﬁc mental health educational resource
Roxy Helliker O’Rourke, University of Toronto; Catherine M. Sabiston,
University of Toronto; Andrea Bundon, University of British Columbia;
Kelly P. Arbour-Nicitopoulos, University of Toronto
Sport offers mental health beneﬁts for athletes with disabilities. Poorquality experiences can lead to negative mental health outcomes. The
Quality Participation (QP) Framework emphasizes six experiential elements– autonomy, belongingness, challenge, engagement, mastery, and
meaning – to ensure quality sport experiences. Disability sport staff play a
critical role in fostering these elements. This study aimed to co-create a
mental health resource with disability sport athletes and coaches to sport
mental health education for disability sport staff. Using the Nominal
Group Technique (NGT), two high-performance athletes and one coach
participated in a session involving: silent idea generation, idea recording,
clariﬁcation, grouping, and ranking. Forty-six priority topic areas for
mental health education were generated. Participants ranked their top 20
topic areas from 1 (least important) to 10 (most important). Mean rank
scores revealed the top 5 priorities: (1) coach understanding of previous
athlete trauma (9), (2) integrating new/fast-tracked athletes (8.7), (3)
awareness of athlete impairment and routines outside of sport (8.3), (4)
awareness of athlete workload outside of sport (8), and (5) coach
emotional intelligence and accountability for in-game behaviour (8). The
top 15 ranked areas, prioritized by at least two participants, were further
analyzed with deductive thematic analysis using the QP Framework’s
experiential elements as pre-determined themes. A resource draft was
developed based on these priorities and reﬁned in a follow-up focus group
with the NGT participants. The ﬁnal resource provides guidance for
fostering quality sport participation and supporting mental health in
disability sport contexts. For example, the autonomy section of the
resource includes strategies for the priority areas of open coach-athlete
communication and ﬂexibility in practice design, tailored to the unique
mental health needs of disability sport athletes. This study highlights the
value of collaborative knowledge-sharing between athletes and coaches
in resource development. The co-created resource offers actionable
strategies to enhance QP and mental health in disability sport environments. Future research efforts include the evaluation of the resource
deployment, engagement, and impact. Funding source: SSHRC CGS-D
to Honour Nelson Mandela, Sport Participation Research Initiative,
SSHRC Institutional Grant.
Young adult athletes’ psychological distress differences by level and
gender identity
Maegan Parrish, East Carolina University; Trent A. Petrie, University of
North Texas; E. Whitney G. Moore, East Carolina University
The young adult population has the highest percentage of new mental
health diagnoses (Edwards & Froehle, 2023). Collegiate sports – Club and
DI level – introduce additional demands and stressors, including heavy
workloads, performance anxiety, and balancing time demands (Edwards
et al., 2023; Peacock, 2022). Women student-athletes have shown higher
prevalence of psychological distress symptoms than men (Wolanin et al.,
2016). Researchers have primarily focused on psychological distress of
NCAA athletes rather than Club athletes (Martin et al., 2021). Therefore,
this study explored differences in psychological distress prevalence between athletes’ level and gender identity. Participants for this crosssectional study included 459 student-athletes: 254 (54%) DI athletes
(95 men; 159 women) and 207 Club athletes (83 men; 122 women).
These student-athletes were asked to provide demographic information
(e.g., gender identity, sport) while completing a mental health screening,
including anxiety and depression symptomology levels measured, respectively, by the General Anxiety Disorder Questionnaire-2 (GAD-2) and
Patient Health Questionnaire-2 (PHQ-2), which are, validated brief versions of the GAD-7 and PHQ-9. The total scores align with classiﬁcations
for clinical (3-6), subclinical (2), and minimal (0-1) symptoms. The chisquare analyses were used to evaluate differences in the prevalence of
clinical, subclinical, and minimal levels of depression and anxiety across
athletic level and gender. There were no signiﬁcant differences in prevalence by gender. By athlete level, there was signiﬁcantly higher prevalence
of subclinical anxiety (26%) among DI athletes compared to Club athletes
(7%). As well as signiﬁcantly higher prevalence of subclinical depression
(18%) among DI athletes compared to Club athletes (6%). Though no other
differences were signiﬁcant, compared to Club athletes, nearly twice as
many DI athletes had clinical level anxiety symptoms and three times as
many had clinical level depression symptoms. Thus, it can be concluded
there is higher prevalence of psychological distress among DI athletes
compared to their Club peers. This may illustrate the effect of having
greater sports performance related stress and time constraints on students’
mental health symptoms. These ﬁndings support the increasing trend and
requirements for NCAA DI Athletics Departments having embedded sport
psychologists and mental health counselors.

Symposia
Sport and Exercise Psychology
Exploring the role of identity in athlete well-being across diverse
populations and sport contexts
Patti C. Parker, Thompson Rivers University (Organizer); Mariya
Yukhymenko-Lescroart, California State University, Fresno (Discussant)
Exploring the role of identity in athlete well-being across diverse
populations and sport contexts
Patti C. Parker, Thompson Rivers University; Mariya Yukhymenko-Lescroart, California State University, Fresno
Identity in sport plays a critical role in shaping the well-being of
individuals involved in athletic activities. This symposium will feature
four presentations exploring various perspectives on identity in sport,
highlighting its impact on wellbeing, resilience in the face of challenge,
and areas for growth. The presentations utilize different theoretical
frameworks, methodologies, and bring forward new, evidence-based
insights into the role of identity across diverse contexts and populations
in sport. Presentation 1 focuses on examining how academic and athletic
identities, and their interaction, contribute to sport satisfaction and
passion for sport in university student-athletes through moderated mediation structural equation modeling (SEM) analysis, highlighting the
importance of balancing both identities for optimal well-being. Presentation 2 shifts focus to young women and girl athletes, using reﬂexive
thematic analysis to reveal how their athlete identities are connected to
self-worth, success, meaningful roles within their teams, and shaped by
key role models. In Presentation 3, the authors examine the role of
athletes’ social identiﬁcation with their sport in important psychosocial
factors and competition anxiety through the Control-Value Theory
framework. Presentation 4 provides a review of several studies on the
athletic and disability identities of athletes with disabilities, exploring
how these identities interact to shape well-being, adaptation, and selfperception across different life and sport stages, thus contributing to the
growing knowledge of athletic identity. Collectively, these presentations
highlight the need to balance academic and athletic identities for university student-athletes’ well-being, support the role of identity in self-worth
and team dynamics for young women athletes, emphasize fostering
positive social identiﬁcation to reduce competition anxiety, and stress
the importance of understanding the interaction between athletic and
disability identities to enhance well-being and self-perception in athletes
with disabilities. These ﬁndings call for targeted support strategies and
inclusive programs across diverse athletic contexts. The symposium will
conclude with closing remarks followed by a period for questions and
answers.
The role of academic and athletic identities in sport satisfaction and
passion among university student-athletes
Mariya Yukhymenko-Lescroart, California State University, Fresno; Michael J. Schmid, University of Bern; Juerg Schmid, University of Bern
University student-athletes balance academic and athletic demands, which
shape their identity and well-being. Identity, encompassing academic and
athletic domains, is central to how student-athletes perceive themselves
and manage these overlapping roles. A key indicator of subjective wellbeing in the athletic context is sport satisfaction, which reﬂects fulﬁllment
and enjoyment athletes often derive from passion-driven participation in
sports. This study examined how academic and athletic identities, along
with their interaction, contribute to sport satisfaction, both directly and
indirectly through different types of passion for sport (harmonious,
obsessive) in European university sports. Participants were 281 studentathletes from a university in Switzerland (43.9% female), with an average
age of 23.1 years and 10.1 years of sports experience. They completed
scales in German (the language of instruction), including the Academic
and Athletic Identity Scale (Yukhymenko-Lescroart, 2014), the German
version of Vallerand et al.’s (2003) Passion Scale (Moeller, 2015), and a 5item measure of Sport Satisfaction. Results from moderated mediation
structural equation modeling analysis showed that harmonious passion for
sport was signiﬁcantly predicted by athletic identity (β = .33), academic
identity (β = -.21), and the interaction between the two (β = .19). Obsessive
passion was predicted by athletic identity (β = .30) and academic identity
(β = -.17). Sport satisfaction was predicted directly by harmonious passion
(β = .64) and obsessive passion (β = .16), but not by identities. The index of
moderated mediation (IMM = .05, p = .001) indicated that sport satisfaction was predicted indirectly by identities through harmonious passion
only. Conditional indirect effects showed the relationship between athletic
identity and sport satisfaction was positive when academic identity was
strong (β = .14), but non-signiﬁcant when academic identity was weak
(β = .04). Conversely, academic identity negatively predicted sport satisfaction when athletic identity was weak (β = -.11), but was non-signiﬁcant
when athletic identity was strong (β = -.01). Practically, these ﬁndings
suggest that student-athletes with strong academic and athletic identities
experience greater sport satisfaction. However, when athletic identity is
weak, a strong academic identity may negatively inﬂuence sport satisfaction, highlighting the importance of balancing both identities for optimal
well-being.
Identity and well-being in sport: Gendered understandings from selfidentiﬁed girl and women youth athletes
Danielle L. Cormier, University of Alberta; Amber D. Mosewich, University of Alberta
Athlete identity is both a source of motivation and a potential challenge for
youth in sport. For self-identiﬁed girls and women, this identity often
intersects with unique psychosocial factors that shape their well-being and
long-term involvement in sport. In this study, we explored the relationship
between how self-identiﬁed girls and women identify as athletes and the
impact this had on their experiences of well-being in youth sport. Using a
holistic approach, we examined the perspectives of the “athlete triangle”.
Three focus groups were conducted: one with youth athletes (2 women),
one with sport parents (3 women), and one with sport coaches (3 women, 1
man). Follow-up one-on-one interviews with selected participants (1
athlete, 1 parent, 1 coach) were conducted to deepen insights, incorporating photovoice reﬂections to capture personal experiences and contexts.
Focus groups and one-on-one interviews were analyzed using reﬂexive
thematic analysis, and several major themes were constructed. First,
athletes frequently tied their athlete identities—involving their perceptions
of performance and role on their teams—to their sense of self-worth. This
connection was evident when participants spoke about athletes feeling
inadequate after perceived failures or exclusions (e.g., underperforming,
deselection). Equally, an afﬁrmed sense of athlete identity was achieved
after experiencing success or fulﬁlling a meaningful team role. Social
identity—a person’s sense of who they are based on their group membership—also appeared to be an important facilitator to athlete well-being.

--- [Página 9] ---
More speciﬁcally, positive connections with teammates and coaches
contributed to enjoyment, while fractured team dynamics eroded emotional well-being. Lastly, participants pointed to the importance of role
models in the construction of their athletic identity. The lack of visible
women role models in sport was identiﬁed as a barrier to sustaining
motivation and well-being. However, existing role models (e.g., siblings,
older athletes) were deeply inﬂuential in fostering aspirations and shaping
identity. These ﬁndings contribute a gendered understanding of how
identity shapes the well-being of athletes by identifying the facilitators
and barriers that self-identiﬁed girls and women commonly face, and offers
some pathways forward for sport support persons who are working to make
sport a more positive environment for all.
Exploring relationships amongst social identiﬁcation, perceived control, and anxiety in sport competitions using a Control-Value Theory
framework
Katrina M. Janzen, Thompson Rivers University; Patti C. Parker, Thompson Rivers University
The extent to which an athlete feels part of a group in a valuable way
(i.e., social identiﬁcation) has been empirically linked to psychological
well-being and anxiety. The present study sought to examine the inﬂuence
of social identiﬁcation in sport on important relationships between athletes’
cognitive appraisals and achievement-related anxiety. Pekrun’s (2006)
Control-Value Theory (CVT) asserts that cognitive control and value
appraisals are key antecedents in the experience of achievement-related
anxiety. In the present study we tested associations amongst the variables,
and speciﬁcally tested the relationship between perceived control and
value (cognitive appraisals) on anxiety, and the moderating role of social
identiﬁcation. We expected social identiﬁcation to be positively linked to
control and value appraisals and negatively to anxiety. Additionally, we
expected social identiﬁcation would moderate the effect of perceived
control on anxiety–whereby the negative relationship between perceived
control and anxiety would be stronger for athletes with higher social
identiﬁcation. Lastly, we explored gender differences amongst the variables. The data was collected in 2024 comprising survey responses from
competitive athletes (Mage = 30.8; 125 women, 121 men). We employed
correlations and moderation regression analyses to test our hypotheses.
Our results revealed social identiﬁcation was positively related to control
and value (rs = .47, .49, respectively). Perceived control negatively predicted anxiety (β = -.13, p = .020) and social identiﬁcation signiﬁcantly
moderated the effect of perceived control on anxiety (β = -.02, p = .045),
when controlling for competition experience and age. Upon probing this
interaction, we found athletes with higher social identiﬁcation and high
control reported the least amount of anxiety, but athletes with higher social
identiﬁcation and low control reported the most anxiety. These ﬁndings
indicate some support for social identiﬁcation and CVT theories in sport
contexts, and imply that social identiﬁcation plays an important role in how
athletes’ appraisals of their sport achievements setting affect anxiety.
Future research could explore the determining causes of why social
identiﬁcation impacts anxiety at varying levels of perceived control.
The dynamics of disability sport identity
Jeffrey J. Martin, Wayne State University
Athletes with disabilities have athletic identities similar to able-bodied
athletes. However, some researchers have suggested a self-athletic identity
is split into both self and social identities, with a social identity reﬂecting
processes whereby athletes with disabilities feel disability sport is undervalued (e.g., disability sport is not “real” sport; Martin, 2013). Even
athletes with “mild” disabilities may diminish the value of sport for those
with severe disabilities and denigrate their athletic achievements.
Quantitative research on disability sport athletic identity suggests that
possessing a strong athletic identity can have positive effects such as the
promotion of competitive goals (Martin et al., 1995). However, there are
also negative effects if athletes possess an exclusive athletic identity.
Qualitative research, providing a more nuanced picture, suggests that sport
participation serves as a catalyst for athletes with disabilities to develop
positive identities and to challenge disablist attitudes. Hence, athletes with
disabilities often possess both athletic and disability identities. Disability
sport athletes may have congenital or acquired disabilities and both
experience identity related challenges. Athletes with acquired disabilities
(e.g., car accident in adulthood), face an additional challenge of acknowledging a forever lost past athletic identity grounded in able-bodied sport
(Guerrero & Martin, 2018). Recruitment efforts for Paralympic teams often
focus on wounded warrior participants (e.g., military veterans) and such
participants may have athletic, military and disability identities that interact
in ways such that holding onto a military identity, even when not actively
serving, in conjunction with an athletic identity, may prove adaptive
(e.g., increased PA and life satisfaction; Martin et al., 2023). Future
researchers have many fruitful avenues to consider. For instance, examining athletes with acquired disabilities’ perceptions of their past, present,
and future selves through the lens of narrative identity and identifying the
capabilities (e.g., compassion, courage) and stressors (e.g., regret) of those
who acknowledge loss (i.e., past able bodied athletic identity) but embrace
current and future selves, would make a strong addition to the knowledge
base in athletic identity.
Physical activity interventions for youth: Moving beyond efﬁcacy
Spyridoula Vazou, Michigan State University (Organizer); Karin Pfeiffer,
Michigan State University (Discussant)
Physical activity interventions for youth: Moving beyond efﬁcacy
Spyridoula Vazou, Michigan State University
This symposium highlights the importance of community-engaged,
mixed-methods research to address logistical and practical barriers to
implementing physical activity (PA) interventions, mainly in the school
environment. Researchers must be prepared to translate knowledge into
practice and rely on extension specialists to provide critical insights for
advancing implementation efforts. Additionally, transitioning from efﬁcacy to effectiveness trials, supplemented by cost-effectiveness studies, is
essential but missing from current research studies. As with any pragmatic
trial, process evaluation must be a major focus, covering not only
implementation extent and ﬁdelity but also perceived feasibility and the
level of satisfaction of educators/providers and students. Barriers to PA as
well as limitation at the physical environment (e.g., school) must be
identiﬁed across multiple levels, including individual, interpersonal, policy, and environmental factors. The symposium features four presentations
addressing key challenges in PA interventions for youth and translating
evidence into practice. The ﬁrst presentation examines systems and
structures inﬂuencing the implementation of whole-school PA programs
at local, regional, and national levels, highlighting scaling challenges of
scaling such programs and the importance of sustained leadership and
collaboration to promote PA in schools effectively. The second presentation will describe the Implementation Mapping as an implementationfocused approach to school-based PA promotion, with ﬁndings on its
impact on teacher ﬁdelity and classroom-based PA programs over time.
The third presentation introduces a conceptual model for physically active
learning, assessing its feasibility and educators’ readiness to implement the
program for underserved youth in summer learning camps. The fourth
presentation identiﬁes barriers and facilitators inﬂuencing participation to
home-based PA interventions for children and evaluates the feasibility of
adaptive intervention designs, such as Sequential Multiple Assignment

Randomized Trials (SMART), through evidence from the InPACT at
Home program. The symposium concludes with a discussant summarizing
key points and providing critical insights on PA interventions and implementation strategies, offering future directions to enhance the translation of
research into practice.
A cross-European study on the role of local and national systems in
supporting whole-school physical activity implementation
Andy Daly-Smith, University of Bradford; Jade Morris, University of
Bradford; Zoe Helme, University of Bradford; Ellen Silva, University of
Bradford; Anna Chalkley, University of Bradford
Whole-school programs have signiﬁcant potential for improving pupils’
physical activity, yet little is known about the national systems and
structures inﬂuencing implementation. While many programs show
short-term efﬁcacy or effectiveness, persistent challenges exist with
scaling as external factors at the local, regional and national levels impact
implementation. The present study reviewed local and national system
structures to understand their role in promoting whole-school physical
activity. We engaged 94 stakeholders from 34 countries, including policymakers, practitioners, researchers and people from non-governing bodies.
Phase one included a stakeholder survey to understand the roles and
experience of working with schools on children’s physical activity. The
survey reached 67 researchers, policy-makers, practitioners and people
from non-governing bodies) representing 27 countries. Survey respondents were recruited for phase two, which involved four online focus
groups to identify whole-of-school physical activity systems within and
across countries, the key stakeholders’ training needs, and how these differ
across countries. Data analysis identiﬁed themes which were reﬁned for
presentation in phase three. Phase three included a second round of focus
groups to review and discuss the themes and data from phase two, ensuring
breadth and depth of insights across as many European countries as
possible. Phase three focus group transcripts were analyzed inductively
to identify new themes; deductive coding was used where data aligned with
phase two themes. Forty-two participants from 26 countries engaged in
phases two and three, including 22 researchers, 13 policy-makers and
seven practitioners. Eight themes were identiﬁed by analyzing the data
captured in phases two and three: what is whole-of-school physical
activity, physical activity not a priority, different support systems across
countries, cross-system collaboration, policy, funding, staff capability and
schools as community hubs. Findings highlighted the fragmented nature of
whole-school physical activity within and across countries. A lack of
authentic and sustained leadership and misaligned beliefs that often
favored sport over broader physical activity led to disconnected systems.
If national governments fail to address the outlined systemic issues, future
whole-school physical activity approaches will fail to scale efﬁciently and
effectively.
Supporting school-based physical activity through an implementation-centric approach: Findings from a feasibility study testing an
implementation strategy to support delivery of physically active
breaks and lessons
Timothy Walker, The University of Texas
Physically active breaks and lessons are evidence-based approaches to
support children’s physical activity (PA). Despite the beneﬁts, implementation remains inadequate. In response, our team developed and tested an
implementation strategy to improve use of classroom-based PA approaches among elementary school teachers. The objectives of this
presentation are to: 1) showcase the implementation strategy and implementation-centric approach to school-based PA promotion; and 2) present
ﬁndings related to the strategy’s impact on teacher implementation ﬁdelity
and determinants of implementation. We used Implementation Mapping
(IM) to co-develop the implementation strategy with school and district
partners. The IM process entailed working with a planning group to guide
the ﬁve IM tasks. After development, we evaluated the strategy’s impact
with two participating elementary schools (one received the strategy, the
other received usual support). We conducted baseline and 6-month followup assessments using electronic surveys. The primary outcome was teacher
implementation ﬁdelity and additional outcomes included implementation
determinants (e.g., knowledge, self-efﬁcacy, and perceived barriers). We
compared implementation ﬁdelity and differences between determinants
over time, and between schools using mixed effects linear regression
models. The developed strategy included three key components: 1)
leadership training, 2) teacher/staff training, and 3) a monthly newsletter.
A total of 63 classroom teachers completed the survey at baseline and/or
follow-up. When examining ﬁdelity, survey results indicated teachers
receiving the implementation strategy signiﬁcantly increased their use of
physically active breaks over time (β =3.2, p<0.001) and had higher levels
of use compared to control schools (β = 2.5, p=0.02). However, there was
no evidence of differences in use of physically active lessons between
schools or over time (β = 0.91, p=0.56). Results also indicated knowledge
for teachers increased over time (β =0.5, p<0.001), self-efﬁcacy for
implementing physically active breaks tended to be higher for the intervention school teachers (β =0.32, p=0.06), and perceived barriers were
lower for the intervention school compared to the control school (β =0.46,
p=0.04). Our ﬁndings suggest the implementation strategy positively
impacted teacher’s use of physically active breaks but had less of an
impact on physically active lessons and improved teacher knowledge
over time.
Feasibility and educator readiness on the Joy of Moving and Learning
physically active learning program
Spyridoula Vazou, Michigan State University; Marcelo Cabral, Michigan
State University; Matthew Pontifex, Michigan State University; Caterina
Pesce, University of Rome Foro Italico
Despite a growing body of evidence showing that physical activity (PA)
does not detract from learning and is beneﬁcial for academic achievement,
“buy-in” from school staff and policymakers remains limited. This gap
may stem from a lack of theory-based research aimed at translating efﬁcacy
studies into practical, fully integrated best practices for daily educational
use. The purpose of this presentation is to: 1) introduce the conceptual
model based on the level of integration and relevance to learning content
used to develop the Joy of Moving and Learning (JOM-L) program, and 2)
present ﬁndings on training, feasibility, and teacher readiness to implement
the program in summer learning camps serving underserved students and
communities. Forty staff members (20 each year) were trained on the JOML program and completed surveys assessing their readiness to implement
physically active learning games. The JOM-L games integrate PA with
mathematics and language arts for emerging (K–2nd grade) and developing (3rd–6th grade) readers. Over two summers, the program was implemented during 4-week full-day camps. Teacher logs were used to track
daily usability and acceptability, while observations and post-implementation interviews further assessed program feasibility. The JOM-L games
were implemented on average 75–78% of the days. Staff reported the
activities as easy to use, developmentally appropriate, and both physically
and cognitively engaging for students. Readiness to implement was high
after training (>90%) and remained stable post-program. Challenges were
related to resources, while common barriers such as lack of time or
demanding preparation were notably absent. The ﬁndings support the
feasibility and usability of the JOM-L program. Future implementation and
scale-up efforts should focus on integrating the program into regular school
curricula while considering varied school-level characteristics.

Optimizing Adherence to Home-Based Physical Activity Programs for
Children: Insights from InPACT at Home and Adaptive Intervention
Strategies
Rebecca Hasson, University of Michigan
Physical activity (PA) is vital for children’s health and development, yet
adherence to structured PA programs, especially in home settings, remains
a challenge. The InPACT at Home program aims to address this by
providing accessible, ﬂexible PA interventions. However, barriers such
as low parental support and skill level hinder adherence, which is crucial
for achieving desired outcomes. Adaptive intervention designs, such as
Sequential Multiple Assignment Randomized Trials (SMART), offer a
dynamic approach to addressing these barriers by tailoring interventions to
individuals’ changing needs over time. This study aimed to evaluate
adherence to the InPACT at Home intervention, identify key barriers and
facilitators, and explore the feasibility of using adaptive intervention
supports to optimize adherence in home-based PA programs for children.
A sequential mixed methods design assessed adherence during a 20-day
InPACT at Home intervention. Participants, aged 7–12 years, were asked
to complete three eight-minute exercise videos, ﬁve days per week.
Adherence and inﬂuencing factors were analyzed using the Consolidated
Framework for Implementation Science Research (CFIR) 2.0. In addition,
a 12-week adaptive intervention was conceptualized using the SMART
design to address adherence challenges. On average, children (n=88)
engaged in 2.2 of the three daily videos, with 23.9% completing all
assigned videos. Facilitators included individual characteristics such as
parental support and compatibility of the intervention design, while
barriers included structural incompatibilities within the home environment
and low adaptability of the intervention. The adaptive intervention (n=30
families) demonstrated feasibility for optimizing adherence, addressing
barriers such as low enjoyment and insufﬁcient support. The InPACT at
Home program successfully added 12–16 minutes of daily PA for children,
though adherence rates highlight the need for enhanced intervention
support. Adaptive interventions, like SMART designs, provide a promising approach to overcoming adherence challenges by tailoring interventions to children’s evolving needs and circumstances.
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

Free Communications: Verbal and Posters
Motor Development Abstracts
Initial psychometric assessment of the Motor Competence Assessment
in American preschoolers
An De Meester, University of South Carolina; Sally Miedema, University
of South Carolina; Ali Brian, University of South Carolina
The Motor Competence Assessment (MCA, Luz et al., 2016) is an
increasingly popular product-oriented tool to assess motor competence
(MC) across different age groups (i.e., early childhood to young adulthood). The MCA includes psychometric properties in European children,
adolescents, and young adults, as well as Brazilian preschoolers. However,
the MCA has not yet been vetted in American preschoolers which is the
purpose of this pilot study. American preschoolers (N = 55; 63.6% boys;
age range: 38-72 months; Mage = 55.04 months, SD = 9.97 months) from an
Early Childhood Center completed three product-oriented MC tests
(i.e., the Test of Gross Motor Development (TGMD)-2, TGMD-3, and
MCA. Z-scores were computed for the overall MC scores for each test
battery as well as the subscales (i.e., locomotion and object control for the
TGMD2 and TGMD3, and locomotion, object control, and stability for the
MCA). Pearson correlations examined concurrent validity of the MCA,
TGMD-2, and TGMD-3 with the inclusion of age and sex, and Cronbach’s
α was used to establish internal consistency. Sex was a non-signiﬁcant
factor among all associations (p > .05), but age associated with all subscales and total scales (p < .001). Total MCA signiﬁcantly correlated with
total TGMD-2 (r = .36, p = .02) and total TGMD-3 (r = .35, p = .02). The
MCA object control and locomotion subscales each associated with the
corresponding subscales from the TGMD-2 (r = .36 and .40, respectively,
p < .02) and the TGMD-3 (r = .35 and .37, respectively, p = .01). Internal
consistency of the total MCA (α = .82), MCA object control (α = .77), and
stability (α = .80) were strong, while the MCA locomotor subscale had a
lower internal consistency (α = .26). The results of this pilot study indicate
preliminary evidence of the concurrent validity and internal consistency of
the MCA and its subscales in American preschoolers. Additional research
in larger samples is required to conﬁrm and expand these ﬁndings.
Early prediction of motor challenges in children: A comparison
between the Ages and Stages Questionnaire and the Little Developmental Coordination Disorder Questionnaire
JERRACO Johnson, University of North Texas; Crystal Alvarez, University of Texas at Arlington; Taelyn Echols, University of North Texas; Kacie
Gray, University of Texas at Arlington; Nicole Cipriano, University of
North Texas; Mary Ocampo-Plazas, University of Texas at Arlington;
Grant Dunn, University of North Texas; Amanda Harrison, University of
North Texas; Addison Harmon, University of North Texas; Mesoma
Onejeme, University of North Texas; James Uanhoro, University of North
Texas; Priscila Tamplain, University of Texas at Arlington
Preschool years are a critical time for optimal development of children’s ﬁne
and gross motor skills for future engagement in physical activity. Early
identiﬁcation of developmental delay is essential for early intervention and
can improve motor outcomes. Most times early identiﬁcation is based on
screening instruments that can detect risk or a potential delay in motor
development. This study compared the ability of two parent screening
instruments to detect preschoolers at risk for Developmental Coordination
Disorder (DCD). Eighty-three parent-child dyads (46 girls and 27 boys,
Mage = 4.1 years) with no known physical impairments participated in the
study. Parents completed the Ages and Stages Questionnaire-Third Edition
(ASQ-3) and the Little Developmental Coordination Disorder Questionnaire
(Little DCDQ). Children’s motor abilities were tested using the Movement
Assessment Battery for Children (MABC-2). Children who scored below the
16th percentile on the MABC-2 were classiﬁed as at-risk for DCD. Sensitivity
and speciﬁcity of the parent questionnaires were examined and referenced
against the MABC-2 classiﬁcations. The sensitivity of the ASQ-3 ﬁne motor
or gross motor scale to detect DCD risk was 68% (1 SD cut-off) and 39% (2
SD cut-off). The speciﬁcity of the ASQ-3 ﬁne motor or gross motor scale was
78% (1 SD cut-off) and 92% (2 SD cut-off). The sensitivity of the Little
DCDQ scale to detect DCD risk was 68% and the speciﬁcity of the Little
DCDQ was 69%. Both the ASQ and Little DCDQ measures had near
adequate sensitivity (70%), yet the ASQ had slightly better speciﬁcity. This
is the ﬁrst study to our knowledge to compare the relationship between the
MABC-2, Little DCDQ, and ASQ-3 in a sample of preschoolers. The Little
DCDQ may be a comparable screening instrument that can be employed at
the population level to identify preschoolers at-risk for DCD who may require
further motor abilities testing and early intervention. The Little DCDQ is both
time and cost-effective for parents.
Automating infant posture detection using deep neural network-based
classiﬁcation
Priya Patel, California State University Fullerton; Harsh Pandya, Woven
by Toyota; Do Kyeong Lee, California State University Fullerton
Previous research has established that infants develop fundamental motor
skills through spontaneous movements during their ﬁrst year of life.
Various methods, including online observation, parent-reported diaries,
video recording, and body orientation tracking using sensors, have been
employed to classify these movements. Each technique has its strengths
and limitations, and the choice often depends on the researcher’s expertise
and the speciﬁc research questions being addressed. Among these, video
recording is widely recognized for its accuracy in capturing infant spontaneous movements. However, it demands extensive effort for frame-byframe behavioral coding, which often limits the observation period to less
than one hour. This study aims to automate the detection of infant postures
in video data using deep neural networks (DNNs), improving annotation
efﬁciency and enabling large-scale motor behavior analysis. For this
purpose, we are using 50 home-recorded videos that captured spontaneous
movements of infants aged 2-months to the walking milestone. Some
participants provided multiple videos at different time points, while others
provided a single video, enriching the training pool and improving model
generalizability. All recordings were taken in home settings. These videos
were annotated using Datavyu as the gold standard for different postures
such as sitting, held by parent, lying on back or tummy, crawling, and
walking, etc. We are using the SlowFast 50 DNN model, a pre-trained
architecture designed to capture both fast, short-term motion and slower,
extended temporal changes. Currently, we are training the model further to
adapt it to our dataset of infant motor behaviors, making it well-suited for
identifying varied postures. We hypothesize that this DNN model will
achieve high classiﬁcation accuracy across postures, especially for complex movements like crawling and standing. We expect this approach to
signiﬁcantly reduce annotation time and improve robustness across varied
home environments. Automating posture annotation addresses the challenge of manual video coding, enhancing the scalability of motor development research. This method has the potential to further detect and
classify movements, which is furthermore labor-intensive in manual

coding. This in turn may facilitate the quantiﬁcation of movements,

Transition from spontaneous movements to walking onset through
behavioral and movement analysis
Do Kyeong Lee, California State University Fullerton; Priya Patel,
California State University Fullerton
How do infants progress from an initial lack of head control to achieving
major motor milestones such as reaching, sitting, and walking? This
remarkable transition is largely driven by the spontaneous movements
exhibited during infancy. Utilizing a variety of research methods—including direct observation, diary tracking, video recording, and sensor monitoring—early studies have identiﬁed rhythmic movements and reviewed their
age of onset, revealing strong correlations with later motor skill development as measured by standard assessment tools. However, a key limitation
of existing studies is the reliance on cross-sectional designs, which lack
longitudinal documentation of changes in infants’ spontaneous movements
over time. This gap makes it challenging to understand how these movements evolve into foundational motor skills. Our case study addresses this
limitation by tracking the evolution of spontaneous movements into fundamental motor skills, providing both foundational and clinical insights into
early motor development. We followed three infants from 1 month of age
until they began walking, conducting monthly assessments that combined
behavioral observations with quantitative movement analysis. Data collection was home-based and included video recordings for behavioral observations and inertial measurement units placed on the arm, leg, and back to
capture movement data. Each session included at least 30 minutes of natural
play. Video data is analyzed for 8 different postures based on their restrictive
and non-restrictive locations, and sensor data is used to quantify the amount
of movement in each posture. The locations where infants play evolve with
their locomotive skills. Younger infants tend to play in restrictive environments, while older infants favor non-restrictive spaces and select their play
areas independently. We also anticipate an increase in different postures
with age and spend progressively more time in postures such as sitting as
they develop trunk control. In terms of movement frequency, we expect
early, frequent spontaneous movements in key postures to exhibit measurable differences in movement patterns across postures over time, reﬂecting
developmental changes. For example, arm movements increase when the
reaching milestone is attained. This study highlights the progression from
infantile movements to motor skills but also offers the potential to bridge
behavioral and movement analyses.
Predictors of perceived competence in young children: Insights from
the CHAMP-ASP stud
Aaron P Wood, University of Michigan; Kara K Palmer, University of
Michigan; Stephanie A Palmer, University of Michigan; Leesi GeorgeKomi, University of Michigan; Karin A Pfeiffer, Michigan State University; Leah E Robinson, University of Michigan
Perceived motor and physical competence are important psychosocial
determinants of physical activity in children and are foundational for
promoting active and healthy lifestyles. Understanding child-level sociodemographic and anthropometric factors that inﬂuence perceived competence is essential for designing tailored interventions to optimize child
development. This study examined how demographic and anthropometric
variables (e.g., age, sex, BMI) are associated with perceived competence in
children enrolled in the CHAMP-Afterschool Program (CHAMP-ASP).
Data were collected as part of the CHAMP-ASP, a cluster-randomized trial
evaluating the efﬁcacy of a mastery climate motor skills intervention.
Participants included children (N = 194; 5-8 years old) from afterschool
programs in Michigan. Baseline assessments included demographic information (age and sex) and anthropometric measures (BMI). Perceived
physical/motor competence were assessed using the Pictorial Scale of
Perceived Competence and Social Acceptance (PSPCSA) and the Digital
Scale of Perceived Motor Competence (DSPMC), respectively. Descriptive statistics showed that mean PSPCSA and DSPMC scores were similar
for boys and girls, with boys scoring M = 3.17 (SD = 0.44) and girls
M = 3.10 (SD = 0.52) on the PSPCSA, and boys scoring M = 3.14 (SD =
0.41) and girls M = 3.13 (SD = 0.42) on the DSPMC. ANOVA results
revealed no signiﬁcant sex differences for either PSPCSA (p = .354) or
DSPMC (p = .911) average scores. Regression analyses indicated that
demographic and anthropometric variables explained a modest amount
of variance in PSPCSA scores (R2 = .054, p = .006), with BMI as the only
signiﬁcant predictor (p < .001). For DSPMC scores, the model was not
statistically signiﬁcant overall (R2 = .026, p = .061), but BMI emerged as a
signiﬁcant predictor (p = .012). These ﬁndings suggest that BMI plays a
role in shaping perceptions of motor competence across both scales, with
no evidence of signiﬁcant sex effects. The results highlight the need to
explore the inﬂuence of physical attributes on competence perceptions and
develop interventions that address these factors to promote equitable
competence development in children. Funding source: National Institutes
of Health (R01-NR-018830)
Physical activity parenting practices, fundamental motor skills, and
MVPA: Structure’s direct and indirect links to positive outcomes
Stephanie Palmer, University of Michigan; Rebecca Hasson, University of
Michigan; Alison Miller, University of Michigan; Anao Zhang, University
of Michigan; Leah Robinson, University of Michigan
Physical activity parenting practices (PAPP) are theoretically linked to
children’s development of physical activity (PA) behaviors and fundamental motor skills (FMS). However, evidence for these relationships
remains limited due to methodological discrepancies. Grounded in bioecological theory and guided by the Integrated Model of Physical Activity
Parenting Practices, this study examined associations between theorybased PAPP and children’s objectively measured FMS and moderate-tovigorous physical activity (MVPA). Additionally, we investigated whether
PAPP mediates the relationship between parents’ PA attributes (e.g., PA
behaviors, self-competence in PA, and PA skills) and children’s FMS and
MVPA. We hypothesized that PAPP would be associated with children’s
FMS and MVPA and would mediate the relationship between parents’ PA
attributes and these outcomes. Parents (N = 55, Mage = 39.64 years, 82.6%
mothers) of children (Mage = 6.36 years, 55.1% female) enrolled in the
Children’s Health Activity Motor Program - Afterschool Program completed three questionnaires: the Determinants of Physical Activity Questionnaire (measuring PA knowledge, self-competence, and skills), the
Godin Leisure Time Physical Activity Questionnaire (measuring parents’
PA behaviors), and the Physical Activity Parenting Practices Questionnaire - Short Form. Subscale scores were calculated for Structure, Autonomy-Promoting, and Controlling PAPP. Children completed the Test of
Gross Motor Development-3 (TGMD-3) and 7-day accelerometry.
TGMD-3 total raw scores and average daily MVPA minutes were retained
for analyses. Linear regression analyses indicated that Structure PAPP was
positively associated with FMS (β = 0.35, CI: [0.13, 0.56], p = .002) and
showed a positive trend with MVPA (β = 0.25, CI: [-0.01, 0.52], p = .063),
while Autonomy-Promoting and Controlling PAPP were not signiﬁcantly
associated with either outcome. Exploratory structural equation modeling
revealed that parents’ PA attributes were indirectly associated with children’s FMS and MVPA via their relationships with Structure and Controlling PAPP (indirect effects: p < .05 to p < .10). These ﬁndings address
critical knowledge gaps about PAPP, highlighting the importance of
structure-related PAPP in fostering positive developmental outcomes and

Changes in functional motor competence and BMI in military college
cadets across three years: A multi-cohort study (2019-2023)
Ryan S. Sacko, The Citadel; David F. Stodden, University of South
Carolina; Bryan M. Terlizzi, The Citadel; T. Cade Abrams, Booz Allen
Hamilton; Giovanna Leone, University of South Carolina; Grace Duncan,
The Citadel; Erin Leonard, The Citadel; Samuel Hymes, The Citadel; Amy
L. Fraley, University of South Carolina
Physical military readiness (PMR) is a core focus promoted in senior
military colleges. Functional motor competence (FMC) demonstrates
gross motor coordination through postural control, locomotor, and object
projection skills and is a signiﬁcant correlate and predictor of PMR.
However, no research has examined whether PMR training in military
colleges impacts Cadets’ FMC. This study examined changes in FMC in
samples from three cohorts of Cadets at a senior military college over
three years. Cohorts of Cadets (N = 136) from 2019-2021, were voluntarily enrolled to participate. Baseline testing occurred prior to the start of
their ﬁrst semester of classes and exit testing occurred in the spring of
their junior year. FMC measurements included Supine-to-Stand time
(STS-sec), standing long jump distance (SLJ-cm), hopping time (7msec), Körperkoordinationstest für Kinder (KTK) balance beam (steps),
throwing speed (TS-mph), throw-catch assessment score (TCA-reps), and
kicking speed (KS-mph). Weight status (BMI) was also assessed. Eight 2
(time) x 3 (cohort) repeated measures ANOVAs assessed changes across
the three years. BMI and KTK balance beam scores increased x = 2.34
(p < .05)
and
4.4
steps
(p < .05)
respectively.
In
contrast,
STS
(x = 0.63 sec; p < .05), SLJ (x = -4.00 cm; p < .05), hopping time
(x = 0.70 sec; p < .05, TS (x = -2.2 mph, p < .05), TCA (x = -0.90 reps;
p < .05) and KS (x = -3.95 mph; p < .05) signiﬁcantly declined. Tukey’s
post hoc tests demonstrated the decline in motor skills was greater in the
second cohort compared to the ﬁrst and third (p < .05). Findings demonstrate a general decrease in FMC within a senior military college
environment across three years. While modes of physical training traditionally promoted in military colleges include group ﬁtness training (PT),
individual ﬁtness training, and intramural sports participation, these
training foci do not seem to facilitate improved FMC across three years.
The decline in FMC metrics (KTK exception) highlights the need for
training interventions that integrate FMC and physical ﬁtness to address
the negative trends in FMC and the notable increase in BMI. Further,
variations in motor skill trajectories between cohorts may reﬂect inconsistent training programs. Tailored PMR training is critical for ensuring
consistent outcomes and mitigating the risks associated with functional
skill deﬁciencies for future military service.
Analysis and validation of commercially available immersive virtual
reality games in typical developing children
Bruna de Souza da Silva, Georgia State University; Grayson Pareizs,
Georgia State University; Bernard Turner, Georgia State University;
Kayla Owen, Georgia State University; Ivette Martinez, Georgia State
University; Rayley Ferrell, Georgia State University; Yuping Chen,
Georgia State University
Immersive virtual reality (IVR) allows players to engage physically and
cognitively within a simulated environment, offering a unique gaming
experience. However, its health implications on pediatrics remain
largely unexplored. This study aimed to examine the effects of ﬁve
IVR games, Fruit Ninja (FN), Tennis (T), Baseball (BB), Bowling (B),
and Beat Saber (BS), on heart rate (HR: max, average, and max change),
total arm movements, percentage of unilateral and bilateral arm movements, and average arm movements per minute in 40 healthy typicallydeveloping children aged 6–12 years (15 male) using the Meta Quest 2
system. Data was collected using a Polar Beat chest monitor, an iPhone
camera, and post-test questionnaires. Repeated ANOVA with paired ttests as the post-hoc paired comparisons were used for data analysis.
Generally, FN and T showed a signiﬁcantly higher max and average HR
as well as max HR change compared to the BB and B games (p < .05).
FN and BS had a signiﬁcantly higher percentage of bilateral arm
movements than T and BB and more arm movements per minute and
total arm movements than T, BB, and B (p < .001). FN also had a
signiﬁcantly lower percentage of unilateral arm movements compared
to all other games (p < .001). BB signiﬁcantly exceeded B in total arm
movements and average arm movements per minute (p < .05). Thirtynine of the 40 children (97.5%) were willing to engage in the games
again. FN was the favorite, receiving 17 votes (42.5%), while B was the
least favored, with 19 votes (47.5%). Our ﬁndings suggest that FN
elicited the largest HR change and promoted the most bilateral arm
movements, whereas BS and FN promoted the most total arm movements. Meta Quest 2 games may improve arm function in children by
encouraging diverse movements, increasing HR, and offering a safe,
engaging environment. IVR may serve as a valuable tool to support
therapy goals while ensuring a fun and motivating experience and
demonstrating potential value for pediatric clients. Funding source:
Field Initiated Program of the National Institute on Disability, Independent Living, and Rehabilitation Research (90IFST0009)
Comparing hip, wrist, and ankle-worn Actigraph accelerometers for
measuring physical activity in children 12-36 months
Tze-Hsuan Wang, Oregon State University; Ameer Helmi, Oregon State
University; Rafael Morales Mayoral, Oregon State University; Lucas Yao,
Oregon State University; April Murray, Oregon State University; Naomi
T. Fitter, Oregon State University; Samuel W. Logan, Oregon State
University
Understanding the relationships among accelerometer placements and the
comparability between age-speciﬁc cut points is essential to enhancing the
accuracy of physical activity measurement for toddlers. This study investigated the relationships amongst activity counts from hip, wrist, and ankle
placements of the ActiGraph GT9X accelerometers in children 12-36
months and assessed the agreement between three sets of hip cut points
(Costa et al., 2014; Trost et al., 2012) and one wrist (Johansson et al., 2015)
cut point. Accelerometer data were collected from nine toddlers (3 girls,
22.2 ± 6.1 months) for three months during weekly 20-minute free-play
sessions. Activity counts were downloaded using speciﬁc epoch lengths
and ﬁlters according to studies validating the cut points. Inter-placement
relationships were examined using Spearman’s rank correlation coefﬁcients. The agreements on estimated time spent in sedentary, light, and
moderate-to-vigorous physical activities (MVPA) between cut points were
assessed using Bland-Altman plots. Ankle data was not analyzed for this
purpose because of lacking validated cut points. Epoch-by-epoch interplacement correlation coefﬁcients were strongest between hip and ankle
vector magnitude counts (rs =.88), while relatively smaller between wrist
and hip (rs =.65) and wrist and ankle (rs =.60). The Bland-Altman plots
indicated that time estimates in sedentary, light, and MVPA were considerably different between most cut points. Among the four sets of cut points,
the hip Costa-vector magnitude cut points estimated the highest average
sedentary time per session (8.49 ± 3.73 min). In contrast, the wrist
Johansson cut points yielded the highest amount of time spent in MVPA
(4.19 ± 2.40 min). We recommend examining the feasibility of validating
ankle cut points for toddlers given the strong correlation between hip and
