Inﬂuence of throwing speed on performance in the throw-catch
assessment
Bryan M. Terlizzi, The Citadel; T. Cade Abrams, Booz Allen Hamilton;
Ryan S. Sacko, The Citadel; Stephen G. Grifﬁn, Georgia Southern
University; Giovanna E. Leone, University of South Carolina; Ozgur
Altintas, University of South Carolina; Amy L. Fraley, University of South
Carolina; David F. Stodden, University of South Carolina
The throw-catch assessment (TCA) is a product-oriented assessment that
evaluates throwing and catching skill in an open context, enabling
performers to exercise ﬂexibility in throwing and catching strategies to
maximize performance. The TCA requires performers to repeatedly
throw a tennis ball at a wall and catch the rebound as many times as
possible within 30 seconds, from a distance of three times their standing
height. Terlizzi et al. (2024) provided preliminary cross-sectional evidence for the developmental validity of the TCA in boys and girls aged 822 years. However, the TCA has not been evaluated against other wellestablished product-oriented assessments of throwing or catching skill.
TCA performance is predicated on the rapid completion of throw-catch
combinations. While the 30s task constraint may incentivize higher
throwing speeds to decrease the time of each throw-catch combination,
throwing speed during the TCA and its relationship to TCA score and
maximum throwing speed is not known. Data from 116 young adults
(Nfemales = 24, Mage = 22.0 ± 3.4) were analyzed, including maximum
throwing speed (MTS; m/s, Stalker, Inc.) from 5 trials, maximum TCA
score from two trials, and average throwing speed (ATS; m/s) during the
highest scored TCA trial. We examined relationships among MTS, TCA
score and ATS using Pearson’s bivariate correlations. The mean TCA
score for the sample was 13.8 ± 3.5 throw-catch combinations, while
mean values for MTS and ATS were 26.2 ± 5.8 and 13.7 ± 2.8 m/s,
respectively. TCA score demonstrated strong correlations with both MTS
(r = .606, p < .001) and ATS (r = .612, p < .001). Additionally, MTS and
ATS were strongly correlated (r = .807, p < .001). Regardless of MTS,
participants utilized similar percentages of their MTS during the TCA.
These ﬁndings suggest that throwing at higher speeds enhances TCA
performance, further validating the TCA as a measure of throwing skill,
particularly in relation to participants’ MTS capability, a developmentally
valid indicator of throwing proﬁciency. Future research should investigate how TCA performance is impacted or constrained by participants’
catching skill.
Exploring the relationship between children’s movement attributes
and parents’ perceptions of their adherence to the physical activity
guidelines
Stephanie Palmer, University of Michigan; Leesi George-Komi, University of Michigan; Kara Palmer, University of Michigan; Aaron Wood,
University of Michigan; Leah Robinson, University of Michigan
Parents are frequently asked to report their children’s physical activity
(PA) due to their direct involvement in daily routines. However, the
accuracy of these reports may be inﬂuenced by varying interpretations
of PA behaviors and parents’ perceptions of their child’s movement
characteristics, such as motor competence (MC). This study investigated the relationships between children’s objectively measured moderateto-vigorous physical activity (MVPA), motor competence, and parents’
perceptions of their child’s adherence to MVPA guidelines. Parents
(N = 48; 94% mothers) estimated their children’s weekday and weekend
PA behaviors using categorical data (1 = ≤30 min; 6 = ≥2.5 hrs), which
were converted into a binary variable (0 = < 60 minutes; 1 = ≥60
minutes). Children’s MVPA was objectively measured using 7-day
accelerometry, and MC was assessed with the Test of Gross Motor
Development-3 (TGMD-3). Children achieving ≥60 minutes of MVPA
or scoring ‘Average’ or higher on the TGMD-3 were coded as 1, while
lower scores were coded as 0. Logistic regression models estimated the
odds of parents accurately reporting their child’s MVPA adherence,
controlling for child sex. Separate models used children’s objectively
measured MVPA adherence and MC as independent variables. Results
showed no signiﬁcant relationship between children’s measured MVPA
adherence and parents’ reports of weekday or weekend PA. However,
parents of children with average or better locomotor skills were 1.80
times more likely to report that their child met MVPA guidelines
through vigorous PA on weekdays (OR = 1.80, 95% CI [0.26, 3.34],
p = .022) compared to parents of children with below-average locomotor skills. These ﬁndings suggest that parents may rely on visible motor
skills rather than actual PA levels when assessing their children’s PA
behaviors. Future research should explore these implications to enhance
the accuracy of parental reporting and better inform interventions aimed
at promoting children’s physical activity. Funding source: NHLBI1R01HL132979; Michigan Institue for Clinical and Health Research
(K12TR004374)
Preliminary evaluation of sticky mittens training on reaching ability in
infants with Down syndrome
Matthew Beerse, University of Dayton; Robert Zeid, Georgia State
University; Amy Talboy, Emory University; Seyda Ozcaliskan, Georgia
State University; Jianhua Wu, Georgia State University
Infants with Down syndrome (DS) may show a two-month delay in
reaching motor development. Early interventions such as the “sticky
mittens” training has been shown to improve reaching development for
typically developing children but has yet to be implemented in infants
with DS. Therefore, this study aimed to evaluate the effect of “sticky
mittens” training on reaching development of infants with DS. Seventeen
infants with DS have engaged in sticky mittens training, a subset of
eleven participants (Mage of 8.36 ± 1.6 months) were analyzed for this
abstract. Over a two-month period, parents administered the training eight
minutes per day, ﬁve days a week by presenting various toys at an armlength distance from the infant. Three minutes of video data was collected
pre-training (T1), post-training (T2), and 5-months post-training (T3)
while researchers held toys in front of the infant to assess their reaching
behavior. OpenPose, a markerless computer vision software, was used to
identify the wrist of the reaching arm. Reaching behaviors were visually
coded and were deﬁned as starting when the hand or trunk initiated
movement in attempt to reach the toy and ended when a grasp or touch of
the toy occurred. Kinematic variables of the wrist were analyzed including the straightness of the reach, peak velocity, peak acceleration, and
peak deceleration. One-way (Time) repeated ANOVAs were conducted
to compare performance among T1, T2, and T3. Peak velocity decreased
across time (p = .048, η2 = .241). Large effect sizes were also found for
straightness (η2 = .132), and peak deceleration (η2 = .164), as well as
moderate effect sizes for number of reaches (η2 = .092) and peak acceleration (η2 = .095). Generally, the straightness of the reach improved
from T1 to T3. Infants completed more reaches from T1 to T2 to T3, and
wrist peak velocity, acceleration, and deceleration decreased from T1 to
T2 to T3. These results suggest that the “sticky mittens” training may
have the potential to accelerate the development of reaching in infants

Effect of treadmill training on spatiotemporal gait parameters in
infants with Down syndrome
Yeon-Joo Kang, Georgia State University; Robert Zeid, Georgia State
University; Alexandre Dos Santos Kotarski, Georgia State University;
Amy Talboy, Emory University; Seyda Ozcaliskan, Georgia State University; Jianhua Wu, Georgia State University
Down syndrome (DS) is a genetic condition characterized by development delays compared to typically developing infants. Studies have
shown that treadmill intervention can promote earlier walking onset and
improve walking quality. However, it remains unclear to what degree
gait patterns differ between those with and without treadmill training.
This study aimed to examine the effect of treadmill training on spatiotemporal gait pattern in infants with DS. Our preliminary study included
12 participants: seven in the training group and ﬁve in the control group.
For the training group, the parents administered the treadmill intervention in their homes for 8 min/day, 5 days/week, with a belt speed starting
at 0.1 m/s and increasing over time until the infants walked three steps
independently. We invited the participants to our lab for a gait assessment at T1 (2 months after walking onset) and T2 (8 months after
walking onset). We used a 9-camera Vicon motion capture system and
the Vicon lower-body model to collect the gait data when the infant
walked barefoot over a 6-m walkway. Spatiotemporal variables included normalized stride and step length, stride and step time, step width,
and swing percentage. Two-way (2 group × 2 time) mixed ANOVA
indicated that both groups increased stride and step length and swing
percentage and decreased stride and step time and step width from T1 to
T2. Also, the training group showed a greater decrease in step width
from T1 to T2 than the control group (a moderate effect size). For the
chronological age, the training group at T2 was close to the control
group at T1 (about 27 months). Our secondary analysis was conducted
between the two groups at this age via t-tests. We found that at about 27
months, the training group exhibited more advanced gait patterns than
the control group such that the training group produced higher stride and
step length and swing percentage and lower stride and step time and step
width. These ﬁndings indicate that treadmill training enhances walking
development in infants with DS by accelerating their progress and
leading to more reﬁned gait patterns.
Association between parent reported gross motor development milestones and motor abilities in preschoolers born during or after
COVID-19
Jerraco Johnson, University of North Texas; Grant Dunn, University of
North Texas; Taelyn Echols, University of North Texas; Amanda Harrison, University of North Texas; Mesoma Onejeme, University of North
Texas; Addison Harmon, University of Texas at Arlington
Previous research suggests the achievement of gross motor milestones in
infancy are related to several health outcomes (e.g., obesity, physical
activity) and may serve as an early indicator of developmental delay.
However, few studies have examined the achievement of gross motor
milestones and motor competence of children born since COVID-19. The
purpose of this study was to (1) compare the age of attainment of three
gross motor developmental milestones (sitting without support, crawling,
and walking alone) in preschoolers born since COVID-19 with the World
Health Organization (WHO) developed normal age ranges, and (2) to
examine the relationship between achievement of motor milestones and
children’s current motor abilities. Children were eligible if they had no
known physical impairments. Parents reported their child’s retroactive
achievement of milestones. The children’s motor abilities were assessed
using the Movement Assessment Battery for Children (MABC-2). Sixtyfour children (37 girls and 27 boys, Mage = 4.1) were included in the ﬁnal
sample. Forty-eight children (75%) scored above the 16th percentile and
were classiﬁed as not at-risk of developmental coordination disorder
(DCD), while 16 children (25%) in this sample were classiﬁed as at-risk
for DCD and scored below the 16th percentile on the MABC-2. Overall,
the samples mean attainment of sitting unassisted fell within the 50th
percentile of the WHO referenced norms, crawling fell within the 25th
percentile, and walking ranged between the 50th and 75th percentile.
Walking was not signiﬁcantly associated with any outcomes of the
MABC-2, while crawling was related to children’s balance score
(p = .047). Sitting unassisted was signiﬁcantly associated with children’s
manual dexterity (p = .035) and balance (p = .041) scores. Future studies
should explore the longitudinal associations of gross motor milestones and
health outcomes of children born since COVID-19 as they transition across
childhood.
How do you balance? Concurrent validity of a balance strategy direct
observation protocol for static balance performance
Devin Ellison, University of Tennessee; Kevin Becker, University of
Tennessee; Kipp Webster, University of Tennessee; Dimetrius Brandon,
University of Tennessee; Abigail Lovingood, University of Tennessee
Static balance is a key aspect of motor development and integral to
complex motor skills. However, accurately evaluating single-leg balance performance requires a force plate, which may not always be
accessible, limiting its use in ﬁeld-based assessments. Therefore, we
developed the Assessment for Balance by Strategy (ABBS) direct
observation protocol to examine arm and leg motion and positioning
during a one-legged stork stance balance task to address this limitation.
This study aimed to provide initial concurrent validity to this direct
observation protocol compared to gold-standard force plate COP measures. Fifty-nine children (Mage = 4.18 ± 0.61 yrs; 45% male) participated, each performing a one-legged stork stance balance task twice on
each leg. COP data was captured with an AMTI force plate (1000 Hz)
and was video recorded (60 Hz). Using Noraxon software, we calculated
balance time, average COP velocity, and normalized 95% conﬁdence
ellipse area. ABBS was assessed by video recording; four components
were independently assessed: arm position (above or below the shoulder), leg position (front, back, side), and arm and leg motion (little-to-no
movement and large-to-continuous movement). A multivariate analysis
of variance was used to examine the relationship between ABBS scores
and COP parameters. 53% of subjects engaged in a raised arm position,
72% engaged in a back leg position, and 64% exhibited little to no
motion during balance. Arm position impacted balance time (p = .045)
and average COP velocity (p = .033). Subjects experienced longer
balance times and a lower average velocity when their arms were below
the shoulder. Arm movement (p = .024) was associated with a 95%
conﬁdence ellipse area, showing that larger and more frequent arm
movement corresponded to a larger ellipse area. Less leg movement was
also signiﬁcantly associated with longer balance times (p = .028) and a
smaller ellipse area (p = .002). These ﬁndings suggest that ABBS can be
a valid tool to assess static balance performance qualitatively. This
simple, direct observation protocol is signiﬁcantly associated with COP
parameters, speciﬁcally balance time and 95% conﬁdence ellipse area.
The study provides preliminary evidence that the ABBS may be
included in ﬁeld-based measures. Funding source: University of
Tennessee.

Using pose estimation to quantify the area of infant spontaneous
movement
Marcelo Ramon Rosales, The Ohio State University; Jill Heathcock, The
Ohio State University
Pose Estimation (PE) is a sophisticated technology that can be used to
objectively quantify movement from video recordings. Recently, our lab
completed a proof of concept for PE’s ability to quantify infant limb
movements in supine with promising results. While progress has been
made, understanding PE methodology needs to be assessed to understand
the capabilities and limitations in markerless motion capture. The purpose
of this study was to assess the proportion of PE data that was extraneous
upon visual inspection. Additionally, a convex hull analysis was used to
quantify the movement area covered for each limb. Twenty-six infants
(age: 9-18 weeks) participating in a longitudinal study were analyzed
using Mediapipe’s full body PE model, python, and MATLAB. The
infants were from two populations: perinatal stroke (PS) (n = 11) and
those with typical development (TD) (n = 15). Individual participants
contributed 8–48 videos. Infants’ spontaneous movements were recorded
in supine for eight 30-second trials. A movement expert visually inspected the PE tracing and documented if the video had movement
artifacts or poor tracing. A convex hull analysis was used to quantify
the area that infants moved both arms and legs, separately. Out of the 755
video ﬁles, 528 ﬁles (70%) correctly applied the PE model according to
visual inspection with no additional process. Results from the convex hull
analysis showed that for infants with TD, median right (R) and left (L),
arm movement covered 0.054 m2 and 0.052 m2, respectively. For PS,
median arm movement covered 0.063 m2 (R) and 0.060 m2 (L). For TD,
median leg movement occurred 0.046 m2 (R) and 0.051 m2 (L). For PS,
median leg movement occurred 0.061 m2 (R) and 0.062 m2 (L). PE with a
single camera has high potential to be used on large video repositories to
quantitively describe infant movement. Number of movements and the
2D area in which they are moving are possible outcomes of this analysis.
These automated data analysis techniques may be used in future studies to
describe infant motor development in populations with and without motor
disabilities from retrospective repositories, home videos, and prospective
studies. Funding source: The Foundation for Physical Therapy’s Pediatric
Research Grant; Ohio State University Presidential Post Doctoral
Fellowship.
Increased overlap in push-off and collision forces during step-to-step
transition in older adults: A potential mechanism for increased
walking energetic cost
Elham Alijanpour, Old Dominion University; Daniel Russell, Old Dominion University
As humans age, the preferred walking speed declines, while the
energetic cost of walking increases. During gait, energy is lost during
the collision with the ground, which is compensated by inputting energy
to the body during the push-off. Modeling predicts that preemptively
pushing off before front foot contact with the ground reduces the
energetic cost of walking. The overlap between push-off and collision
forces can be quantiﬁed by Sequence (S) index, where 0 indicates pushoff completed before collision and 0.5 indicates the push-off and
collision occur during the same period. Young adults complete most
of the push-off before the collision (S index around 0.047) with the
overlap decreasing with speed, while a delayed and smaller push-off
force has been found in older adults. Given the increased energetic cost
of walking in older adults we hypothesize that older adults will show a
higher S index across walking speeds. We quantiﬁed the overlap
between push-off and collision forces in young and older adults. Seven
young and seven older adults walked on a split-belt treadmill with speed
incrementally increased from 0.5 to 1.7 m/s. The overlap was quantiﬁed
as S index based on Adamczyk and Kuo (2009). There was a signiﬁcant
main effect of age, F(1,11) = 12.68, p < .004, h = 0.53 and a signiﬁcant
main effect of walking speed, F(6,66) = 79.58, p < .001, h = 0.88. Increasing walking speed resulted in decreasing overlap in push-off and
collision forces (lower S index). Across all speeds, older adults walked
with signiﬁcantly greater overlap of collision and push-off forces. Older
adults achieved the same S index as young adults, but at a higher speed
(e.g., 1.3 m/s in older adults compared to 1.1 m/s in young adults). Even
though older adults can walk with less overlap in push-off and collision
forces, they walk with greater overlap in push-off and collision forces
than young adults, at the same speed. This likely contributes to the
higher energetic cost of walking with age. These ﬁndings may guide
interventions to improve gait in aging, supporting mobility and independence in older adults.
Imaginative play in a structured physical education program: Does it
matter?
Candice Howard-Smith, Troy University; Faith Brown, Troy University;
Bryant Sanford, Troy University
Structured physical education programs (SPEP) signiﬁcantly contribute to
a preschool-aged child’s fundamental motor skill (FMS) development.
Preschools with SPEP focus on instructional time of FMS using ageappropriate activities and equipment. Imaginative play (IP), also known as
pretend play encourages gross and ﬁne motor skill development, hand-eye
coordination and balance. It can also help children develop other skills,
such as problem-solving, language, and social skills. The present student
examined the impact of IP offered during a SPEP on FMS performance. It
was hypothesized that students in both programs (SPEP-IP with imaginative play and SPEP) would progress in FMS performance, but those in the
SPEP-IP would perform better. Preschool-aged children (N = 80, Mage =
5.1 ± 1.3 months) enrolled in a SPEP participated in the study. A pretest
and posttest using the Test of Gross Motor Development-3 (TGMD) was
conducted and students participated in a SPEP for eight weeks with the
intervention group utilizing IP (SPEP-IP, n = 41). A t-test was used to
compare mean scores on the TGMD-3 subscales (locomotor and ball
skills). When locomotor scores were compared, the SPEP-IP group
performed
better
than
the
control
group
(Locomotor:
SPEP-IP
40.9 ± 0.6, p = .0007; SPEP 37.3 ± 0.7, p = .03; Ball Skills: SPEP-IP
37.4 ± 0.5, p = .009; SPEP 38.2 ± 1.2, p = .04). While this study has a lot
of data to analyze and topics to explore, there are important aspects of IP to
consider. IP is beneﬁcial and should be integrated into a well SPEP with
clear goals and guidance from teachers to ensure children are practicing a
variety of motor skills. Age appropriateness is important also as the
complexity of IP activities should be tailored to the developmental level
of the preschoolers. Included in a SPEP, IP can be effective and beneﬁcial
while promoting physical activity in a fun way for preschool-aged children
in both the physical education and classroom environments. Researchers
intend to further investigate and compare speciﬁc locomotor and ball skills
and continue to implement imaginative play in SPEP.
Shaping daily spontaneous movement: the role of parental support
and birth Order on Infants’ motor development
Aron Rosas, California State University Fullerton; Jo-Chen Shih, California State University Fullerton; Ivan Villegas, California State University Fullerton; Alicia Springﬁeld, California State University Fullerton;
Do Kyeong Lee, California State University Fullerton
Environments in infants’ ﬁrst years shape their motor behaviors and
movement opportunities. The study focused on different environmental
contexts and their inﬂuence on achievement/onsets of gross motor skills
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS

--- [Página 18] ---
like sitting, crawling, and cruising milestones. It compared the experiences, timing, and progression of ﬁrst-born and later-born infants. In a
similar study, it was found that ﬁrstborn children appeared to achieve ﬁne
motor skills faster. However, there were no signiﬁcant differences
between the achievement of gross motor skills of the ﬁrst and later-born
(Krombholz, 2023). Eighteen (8 males, 11 ﬁrst-born) were observed
monthly during natural play sessions in their homes, with an average
session duration of 29.26 minutes. Home visits began as early as one
month of age and continued until 12 months (average of 9.27 visits per
infant). Video recordings were analyzed frame by frame using Datayvu
software to code eight distinct play locations, categorized as restricted or
non-restricted areas. The proportion of time spent in each location was
calculated for each visit. Milestone onset was assessed using the BayleyIII Motor Scale. The analysis revealed that while infants spent more time
in non-restricted areas as they grew older, birth order had no signiﬁcant
impact on this behavior. Speciﬁcally, being a ﬁrst-born or second-born
did not inﬂuence how much time infants engaged in their spaces. When
examining speciﬁc locations, older infants were likelier to play on the
ﬂoor and stairs, whereas younger infants spent more time on the couch or
in the baby bed. In restricted areas, parents of younger infants were more
likely to hold their babies, but no notable age-related differences were
found in the use of other speciﬁed locations. Additionally, birth order did
not inﬂuence the timing of major milestone achievements. While ﬁrstborn infants tended to reach sitting, crawling, and cruising milestones,
there were no signiﬁcant differences in milestone timing based on birth
order. Infants become more independent in non-restricted play areas as
they age, with no signiﬁcant inﬂuence of birth order on spatial use or
developmental milestones. Motor progression appears primarily driven
by experience and age rather than sibling status.
Teaching PE to students with disabilities: How does self-efﬁcacy differ
among Texas teachers?
Jamie York, Texas Woman’s University; Kerri Staples, Texas Woman’s
University
Due to an increasing call for greater inclusivity in public schools,
integrated physical education (PE) classes are growing in prevalence
across the United States. Teachers are responsible for providing high
quality instruction to students with and without disabilities in their PE
classes; however, current literature indicates that PE teachers feel inadequately prepared to meet all of their students’ needs. Consequently, both
students and teachers are negatively impacted by such experiences.
Therefore, this study aimed to explore the self-efﬁcacy of in-service Texas
PE teachers and its relation to their teaching settings. Researchers used
snowball recruitment methods to collect survey data from 55 general
(n = 34), adapted (n = 12), and integrated (n = 9) PE teachers. A researcherdeveloped questionnaire and the Teaching Students with Disabilities
Efﬁcacy Scale were used to quantify teachers’ self-efﬁcacy among ﬁve
subscales (Instruction, Professionalism, Teacher Support, Classroom Management, and Related Duties). Descriptive statistics indicated that adapted
PE teachers were more self-efﬁcacious on all subscales than the others.
Non-parametric and post hoc testing revealed that the general and adapted
PE groups’ self-efﬁcacy differed signiﬁcantly regarding the Instruction
subscale only (χ2 (2) = 6.332, p = .042). Although no additional signiﬁcant
differences were found, integrated PE teachers were less self-efﬁcacious on
three of the ﬁve subscales than the other teacher groups. This is perhaps
more concerning due to the classes that they teach, such as Uniﬁed or
Partner PE, in which students with and without disabilities are taught
simultaneously. Although the integrated PE teachers reported teaching a
similar range of disability categories as the adapted PE teachers, they did so
with fewer years of hands-on experiences when compared to the adapted
PE teachers (M = 7.29 ± 7.23; M = 12.17 ± 8.60). This study’s ﬁndings
emphasize the critical need for exploring how the reduced self-efﬁcacy
of integrated PE teachers impacts student outcomes in PE.
Examining gender and BMI differences in MABC-3 performance
among 18-25 year olds
Dimetrius Brandon, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; Abigail Lovingood, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; Devin Ellison, University of
Tennessee, Knoxville; Taliah J. Carlson, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; E. Kipling Webster, University of Tennessee, Knoxville
Motor skills are important for lifelong health and are often assessed in
childhood, with well-documented gender differences between males and
females. Furthermore, higher body mass index (BMI) has been linked to
limitations that hinder motor skill development compared to those with
lower BMI. However, how these differences persist into early adulthood is
less understood. Therefore, the purpose of this study is to examine gender
and BMI differences in motor skills among adults, aged 18–25 years, using
the Movement Assessment Battery for Children-3 (MABC-3). A total of
131 participants (46% male, Mage = 21.86 ± 1.87 years) from the Southeastern and Midwestern portion of the US completed Age B and three of
the MABC-3. Participants self-reported demographic information. Height
and weight were objectively measured; BMI was calculated (kg/m2) and
weight status was classiﬁed with BMI >25 as overweight and <25 as
normal weight. Independent t-tests were conducted to assess gender and
BMI differences across the MABC-3 subscales (manual dexterity, aiming
and catching, and balance) and overall motor performance. Age-speciﬁc
percentiles were used for all analyses. The results found signiﬁcant
differences in manual dexterity scores, t(123) = -4.97, p < .001, with higher
scores
for
females
(M = 45.41 ± 25.26)
compared
to
males
(M = 25.07 ± 20.63). Similarly, females (M = 50.59 ± 24.03) demonstrated
signiﬁcantly higher balance scores, t(124) = -2.43, p = .02, compared to
males (M = 40.19 ± 23.86). Conversely, males (M = 60.59 ± 21.21) outperformed
females
(M = 42.09 ± 24.16)
in
aiming
and
catching,
t(124) = 4.53, p < .001. No signiﬁcant gender differences were found in
overall motor performance percentiles (p = .055). Additionally, no signiﬁcant BMI group differences in manual dexterity, t(121) = 0.21, p = .84,
aiming and catching, t(121) = -0.13, p = .90, and overall motor performance, t(121) = 0.71, p = .48 were found. Participants with normal weight
(M = 51.65 ± 22.80) had signiﬁcantly higher balance percentile scores than
those classiﬁed as overweight (M = 40.76 ± 25.02), t(121) = 2.52, p = .013.
These ﬁndings emphasize the need to further explore how gender and
weight inﬂuence motor skills in adults and how these relationships are
mediated by physical activity, health-related ﬁtness, and other health
outcomes.
Comparing TGMD-3 and MABC-3 performance in preschool-age
children’s motor skills
Dimetrius Brandon, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; Abigail Lovingood, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; Devin Ellison, University of
Tennessee, Knoxville; Dawn P. Coe, University of Tennessee, Knoxville;
E. Kipling Webster, University of Tennessee, Knoxville
Motor competence is a complex construct that is comprised of multiple
elements that reﬂect a degree of proﬁciency in performing a range of
skills. The measurement of fundamental motor skills (FMS) is likely not
explained by a single measurement and a holistic approach would suggest
examining a range of skills to better understand early skills acquisition.
Popular FMS assessments in early childhood include the Test of Gross
Motor Development (TGMD) or the Movement Assessment Battery for
Children (MABC), which focus on process- and product-oriented outcomes of FMS. The purpose of this study is to examine the concurrent
validity of the MABC-3 and TGMD-3 in FMS performance among
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025

preschool children. Twenty-seven preschool-age children (52% boys,
Mage = 4.12 ± .49 years) completed the TGMD-3 and MABC-3 assessment. The TGMD-3 subscales (locomotor and ball skills) and MABC-3
subscales (manual dexterity, aiming and catching, balance) were converted to age-speciﬁc percentiles, including total motor percentile scores
for each assessment. Pearson and Spearman’s rho correlations were
performed to evaluate the association between TGMD-3 and MABC-3
percentiles, including their respective subscales. In this sample, performance on the TGMD-3 was overall below average, 27th percentile
(M = 27.85 ± 23.71;
locomotor,
M = 20.77 ± 20.93;
ball
skills,
M = 45.35 ± 27.38), while performance on the MABC-3 was closer to
average overall in the 48th percentile (M = 48.19 ± 26.90; manual dexterity, M = 55.74 ± 26.19; aiming and catching, M = 50.67 ± 27.12; balance, M = 46.30 ± 31.13). There were no signiﬁcant associations between
total TGMD-3 and total MABC-3 scores (rs = -.08, p = .69). Additionally,
no signiﬁcant correlations between ball skills and aiming and catching
(p = .14) or manual dexterity (p = .34). However, a signiﬁcant negative
correlation was found between ball skills and balance (rs = -.42, p = .03).
Furthermore, no signiﬁcant relationships were found between locomotor
scores and aiming and catching (p = .74), manual dexterity (p = .85), or
balance (p = .41). Results show limited overlap between the TGMD-3 and
MABC-3 in assessing FMS in preschool-age children, with only one
signiﬁcant correlation found. These results highlight the distinct approach
each assessment takes in evaluating FMS and underscores the importance
of selecting appropriate instruments based on the research question to
fully understand FMS performance.
Impact of a classroom teacher-led gross motor intervention on automaticity of skill learning
Sally Miedema, University of South Carolina; Jeremy Lewis, University of
South Carolina; Jaqueline Goodway, Michigan State University; Ali
Brian, University of South Carolina
Fundamental motor skills (FMS) are historically measured through
process
(e.g.,
TGMD,
developmental
sequences)
or
product
(e.g., speed or distance) measures. Automaticity refers to how quickly
one can process and perform a skill. However, automaticity has not been
examined speciﬁcally in motor skill development. Therefore, the purpose
of this study was to examine the effects of a nine-week classroom teacherled motor skill intervention on changes in TGMD-2 trial performance and
automaticity scores. We randomly assigned children (N = 114; girls = 64;
Mage = 4.72, SD = .58 years) and teachers (N = 10) from urban Head Start
centers in the midwestern United States to intervention (children = 57;
teachers = 5) and control (children = 57; teachers = 5) conditions. We
calculated automaticity as the time from the assessor stated “go” to the
ﬁrst initiated movement of the skill in seconds. For TGMD-2 trial
performance, we utilized a correct vs. incorrect trial score (1, 0) based
upon understanding of the skill and not the quality of the movement. To
assess our aims, we conducted multiple 2 Group x 2 Sex ANOVAs using
change scores of TGMD-2 trial performance and automaticity for locomotor (LOC) and object control (OC) subscales. For automaticity of OC
all outcomes were non-signiﬁcant (p > .05) however, there was a Sex x
Group interaction which approached signiﬁcance (p < .10, ηp
2 = .03). For
OC correct trials, there was a signiﬁcant main effect of group (p < .001,
hp
2 = .16) with all else being non-signiﬁcant (p > .05). For automaticity of
LOC, there was a signiﬁcant main effect of group (p < .001, hp
2 = .10)
with all else being non-signiﬁcant (p > .05). For LOC correct trials, there
was a signiﬁcant main effect of group (p < .001, hp
2 = .26), a near main
effect of sex (p = .051, hp
2 = .04) but no signiﬁcant interaction (p > .05).
Overall, intervention impacted automaticity and correct TGMD-2 trials.
Future research should explore impact longitudinally and across various
skill types.
Examining the role of static balance in predicting fundamental motor
skill performance in preschool-aged children
Abigail Lovingood, University of Tennessee; Dimetrius Brandon, University of Tennessee; Devin Ellison, University of Tennessee; Kevin A. Becker,
University of Tennessee; E. Kipling Webster, University of Tennessee
Balance is a multifaceted component of motor skill development that
plays a critical role in coordinating movements and maintaining stability.
It’s a crucial aspect of fundamental motor skills (FMS) performance,
critical for lifelong physical activity. This study aims to determine how
static balance inﬂuences FMS performance in preschool-aged children.
Participants were 67 preschool-aged children (46.3% male; Mage = 49.26
± 8.03 months) recruited from the Southeastern US. Each child performed a one-legged stork stance balance task twice on each leg. Balance
performance was measured objectively via product-oriented center of
pressure (COP) values captured at 1000 Hz on an AMTI force plate and
was video recorded. Key balance metrics, including balance time, average
velocity, and normalized 95% conﬁdence ellipse area, were determined
using Noraxon software. Preferred leg was deﬁned as the leg which
produced the longest balance time. Participants also completed the
process-oriented Test of Gross Motor Development – 3rd edition
(TGMD-3); total raw scores and subscale scores (locomotor, ball skills)
were evaluated. Multiple regressions were performed to identify which
COP measures signiﬁcantly predicted TGMD-3 raw scores and subscales.
Children in this sample had on average low FMS performance (22nd
percentile) and performed the static balance task for 10.66 ± 7.55 seconds
on their preferred side and 6.29 ± 5.14 seconds on their non-preferred
side. The only signiﬁcant predictor for overall TGMD-3 scores was the
non-preferred
side
normalized
95%
conﬁdence
ellipse
area
(F(1,53) = 4.003, p = .05), accounting for 7% of the variance (β = -
0.001). Additionally, locomotor raw scores were signiﬁcantly predicted
by
the
non-preferred
side
normalized
95%
conﬁdence
ellipse
(F(1,53) = 5.789, p = .02), explaining 9.8% of the variance (β = -
0.001). No signiﬁcant predictors were found for ball skills raw scores
(p > .05). This study provides preliminary evidence on how static balance
performance contributes to a range of motor skills, speciﬁcally locomotor
skills in early childhood. Additionally, this work provides evidence that
more complex COP measures add value to balance assessments beyond
recording just balance time, which is the traditional methodology in ﬁeldbased assessments. More work is needed to expand on how early balance
strategies and performance may inﬂuence and support early FMS
promotion.
Power mobility device use and sleep health of toddlers with cerebral
palsy: A pilot study
Samuel W. Logan, Oregon State University; Bethany M. Sloane, Oregon
State University; John R. Sy, Oregon State University; Ruth K. Brombach,
Oregon State University; Avani Stevens-Rose, Oregon State University;
Jessica R. Dietch, Oregon State University
Children with cerebral palsy (CP) experience higher rates of sleep disturbances that include delayed sleep onset, frequent night wakings, and
reduced overall sleep duration. A complex interaction between motor
impairments and other factors disrupts sleep and highlights a need for
interventions that address motor function and sleep health. Power mobility
device use may affect sleep health due to the cognitive load associated with
self-initiated mobility. One previous study (Tefft et al., 2011) found a
positive association between power mobility device use and sleep health.
This pilot study takes the next step in this line of inquiry. Toddlers with CP
(N = 12, Mage = 19.3 ± 6.2 months) completed a 12-week power mobility
intervention using the Explorer Mini device. Caregivers reported the
number of days the Explorer Mini was used. Following the intervention,
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S16
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 20] ---
caregivers were prompted to think about the days when their child used the
Explorer Mini and answer questions using a Likert scale ranging from 0 to
4. Higher values indicated more favorable sleep when the Explorer Mini
was used. Questions asked included 1) sleep onset latency, 2) sleep
duration, and 3) number of awakenings for naps or overnight sleep.
Toddlers were considered to have “stable/worse sleep” (n = 5) if caregivers
endorsed no difference or worse sleep health on nights after using the
device and “better sleep” (n = 7) if caregivers endorsed better sleep health
on any of the questions on nights after using the device. An independent
samples Mann-Whitney U test was calculated to determine if signiﬁcant
differences existed between sleep groups on the total number of days the
Explorer Mini was used. A Mann-Whitney U test indicated a medium
effect size difference between groups (r = .35) though it was not statistically signiﬁcant (p = .238). The stable/worse sleep group reported
22.3 ± 12.1 days and the better sleep group reported 34.0 ± 17.1 days of
Explorer Mini use. The effect of power mobility use on sleep health of
toddlers with CP remains unclear. The medium effect size suggests this
topic warrants further examination. Future research should address study
limitations such as small sample size and method of sleep assessment.
Funding source: John C. Erkkila, MD, Endowment for Health and Human
Performance.
Exploring contextual inﬂuences on school-based physical activity
programs for youth with disabilities: A scoping review
Darice Brooks, Michigan State University; Youngjun Lee, Michigan State
University; Lauren Bullard, Michigan State University; Dr. Janet Hauck,
Michigan State University; Dr. Spyridoula Vazou, Michigan State
University
Youth with disabilities often struggle to meet physical activity (PA)
guidelines due to barriers such as cost of participation or equipment,
inaccessible environments, and low self-esteem regarding their ability to
participate. School-based PA interventions provide the opportunity to
participate in PA in an inclusive, accessible, and cost-effective manner
for youth and their families. The purpose of this review was to examine
school-based PA interventions outside of regular school instructional
lessons (i.e., physical education class) for youth with disabilities. This
review yielded 27 peer-reviewed articles collected from databases such as
PubMed, PsycINFO, ERIC, and Google Scholar from the years 20002023. Studies were categorized based on disability type, intervention
characteristics, duration, setting, and other relevant contextual factors,
such as physical and social environment. The review found that intervention programs held within school for youth with disabilities comes with
signiﬁcant challenges, such as timing constraints, environmental constraints, programming time/exercise choice, and overall generalizability
due to small sample sizes. While challenges were prominent, this review
also identiﬁed commonalities between intervention programs such as
barriers faced (i.e., identifying exercises that were appropriate for the
group). This review aims to shine a light on the need for complete in-school
intervention programming (i.e., ensuring pilot programming is conducted
to ensure all qualities of the study that are desired are met) paired with a
collective effort from not only researchers but healthcare professionals,
teachers, and the community.
From effort to ease: Exploring the relationship among automaticity
and perceived and actual motor competence
Jeremy Lewis, University of South Carolina; Sally Miedema, University of
South Carolina; Jacqueline Goodway, Michigan State University; Ali
Brian, University of South Carolina
In the early developmental years, a high cognitive demand is required to
learn fundamental motor skills requisite for motor competence (MC –
e.g., goal-directed movement). Cognitive-demand is high due to attentional focus and motor planning required to perform various aspects of
novels skills with accuracy and competency. As children learn functional motor skills (FMS), they cognitively shift from controlled processing (e.g., slow, conscious, error-ridden) to automaticity (e.g., quick,
unconscious, accurate) often yielding higher rates of retention and
transferability of FMS across a variety of movement contexts. As motor
skills shift to the automatic stage of development, children may develop
greater awareness of their skill relative to their peers. Thus, this study
aims to explore whether a motor skill intervention strengthens the
historically non-signiﬁcant association among perceived and actual
motor skill competence as skills become more automatic. We recruited
teachers
(N = 10)
and
children
(N = 122;
girls = 68,
boys = 54;
Mage = 4.27 years, SD = .668) enrolled in Head Start Centers to participate in the study. We assessed all children prior to and after the nineweek teacher-led MC intervention on three measures. Measures included the Test of Gross Motor Skill Development (TGMD-2), the Pictorial
Scale of PMC (Barnett) and automaticity of movement (time from audio
“Go” to initial movement). Spearman rank-order correlations assessed
associations among MC, PMC, and automaticity scores. There was a
signiﬁcant moderate association between the MC and automaticity at
pre-test (r = -.467, p < .001), but not MC and PMC or automaticity and
PMC. At the post-test, there was a signiﬁcant moderate association
between MC and automaticity (r = -.643, p < .001), the MC and PMC
(r = .242, p = .007), and automaticity and PMC (r = -.266, p = .004).
Interventions should focus on structured, repetitive practice to shift
from cognitive to automatic stages of learning. Thus, allowing children
to focus on advanced aspects of motor planning and skill performance
such as speed, accuracy, distance, and complexity of movement. Future
trials should increase sample sizes and explore retention of learning
longitudinally.
A longitudinal study: Analyzing the inﬂuence of parental support in
infant cruising milestones between ﬁrst and non-ﬁrst-born infants
Valeria Reyes, California State University Fullerton; Alan Leon, California State University Fullerton; Jakelyne Carrera, California State
University Fullerton; Alicia Springﬁeld, California State University Fullerton; Do Kyeong Lee, California State University Fullerton
Within the ﬁrst year of life, the progressive mastery of an infant’s
motor skills facilitates locomotion opportunities, enhancing their
ability to explore their physical environment and engage with their
caregivers. Achieving motor skills supports the infant’s physical health
(CDC, 2023), cognitive (Kretch et al., 2022), language (Walle &
Campos, 2014), and social (Kretch et al., 2022) development. The
current study aims to compare the degree of parental support provided
to ﬁrstborn and non-ﬁrstborn infants in relation to the onset of the
cruising milestone between both groups. Limited literature has analyzed parental support across infant birth order however, some related
studies found that older siblings’ inﬂuence on non-ﬁrstborn infants’
motor development had a negative impact (Cruise & O’Reilly, 2014),
produced mixed results (Berger & Nuzzo, 2008), or found no signiﬁcance (Kromblolz, 2023). In this study, naturalistic and longitudinal
(1-12 months) observations were video recorded for 20-30 minutes in
the infant’s home. The postural patterns of 18 infants (11 ﬁrst-born, 8
males) were recorded during their playtime. Datayvu software was
used to code the videos frame-by-frame to determine the frequency and
duration of the infants’ postures: supine, sitting, prone, cruising,
carried by parent, and transition. The onset for the cruising milestone
was assessed utilizing the Bayley-III Motor Scale. Our preliminary
data indicate that as infants age, they rely less on furniture or caregiver
support and become more independent in maintaining an upright
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S17

--- [Página 21] ---
posture (p < .001). No distinct patterns of support change were observed between ﬁrst-born and non-ﬁrst-born infants. At 10 months of
age, just before most infants achieved cruising skills, ﬁrst-born infants
demonstrated a higher proportion of unsupported upright posture than
non-ﬁrst-born infants (p < .02), while no differences were observed in
supported upright posture. Furthermore, greater independence in upright posture at 10 months was not associated with an earlier onset of
cruising skills (r = -.331) In conclusion, ﬁrstborn infants engaged more
frequently in the unsupported upright posture compared to non-ﬁrstborn infants whilst approaching the 10-month mark. Parental support
was not linked to this outcome. Birth order did not appear to inﬂuence
an earlier onset of cruising.
The impact of a GAMIFIED sport program on children with developmental disabilities
Jillian Gonzalez, Sonoma State University; Poram Choi, Sonoma State
University; Yonjoong Ryuh, Sonoma State University
In an effort to develop a device-free exergaming that can also be
implemented outdoors, this research explores an impact of a gamiﬁed
soccer program on physical activity (PA) level, enjoyment, and soccer
skills in children with Developmental disabilities (DD). The soccer
program incorporates gamiﬁcation elements including narrative in each
stationary activity, point system, and sticker reward system on a board.
Total eight children (4 female and 4 male), four children with Down
syndrome, three with developmental delay, and an autistic child,
participated in this intervention study. The intervention program was
for 4 weeks, 3 days a week with each session lasting for 90 minutes.
Each session, both daily Feeling Scale and PA level using an Actigraph
Accelerometer were assessed and the Special Olympics Soccer Skill
Test was assessed before and after the intervention period. The results
showed a signiﬁcant increase in the Feeling Scale, indicating enhanced
enjoyment: t(7) = 2.39, p = .048, with a large effect size (Cohen’s
d = 0.85). Composite scores of dribble, pass, and shooting skill scores
also signiﬁcantly improved: t(7) = 5.48, p < .001, with a large effect size
(Cohen’s d = 1.94). However, inconsistent with our hypothesis, PA
level in the ﬁrst versus last session remained insigniﬁcant: t(7) = 2.1,
p = .07, with a medium-large effect size (Cohen’s d = 0.74). Although
the PA levels were not signiﬁcantly impacted, these ﬁndings suggest
that incorporating gamiﬁcation into adaptive sports and PA interventions for individuals with DD could enhance enjoyment and motor skill
development. Thus, adding gamiﬁcation to in-person community adaptive sports programs is recommended to foster sustainable healthy
behaviors in individuals with DD. Further research should focus on
developing effective implementation strategies and include a comparison or control group in the study design.
Exploring upper extremity movement parameters in the ﬁrst year of
life using inertial sensors
Anastasia
Kyvelidou,
Creighton
University;
Dimitrios
Katsavelis,
Creighton University; Kimberly Scott, Creighton University
Quantifying upper extremity movement in infancy is critical for understanding intervention dose-response relationships following early brain
injury. This study aimed to measure unimanual and bimanual upper
extremity movements during a structured play session with a clinician.
Eighteen infants, aged 3–12 months, participated in the study. Inertial
sensors were placed on both wrists of the infants and on each toy to capture
movement data. Toys were presented at a sequence that promoted incremental use of bimanual arm involvement. Key dependent variables
included the percentage of time spent in unimanual and bimanual play,
acceleration magnitude, and movement smoothness. Bimanual percent
activation was deﬁned as the time that both wrist accelerometers detected
activity above the threshold that was previously determined (0.2m/s above
baseline), while unimanual percent activation was deﬁned as the overall
time that either arm were active minus the bimanual time, divided by the
overall time of toy exposure. A two-way mixed ANOVA was conducted to
examine differences across age groups (3–6, 6–9 and 9–12 months) and toy
types. Among the dependent variables, signiﬁcant differences emerged in
the percentage of time spent in bimanual play (F = 3.497, p = .04,
η2 = .189) and unimanual play (F = 2.832, p = .014, η2 = .159) based on
toy type. However, no differences were observed among age groups, and
no interaction effects were identiﬁed. The percentage of time spent in
unimanual play showed a decreasing linear trend with toy presentation
(F = 7.485, p = .015, η2 = .333), while bimanual play exhibited an increasing trend (F = 7.787, p = .014, η2=.342). This pattern was anticipated, as
the standardized sequence of toy presentations required progressively
more bilateral engagement. Speciﬁcally, toys such as a monkey with
rings, a rubber ring, two plastic discs, and a tambourine elicited greater
bilateral activation compared to a rattle. Conversely, the rattle prompted
greater unimanual activation than a cloth crinkle book, rubber ring, or
tambourine. These ﬁndings demonstrate that inertial sensors effectively
differentiate between unilateral and bilateral upper extremity movements
based on toy properties. Future work will focus on reﬁning the protocol to
detect age-related differences and establish concurrent validity between
inertial sensor data and videography. Funding source: National Institutes
of Health.
Characteristics of trunk kinematics in infants with Down syndrome
following sticky mittens training
Robert Zeid, Georgia State University; Jackelyne Perez, Georgia State
University; Amy Talboy, Emory University; Seyda Ozcaliskan, Georgia
State University; Jianhua Wu, Georgia State University
Successful reaching requires visual perception, cognitive processing,
and limb and trunk coordination. Trunk ﬂexion may be used as a
strategy during arm reaching to position the hand closer to the target.
In children with Down syndrome (DS), muscle hypotonia and delayed
motor development often lead to less coordinated trunk motion needed
for successful reaching. Targeted practice using the “sticky mittens”
(SM) may help advance reaching and grasping development in infants
with DS. Here, we aimed to understand the progress of trunk kinematics
during arm reaching in infants with DS with and without receiving the
SM training. Our preliminary results included 11 infants with DS (6M/
5F). At the entry, participants were randomly assigned to either the
gross motor (GM) group (n = 6) who did not receive the SM training or
the gross+ﬁne motor (GM+FM) group (n = 5) who received the SM
training. Reach and grasp trials were recorded monthly and reﬂective
markers were placed on the infant’s right iliac crest, shoulder, elbow,
and wrist. Kinematic variables included trunk range of motion (ROM)
relative to the vertical, peak trunk ﬂexion (PTF), and timing of PTF from
two time points: T2 (at the conclusion of the SM training for the GM
+FM group; GM mean age = 12.6 months; GM+FM Mage = 11 months)
and T3 (about 6 months thereafter). A two-way (2 group x 2 time) mixed
ANOVA was conducted at a signiﬁcant level of α = 0.05. From T2 to T3,
the GM+FM group increased trunk ROM (11.94° to 15.19°) compared
to the GM group (12.40° to 11.66°). The timing of PTF increased in the
GM+FM group (69.69% to 85.94%) compared to GM (86.37% to
84.47%). Additionally, PTF increased from T2 to T3 (GM+FM:
7.80° to 9.70° and GM:11.97° to 14.10°). Effect sizes were moderate-to-large for trunk ROM (0.061), PTF (0.283), and timing of PTF
(0.091). Preliminary results suggest that the SM training may enhance
trunk coordination with greater trunk engagement during arm reaching
in infants with DS. Funding source: NIH R21

Exploring links between perceived motor competence and mental
health in school-aged children
Leesi George-Komi, University of Michigan; Stephanie A. Palmer, University of Michigan; Aaron P. Wood, University of Michigan; Kara K.
Palmer, University of Michigan; Leah E. Robinson, University of
Michigan
Children’s mental health and motor development are interconnected and
can signiﬁcantly impact overall child well-being. As children develop
more accurate self-perceptions of their motor competence (PMC), these
perceptions may inﬂuence their mental health outcomes, particularly
emotional and behavioral difﬁculties like anxiety, depression, and
ADHD. Therefore, this study aimed to investigate the relationship
between children’s PMC and their mental health. Participants included
112 school-aged children (Mage = 6.73 ± 0.99 years) enrolled in the
CHAMP-After School Program Study. PMC was assessed via the
Pictorial Scale of Perceived Competence and Social Acceptance
(PSPCSA). Mental health outcomes were assessed with the Strengths
and Difﬁculties Questionnaire (SDQ), a screening tool that has been
validated against diagnostic measures of anxiety, depression, and
ADHD. The SDQ has eight continuous outcomes: hyperactivity/inattention, conduct problems, peer relationship problems, emotional problems, prosocial behavior, internalizing symptoms (emotional and peer
problems subscales combined), externalizing symptoms (conduct problems and hyperactivity/inattention subscales combined), and total
difﬁculties (all subscales combined, except the prosocial behaviors
scale). Additionally, all subscales and the total difﬁculties scores were
ranked and classiﬁed as normal or borderline/abnormal. Spearman
correlations showed that lower PMC scores were signiﬁcantly associated with higher emotional symptoms (rs = −0.25), higher internalizing
symptoms (rs = −0.21), and higher total difﬁculties scores (rs = −0.23),
each p < .05. Mann-Whitney U tests revealed that children classiﬁed as
having borderline/abnormal emotional symptoms had signiﬁcantly
lower PMC scores compared to those classiﬁed as normal (W = 1565,
p < .01). These ﬁndings suggest that children’s PMC is linked to mental
health outcomes, particularly emotional and internalizing symptoms.
Interventions should focus on enhancing PMC to improve children’s
mental health, while future research should explore potential moderators such as socioeconomic status to inform more targeted strategies.
Funding source: National Institutes of Health Grant (NHLBI- R01NR018830)
Changes in ﬁtness and physical activity among children and youth
with disabilities: An adapted physical activity program evaluation
Emily Bremer, Acadia University; Brielle Pyette, Acadia University;
Ngaire Leach, Queen’s University; Roxanne Seaman, Acadia University
Children and youth with disabilities tend to engage in lower levels of
physical activity and have lower levels of ﬁtness than their peers without
disabilities. Adapted physical activity programming can help to reduce
some of the barriers to physical activity; hopefully leading to increased
physical activity and improved ﬁtness. The purpose of this program
evaluation is to explore the impact of one academic year (16 weeks) of
adapted physical activity programming on the ﬁtness and physical
activity of its participants. A within-subject repeated measures design
was employed. Participants were assessed at baseline (March 2023),
pre- (September 2023), post- (March 2024), and follow-up (September
2024) using a range of brief ﬁtness assessments (grip strength, standing
long jump, 2-minute walk test) and physical activity measures (accelerometry and caregiver-reported), among other outcomes. Participants
(N = 55) included 34 males and 21 females between 4-24 years of age
(Mage = 13.6 years) with a range of disabilities (mostly developmental).
A series of repeated measures ANOVA revealed no signiﬁcant time
effect for the standing long jump (F(3, 48) = 1.73, p = .173, ηp
2 = .098)
or 2-minute walk test (F(3, 27) = 2.54, p = .07, ηp
2 = .22). There was a
signiﬁcant time effect for grip strength (F(3, 48) = 5.19, p = .003,
ηp
2 = .245) indicating improvement over the course of the program.
Final accelerometry cleaning and analysis needs to be completed and
results will be presented at the conference. We will also explore
relationships between motor skill, ﬁtness, and physical activity across
the program. Funding source: Research Nova Scotia New Health
Investigator Grant.
Exploring the associations among physical activity, motor proﬁciency,
and cognition in early childhood
Meghan T. McKenzie, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; Dimetrius
Brandon, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; Abigail Lovingood, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; E. Kipling Webster, University of Tennessee,
Knoxville; Dawn P. Coe, University of Tennessee, Knoxville
Signiﬁcant motor and cognitive development occurs during early childhood. Research focusing on the relationships between motor and cognitive development and the concurrent effect of physical activity (PA) is
limited. Therefore, the purpose of this study is to examine the associations
among PA intensities (light, moderate, vigorous), motor proﬁciency, and
cognition in preschool-aged children. Participants were 17 children
(Mage = 4.6 ± 0.6y; girls = 59%) in the Southeastern US. PA was assessed
over seven days using hip-worn accelerometry. The Test of Gross Motor
Development–3rd edition (TGMD-3) assessed process-oriented fundamental motor skills (locomotor, ball skills), while the Bruninks-Oseretsky
Test of Motor Proﬁciency 2nd edition-Short Form (BOT-2) assessed
product-oriented motor proﬁciency (ﬁne motor precision and integration,
manual dexterity, bilateral coordination, balance, speed/agility, upperlimb coordination, and strength). Seven cognitive assessments were
administered:
Dimensional
Cart
Sort
(DCS),
Head-Toes-KneesShoulders (HTKS), Flanker, Go/No Go, Picture Sequence Memory
(PSM), Speed Matching (SM), and Visual Reasoning (VR) which
measured attention, inhibition, working memory, cognitive ﬂexibility,
sequencing, processing speed, and reasoning. Variables were created
using validated cutpoints (PA) and age- and sex-speciﬁc standard scores
and percentiles (motor and cognition). Pearson and spearman correlations
determined associations among variables. Children engaged in PA 19%
of the day, averaged 47th percentile in motor scores, and ranged from
42nd-63rd percentile in cognitive measures. The BOT-2 standard score
was associated with TGMD-3 ball skills raw score (r = 0.762; p = .047).
There were no associations among PA and motor or cognitive variables
(p > .05); however, there was a trending association between BOT-2 and
vigorous PA (r = 0.566; p = .070). No associations were detected between
TGMD-3 and cognitive variables. BOT-2 was associated with PSM
(r = 0.74; p = .019). Fine motor precision was associated with PSM
(r = 0.659; p = .038), Go% (r = 0.0615; p = .044), and VR (r = 0.673;
p = .047). Speed/agility (r = 0.711; p = .021) correlated with PSM and
balance was associated with PSM (r = 0.640; p = .046) and VR (r = 0.746;
p = .021). Fine motor integration correlated with DCS (r = 0.798;
p = .017) and Flanker (r = 0.688; p = .029). These preliminary ﬁndings
demonstrate differential associations among product-oriented motor
skills and cognitive skills. Future research should explore these associations across early childhood to elucidate the connection between motor
and cognitive development.
Is Acanthosis Nigricans related to motor skill competence in children,
taking race, sex, and weight status into account?
Christian Burke, Michigan State University; Karin Pfeiffer, Michigan
State University; Larissa True, New Mexico State University
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S19
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 23] ---
Acanthosis Nigricans (AN) is a dermatological condition characterized by
hyperpigmented, velvety skin changes, observed in body folds including
the neck and underarms (Hermanns-Le et al., 2004). Frequently associated
with insulin resistance and obesity, the relationship between AN and motor
skill competence remains underexplored. This study aims to investigate the
inﬂuence of AN on motor skill competence, while considering race, sex,
and weight status. Children (N = 297) were recruited from four elementary
schools in/near Flint, MI. Race and sex data were obtained through a
demographic questionnaire completed by guardians. AN presence was
assessed by a trained pediatric physician via neck examination. Height
(cm) and weight (kg) were measured, and body mass index percentiles
calculated using CDC guidelines to determine weight status. Motor skill
competence was evaluated by a trained investigator using the Test of Gross
Motor Development-2 (TGMD-2), categorizing skills into Locomotor,
Object Control, and Total scores. Multiple linear regression analyses
assessed the effects of AN, race, sex, and weight status on these scores.
Our analysis revealed that when AN, race, sex, and weight status were
combined, the model explained 11.2% of the variance in Object Control
scores (F(4, 291) = 9.219, p < .001), 4.9% in Locomotor scores (F(4,
291) = 3.720, p = .006), and 9.1% in Total TGMD-2 scores (F(4,
291) = 7.319, p < .001). Within the full model, males showed a positive
effect on Total TGMD-2 scores (B = 5.912, p < .001), while Black children
exhibited a negative effect (B = -2.689, p = .019). AN analyzed alone,
accounted for 3.7% of the variance in Locomotor scores (F(1,
294) = 11.230, p < .001) and 2.4% in Total TGMD-2 scores (F(1,
294) = 7.135, p = .008), showing signiﬁcant negative effects. However,
AN alone was not associated with Object Control scores (p = .137).
Although literature has linked AN to obesity (Palhares et al., 2018), lack
of examination of AN’s association with motor skill competence makes
comparison difﬁcult. Further research is needed to investigate whether AN
has a relationship with motor skill competence. Funding source: Crim
Fitness Foundation.
Observation of trunk control as a function of quality of caregiver
interaction
Erika Garcia MOra, Children’s Hospital Los Angeles; Beth Smith,
Children’s Hospital Los Angeles
The progression of infant development is highly inﬂuenced by the
infants’ parents and/or caregivers. Primary caregivers facilitate the
complex and inter-related processes of social (Landry et al., 1998),
cognitive (Cabrera et al., 2009), and motor (Rocha et al., 2000)
development. Limited evidence exists regarding the cascading interactions between modalities during the ﬁrst year of infancy. The goal of this
project is to employ an assessment method that systematically evaluates
both the quality of caregiver-infant interactions and trunk control in a
longitudinal observational study. We hypothesize that dyads who
exhibit a higher quality of interaction will have different longitudinal
trajectories of trunk control than dyads with lower quality of interaction.
Ten infants (Mage = 2.8 months at the ﬁrst visit) were assessed across 5
monthly longitudinal visits. The caregiver-infant relationship was assessed using the Nursing Child Assessment Satellite Training (NCAST)
– Teaching scale at Visit 2. Each dyad was recorded during a parent
selected teaching activity and later evaluated according to behavioral
characteristics used to measure caregiver-infant quality of interaction.
Trunk control was obtained by administering the Segmental Assessment
of Trunk Control (SATCo) at each visit. Infants were positioned on a
bench and supported thoroughly at certain parts of the trunk; infant
performance was recorded and later dichotomously evaluated for control at different regions of the trunk for static, active and reactive
control. Poisson longitudinal multilevel modeling was performed to
assess for different trajectories of trunk control (static, active, reactive
postural controls) between low and high dyad quality of interaction.
SATCo scores showed signiﬁcant linear growth of trunk control for all
postural controls - static (β10 = 0.86, p = .007); active (β10 = 1.12,
p = .003); reactive (β10=1.33, p = .006), across all visits. There was no
effect of dyad quality of interaction. Our hypothesis that dyads with
higher quality of interaction would have distinct longitudinal trunk
control trajectories was consistent with previous work showing trunk
control increases with age. Our ﬁndings do not support that the
development of trunk control is inﬂuenced by a caregiver’s ability to
identify and be sensitive to infant cues. These results should be interpreted with caution given our small sample size. Funding source:
National Institutes of Health - DHHS (R01HD101641)
Reﬂections of a community based physical activity program: Perspectives from autistic children
Franziska Loetzner, Oregon State University; Samantha Miller, Wayne
State University; Leah Ketcheson, Wayne State University
The beneﬁts of physical activity for children are well documented.
Concerningly, autistic children often do not have the opportunity to
participate in health enhancing physical activity due to the lack of
adequately trained personnel and access to programs or inclusive sports.
One way to combat these barriers is to implement community based
physical activity initiatives designed speciﬁcally for autistic children.
These programs have a track record of increasing motor skills, physical
activity, and socialization in autistic children. However, these outcomes
have been generally documented through objective and/or parent proxy
measures. Autistic children rarely have the opportunity to share their
opinions on these physical activity or health initiatives with programmers
or stakeholders. Therefore, the primary objective of this study was to
explore autistic children’s perceptions of a community based physical
activity program. A total of 27 families participated in the intervention for
90 minutes (about three hours) per week for one year, including 29
autistic children (83% male, M = 8.28 ± 3.60 years). Semi-structured
interviews guided by The Consolidated Framework for Implementation
Research (CFIR) were administered at the end of the program to 14
autistic children in the program who had mild to moderate autism
determined by the Social Responsiveness Scale. Data were analyzed
using constant comparison and deductive analysis to identify themes.
Major themes related to child needs, program design, adaptability, and
socialization emerged. This study highlighted the importance of gathering perspectives from autistic children on programs they participate in
and that are designed for them. Understanding the unique needs of autistic
children from their own perspective can foster curated development of
community-based physical activity programs. Findings from this study
should be considered by programmers and researchers when creating
physical activity programs for autistic children. Funding source: Michigan Health Endowment Fund.
Unveiling a global motor factor in the TGMD-3: insights from BCFA
and ESEM in Brazilian children
Glauber Carvalho Nobre, Universidade Federal do Rio Grande do Sul;
Marcelo Gonçalves Duarte, Universidade Federal do Mato Grosso do
Sul; Clarisse Martins, Universidade do Porto; Nadia Cristina Valentini,
Universidade Federal do Rio Grande do Sul
The TGMD-3 is widely used for assessing fundamental motor skills (FMS)
worldwide. Originally designed with a bidimensional structure comprising
locomotor and ball skills, evidence supporting this structure has been
reported across several countries, including Brazil, Peru, Germany, Iran,
Bosnia and Herzegovina, Slovenia, Spain, Ireland, and Italy. However,
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
