--- [Página 24] ---
recent ﬁndings suggest high correlations between locomotor and ball skill
dimensions, and cross-loading of items, raising questions about whether a
single underlying dimension—the global motor skill factor—might better
explain the data. Advanced analytical methods such as bifactor conﬁrmatory factor analysis (BCFA) and exploratory structural equation modeling
(ESEM) have been proposed as appropriate tools to address this concern.
These methods can account for the shared variance across tasks and the
unique contributions of distinct dimensions. The objective of this study
was to examine the internal structure of the TGMD-3 in Brazilian children
using BCFA and ESEM and to compare these approaches with traditional
conﬁrmatory factor analysis (CFA). A total of 1,454 children (51% girls,
n = 742), aged 3 to 10.9 years, from Brazil’s ﬁve macroregions (North,
Northeast, Central-West, Southeast, and South), participated in the study.
Five models were tested, a unidimensional, two ﬁrst-order factor, and
bifactor models using CFA and ESEM approaches. Of these, the bifactor
models demonstrated the best ﬁt. Speciﬁcally, the bifactor CFA model
achieved ﬁt indices of χ2(60) = 450.057, CFI = 0.91, TLI = 0.90,
RMSEA [90% CI] = 0.067 [0.045,0.063], and SRMS = 0.069. Meanwhile, the bifactor ESEM model showed superior ﬁt indices with
χ2(41) = 196.228, CFI = 0.97, TLI = 0.94, RMSEA [90% CI] = 0.051
[0.044,0.058], and SRMS = 0.022. These results provide strong evidence for the validity of a bifactor representation of the TGMD-3,
revealing the presence of a global motor factor in addition to the
locomotor and ball skill dimensions. This global factor likely represents
shared motor components that form the foundation of movement
actions. These ﬁndings highlight the role of shared constraints and
actions across motor tasks, emphasizing the need to consider global and
speciﬁc dimensions when assessing FMS. Funding source: CAPES
and CNPq.
Identifying holistic developmental proﬁles based on motor, cognitive
and social-emotional assessments in 1- to 3-year-old toddlers by means
of a person-centered approach
Eva DHondt, Vrije Universiteit Brussel; Lisa Mertens, Vrije Universiteit
Brussel; Matthieu Lenoir, Ghent University; Eline Coppens, Ghent
University
Early childhood is a critical period of rapid development in various
domains and requires a holistic approach. However, studies simultaneously evaluating motor, cognitive and social-emotional functioning
are scarce, resulting in a lack of insight into the variety of developmental
proﬁles during the ﬁrst years of life. To assess the current status of those
developmental domains, more than 200 Flemish toddlers (aged 13 years) participating in the MoMoCo project were evaluated. They
were recruited via child daycare centers and assessed with the Peabody
Developmental Motor Scales 2nd edition (PDMS-2) as well as the
cognitive and social-emotional subtests of the Bayley Scales of Infant
and Toddler Development 4th edition (Bayley IV). Complete data on
motor competence (MC – Total motor quotient), cognitive functioning
(CF – Scaled subtest score) and social-emotional functioning (SEF –
Scaled subtest score) could be collected in 171 toddlers (49% boys,
21.9 ± 5.8 months). After removing univariate and multivariate outliers,
cluster analyses revealed ﬁve different proﬁles based on toddlers’
relative levels of MC, CF and SEF within the ﬁnal study sample
(N = 167). These cluster variables were labelled as high (z-score >
+0.50), average (+0.50 ≥z-score ≥-0.50) or low (z-score < -0.50). Accordingly, one proﬁle was identiﬁed with completely convergent levels
of high MC, high CF and high SEF (n = 30, 18%), whereas these
developmental domains were only partly convergent in the other four
proﬁles including toddlers with average MC, average CF and low SEF
(n = 49, 29%); with average MC, average CF and high SEF (n = 35,
21%); with average MC, low CF and average SEF (n = 15, 9%); or with
low MC, average CF and low SEF (n = 38, 23%). Comparing the
identiﬁed proﬁles, there was no signiﬁcantly different distribution
according to toddlers’ biological sex (χ2 = 4.430, p = .351) or their
parents’ educational level (χ2 = 5.199, p = .267). One-way ANOVAs
indicated signiﬁcant differences between proﬁles in terms of toddlers’
age (F = 2.665, p = .034) and BMI (F = 2.740, p = .031), although Tukey
post-hoc analyses only revealed some trends. Nevertheless, applying a
person-centered approach in our ﬁeld of research is valuable to further
advance our knowledge and understanding in view of fostering optimal
developmental outcomes and trajectories from a young age onwards.
Funding source: Research Foundation – Flanders.
Shared phenotypes in autism spectrum disorder and developmental
coordination disorder: longitudinal risk factors and developmental
trajectories in preterm children
Nadia Cristina Valentini, Universidade Federal do Rio Grande do Sul;
Carolina Panceri, Hospital de Clínicas de Porto Alegre; Glauber Carvalho Nobre, Instituto Federal de Educação, Ciência e Tecnologia do
Ceará; Rita de Cássia Silveira, Universidade Federal do Rio Grande
do Sul
Preterm birth signiﬁcantly increases the risk of neurodevelopmental disorders, including Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD) and Developmental
Coordination Disorder (DCD). Preterm children also face an elevated risk
of developmental delays, with low scores commonly observed in motor,
cognitive, and language development during infancy and childhood. This
prospective longitudinal study aimed to (1) examine motor, cognitive, and
language development over the ﬁrst 36 months of life in preterm children
diagnosed with ASD or DCD compared to age-matched typically developing (TD) peers, and (2) investigate how motor development inﬂuences
cognitive and language trajectories across these groups. Developmental
outcomes were assessed using the Bayley Scales of Infant and Toddler
Development (BSID-III). Parents provided socioeconomic data through
questionnaires, and hospital records detailed health proﬁles. Findings
revealed that children with ASD experienced consistent declines in
motor, cognitive, and language scores from 12 to 36 months, while those
with DCD exhibited declines in motor and cognitive scores after 24
months. Both clinical groups displayed signiﬁcantly impaired development compared to TD controls. Longitudinal analyses indicated that
motor development was a strong predictor of cognitive and language
outcomes across all groups, with particularly robust associations in
children with DCD and TD peers. These results underscore substantial
variability in neurodevelopmental outcomes across motor, cognitive,
and language domains, particularly for children with ASD. Notably,
declines in developmental trajectories among preterm children with
ASD reﬂect challenges in mastering complex tasks, such as understanding toy functions and using gestures and expressive language to
communicate. This study highlights the pressing need for early, tailored
interventions to address neurodevelopmental vulnerabilities and optimize outcomes in high-risk preterm populations. Funding source:
CAPES and CNPq.
Motor performance and cognitive resource allocation are not affected
by self-talk cues that promote an external focus of attention
Jet Taylor, Boise State University; Juliana O. Parma, San Francisco State
University; Katelin Crutcher, Boise State University; Thomas Simpson,
Edge Hill University; Takehiro Iwatsuki, University of Hawaii at Hilo;
Eric M. Martin, Boise State University; Matthew W. Miller, Auburn
University; Mariane F. B. Bacelar, Boise State University
A growing body of evidence suggests that instructional self-talk enhances motor performance as compared to control conditions. Yet, the
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S21
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 25] ---
type of self-talk cues adopted across studies varies signiﬁcantly, and the
speciﬁc content of the cues is often overlooked. The type of cue adopted
can direct one’s focus of attention either internally or externally, and
evidence from motor learning studies suggests that an external focus of
attention is more beneﬁcial for motor performance. To examine whether
the content of self-talk cues affects motor performance differently, as
predicted by the focus of attention literature, we had 34 participants
perform a golf-putting task while implementing instructional self-talk
with
external-focus
(e.g.,
“putter-through”)
and
internal-focus
(e.g., “arms-though”) cues and recorded their putting accuracy via
radial error. Since the mechanisms underlying self-talk remain unclear,
we also recorded participants’ brain activity throughout the experiment
and analyzed their motor upper-alpha power, an electroencephalographic measure inversely correlated with cognitive resource allocation
during movement preparation. After a performance stabilization phase
followed by self-talk implementation training, participants performed
four blocks of 20 putts under the external- and internal-focus self-talk
conditions which were performed in an alternating and counterbalanced
order following a randomized schedule (e.g., external-internal-externalinternal). Contrary to our prediction, results showed no signiﬁcant
differences in putting accuracy between the external- and internal-focus
self-talk conditions (p = .756). Additionally, aligned with previous
evidence, motor upper-alpha power was higher in the seconds preceding
the putt than during movement execution (p ≤.046), but this effect was
not moderated by condition (p = .811). Together, these ﬁndings suggest
that the use of instructional self-talk with external-focus cues might not
lead to better motor performance or affect cognitive resource allocation
for motor programming.
Stay-play-talk: A peer-mediated motor intervention for children with
autism spectrum disorder
Yuemei Lu, East China Normal University; Xiaozan Wang, East China
Normal University
An inclusive environment enables children with special needs to engage
with neurotypical peers through shared activities. For children with Autism
Spectrum Disorder (ASD), considering their core symptoms, creating
structured peer-mediated environments is especially critical. This study
employed a single-case study design to evaluate the effects of a 15-day
motor intervention based on the Stay-Play-Talk (SPT) framework on four
children with ASD. Three boys aged 11-12 and one girl aged 8 participated
in this study. Each child was paired with a neurotypical peer in daily 90minute sessions. Activities included rhythmic dance, skill-based motor
tasks, and interactive games. The SPT framework emphasizes three
principles during the program: Stay (maintaining proximity), Play (engaging in structured and cooperative motor tasks), and Talk (using verbal
prompts and encouraging communication). Video coding was applied to
analyze behaviors, including task engagement, physical activity participation, repetitive and stereotypic behaviors, and social interactions (e.g., eye
contact, verbal communication, and physical interactions). Five-minute
video segments were randomly sampled from each session and coded at
10-second intervals. Inter-rater reliability exceeded 93%, and the behavior
trends were analyzed to identify patterns of improvement over time.
Results showed a substantial reduction in repetitive and stereotypic
behaviors across participants. One participant’s stereotypic behaviors
decreased from 30% at baseline to less than 5% by the ﬁnal sessions,
while another exhibited a decline from 20% to 10%. Task engagement
rates increased signiﬁcantly for all participants, with three children achieving 100% engagement by the ﬁnal sessions and the fourth improving from
65% to 95%. Physical activity participation consistently exceeded 90% for
all children throughout the intervention. Social skills demonstrated varied
improvements, with sustained eye contact increasing by an average of
35%, and one participant showing a 20% increase in verbal interactions
during collaborative tasks. These ﬁndings highlight the effectiveness of
motor intervention using the SPT framework in reducing challenging
behaviors, enhancing task engagement, and promoting social skills in
children with ASD within inclusive environments. Future efforts should
prioritize integrating such peer-mediated motor programs into school and
community settings to help children with special needs, fostering their
social participation and overall adaptability in real-world environments.
Funding source: China Postdoctoral Science Foundation (2023M731113)
Tiny steps, growing minds: Exploring motor, cognitive and socialemotional functioning in Flemish toddlers
Eline Coppens, Ghent University; Lisa Mertens, Vrije Universiteit Brussel;
Matthieu Lenoir, Ghent University; Eva D’Hondt, Vrije Universiteit
Brussel
Early childhood is characterized by rapid motor skill development,
building the foundation for more complex movements later in life. These
early motor skills are essential for a wide range of activities and play a key
role in overall developmental progress. However, recent studies highlighted a concerning decline in motor competence (MC) among young children,
encouraging investigations into potential associations with other developmental domains, such as cognitive functioning (CF) and social-emotional
functioning (SEF). This study aims to (1) assess levels of MC, CF and SEF
in 1- to 3-year-old Flemish toddlers, (2) explore the relationship between
these domains, and (3) investigate the predictive value of baseline MC, CF,
SEF and age on change in MC. A total of 261 toddlers (22.27 ± 5.72
months) participated in the study, with MC assessments at baseline (T0)
and six months later (T1) using the Peabody Developmental Motor Scales,
2nd Edition (PDMS-2). CF and SEF were evaluated using the Bayley
Scales of Infant and Toddler Development, 4th edition (Bayley-IV).
Statistical analyses included (1) One-sample T-tests, (2) Spearman correlations and (3) Multiple linear regression. The ﬁndings revealed that (1)
Flemish toddlers scored below average in gross MC (t(237) = -12.106,
p < .001, d = −.785) compared to the PDMS-2 reference values, primarily
due to lower locomotion scores. In contrast, ﬁne MC (t(247) =.416,
p = .678) did not signiﬁcantly differ from those normative values. (2)
Weak to moderate positive correlations were observed between all variables (.172 < r <.357, all p < .05). (3) Baseline MC, CF, SEF and age
signiﬁcantly predicted change in MC (Δ MC=MCT1-MCT0; F = 16.835,
p < .001, Adjusted R2 = .416). The strongest predictor of Δ MC was
baseline MC (β = −.702, p < .001), indicating that higher levels of baseline
MC are associated with less progress relative to age-expected norms.
Additionally, baseline CF (β = .200, p = .032) and SEF (β = .169, p = .044)
were positively associated with Δ MC, although their effects were smaller.
This study suggests that toddlers’ motor development is interconnected to
other developmental domains underscoring the importance of early and
targeted interventions that adopt a longitudinal and holistic perspective.
Funding source: Research Foundation Flanders (FWO)

Children diagnosed with Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD) and Developmental Coordination Disorder (DCD) show challenges in postural control

diagnosed with DCD. The aim of this study was to identify and compare
proﬁles of PC measured by the NeuroCom Sensory Organization Test
(SOT). Seventy-ﬁve children (M = 49; F = 26) between 5-16 years of age
participated, 25 ASD (Mage= 8.7 ± 2.8), 25 DCD (Mage = 8.3 ± 3.1) and 25

conditions of the SOT – eyes open quiet stance, eyes closed quiet stance,
eyes open surround wall movement, eyes open force-plate perturbations,
eyes closed force-plate perturbations, eyes open force-plate perturbations
and surround wall movement. Scoring evaluated the minimization of
anterior/posterior sway over three trials to produce an equilibrium score
out of 100% as well as a strategy analysis quantifying the relative amount
of movement about the ankles and hips used to maintain sway. A mixed
model analysis indicated signiﬁcant differences in equilibrium scores
between groups in all conditions over trials and composite scores. Posthoc comparisons indicated TD group scored signiﬁcantly higher (p < .05)
than both ASD and DCD groups. Strategy scores indicated the TD group
used predominantly ankle strategy across all conditions with minimal hip
strategy during force-plate perturbations, while ASD and DCD groups
used signiﬁcantly more hip strategy during force-plate perturbations. Posthoc comparisons indicated ASD group strategies were different than TD
(p < .05) across all conditions. There was no difference between TD and
DCD groups during eyes open/closed quiet stances and during inaccurate
visual information delivery while force-plate was stable. DCD group
strategies were signiﬁcantly different from TD only during force-plate
perturbations (p < .05). There was no signiﬁcant difference in strategy and
equilibrium maintenance between ASD and DCD groups. While DCD and
ASD remain similar in equilibrium and anterior/posterior sway maintenance, inaccurate visual information seems to affect ASD strategy performance and not DCD when compared to TD. These ﬁndings add to the body
of literature and support the need for a differentiated approach when
assessing and intervening for postural control in these groups. Funding

How fast can you walk with the Knee Crutch? Continuous preferred
gait speed and fastest gait speed with the Knee Crutch compared to
normal gait speed on a treadmill
Garrett Belding, Oakland University; Abby Henrikson, Oakland University; Evan Rekowski, Oakland University; Joshua Haworth, Oakland
University
Previous research conducted on participants using the Knee Crutch (KC)
has measured participants’ gait speed using the Six Minute Walk Test
(6MWT). They found that gait speed with the KC was reduced to less than
half that of the Normal Gait Speed (NGS) condition. The 6MWT in that
study did not measure the difference between participants’ Knee Crutch
Preferred Gait Speed (KCPGS) and their Knee Crutch Fastest Gait Speed
(KCFGS). The present study examined participants’ NGS, KCPGS and
their KCFGS on a treadmill. It was hypothesized that both KC conditions
would be slower than NGS and KCPGS would be slower than KCFGS.
The purpose of our study was to further describe differences between
participants KCPGS, KCFGS, and NGS. Our participants (N = 20, (6
women and 14 men) Mage = 22.20 ± 1.7 years) Were ﬁtted with the KC
and allowed to practice until they reported feeling comfortable using the
device on ﬂat level ground. After the KC was removed participants were
ﬁtted into the safety harness on the treadmill. A NGS assessment was
conducted. The speed was increased until participants reached NGS;
deﬁned as “When you are walking to class but not late”. Participants
were then ﬁtted with the KC and asked to walk on the treadmill. Treadmill
speed was increased until participants’ KCPGS was found with the same
instructions as NGS. Participants continued walking for 90 seconds. Then
treadmill speed was increased to KCFGS; deﬁned as “The fastest speed
you can maintain for 90 seconds”. Participants remained at this speed for
90 seconds. A one-way ANOVA was used to compare the self-selected
speeds across the three conditions. Mean NGS (M = 2.1 ± 0.19 mph),
KCPGS
(M = .74 ± .29
mph),
KCFGS
(M = 1.03 ± .074
mph),
p = 6.09E-20. These results are additive to the 6MWT and further describe
self-selected gait speeds of KC users.
Which one is better? A comparison between a clinical and a researchbased motor assessment to identify motor challenges in typically
developing, autistic, and children with Developmental Coordination
Disorder
Crystal Elizabeth Alvarez, University of Texas at Arlington; Priscila
Tamplain, University of Texas at Arlington
It is well known that children diagnosed with Autism Spectrum Disorder
(ASD) and Developmental Coordination Disorder (DCD) have signiﬁcant
motor challenges compared to typical development (TD). Recently, the
third edition of two widely used motor assessments were released:
Movement Assessment Battery for Children (MABC-3), which is the
gold-standard for motor challenges and largely used in research settings,
and Bruininks-Oseretsky Test of Motor Proﬁciency (BOT-3), which is
commonly used in clinical evaluations in the United States. This study
compared the results of the assessments in three groups: ASD, DCD, and
TD. Sixty-one children (M = 39; F = 22) between 5-18 years of age
participated, 14 ASD (Mage = 10.6 ± 3.8), 17 DCD (Mage= 8.7 ± 3.5), 30
TD (Mage=10.1 ± 2.8). Each child completed both motor skill assessments
in counterbalanced order. MABC-3 measures possible movement difﬁculty and includes 10 items across 3 components: manual dexterity, aiming &
catching, balance & locomotion. The BOT-3 measures motor proﬁciency
and includes 63 items across four sub-tests: ﬁne manual control, manual
coordination, body coordination, strength & agility. We used percentile
ranks for the analyses. Composite motor scores of 15th & 16th percentiles
and below indicates severe movement difﬁculty, respectively. A mixed
model analysis indicated signiﬁcant differences (p < .001) within assessments, with lower scores in the MABC-3 when compared to the BOT-3:
TD
(MABC-3 = 52.5 ± 20.9;
BOT-3 = 61.8 ± 19.8),
ASD
(MABC3 = 3.5 ± 3.9; BOT-3 = 16.3 ± 23.2), DCD (MABC-3 = 9.8 ± 9.9; BOT3 = 23.8 ± 18.5). Those with DCD and ASD had signiﬁcantly lower scores
than the TD group. BOT-3 scores failed to identify 10 children with
possible motor challenges (ASD = 9, DCD = 9) compared to the MABC-3
(ASD = 14, DCD = 14). While both assessments were able to differentiate
motor ability of children with DCD and ASD from those TD, the BOT-3
assessment did not detect motor challenges in several children in those
groups. The BOT-3 is used in clinical settings in the US and may not be as
effective in identifying motor challenges in children who may need
intervention. Children who do not receive needed interventions may face
numerous consequences and struggle with daily tasks and academic
performance, which can lead to an impact in overall quality of life
throughout
adolescence
and
adulthood.

source:

The role of instructions in 1-on-1 collision avoidance interactions: A
pilot case study
Kazuyuki Sato, Friedrich Schiller University Jena; Johannes Keck, Friedrich Schiller University Jena; Rouwen Ca˜nal-Bruland, Friedrich Schiller
University Jena
Pedestrians navigate crowds and avoid collisions every day. While collisions do not occur frequently, they sometimes have dramatic consequences
(e.g. injuries). Therefore, it is important to understand how collisions can
be avoided. Yet, studying natural near-collisions in the laboratory is
challenging. Researchers address this challenge either by instructing
participants where to move so as to induce near-collisions (Honma
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S23
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 27] ---
et al., 2015) or without instructions letting collisions emerge naturally
(Olivier et al., 2012). While the former approach increases the likelihood of
near-collisions, it may not necessarily resemble natural interactive behaviors. In this pilot case study, we compared the two approaches regarding
their effect on the interaction dynamics. In two dyads, male participants
approached each other in a collision avoidance scenario in an instructed or
uninstructed condition. The set-up mirrored Honma et al. (2015): i.e., we
had a 600 cm walking space with a center line and three different
rectangular free space zones with different lengths (20, 80, and 140 cm;
same width: 170 cm). Participants walked straight toward each other while
maintaining the center of mass (COM) on the center line. On entering the
free space zone, they were either instructed to move left or right either
keeping them on a collision course (in 50%-80% of the trials) or not
(instructed condition), or free to move left or right in order to avoid
collision (uninstructed condition). Each dyad completed 20 trials per free
space length in both conditions (240 trials in total), while their full body
movements were captured using a motion capture system (OptiTrack;16
cameras). As an indication of avoidance movements, we calculated lateral
Jerk of the COM. To analyze the interaction dynamics, the Jerk differences
between the participants were calculated for each free space length. For the
140 cm free space zone, results revealed a larger Jerk difference between
participants in the uninstructed condition than in the instructed condition
(U = 1142, p < .01, mean difference = 0.16 ± 0.04 sec, Cohen’s d = -.76),
providing initial evidence that instructing or not instructing participants
when examining near-collision scenarios seems to affect the interaction
dynamics of collision avoidance behaviors.
Human path integration is robust to temporal gait manipulations
Tyler Wiles, University of Nebraska at Omaha; Alli Grunkemeyer, University of Nebraska at Omaha; Nick Stergiou, University of Nebraska at
Omaha; Aaron Likens, University of Nebraska at Omaha
A person guided 10 meters while blindfolded can turn around and
reproduce that same distance alone. This process–known as path integration–has been studied for decades by manipulating gait features such as
speed, cadence, and stride length to make this skill more or less accurate.
Addressing literature gaps, we manipulated the long-range correlations
(LRCs) of each participant’s gait to be stronger (hypothesized to make
distance perception better) or weaker (hypothesized to make distance
perception worse) by asking participants to synchronize their strides over
10 and 30 meters to an isochronous, strong, or weak metronome, and no
metronome condition. If the gait manipulation improved distance perception, error (reproduced distance – original distance) was closer to zero.
When young, middle, and older adults attempted to reproduce distances—
synchronizing to the metronome during both the original and reproduced
distances (E1) or only during the original distance (E2)—we did not ﬁnd
evidence of a metronome condition interaction or main effect (BF10 < 1).
Consistent with the larger literature, there was extreme support of a
distance main effect (E1 & 2: BF10 > 100) showing that for both experiments, 30 meters (E1: -3.78 ± 4.09 meters; E2: -4.79 ± 4.51 meters) was
more difﬁcult than 10 meters (E1: 0.41 ± 2.32 meters; E2: -0.15 ± 2.47
meters). We then conducted a Bayesian regression to determine if LRCs
(during 4-minute self-paced walk) biased distance perception. With credible intervals containing zero, a one standard deviation increase in the
strength of LRCs led to slightly more underestimation (E1: -0.63 meters;
E2: -0.06) for both distances. At 30 meters, credible intervals did not
contain zero, suggesting larger underestimations (E1: -1.13 meters) and
overestimations (E2: 0.95 meters) per experiment. These results suggest
that an individual’s inherent gait variability subtly inﬂuences distance
perception. With each stride, a new behavior may emerge as a stronger
inﬂuence of gait variability takes hold over greater distances. In conclusion, our direct manipulation had no effect and a person’s gait variability
may play a subtle role in distance perception, which is more difﬁcult at
longer
distances.

Isolated pressure conditions affect perceived pressure, but not performance in a dart throwing task
John Henry Ballard, University of Tennessee; John Henry Douglas,
University of Tennessee; Christian Duffy, University of Tennessee; Zackery Quieti, University of Tennessee; Kevin Becker, University of Tennessee
Pressure is seen as “any factor or combination of factors that increase the
importance of performing well on a particular occasion” (Baumeister,
1984, p. 610). Pressure is often assumed to impair performance, and
several studies consider pressure training as an intervention to inoculate
participants to this impairment (Low et al., 2021). Often these studies
combine multiple pressure types to create a pressure inducing environment
for the participant. However, recent research suggests that when administered individually, pressure conditions can enhance, impair, or have no
effect on performance (Henderson et al, 2024; Stoker et al. 2019). The
purpose of this study was to determine the effects of six different pressure
conditions on dart throwing performance, perceived pressure, and state
anxiety. Participants (N = 18) completed six dart throws in seven different
conditions: baseline, crowd simulation, time pressure, auditory distractor,
visual distractor, judgement, and monetary incentive. Participants were
informed the goal of each trial was to throw the dart as close to the center of
the crosshair as possible. Radial error (RE) was recorded as a performance
measure, while psychological variables were obtained via the Perceived
Pressure Question (PPQ) and the Competitive State Anxiety Inventory-2
Revised (CSAI-2R) after each condition. Repeated measures ANOVAs
with Bonferroni post-hoc tests were conducted on PPQ, CSAI-2R, and RE
in each condition. The main effect of condition was signiﬁcant for PPQ
(p < .001). Speciﬁcally, crowd simulation (p = .048), time pressure
(p < .001), and monetary incentive (p < .001) conditions yielded higher
levels of perceived pressure when compared to baseline. No other conditions within PPQ differed from baseline (p > .05). RE and CSAI-2R did
not signiﬁcantly differ between conditions (p’s > .05). The present data
suggests perceived pressure can be elicited within a laboratory environment using speciﬁc pressure conditions. However, these conditions did not
impact throwing performance or levels of state anxiety. These lack of
differences may be attributable to insufﬁcient statistical power. Alternatively, it is possible that heterogenous responses to different pressure
conditions among individuals prevented the observation of group level
differences.
Integration of attentional focus instructional cues and feedback on
reﬂex testing clinical psychomotor skill acquisition
Aleiza Higgins, The University of North Carolina Greensboro; Aaron
Terranova, The University of North Carolina Greensboro; Louisa Raisbeck, The University of North Carolina Greensboro
Motor learning research demonstrates that external focus of attention
(EFA; i.e., focus on the effect of the movement) facilitates motor skill
acquisition more effectively than internal focus of attention (IFA;
i.e., focus on movement characteristics). While literature has applied these
concepts to clinical psychomotor skills, application within the ﬁeld of
athletic training remains underexplored. This pilot study examined the
effects of attentional focus instructional cues and feedback on the acquisition of C7 triceps deep tendon reﬂex testing skills among graduate athletic
training students (ATSs). We hypothesized that EFA cues would lead to
superior performance compared to IFA cues. Fourteen ATSs (EFA = 7;
IFA = 7; 13 women; Mage = 21.7 years) were taught to perform reﬂex
testing by a prerecorded observational modeling demonstration video.

Participants received either EFA or IFA instructional cues before performing 10 trials on a standardized patient, followed by EFA or IFA feedback
paired with video footage of their performance. They then completed
another 10 trials. Performance was assessed by a psychomotor skill
checklist and the percentage of trials correctly eliciting a grade 3 (normal)
reﬂex. Mann-Whitney U Tests revealed no signiﬁcant differences by
attentional focus for the checklist scores or percent correctly elicited
reﬂexes for either trial (z = -0.452, p = .651; z = -1.590, p = .112; z = -
0.916, p = .360; z = -0.0535, p = .593). However, Wilcoxon Signed Rank
Tests showed signiﬁcant improvement in checklist scores after feedback,
regardless of attentional focus (z = -2.565, p = .010), though there was no
signiﬁcant improvement based on the percentage of correctly elicited
reﬂexes alone (z = -1.389, p = .165). This study highlights the potential
value of feedback for skill acquisition but underscores the need for further
research with larger sample sizes to evaluate the application of attentional
focus principles in athletic training. Additionally, clearer deﬁnitions and
standardized methods for assessing psychomotor skill competency are
needed in athletic training education.
Comparing functional brain networks in unimanual and bimanual
grasping: A graph theory approach
Shaochen Huang, Texas Woman’s University
Compared with unimanual grasping, bimanual grasping has been considered more complex as there are greater demands of coordination and
synchronization between the two hands (e.g., Kelso et al., 1979). Studies
showed that the right parietal cortex that facilitating left-hand grasping
was crucially involved in bimanual grasping (Le et al., 2017; 2019).
Furthermore, cortical activation patterns during bimanual grasping more
resembled those of left-hand grasping than right-hand grasping (Guo
et al., 2021). We thus hypothesized that the cortical networks involved in
bimanual grasping would demonstrate stronger bilateral connectivity
compared with unimanual grasping and would more resemble left-hand
grasping networks than right-hand grasping networks. To examine this
hypothesis, we conducted a secondary analysis on EEG data recorded via
a 64-channel system in 15 right-handed participants who performed
visually guided unimanual and bimanual grasping tasks (see in Guo
et al., 2021).The grasping tasks were left-hand, right-hand (both using the
thumb and index ﬁnger), and bimanual grasps (using both index ﬁngers).
In each trial, participants viewed an object for 500-1000ms, followed by a
Go cue that signaled which type of grasp to make. We applied Graph
Theory Analysis on 250ms EEG segments aligned to the Go cue and
assessed network properties such as node clustering, network efﬁciency,
and brain modularity. Our results showed that bimanual grasping yielded
small-world networks with signiﬁcantly higher clustering and global
efﬁciency compared to unimanual tasks, reﬂecting faster and more
efﬁcient information processing. Modularity analyses also revealed
functional modules across frontal, parietal, and occipital regions and
documented a greater number of electrodes engaged during bimanual
grasping in frontal areas. Consistent with our hypotheses, both left-hand
and bimanual grasping showed extensive bilateral frontoparietal connectivity, whereas right-hand connectivity was more restricted to the left
hemisphere. These ﬁndings point to the enhanced bilateral interplay and a
specialized role of the left, non-dominant hemisphere involvement in
bimanual grasping.
Amp up your performance: Effects of transcranial direct current
stimulation combined with motor imagery training
Judith Bek, University of Toronto; Amna Aziz, University College Dublin;
Nuala Brady, University College Dublin
Motor imagery training (MIT) is a form of mental practice based on
imagined
movement,
which
can
enhance
motor
learning
and
rehabilitation across different populations. MIT can be integrated with
transcranial direct current stimulation (tDCS) to optimize outcomes by
enhancing consolidation and promoting neuroplasticity. While there is
substantial literature on both of these approaches used separately, fewer
studies have examined combined MIT + tDCS interventions. This narrative review evaluated the evidence for synergistic behavioral effects
of MIT combined with tDCS. After screening and exclusions, the
review included 16 studies with 432 participants in total, including
both healthy and clinical populations. Results were heterogeneous, but
the overall ﬁndings indicated that concurrent anodal tDCS over primary
motor cortex during MIT could have beneﬁcial effects on motor
performance and learning outcomes in healthy participants. One study
also found improved MIT outcomes with anodal stimulation of the
dorsolateral prefrontal cortex. Several studies utilizing tDCS to boost
MIT in the context of brain-computer interface (BCI) paradigms (n = 6)
found less conclusive results. The inconsistency in results may reﬂect
differences in timing of stimulation (i.e., tDCS was used for priming
prior to BCI protocols), population characteristics (i.e., BCI studies
involved stroke patients rather than healthy participants), or alignment
of outcome measures with trained tasks. Further research should involve
larger samples and longer intervention durations, and should investigate
different stimulation targets and timing, to ascertain the optimal parameters for combined MIT + tDCS interventions in healthy and clinical
populations.
Neuromuscular activity after balance training in young sub-elite
soccer players: An exploratory study
Sam Limpach, Saarland University, Germany; Katharina Borgmann,
University of Duisburg-Essen, Germany; Thomas Muehlbauer, University
of Duisburg-Essen, Germany; Dirk Krombholz, Saarland University,
Germany; Peter Leinen, Saarland University, Germany; Stefan Panzer,
Saarland University, Germany
Inter-limb asymmetries in balance performance resulting from the
extensive use of one limb compared to the other during speciﬁc balance
tasks such as soccer (e.g., superior balance performance of the supporting leg compared to the kicking leg) are negatively associated with
performance and health-related outcomes. Reducing inter-limb asymmetry in balance capabilities is essential for maximizing performance
and minimizing the risk of lower extremity injuries. Despite its importance, research on the neuromuscular activation behind adaptations
from balance training remains scarce. This study aims to address this
gap by examining changes in neuromuscular activation between dominant and non-dominant lower leg muscles across various balanceoriented training regimens. Seventeen young sub-elite soccer players
participated in one of three 10-week training programs: balance exercises only (BAL; n = 5), a combination of balance and plyometric
exercises (PLY; n = 6), or a control program (CON; n = 6) involving
mobility and cardiovascular exercises. Time to stabilization (TTS) was
assessed before and after training, requiring participants to perform
three unipedal drop landings with both legs from a height of 35 cm onto
an unstable surface placed on a force plate. Surface electromyography
(sEMG) recorded the activity of four lower leg muscles (gastrocnemius,
tibialis anterior, peroneus longus, and soleus). After applying low- and
high-pass Butterworth ﬁlters, sEMG data was segmented based on TTS
data from the force plate. The data was then normalized using a
previously performed dynamic maximal voluntary squat jumps.
Changes in the limb symmetry index (LSI) based on the root mean
square of the normalized sEMG for all muscles were analyzed pre- and
post-training. For the landing leg, BAL and PLYO group showed
increased LSI values for all four muscles (except for the tibialis anterior
in the BAL group). In the CON group, two muscles showed

Walking the plank improves balance conﬁdence and walking speed in
community-dwelling older adults
Ben Sidaway, Husson University; David Anderson, San Francisco State
University
A third of community-dwelling older adults will fall at least once per
year with walking being the leading cause of such falls. In such
individuals, the greatest postural instability during walking occurs in
the mediolateral direction and thus lateral instability is a signiﬁcant
risk factor for falls. The current study uses one-month of beam walking
as a means to challenge the dynamic mediolateral stability of older
adults in an attempt to improve balance conﬁdence and falls risk.
Participants were 24 community dwelling older adults over 70 years of
age (Mage = 83.5 years). Baseline assessment included a fear of falling
questionnaire, the Activities-speciﬁc Balance Conﬁdence (ABC) scale,
the Dynamic Gait Index (DGI), and collection of multiple gait parameters during normal and quick walking. Participants were then
assessed on their ability to walk along a series of six wooden beams
(6 m long, 2 cm thick) of decreasing widths. Following these pretest
measurements participants practiced walking on the beams for 20
minutes twice a week for four weeks. Participants progressed to the
next narrower beam once they were able to achieve three successful
passes on a given beam. Gait parameters were collected after the last
day of training. One week later all pretest measures were collected
again. At the pretest the 24 participants reported a total of 38 falls in the
last six months. A 6-month follow up revealed a total of 11 falls since
the training. At the pretest 83% of the participants reported they were
somewhat afraid of falling while at the retention test 37% were
somewhat afraid of falling. Analysis of the ABC data revealed a
signiﬁcant improvement in balance conﬁdence from the pretest
(M = 61%) to the retention test (M = 90%). The DGI also improved
from the pretest to the retention test. Gait analysis revealed that stride
length and gait velocity increased signiﬁcantly following beam training while stride length and stride width variability decreased. Beam
training appears to be a simple intervention that can improve dynamic
mediolateral stability consequently reducing the falls risk of older
adults.
“Watch and learn”: Combined action observation and motor imagery
is no more effective at inducing motor cortex plasticity than action
observation alone
Markus Lenizky, University of Toronto; Judith Bek, University College
Dublin; Ben Marshall, Manchester Metropolitan University; David J.
Wright, Manchester Metropolitan University; Timothy N. Welsh, University of Toronto
Action observation (AO) and motor imagery (MI) can have positive
effects on motor learning and neurorehabilitation. Neuroimaging evidence indicates that AO and MI engage neural networks that overlap
with those engaged during physical execution. Studies examining
combined AO and MI (AOMI) indicate that more robust effects may
be facilitated when engaging in both methods simultaneously, compared to AO or MI alone. Hence, AOMI training could provide a more
efﬁcient method to promote cortical reorganization. To test this prediction, the current study compared the effects of AO and AOMI training
using a transcranial magnetic stimulation (TMS)-evoked thumb
movement paradigm. Previous studies using a similar paradigm found
that physical training and MI training both led to plasticity-related
changes in the direction of practiced thumb movements. In the present
study, right-handed participants (N = 30), received TMS to the left
primary motor cortex to evoke thumb movements at baseline and after
either AO (n = 15) or AOMI (n = 15) training. The training involved
repeatedly watching a video of a thumb movement in the opposite
direction (ﬂexion or extension) to participants’ dominant baseline TMSevoked thumb movement across ﬁve blocks of 300 trials (1500 trials in
total). Participants in the AO group simply watched the thumb movement video, whereas participants in the AOMI group were additionally
asked to simultaneously imagine the feeling of making the movement
they observed (kinesthetic MI). Results showed a signiﬁcant change in
the proportion of TMS-evoked thumb movements in the trained direction after two blocks (600 trials) in the AO group (p = .04) and 4 blocks
(1200 trials) in the AOMI group (p = .006). The patterns of these
changes are comparable to previous ﬁndings from MI-only training.
Overall, these results indicate that AO, MI, and AOMI can induce motor
cortical reorganization after sufﬁcient training. The present study,
however, did not provide evidence that AOMI training leads to larger
or more efﬁcient training effects than AO or MI alone. The absence of a
difference between the effects of AO, MI and AOMI training might be
due to the simplicity of the motor task in the present study. Funding
source: National Science and Engineering Research Council.
Bimanual reach-to-grasp performance with the absence of visual hand
displacement information in Virtual Reality
Guilherme Martins, University of Wisconsin - Madison; Bryce Sprecher,
University of Wisconsin - Madison; Andrea Mason, University of Wisconsin – Madison
Many daily activities require spatial and temporal coordination of the two
hands to successfully reach and grasp objects. These interactions are
facilitated by visual feedback, however the sensorimotor mechanisms
involved in performing visually guided skills are complex. A considerable
challenge to a complete understanding of how vision is used in bimanual
performance relates to our ability to precisely manipulate visual information in real world experimental settings. This is especially true when trying
to only occlude parts of the body or environment. Virtual Reality (VR) has
emerged as a solution in motor control research, allowing precise control
over what is seen inside the simulations. The goal of this study was to
investigate how the presence or absence of a graphic representation of one
or both hands inﬂuences reaching and grasping when target objects are the
same size (congruent) or different (incongruent) sizes. Thirteen righthanded young adults participated in the study, performing bimanual reachto-grasp movements in VR. Congruent or incongruent virtual target object
pairs were presented under four visual feedback conditions: full vision of
both hands (FV), right hand absent (RA), left hand absent (LA), both hands
absent (BA). Measures of interest were movement times (MT) for both
hands and accuracy (i.e. successfully grasping and lifting the object). A
main effect of condition (F(3, 36) = 5.525, p = .003) revealed that movements in which the left hand was absent (LA and BA) produced longer
movement times for both hands (LA = 1134 ± 56 ms, BA = 1177 ± 74 ms),
than the FV (1053 ± 50 ms) condition. Additionally, there was a Hand X
Condition interaction (F(3, 36) = 7.438, p < .001) which indicated that
accuracy for the left hand was reduced in the LA and BA conditions when
compared to the FV and RA conditions, while accuracy of the right hand
was similar across all conditions. These ﬁndings highlight the critical role
of visual feedback in guiding ﬁne motor actions in VR and suggest that
lack of visual feedback about the non-dominant hand (left) is detrimental to
coordinated movement. These results also point to an increased ability to
use proprioception to guide movements produced by the right hand.

Subtyping Parkinson’s disease by symptom dominance reveals group
differences in postural sway
Taylor M. Gauss, Louisiana State University; Reuben N. Addison, DePauw University; Donald Ingram, Pennington Biomedical Research
Center; Rebecca Whiddon, The Neuromedical Center; Gerald Calegan,
The Neuromedical Center; Catherine M. Champagne, Pennington Biomedical Research Center; Joseph Francis, Louisiana State University; Jan
M. Hondzinski, Louisiana State University
Assessment of motor symptoms through the Uniﬁed Parkinson’s Disease
Rating Scale, section III (UPDRS-III) and functional postural and gait
measures can offer researchers and clinicians insight into disease progression and treatment outcomes. Investigators use subjective UPDRS-III
scores to quantify the cardinal symptoms of Parkinson’s disease (PD),
including tremor, rigidity, bradykinesia, and postural instability, in addition to the dysfunctional outcomes, like changes in gait and standing
posture. Since total UPDRS-III scores include functional outcomes, the
positive correlation with objective measures of posture and gait are
expected. Subtyping people with PD and subtotaling scores from the
UPDRS-III would offer better insight into the relationship between
symptoms and functional measures of postural control and gait. Thus,
we examined whether objective postural and gait measures differed
between subtypes of PD and/or correlated with subtotal scores to determine
whether symptom and functional differences aligned. We assessed 20
people with PD to characterize them as tremor-dominant (TD) and
akinetic-rigid (AR) subtypes using the UPDRS-III (Schiess et al., 2000)
and quantiﬁed their postural sway while standing still on a force plate with
eyes open/closed and measured their gait kinematics while walking on an
instrumented walkway under varied conditions. Total UPDRS-III scores
did not differ between groups. Postural sway for TD exceeded AR to
suggest poorer postural control in TD than AR. Only tremor subtotals of
the UPDRS-III positively correlated with postural sway measures with
eyes open, so that people with greater tremor scores swayed more with
visual inputs available. Hypokinetic scores negatively correlated with
double support during fast walking, so that people with greater hypokinetic
scores spent less time in double support when asked to walk quickly. We
conclude postural sway differences between PD subtypes of TD and AR
exist and that tremor subtotals may help differentiate performance differences in multiple aspects of postural control. Funding source: Wild
Blueberry Association of North America.
Toe clearance when navigating objects relies on visual feedback
regardless of obstacle removal
Audrey K. Abadie, Louisiana State University; Tia Watts, Louisiana State
University; Abayomi G. Adeyemo, Louisiana State University; Taylor M.
Gauss, Louisiana State University; Jan M. Hondzinski, Louisiana State
University
People often use visual cues in a feed-forward manner to plan movements needed for obstacle clearance during gait. In fact, we previously
determined that participants increased toe clearance when stepping over
an obstacle with a visual illusion which inﬂuenced perceived obstacle
height compared to stepping over a plain obstacle and a second obstacle
two steps later. We wanted to determine whether people would maintain
this same look-ahead strategy when asked to step over a remembered
obstacle with and without illusory application. Thus, we asked participants to step over a real or remembered obstacle (34.2 cm x 12 cm x
23.6 cm shoe box) with a plain or an illusory (vertical lines) surface on
its rise, while walking across a room at a comfortable pace. Placement of
the plain or illusory box was approximately three or four steps into the
gait path so that each participant used the dominant lead leg to step over
the obstacle. The box remained in place for real trials and was removed
for remembered trials two steps prior to obstacle location. Markers
placed on each hallux and lateral malleoli were monitored using a fourcamera passive marker Qualisys system (Qualisys Medical AB, SE).
Lead and trail toe clearance above the obstacle were calculated and used
as the primary variables of interest. Means were compared using
Friedman ANOVAs and Wilcoxon Matched Pairs test with Bonferroni
corrections (p < 0.05). Factors included box type (plain, illusory),
clearance type (real, remembered), and leg (lead, trail). Results indicated that toe clearance for real trials exceeded toe clearance for
remembered trials regardless of box type or leg. Additionally, lead toe
clearance exceeded trail toe clearance in many cases, so that participants
did not always step over the remembered obstacle with their trail leg. We
concluded that illusory cues become irrelevant and that people can alter
their look-ahead strategy when traversing remembered obstacles that
produce no threat to tripping.
Empowering communities: Insights from the student-ﬁghter project
on motor development and Parkinson’s disease
Jackson Wagner, Texas A&M University; Madison Weinrich, Texas A&M
University; Brock Balthazor, Texas A&M University; Cindy Conte, Robert
Conte Foundation for Parkinson’s Disease and Movement Disorders, Inc.;
Deanna Kennedy, Texas A&M University
Parkinson’s disease (PD) signiﬁcantly impacts motor and functional
abilities, posing challenges for individuals in maintaining independence
and quality of life. The Student-Fighter Project was developed as a
community-based experiential learning initiative to empower individuals
with PD, referred to as “ﬁghters,” through tailored motor development
programs while providing kinesiology students with real-world application
of motor learning principles. This study aimed to evaluate the impact of
student-designed motor programs on ﬁghters’ motor performance and to
explore the educational and personal growth experienced by students
involved in the project. Participants included individuals with PD (ﬁghters)
and undergraduate kinesiology students. The project involved multiple
stages: (1) volunteer training, (2) interviews with ﬁghters to assess needs
and goals, (3) pre- and post-program motor performance assessments using
standardized tools, and (4) the development and implementation of
individualized motor programs targeting balance, strength, ﬂexibility,
and/or ﬁne motor control. Data were collected through reﬂective essays,
thematic coding of qualitative reports, and performance assessments.
Thematic analysis revealed four core themes: (1) training enhanced
students’ knowledge of PD and motor development principles, (2) increased awareness of the diverse challenges faced by individuals with PD,
(3) meaningful engagement with ﬁghters fostered empathy and emotional
connection, and (4) students experienced signiﬁcant personal and professional growth, including improved conﬁdence and communication skills.
Quantitative assessments showed improvements in ﬁghters’ motor performance, particularly in balance and functional movement. The StudentFighter Project demonstrates the dual impact of experiential learning
initiatives: empowering individuals with PD through tailored interventions
while fostering students’ academic and personal development. These
ﬁndings highlight the value of integrating community-based research into
kinesiology curricula and emphasize the importance of fostering empathy
and connection in motor development education. Future research should
explore the long-term impacts of these programs on participants and their
communities.
Exploring the impact of variable practice on speciﬁcity and guidance
effects
Sameeha Jafri, University of Toronto; Gabriela Oancea, University of
Toronto; Fiona Magill, University of Toronto; Liam Tran, University of
Toronto; Luc Tremblay, University of Toronto
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S27
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 31] ---
The speciﬁcity of practice hypothesis suggests that learning is speciﬁc to
the source of sensory feedback more likely to favor optimal accuracy
(Proteau, 1992; see also Toussaint et al., 2016). Thus, providing practicing
with highly reliable cues during practice enhances performance during
acquisition but performance is impaired when such feedback is withdrawn
(e.g., Tremblay & Proteau, 1998; see also guidance effects: e.g., Hodges &
Campagnaro, 2012). Inspired by robotic guidance studies from our lab
(e.g., Bested et al., 2019), we recently explored a mixed practice schedule
that combined laser-guided and no-vision trials (i.e., mixed practice) when
aiming to a single target (i.e., constant practice) (Tremblay et al., 2024).
Such a mixed guidance approach is presumed to balance the provision of a
reference-of-correctness and enabling error detection-correction mechanisms. These recent results indicated that mixed practice mitigated the
dependence on the laser. Considering that variable practice is more
effective than constant practice for skill acquisition (Schmidt & Lee,
2011) and can also mitigate speciﬁcity effects (e.g., Tremblay et al.,
2001), the current study replicated our mixed practice, laser-guided
experiment with the addition of variable practice. Participants practiced
an elbow ﬂexion task, aiming their forearm to one of three target positions.
They were either assigned to a no-vision group (NV), a laser-only group
(LAS), which visually guided their arm movements, or a mixed group
(MIX), which alternated between the conditions across acquisition blocks.
Participants completed 100 acquisition trials, followed by 10 transfer trials
without vision. During acquisition, the absolute constant error (ACE) data
indicated that the laser group performed the best (M = 3.1mm), followed by
the mixed group (M = 4.9), and the no-vision group performing the worst
(M = 6.4mm). In contrast, ACE did not signiﬁcantly differ between groups
in the retention test (LAS: M = 6.0mm; MIX: M = 6.4mm; NV: M = 5.0).
Similar results were found for variable error (VE). These ﬁndings contrast
with our previous study and suggest that variable practice may help
mitigate the speciﬁcity effects associated with receiving visual guidance
on every trial. Funding source: Natural Sciences and Engineering Research
Council of Canada (NSERC)
Reinforcing temporal judgement boosts sense of agency and modulates its underlying neural correlates
Leonardo Barzi, Louisiana State University; Chris Hill, Louisiana State
University; Numa Samnani, Northern Illinois University; Matt Wilson,
Northern Illinois University
The sense of agency (SOA), a feeling of being in control of one’s actions, is
fundamental to the formation of action-outcome relationships. Reinforcement and its valence also change the action-outcome relationship, either
through behavior promotion or diminishment. However, the relationship
between reinforcement and SOA is not well understood. This study
evaluated how reinforcement valence modulates SOA, via intentional
binding (IB) and brain activity. Thirty-three young healthy adults (Mage ±
SD: 21.84 ± 2.52 years) were randomly and equally allocated to one of
three feedback groups (Reward, Punishment, Control). Participants performed counter-balanced active and passive interval estimation tasks,
where either themselves (active) or the experimenter (passive) pressed a
button triggering an audio tone. Participants then estimated the length of
time between the button press and tone which was presented at one of three
intervals (200, 400, or 800ms ± 100ms random jitter). Estimation error
(EE) was calculated as the difference in actual and estimated time and
presented on a computer screen according to group assignment and
accuracy. An EE difference of <100ms was presented in green for Reward
and white for Punishment. Conversely, EE difference of >100ms was
presented in white for Reward and red for Punishment. Reward and
Punishment were associated with monetary gain or loss respectively.
Control received white EE feedback regardless of EE accuracy. IB was
calculated by subtracting the mean active EE from mean passive EE, across
time intervals. Electroencephalography recorded P300 amplitude, associated with EE feedback presentation. Punishment increased IB between the
button press and tone more than reward (ANOVA MD: 52.588, p = .039)
but not control (MD: 31.747, p = .244). Punishment elicited greater P300
compared to reward (RMANOVA MD: 5.554, p = .041) and control (MD:
7.131, p = .006). Punishment (MD: 2.535, p = .023), and reward (MD:
2.421, p = .038) P300 amplitude diminished when the participants did not
actively evoke the tone. Our ﬁndings showcase that reinforcement boosts
SOA and modulates associated neural activity more than no reinforcement,
as a function of increasing attention and arousal. These ﬁndings illuminate
the effect reinforcement on behavior and brain activity by its modiﬁcation
of SOA, which is important for the development of treatments in neuropsychiatric diseases.
How internal and external focus of attention affect clutch performances in golf putting
Taylor Cowell, New Mexico State University; David Eccles, Florida State
University; Christopher A. Aiken, New Mexico State University
Research has supported the beneﬁt of an external compared to an internal
focus of attention. Little research has investigated the effects of performance pressure while utilizing various attentional foci. The purpose of this
study was to investigate the effects of an internal and external focus on golf
putting while under increased performance pressure. It was hypothesized
that putting accuracy would be higher in the external focus conditions
compared to the internal focus conditions, regardless of performance
pressure. The primary dependent variables to measure performance were
putting accuracy and duration. Accuracy was scored as a miss (0 points),
near miss (0.5 points), or make (1 point). Putt duration was the amount of
time used in preparation for skill performance. Thirty-six male golfers
volunteered and performed a 6’ golf putting task under four experimental
conditions, Internal-High, Internal-Low, External-High, and ExternalLow. Eight trials were completed under each condition in a counterbalanced order. Separate one-way repeated measures ANOVAs were
conducted for each dependent variable. A signiﬁcant main effect was
observed for putt duration (p < .05). Sidak post-hoc tests revealed that
external-low had signiﬁcantly longer putt durations than internal-low
(p < .05). No signiﬁcant effects for putt accuracy were observed. The null
effect for putt accuracy is in disagreement with majority of the attentional
focus research where we expected an external focus to improve performance. The increased putt duration for external-low suggests more
cognitive processing occurred which could be a result of the focus on
the environment. Future research should use a between-subject approach
for each condition to reduce potential confounds such as carry-over with
pressure and practice effects. More research is needed to understand how
altering attentional focus while under pressure can be a useful tool for
performance.
Cognitive-motor performance and trust assessment during joint task
execution under varying levels of interdependence and demand levels
Jayesh Jayashankar, University of Maryland, College Park; Ruby Barredo, University of Maryland, College Park; Anna Packy, University of
Maryland, College Park; Arya Teymourlouei, University of Maryland,
College Park; Garrett Katz, University of Maryland, College Park; James
Purtilo, University of Maryland, College Park; James Reggia, University
of Maryland, College Park; Rodolphe Gentili, University of Maryland,
College Park
The examination of human cognitive-motor processes during collaborative
performance with a robotic partner offers an approach to alter speciﬁc
teaming components while possibly mitigating confounding factors due to
complex social dynamics. Although previous work has investigated
interdependence in human-robot teaming, none have examined how this
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S28
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 32] ---
notion relates to human cognitive-motor performance when individuals
complete complex action sequence tasks under various demands. This
study examined the impact of various team interdependence levels
(i.e., varying the degree to which teammates rely on each other) on human
performance, mental workload and trust when individuals solve spatial
reasoning puzzles of varying demand. Fourteen individuals solved puzzles
with a humanoid robot under a low and a high cognitive task demand and
three levels of team interdependence (human was more and less involved
in task completion or equally involved). For each condition participants’
performance was assessed via the Sequence Deviance Error (SDE) which
measures the discrepancy between executed and optimal sequences, and
via surveys of the mental workload and teammate trust level (predictability, reliability, overall trust). The ﬁndings revealed that i) a greater
involvement of the robot led to a decrease of SDE and perceived mental
workload; ii) enhanced task demands were accompanied by an increase of
SDE and mental workload as well as a reduction of the trust scores. These
results suggest that a greater contribution of the robotic teammate led to
performance enhancement along with an attenuated mental workload,
ultimately enhancing cognitive-motor efﬁciency without, however affecting the human trust in its partner. An elevation of task demand resulted in
reduced cognitive motor efﬁciency while humans perceived the robot as
being less predictable, reliable, and trustworthy. Altogether, these results
indicate that while a greater involvement of the robot enhanced human
cognitive-motor efﬁciency, human trust in their partner was only modulated by task demands. Future work will consider a greater pool of
participants, a broader spectrum of cognitive demand and team interdependence as well as usage of EEG to capture brain dynamics. This effort
can provide insights into human performance in a social context and the
development of AI-based robotic architectures for teaming applications.
Neurocognitive processes underlying team performance during joint
task execution under different levels of cognitive difﬁculty
Jayesh Jayashankar, University of Maryland, College Park; Anna Packy,
University of Maryland, College Park; Arya Teymourlouei, University of
Maryland, College Park; Hyuk Oh, University of Maryland, College Park;
Garrett Katz, Syracuse University, Syracuse; James Purtilo, University of
Maryland, College Park; James Reggia, University of Maryland, College
Park; Rodolphe Gentili, University of Maryland, College Park
Prior efforts have examined the effects of teaming on human performance
through human-human collaborations during the execution of action
sequences in tasks of varying complexity. However, only limited efforts
have focused on the cognitive-motor processes by investigating both the
behavioral and the cortical dynamics during collaborative task performance with a synthetic teammate which can mitigate some of the complexity underlying teaming with humans only. This work assessed the
performance and EEG-based brain dynamics when individuals solved a
puzzle task under various levels of challenge with and without the
collaboration of a humanoid robotic teammate incorporating AI-based
cognitive planning abilities. Right-handed participants executed action
sequences to jointly solve spatial reasoning puzzles with a humanoid robot
under two cognitive demand levels in a customized 3D virtual environment. Participant’s performance was assessed via the sequence deviance
error (SDE) which quantiﬁes the difference between the executed and
optimal sequence to complete the task. The cortical dynamics were
examined via spectral power analysis of EEG that was recorded throughout
the study. The ﬁndings revealed that teammate involvement and task
demand elevation led to an attenuation and an elevation of SDE, respectively. The results also revealed a reduction of theta power across the whole
scalp along with enhanced performance when the synthetic teammate was
involved. These ﬁndings suggest that teammate involvement led to
enhanced performance and may have resulted in a reduction of memory
processing when executing the task. However, the engagement of the
synthetic teammate could not fully compensate for modulations of task
demands, which when elevated, still led to reduced performance. Future
work will consider a greater pool of participants, additional spectral
analyses and continuous changes of task demand. This work can inform
the human cognitive-motor performance in a teaming context and provides
insights into the design of AI-based robotic teammates to promote adaptive
team dynamics for applications involving rehabilitation, military, and
human factors engineering.
Motor skill learning in amnestic mild cognitive impairment
Kylie Tomlin, University of Maryland, Baltimore; Kelly Westlake, University of Maryland, Baltimore
Global population aging has led to an increased prevalence of neurocognitive disorders, including mild cognitive impairment (MCI), necessitating
an improved understanding of how age-related cognitive decline affects
facets of motor training and rehabilitation. It remains unclear whether
motor learning is impaired in amnestic MCI (aMCI). The aim of this
ongoing original research study is to compare acquisition, retention, and
transfer of a modiﬁed mirror star tracing task (MTT) in older adults with
aMCI and non-cognitively impaired (NCI) matched controls, using behavioral and neurophysiological approaches. Participants (n = 6 aMCI,
n = 6 NCI; target n = 30 [15 MCI, 15 NCI]) trained a rotated-cursor tracing
task on a movement tracking device (KinereachTM) over three visits.
Online acquisition, 24-hour ofﬂine consolidation, 1-week delayed retention, and intermanual transfer were assessed using normalized change in
movement time and accuracy. Task-based cognitive workload was index
by EEG ERP responses to task-irrelevant auditory stimuli. Preliminary,
non-parametric between-group comparisons suggest preserved acquisition
but impaired overnight consolidation and 1-week retention in the aMCI
sample relative to matched controls, despite comparable sleep and motivation scores between groups. Initial ﬁndings indicate selective impairment of motor skill retention in aMCI. Findings from the completed
study will have implications for physical rehabilitation and motor skill
training in older adults with aMCI. Future research should explore motor
training approaches to optimize learning outcomes for functional motor
tasks in larger samples. Funding source: T32 AG000262
Leading and trailing limb behavior when stepping over obstacles of
different heights in the natural and virtual environment
Alejandra Padilla, University of Wisconsin-Madison; Bryce Sprecher,
University of Wisconsin-Madison; Kevin Ponto, University of Wisconsin-Madison; Kristen Pickett, University of Wisconsin-Madison; Andrea
Mason, University of Wisconsin-Madison
When individuals step over obstacles of varying heights, they plant their
trailing leg further from the obstacle when vision of their limbs is occluded.
It is unclear whether these same gait behaviors occur in virtual reality (VR)
or whether individuals adopt more conservative gait patterns in VR that are
independent of obstacle characteristics. The aim of this study was to assess
whether the absence of visual feedback of the lower limb in VR inﬂuences
trailing leg toe-distance and leading leg heel-distance when stepping over
obstacles of different heights. Young adults (n=10) walked across a 5.2m
Zeno gait mat under two visual conditions: (i) Natural Environment/
normal vision (NE), and (ii) Virtual Reality/no representation of lower
body (VR). Participants stepped over obstacles of varying heights (0, 3, 7,
12, 17 cm) positioned at the mat midpoint. Results of the two (vision) x ﬁve
(obstacle height) repeated measures ANOVA revealed a main effect of
visual condition on toe distance (F(1, 9) = 6.251, p = .034). Post hoc
analysis showed in VR, the trailing leg was planted further from the
obstacle (22.8 ± 1.253 cm) than in the NE (19.2 ± 1.306 cm). Pearson

Correlations were used to assess the linear relationship between toe
distance and heel distance across visual conditions and block heights.
Results indicated no signiﬁcant relationships between heel and toe distance
for NE but moderate to strong negative correlations for the 3 cm (r(8) = -
0.373, p = .288), 7 cm (r(8) = -0.569, p = .086), 12 cm (r(8) = -0.613,

does not allow for direct comparison of visual occlusion of the lower limbs,
results of this study support previous ﬁndings that vision of the legs effects
where to plant the foot before crossing over obstacles of different heights.

The effects of aerobic ﬁtness on bilateral transfer in young adults
Sean Cochran, Roanoke College; Taylor Rowley, Roanoke College;
Claudia Disbrow, Roanoke College; Austin Nielsen, Roanoke College
Bilateral transfer refers to the change in motor performance of one limb as a
result of practicing with the opposite limb (Parlow & Kinsbourne, 1989).
Research shows that motor skill transfer between limbs in younger adults is
often asymmetrical, with the non-dominant limb improving more after
training with the dominant limb than vice versa (Pan & van Gemmert,
2013). In young adults, this asymmetry is attributed to lateralized hemispheric control of movement parameters according to the dynamic dominance model (Sainburg, 2002), irrespective of aerobic ﬁtness level
(McGregor et al., 2013). Previous research indicates that low reported
physical activity in young adults exhibits symmetrical transfer, suggesting
a loss of lateralized control similar to what is observed in an aging
population (Cochran, 2024). Additionally, acute bouts of exercise have
been shown to affect bilateral transfer (Neva, 2019). However, bilateral
transfer has not been assessed in relation to aerobic ﬁtness. The purpose
of this study was to examine the effects of aerobic ﬁtness on bilateral
transfer
in
young
adults.
Fourteen
right-handed
participants
(Mage = 20.7 ± 0.73 years) were assessed for hand dominance and aerobic
ﬁtness. Participants were grouped by aerobic ﬁtness level as determined
via a 2-minute walk test (Rowley, 2022). Individuals performed a 30°
visual rotation drawing task assessing movement parameters involved with
motor planning. Pre-test of two trials with each limb established baseline
performance, followed by 40 practice trials on their left limb. Post-tests
mirrored pre-tests, assessing the transfer of motor planning parameters
including movement time, normalized jerk, trajectory length, and initial
direction error. Preliminary results show transfer to the right limb for initial
direction error in participants with high aerobic ﬁtness (p < .05) compared
to no transfer in those with low aerobic ﬁtness. No transfer was found for
other motor planning parameters in both low and high aerobic ﬁtness
groups. Findings suggest high aerobic ﬁtness may facilitate transfer of
initial direction error in a young adult population. Contradictory to
previous literature, current ﬁndings show low aerobic ﬁtness may hinder
normal bilateral transfer in young adults.
Effects of compromised upper extremity biomechanics on cognitivemotor performance and fatigue during completion of a sequential task
under different levels of challenge
Hunter Frisk, University of Maryland; Alexandra Shaver, University of
Maryland; Christopher Gaskins, University of Maryland; Rodolphe Gentili, University of Maryland
Previous efforts have examined the effects of compromised limb biomechanics on cognitive-motor performance, however only a few have
examined upper, rather than lower limb alterations. Additionally, these
previous works have not assessed the outcome of compromised upper
extremity biomechanics while problem-solving under various levels of
cognitive challenge. This study examined the effects of compromised
hand biomechanics on performance and mental workload while individuals solve a puzzle-task under two cognitive demands. Twelve healthy
participants performed the Tower of Hanoi puzzle under low (three rings)
and high (four rings) cognitive levels with their hand either biomechanically compromised utilizing a brace or under normal conditions (no
brace). Performance was assessed utilizing Levenshtein distance (LD)
and its operators (number of insertions, deletions and substitutions), total
sequence completion time (SCT), and number of drops (NDR). Concluding each condition, participants completed the NASA-TLX and a
visual analog scale survey to evaluate mental workload, physical demand,
cognitive and physical fatigue. Findings revealed that hand biomechanics
alterations did not affect LD and its operators, however led to greater
SCT. Additionally, individuals with compromised biomechanics reported
greater levels of perceived mental workload, cognitive and physical
fatigue. Increased cognitive task demand led to elevated LD (driven by
a greater number of insertions and substitution) and SCT, as well as
mental workload, cognitive and physical fatigue. Biomechanics alterations resulted in larger NDR in the high cognitive task demand, however
this was not observed in the normal hand condition. Findings suggest
biomechanics alterations, which involve a misalignment between the
internal model of the actual effector (i.e., hand), compromise motor
control mechanisms leading to performance decrement while further
engaging executive functioning resulting in elevated mental workload,
physical and cognitive fatigue. When biomechanics are compromised,
executive functions would be further engaged as task demand increases,
leading to performance decrement, enhanced mental workload, cognitive
and physical fatigue. Possibly, task execution with compromised biomechanics under high (but not low) cognitive demand challenged the
allocation of shared executive resources, leading to poorer ﬁne motor
performance (greater NDR). This extends previous work by examining
how compromised upper limb biomechanics affect cognitive-motor
performance under various cognitive demands.
Dual-task demands reduce force accuracy in older adults: A pilot
study of bimanual force control
Abigail Bower, University of Delaware; Jae Woo Chung, University of
Minnesota Medical School; Nancy Getchell, University of Delaware;
Roxana Burciu, University of Delaware
With the increasing demand for multitasking in daily life, understanding
how divided attention affects motor and cognitive performance is
crucial. Prior work from our lab in healthy young adults (manuscript
in preparation) showed that performing a concurrent cognitive task
during a bimanual force control task at low to moderate force levels
reduced force accuracy and increased variability compared to the motor
task alone. Given that older adults often experience greater cognitive
and motor deﬁcits, we hypothesize that dual-task demands will have a
greater impact on force control in aging. This pilot study included 5
older adults (5F, 1M; Mage = 64.8 ± 7.46 years) who completed a
bimanual pinch-grip isometric force production task at 15% and 30%
of their maximum voluntary contraction (MVC). Participants used both
hands to adjust their force to lift a bar on a monitor to match a target bar
set at the speciﬁed force levels. Ten trials for each force level were
conducted in a block design, alternating 20s of sustained force production with 20s of rest, performed once as a stand-alone motor task and
once as part of a dual-task condition. During dual-task trials, participants listened to 20s audio clips of random numbers and letters while
sustaining the force and counted the number of letters heard, providing
an answer at the end of each trial. Participants also completed the
