cognitive task as a stand-alone task, in addition to standardized cognitive assessments. The testing order was randomized. Analysis of the
force data revealed that at 15% MVC, the addition of a cognitive task
increased absolute error (AE), resulting in a dual-task cost and a 20%
increase in AE. While there were no signiﬁcant changes in cognitive
performance between the single- and dual-task conditions, participants
with better selective attention based on the Brief Test of Attention
showed less interference from the dual-task on motor performance.
These results suggest that at lower force levels, such as in ﬁne motor
control tasks, a cognitive task increases motor error in healthy older
adults. Further data collection is planned to investigate this ﬁnding more
thoroughly.
Relationship between gait and gaze in autistic and non-autistic adults
during dual motor tasks of increasing complexity
Ralph Nelson, University of Wisconsin - Madison; Kristen Pickett, University of Wisconsin - Madison; Brittany Travers, University of Wisconsin
- Madison; Andrea Mason, University of Wisconsin – Madison
When individuals perform upper- and lower-limb locomotor tasks simultaneously, gait (primary task) becomes more conservative, likely to
prioritize balance while successfully completing the upper-limb (secondary) task (Abbruzzese et al., 2014, Mason et al., 2022). This effect is
similar in both autistic and non-autistic populations. However, the degree
to which the location of visual attention and the length of gaze ﬁxations,
may be related to gait while performing dual-tasks needs further investigation. In this study, both autistic (n = 14, Mage = 23.4 ± 0.8 years) and nonautistic (n = 16, Mage = 24.5 ± 1.2 years) adults walked across a Zeno
Walkway ™(4.87m) while performing four motor tasks: A) simple
walking, and walking while carrying a B) empty tray, C) tray with
unstacked blocks, and D) tray with stacked blocks. Pearson correlations
were used to determine the linear relationship between: Step length (SL),
velocity (V), and double limb support (%DS), as well as total gaze time
(TGT), average single gaze duration (SGD), and number of gazes (NG) at
either the tray/blocks, ﬂoor, or wall (gaze up, directed forward to the wall
beyond the task space). For all conditions, signiﬁcant correlations were
found in the non-autistic group for TGT (overall) and V (r < -.550; p < .05).
For conditions C and D, both TGT and SGD at the wall were signiﬁcantly
correlated with SL for the non-autistic group (r < -.545; p < .05). For
condition D, TGT at the wall was correlated with V for the autistic group
(r = .602, p < .05), and SGD at the wall was correlated with V for both the
non-autistic (r = -.548, p < .05) and autistic (r = .624, p < .05) groups. Our
results suggest that increased dual-task complexity results in stronger
relationships between gait and gaze parameters in both autistic and nonautistic adults. The positive correlation between gazes on the wall and
measures of locomotion for the autistic group and negative correlations for
the non-autistic group may indicate fundamental differences in how these
groups use visual information for the coordination of movement. Funding
source: Kenzi Valentyn Research Grant Award - UW Madison.
EEG and gait signal processing: Comparison between a novel ﬁlter
and more traditional ﬁltering techniques
Reza Pousti, Old Dominion University; Russell Daniel M., Old Dominion
University; Monroe Derek C., University of North Carolina at Chapel
Hill; Rhea Christopher K., Old Dominion University
Filtering out noise to focus on the meaningful aspects of brain and bodily
signals is a classic challenge in human movement and neuroscience. The
Butterworth, Chebyshev, and Elliptic ﬁlters are commonly used to address
this challenge, but each has unique limitations from their polynomial
function that cause ripple, distortion and attenuation. To address these
limitations, we developed the ‘Reza’ ﬁlter, which is based on the exponential function. This study evaluated the performance of the novel, Reza
ﬁlter by comparing post-ﬁlter signal characteristics against those generated
by traditional ﬁlters. Electroencephalography (EEG) and inertial measurement unit (IMU) gait data were concurrently collected from participants
(n = 45 with EEG and n = 43 with IMU) during an outdoor walking session
and made publicly available by the authors, Hanada, Kalabic, and Ferris
(2024) were used for this study. Signals were constrained to the 0.5-50 Hz
range using each ﬁlter (Reza, Butterworth, Chebyshev, and Elliptic).
Signal characteristics, the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) and broadband
power spectral density (PSD), were calculated from each resulting time
series. Four separate one-way ANOVAs were run to test differences in
SNR and PSD for EEG and IMU signals between the ﬁlters. For EEG, the
SNR was signiﬁcantly different between the ﬁltered signals (p < .001),
with the Chebyshev ﬁlter producing the greatest SNR signal (-
10.4 ± 1.6 dB), followed by the Elliptic (-10.8 ± 1.6 dB), Reza (-
12.5 ± 1.9 dB), and Butterworth (-13.2 ± 1.9 dB). No differences in EEG
PSD were observed (p = .24). For the IMU gait data, the SNR was
signiﬁcantly different between ﬁltered signals (p < .001), with the Reza
and Butterworth ﬁlters producing the greatest SNR (18.3 ± 0.4 dB and
17.3 ± 0.4 dB, respectively), followed by the Elliptic (16.7 ± 0.3 dB) and
Chebyshev (15.9 ± 0.3 dB). Likewise, differences in IMU PSD were
observed (p > .001), with the Reza ﬁlter exhibiting the highest average
PSD (8.6 ± 0.4), followed by the Butterworth (7.5 ± 0.3), Chebyshev
(7.1 ± 0.3), and Elliptic (7.0 ± 0.3). Collectively, the results show that
various ﬁlters lead to different SNR and PSD characteristics in the time
series. Thus, it is important for researchers to select the right ﬁlter for their
research question, as the passband ripple or amplitude attenuation characteristics of each ﬁlter could inﬂuence their results.
Shoot/don’t-shoot performance of law enforcement ofﬁcers and civilians under normal and high cognitive load
Robert Horn, Montclair State University; Skylar Paletta, Montclair State
University; Gustavo Heidner, Montclair State University; William Lewinski, Force Science Institute; Lon Bartel, ViRtra Inc; Daniel Gwon,
Montclair State University; Nicholas Murray, East Carolina University
We compared shoot/don’t shoot task performance in law enforcement ofﬁcers
(LEOs; n =32) and non-ofﬁcers (n = 29) under normal cognitive load, and
under higher load induced by processing a complex dispatch message.
Dependent measures were errors (shoot; fail-to-shoot), response time, and
shooting accuracy. We also compared levels of reinforcement sensitivity
(rRST-Q) and impulsivity (BIS-11) between the groups and examined their
effects on performance. Controlling for age and sex, ofﬁcers had lower
behavioral inhibition system scores, lower freeze scores, lower attention and
non-planning impulsivity sub-scores (all p < .05), and lower total impulsivity
(p < .01) than non-ofﬁcers. Compared to non-ofﬁcers, LEOs were more
accurate and made fewer fail-to-shoot errors under normal and higher
cognitive load (all p < .01). However, ofﬁcers were not faster, and critically,
did not make fewer shoot errors. Compared to normal cognitive load, under
higher load, participants were quicker (p < .01) and more accurate (p < .05)
but made more shoot errors (p < .01). There was no effect of cognitive load on
fail-to-shoot errors. The higher cognitive load condition revealed effects that
were not present under normal load. Participants making more shoot errors
had higher behavioral activation (p < .01) and behavioral inhibition scores
(p < .05) than those making fewer or no shoot errors. Also, participants
making multiple fail-to-shoot errors had higher non-planning impulsivity
(p < .01) and total impulsivity (p < .05) than those making one fail-to-shoot
error. The results suggest that training and experience do not necessarily offer
protection from making shoot errors, and that under cognitive load, participants risk prioritizing speed and accuracy over making shoot errors. The
results also suggest that reinforcement sensitivity and impulsivity may be
useful tests for identifying those at higher risk of making shoot- and fail-toshoot errors under high cognitive workload.

Multi-joint coordination variability using an ellipse-based vector
coding analysis
Hwigeum Jeong, University of Massachusetts Amherst/Kinesiology Department; Richard van Emmerik, University of Massachusetts Amherst/
Kinesiology Department
Vector coding is an analytic technique widely applied in movement science
to quantify intralimb and interlimb coordination dynamics and variability.
Existing methods are restricted to bivariate analyses focusing on coordination between two joints or segments. This may not fully explain the dynamic
properties of motor behavior that are typically achieved by multi-component
coordination. Recent advances in vector coding methods assessing coordination variability have further developed an ellipse-based method that
addresses methodological confounders of earlier approaches. Unlike the
conventional methods, the ellipse-based approach is not limited to bivariate
coordination variability but allows identiﬁcation of multi-variate coordination variability. The present study explored effects of walking speed on
bivariate coordination variability of the sagittal plane hip-knee and kneeankle couplings, respectively, and a trivariate coordination variability of the
sagittal plane hip-knee-ankle multi-component coupling. Ten healthy individuals (5 men and 5 women, Mage: 24 ± 3.8 years) completed a 5-min
walking trial on a treadmill at slow (70% of preferred; average 0.79 m/s) and
fast walking speeds (130% of preferred; average 1.47 m/s). The normalized
joint angular velocities across the gait cycle for 80 strides were used for both
the bivariate and trivariate coordination variability. For statistical analysis, a
paired t-test using statistical parametric mapping was applied (α = .05) to
identify when statistical differences occurred in the gait cycle. Our ﬁndings
showed that the fast-walking condition signiﬁcantly increases the hip-knee
coordination variability at terminal stance (p = .003) and the knee-ankle
coordination variability at terminal stance (p = .047), pre-swing (p = .005),
and initial swing (p = .007) compared to the slow walking speed. In contrast
to the observed bivariate effects, there were no statically signiﬁcant
differences as a function of gait speed for the trivariate hip-knee-ankle
coordination variability. In conclusion, the consistent coordination variability of the multi-component coupling across walking speeds may indicate
that these bivariate coordination variability changes are compensatory,
resulting in an overall invariance in lower extremity coordination variability
when gait speed is changed. This result is consistent with the idea that not all
local ﬂuctuations impact macroscopic coordinative function and variability.
An abbreviated Grooved Pegboard test for assessing manual dexterity
and associations with cognitive function in older adults
Toby Halbert, Seattle University; Brittany Heintz Walters, Seattle
University
Manual dexterity impairments in older adults, commonly assessed using
the 25-hole Grooved Pegboard test, may be critically inﬂuenced by agerelated changes in cognitive processes. This study evaluated the validity of
an abbreviated 10-hole Grooved Pegboard test and its associations with
cognitive deﬁcits in older adults. A total of 23 healthy young adults (Mage:
25.30 ± 4.40 years; 12 female, 11 male) and 28 healthy older adults (Mage:
72.64 ± 6.63 years; 13 female, 15 male) completed the standard 25-hole
and an abbreviated 10-hole Grooved Pegboard test under a single-task
condition and while performing a secondary cognitive task. Performance
on standardized tests of attention was also measured. Older adults took
signiﬁcantly longer to complete the 10-hole test compared to young adults
(31.43 s ± 6.31 s and 21.62 s ± 2.46 s, respectively), with further performance impairments under the dual-task condition (54.56 s ± 5.07 s). In
older adults, increased pegboard completion time during the dual-task
condition was correlated with decreased performance on the Trail Making
Test A (r = 0.589, p < .001) and Trail Making Test B (r = 0.613, p < .001),
indicating an association between motor performance and cognitive
impairments. No such associations were observed in young adults. These
ﬁndings support the validity of the abbreviated Grooved Pegboard test as a
time-efﬁcient alternative to the standard version; for example, in populations with reduced sustained attention or increased susceptibility to muscle
fatigue. The results also underscore the importance of incorporating
cognitive assessments when evaluating motor impairments in older adults.
More work is needed to assess the validity and reliability of the abbreviated
Grooved Pegboard test and to further explore the interplay between
cognition and motor function in the aging population.
Assessment of mental workload during cognitive-motor performance:
from humans to AI-based humanoid robots
Rodolphe Gentili, University of Maryland - College Park; Garrett Katz,
Syracuse University; James Reggia, University of Maryland - College Park
The continuous advances of AI and human neurosciences gave rise to
humanoid robots with advanced cognitive-motor mechanisms enabling
highly adaptive behavior when they perform alone or with a human
partner. The availability of such robots has naturally led to parallel
investigation of human neuroscience concepts such as mental imagery,
trust, and consciousness in adaptive artiﬁcial systems. Although widely
considered in humans, the concept of mental workload in advanced
cognitive-motor humanoid robots is limited. It would be informative to
investigate if a construct comparable to mental workload can be identiﬁed
for a cognitive-motor humanoid robot when it differently engages its
computational processing resources to perform under varying demands or
when learning novel tasks. In this work we propose and discuss a ﬁrst
theoretical framework which attempts to draw a parallel between mental
workload in humans and AI-based robots by considering the relationship
between processing resources and task demands. This framework is
illustrated by an example where metrics based on the internal states of
a cognitive-motor architecture controlling a humanoid can be derived to
evaluate the computational processing effort reminiscent of human mental
workload. The development of a synthetic mental workload based on the
processing effort of the robot’s cognitive architecture could have several
implications. It would enable a more comprehensive assessment of the
robot’s performance beyond behavioral analysis. In turn, both the behavioral outcome and the amount of information processing resources deployed by the robot to complete a task could be combined to represent
cognitive-motor efﬁciency as done in humans, providing thus a more
comprehensive performance assessment. Being able to evaluate the modulation of such a synthetic mental processing effort is important since it can
inﬂuence an AI-based humanoid robots’ behavior and in turn affect
humans when individuals collaboratively work with their robotic teammates. In particular, the robot could be used as a synthetic teammate in
which the information processing resources could be manipulated to
examine human neurocognitive mechanisms in a team environment.
Anticipation and visual search strategy of deceptive skills in handball
defense situation
Ayoub Asadi, Iowa State University; Zahra Nezakat Alhossein, Islamic
Azad University Isfahan Khorasgan Branch; Itay Basevitch, Barry University; Ann Smiley, Iowa State University
This study examined the anticipation and visual search strategies employed
by elite youth handball players when facing deceptive skills (feints) in
simulated defensive scenarios. The study involved 20 male handball
players aged 17-19, all members of national youth and pioneer teams.
Participants as defenders observed 15 video clips (each clip ﬁve seconds)
featuring attackers in three different situations performing feints, shots, or
passes. Temporal occlusion was applied by cutting the clips at critical

moments (i.e., 80 ms before the action’s initiation) to assess participants’
anticipation of the subsequent action. After the clip was occluded, participants verbally anticipated and selected one of the options: feint, pass, or
shot. Eye-tracking data were recorded to analyze participants’ visual
search behavior during the task. Based on research aims, the feint clips
(ﬁve clips) were isolated, and responses were categorized as correct (32%)
or incorrect (68%). Results revealed distinct visual search patterns between
correct and incorrect responses. During correct trials, participants exhibited signiﬁcantly longer ﬁxation durations on the attacker’s hand. Conversely, incorrect responses were associated with a higher number of
ﬁxations and longer ﬁxation durations on the attacker’s head and body.
These ﬁndings suggest that effective anticipation in deceptive scenarios
relies on allocating visual attention to speciﬁc action-relevant cues, such as
the hand, rather than more general or less predictive areas like the head or
body. The results underscore the potential of developing training programs
that reﬁne athletes’ visual search strategies, enabling more accurate and
efﬁcient responses to deceptive movements.
Insight into the Vestibular Ocular Motor Screening test: Is symptom
data predictive of long-term concussion recovery?
Samantha Gallagher, Montclair State Univeristy; Julia Rose Duardo,
Montclair State University; Natalie Mileski, Montclair State University;
Samantha Hagenbush, Montclair State University; Luis Torres, Montclair
State University; August Price, Bellapianta Orthopaedics & Sports Medicine; Robert Horn, Montclair State University; Gustavo Sandri Heidner,
Montclair State University
Concussions, a type of traumatic brain injury caused by an impact to the head,
are characterized by a wide range of symptoms. Accurate diagnostics of
concussions remains challenging due to variability in symptom presentation
and limitations in the precision of existing testing methods, especially more
than two weeks post-trauma. This gap has led to increased interest in the
development of different tools, such as the Vestibular Ocular Motor Screening
(VOMS). The VOMS assesses vestibular and ocular motor function, providing insights into symptoms like dizziness, headache, nausea, and fogginess.
Typically applied in the clinical setting and the side of the ﬁeld in sports, it is
still unclear whether its symptom invocation effect can accurately predict
concussion recovery past the acute phase. Adult patients (N = 46, 29 females)
completed the VOMS at a local clinic as part of their treatment for concussion
(Mdays since injury = 159.47, SD = 215.17, Range = 8-1152). As part of the
test, patients scored their symptoms (headache, dizziness, nausea, and
fogginess) on a 0-10 scale at baseline and after completing smooth pursuits,
saccades, convergence, and vestibular-ocular reﬂex (VOR) paradigms. A
multiple regression revealed that symptoms were not predictive of Time Since
Injury at baseline (p = .396, Adj R2 = .007), smooth pursuit (p = .393, Adj
R2 = .007), horizontal saccades (p = .266, Adj R2 = .037), vertical saccades
(p = .426, Adj R2 = .001), near point convergence (p = .357, Adj R2 = .015),
horizontal VOR (p = .694, Adj R2 = -.048), and vertical VOR (p = .518, Adj
R2 = -.016). These results suggest that the VOMS may not be an adequate tool
for long-term concussion symptoms assessments when used in isolation. No
correlations were found between invoked symptoms and time since injury. A
small number of patients (n = 5) were in the acute concussive stage (<15
days). More investigations are needed to determine which the factors are
inﬂuencing the results and if these factors can be controlled to increase test
sensitivity.
An uncontrolled manifold analysis of joint kinematics in infants with
Down syndrome during treadmill stepping
Alexandre dos Santos Kotarski, Georgia State University; Matthew
Beerse, University of Dayton; Amy Talboy, Emory University; Seyda
Ozcaliskan, Georgia State University; Jianhua Wu, Georgia State
University
Down syndrome (DS) is a genetic disorder associated with delayed
psychomotor development. Previous studies have shown that treadmill
intervention is effective in advancing walking onset and quality. However,
the neuromotor control of treadmill stepping is unknown. The uncontrolled
manifold analysis (UCM) is a good framework to analyze the regulation of
motor variability in repeated tasks, such as walking. This study aimed to
examine motor variability via UCM in infants with DS during bodyweightsupported treadmill stepping. Thirteen infants with DS (9M/4F, 11.3 ± 2.3
months) entered the study for treadmill intervention. A pediatric treadmill
was provided to the family, and the parents conducted the training for
8 min/day, 5 days/week, with a belt speed starting at 0.1 m/s and increasing
overtime. At our monthly visit, we used a high-speed camera and four
reﬂective markers that were placed on the infant’s right hip, knee, ankle
and toe to record a 3-minute bodyweight-supported stepping trial. Preliminary results were from the last two visits (V1 and V2) before walking
onset (i.e., two and one month before walking onset, respectively). We
established a geometrical model with toe positions as the task-level
variable and hip, knee and ankle joint angles in the sagittal plane as the
elemental variables. UCM variables comprise of goal equivalency variance
(GEV), non-goal equivalency variance (NGEV), and the index of motor
abundance (IMA). Results showed both GEV and NGEV decreased from
V1 to V2 at foot contact, mid-stance, and foot off. IMA decreased from -
0.54 to -0.56 at foot contact, -0.26 to -0.55 at mid-stance, and -0.28 to -0.49
at foot off from V1 to V2. These results indicate that while infants with DS
reduced variances in both GEV and NGEV from V1 to V2, an advanced
pattern of higher GEV and lower NGEV in controlling toe positions has not
emerged during bodyweight-supported treadmill stepping. It suggests that
infants with DS are not capable of maintaining a consistent toe position
during bodyweight-supported treadmill stepping, or stabilizing a consistent toe position across steps is not yet a task-level variable that is regulated
by the nervous system. Funding source: NIH.
Unifying basic and applied motor learning research
Eric Griessbach, Champalimaud Centre for the Unknown; Adrian Haith,
Johns Hopkins University
Motor learning research is fundamentally driven by two central questions:
(1) how do humans learn motor skills, and (2) how can we accelerate this
learning process in real-world settings? These questions are inherently
interconnected—a deeper understanding of basic learning mechanisms can
inform and guide strategies to accelerate skill acquisition, while effective,
ecologically valid techniques can generate novel research questions and
offer valuable insights into the underlying learning processes. Despite
these natural synergies, current research efforts—encompassing both basic
research and applied research—too often operate in isolation. The disconnect between these approaches is evident in distinct citation patterns,
separate conference attendance, and diverging journal preferences. Basic
research, largely driven by neuroscience and computational approaches,
examines
underlying
mechanisms
through
controlled
experiments
(e.g., adaptation paradigms and motor sequence learning tasks). Prominent
theories in this domain often draw on principles from engineering and
machine learning – including Bayesian statistics, reinforcement learning,
and system identiﬁcation. Meanwhile, applied research, primarily driven
by sports science, focuses on enhancing skill acquisition in real-world
settings, for example, by manipulating augmented feedback, optimizing
attentional focus, or reﬁning training schedules. To bridge this gap and
promote a more integrated approach, we propose four complementary
strategies. First, many topics—such as explicit versus implicit learning—
are investigated in both domains, yet often in isolation. Synthesizing
ﬁndings on these shared topics could yield a more complete picture.
Second, effective ecological interventions may be analyzed through the
lens of basic learning mechanisms, revealing conditions or strategies that
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S33
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS

can further accelerate learning. Third, the basic approach often relies on
simpliﬁed tasks that do not fully capture the complexity of real-world
motor learning. Incorporating insights from ecological contexts can help
extend these tasks and identify gaps in current theories. Finally, testing
learning mechanisms and practical interventions across a range of settings
—from controlled laboratories to real-life environments—is essential for
their validation. Collaboration between basic and applied laboratories can
facilitate this process, ultimately beneﬁting both approaches. We propose
that a more uniﬁed perspective can yield more robust theories and more
effective interventions, advancing both scientiﬁc understanding and practical applications in motor learning. Funding source: Meta, Champalimaud
Foundation.
Beyond technique: Applying an ecological dynamics framework to
Brazilian Jiu-Jitsu instruction
Bren I. Veziroglu, San Jose State University; Emily H. Wughalter, San Jose
State University
Advances in motor learning challenge traditional pedagogical approaches.
While martial arts instruction has historically relied on prescriptive
instruction and repetitive practice, evidence from ecological dynamics
suggests this practice may not be optimal for skill development. This
project focused on ecological systems as they apply to combat sports, with
a particular focus on No-Gi Brazilian Jiu-Jitsu (BJJ). A comprehensive
search of EBSCO and Google Scholar databases was conducted using the
keywords “constraints-led approach, differential learning, prescriptive
instruction, and explicit instruction” for experimental studies comparing
ecological dynamics-based approaches with traditional information processing approaches. Foundational texts in ecological dynamics, ecological
psychology, non-linear pedagogy, and the constraints- led approach were
also identiﬁed manually. Evidence from recent studies across multiple
sports suggests superior skill acquisition, retention, and transfer when
using ecological approaches compared to traditional pedagogies. An
ecological dynamics framework in BJJ pedagogy may enhance motor
skill learning by developing more robust and adaptable movement strategies that are better suited to the emergent dynamics of live combat
situations. In addition, speciﬁc recommendations are made for how
coaches and practitioners can use an ecological dynamics framework to
design practices. Speciﬁc ecologically-based task constraints and learning
activities are outlined which allow for exploration of functional movement
solutions based on interviews with expert coaches and real world implementation. This work provides a foundation for practitioners looking to
implement contemporary motor learning principles while maintaining the
technical depth and tactical complexity of BJJ.
Learning by decomposition in a two-dimensional control task
Eric Griessbach, Champalimaud Centre for the Unknown; Adrian Haith,
Johns Hopkins University
Humans demonstrate remarkable efﬁciency in learning high-dimensional
motor tasks, yet the mechanisms underlying this rapid acquisition remain
poorly understood. Preliminary data from our lab suggest that participants
spontaneously decompose bimanual movements when given a free choice,
hinting that such a strategy might underlie their generalization abilities. It
has been proposed that learning through decomposition facilitates skill
development by breaking high-dimensional movements into lower-dimensional components that can be mastered independently before being
recombined. To test this hypothesis, we examined decomposition in motor
skill learning using a novel cursor control task. Participants controlled a
cursor’s 2D velocity using ﬁnger forces through a non-intuitive mapping.
We compared a decomposition group trained only on single-dimension
movements (cardinal directions: 0°, 90°, 180°, 270°), with a control group
that did not receive training on single-dimension movements. If humans
can effectively learn through dimensional decomposition, we predicted the
decomposition group would show superior performance in a transfer test
with diagonal movements requiring coordination of both dimensions.
While the decomposition group moved faster to diagonal targets after
learning, a more detailed analysis revealed that this improvement primarily
stemmed from enhanced control of cardinal movements and reduced idle
time rather than improved bimanual coordination (measured by diagonal
velocity and proportion of diagonal versus cardinal movement patterns).
These ﬁndings suggest that humans’ remarkable motor learning efﬁciency
does not rely on dimensional decomposition as is commonly believed.
Notably, preliminary data from our lab show that participants tend to
decompose bimanual movements spontaneously when given a free choice
—an approach that, in light of this result, may hinder the development of
the performance in control tasks. Future research could investigate whether
and under what conditions such spontaneous decomposition impedes or
facilitates motor learning. Funding source: Meta.
Differences in kinematic sequences and coordination between different levels of baseball players
Min-Jae Ju, Yonsei University; Yong-Jin Yoon, Yonsei University; SeonYoung Ahn, Yonsei University; Soo-Jung Yang, Yonsei University; ChiHoon Song, Yonsei University; Seong-Kwan Cho, Texas A&M International University; Sibak Sung, Florida State University
A successful baseball swing involves a series of proximal to distal
rotations, starting with the pelvis, followed by the shoulders, then the
upper arm, then the lower arm, and ﬁnally the wrist, which transfers the
force to the bat for a powerful swing. From a hitter’s perspective, if the
swing time can be shortened through training, the hitter can search for the
pitch thrown by the pitcher for a longer time and can respond more quickly
to the pitch of the ball, thereby improving the ability of temporal timing.
Therefore, this study aims to analyze the kinematic sequences and
coordination of skilled and unskilled baseball players. To accomplish the
purpose of the study, the subjects selected 10 KBO professional baseball
players as the skilled group, and 10 amateur baseball players as the
unskilled group. In this study, IMU inertial sensors were attached to the
pelvis, thoracic spine, upper arm, forearm, and baseball bat, and their
movements were measured at 500 Hz. In addition, data on batted balls
(speed, angle, and distance) were measured using Hittrax. All study
subjects hit the ball 25 times randomly ﬂying at 11m, 55 miles, and
0.44 seconds in the same laboratory. The data was analyzed using a t-test.
The results of the study are as follows: ﬁrst, the skilled group demonstrated
rapid acceleration across various courses, whereas the unskilled group
failed to adapt to the diverse courses, resulting in an inability to achieve
acceleration. Second, when evaluating the ability to strike balls arriving at
different courses and timings, the skilled group exhibited not only high
accuracy but also powerful hits. In contrast, the unskilled group showed a
signiﬁcantly higher rate of errors. These results suggest that the skilled
group can generate acceleration due to a shorter swing time and faster chain
rotation than the unskilled group. In addition, it also suggests that the
skilled group is able to hit the ball more accurately and use force more
efﬁciently when hitting different courses and at different times.
Comparing cognitive-motor processes during physical versus virtual
execution of a simple sequential task
Alexandra Shaver, University of Maryland- College Park; Hunter Frisk,
University of Maryland- College Park; Jayesh Jayashankar, University of
Maryland- College Park; Emily Liu, University of Maryland- College
Park; Kyle Pietro, University of Maryland- College Park; James Purtilo,
University of Maryland- College Park; James Reggia, University of
Maryland- College Park; Rodolphe Gentili, University of MarylandCollege Park
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

--- [Página 38] ---
Although individuals perform virtual tasks in various contexts, the neurocognitive processes that support performance in such simulated environments are still relatively unknown. In particular, it remains unclear to
what extent the engagement of cognitive-motor processes differs when
executing physical versus virtual tasks, given the possible differences in
sensorimotor mapping. This study examined how cortical dynamics
underlying performance and mental workload are affected when executing
a simple sequential task under normal (physical environment) and altered
(virtual environment) sensorimotor mapping conditions. Twenty-two participants performed a simple motor action sequence involving moving four
disks with different diameters between three evenly-spaced pegs in an
obvious order. This simple sequence was executed under normal and
altered sensorimotor conditions where the disks were manipulated by hand
and using simple point-and-click computer-mouse controls, respectively.
Video and electroencephalography (EEG) were recorded to assess performance and cortical dynamics while perceived mental (MD) and physical
(PD) demand were reported using the NASA-TLX survey. Performance
was assessed using two aspects of sequence completion time, motor
(SCTm; i.e., time spent moving) and cognitive (SCTc; i.e., pauses), as
well as Levenshtein distance (LD; lower values represent enhanced
performance). The results revealed that compared to the altered conditions,
the use of the normal sensorimotor map resulted in i) an increase of SCTm
and PD whereas LD, SCTc and MD remained unchanged and ii) enhanced
theta over the anterior-frontal region and attenuated mu power over the
central region along with greater frontal theta/parietal alpha and frontal
theta/frontal alpha ratios. These ﬁndings suggest that the ﬁne motor
manipulation of the physical disks resulted in a greater recruitment of
the motor regions relative to the altered condition for which individuals
moved the disks using simple point-and-click coordination generating
smaller motor time and perceived physical demand. The changes in
anterior-frontal theta power suggest that relative to the altered condition,
the normal condition further engaged working memory and action/error
monitoring processing resources. This work provides insight into neurocognitive processes present when operating in physical and virtual environments and the associated sensorimotor mappings.
Assessing neuromotor and postural control after repetitive subconcussive soccer headers: A narrative review
Joshua Lawton, Old Dominion University; Christopher Rhea, Old Dominion University; Eric Schussler, Old Dominion University
Head impacts in sports have received considerable attention in the research
and clinical communities over the past two decades due to the known shortand long-term effects on brain and behavioral health. However, most of
this research has focused on concussive head impacts despite the observation that the vast majority of head impacts in sports are subconcussive in
nature. To address this in a controlled laboratory setting, a soccer heading
task has been used to induce subconcussive head impacts in order to
evaluate their potential connection to short-term changes in neuromotor
and postural control. However, what remains unknown are characteristics
of the heading task (e.g., number of headers, magnitude of the head
impacts) that lead to short-term changes in behavior, which is likely due to
diversity in heading task protocols and assessments. The purpose of this
narrative review was to examine past studies that tested the neuromotor
and/or postural control effects of soccer heading. Three online databases
were searched: Google Scholar, PubMed, CINAHL Complete, using the
key words “soccer heading”, “neuromotor control”, and “postural control”.
Fifteen studies assessed neuromotor and/or postural control before and
after a bout of soccer heading. Four of these studies used King-Devick tests
to assess neuromotor control, while four separate studies used the Balance
Error Scoring System (BESS) test to assess postural control. Eleven of the
studies reported a decline in neuromotor and/or postural control following
repeated soccer headers. One study compared collegiate soccer and nonsoccer athletes, showing soccer athletes had less postural control decline
following the impacts, while another study evaluated the effectiveness of a
soccer heading intervention, which did not show a signiﬁcant difference.
The variation found in the protocols in this line of research are the
inconsistency of number of headers, velocity of the ball (which related
to magnitude of the head impacts), and the assessments used to examine
neuromotor and/or postural control. Future research should focus on
systematically altering characteristics of the heading task to better understand if there is a dose response with respect to neuromotor and/or postural
control changes after repetitive subconcussive soccer headers.
Study the relationship of posture stability scale score recognized by
Artiﬁcial Intelligence deep learning and clinical evaluation tools
YING PO HSU, Taoyuan General Hospital, Ministry of Health and
Welfare, Taiwan/National Taiwan University; YUEH HSIA CHEN, National Taiwan University; YI HUI LU, Taipei Veterans General Hospital,
Taipei, Taiwan
Falls represent a critical health concern among the elderly population.
Healthcare providers employ various clinical balance assessment methods
and tools to identify individuals at high risk of falling. However, conventional clinical assessment tools exhibit ceiling effects, limiting their
capacity to detect subtle changes in balance ability during early stages
of decline. This study aims to address this limitation by utilizing Artiﬁcial
Intelligence-based image recognition, speciﬁcally implementing the Keypoint RCNN deep learning neural network provided by Torchvision, to
identify human joint positions and investigate correlations between image
data analysis results and conventional clinical postural stability scale
scores. The study enrolled 60 community-dwelling elderly participants
aged 65 years and above. Participants underwent a comprehensive assessment battery including the Short Form Berg Balance Scale (SF-BBS), 30second sit-to-stand test, and Romberg test, with all assessments recorded
via smartphone video. Participants who achieved a score of four points on
the SF-BBS were further stratiﬁed into normal and frail groups based on
their performance in the 30-second sit-to-stand test. Preliminary ﬁndings
revealed that the frail group exhibited signiﬁcantly reduced displacement
distances and decreased acceleration of body center of mass and associated
sway patterns compared to the normal group. These results suggest that
elderly individuals demonstrating lower performance in the 30-second sitto-stand test may adopt a more rigid postural control strategy during singleleg standing assessment. This compensatory balance strategy potentially
serves as an early indicator of balance function decline in the elderly
population. The implementation of deep learning neural networks for
detecting changes in anatomical keypoints from video imagery demonstrates promise in overcoming the ceiling effects inherent in traditional
physical balance assessments. These ﬁndings warrant further development
and clinical application, potentially revolutionizing early detection of
balance impairments in the elderly population. Funding source: Taoyuan
General Hospital, Ministry of Health and Welfare, Taiwan.
Video-based penalty kick direction predictions in adolescent soccer
players: Asymmetric cues at ball contact?
Fiona Magill, University of Toronto; Rebecca Hirst, Trinity College
Dublin; David McGovern, Dublin City University; Maxime Trempe,
Bishop’s University; Luc Tremblay, University of Toronto
Soccer requires that players rely on a combination of prior experience and
ongoing afferent information. It has been shown that expert soccer players
can predict others’ actions, especially utilizing hip and waist kinematics
during the penalty kick approach and at ball contact (Makris & Urgesi,
2015; Tomeo et al., 2013). In this exploratory study, we attempted to

replicate a video-based soccer penalty kick direction prediction task with
adolescent soccer players, who had at least 200 hours of soccer experience (approximately two years). Fifteen adolescent soccer players (Mage =
13.6 ± 1.18) completed the ball-path prediction task, using a scale of 1-8
(1 being leftmost, 8 being rightmost) to judge the direction of the ball after
a congruent or incongruent penalty kick video sequence, with the last
frame being the ball contact point. Speciﬁcally, in the incongruent
condition, the last frame of the video was replaced with a frame of a
kick in the opposite direction to the initial sequence (e.g., initial approach
of a rightward kick and last ball contact frame of a leftward kick). Such a
method allows one to determine if information from hip and waist during
the approach is overturned by the critical information at ball contact. In
the overall binary direction judgement (i.e., left/right), participants’
accuracy at predicting the ﬁnal ball location was above 95% in the
congruent condition and appeared near chance levels (i.e., ∼50%) in
incongruent conditions. However, given the signiﬁcant interaction between congruency type and kick direction (F(1,14) = 194.04, p < .001,
ηp
2= .93), the effect of incongruency at ball contact yielded a larger
change in ball path direction prediction with the left approach/left kick to
left approach/right kick signiﬁcantly impairing prediction compared to
the reverse condition (p = .009). Thus, these adolescent soccer players
were less affected by deceptive actions in the degree of direction
prediction when the ball was kicked towards their right side. Overall,
we were able to replicate previous ﬁndings with adolescents including
lateral asymmetries of ball contact information for penalty kick ball path
trajectory prediction.

Effects of post-activation performance enhancement on countermovement jump and forward lunge
Kristoph Lopata, Wayne State University; Mohammed Bila, Wayne State
University; Qin Lai, Wayne State University
Post-activation performance enhancement (PAPE) is an intense but nonfatiguing voluntary exercise attempting to enhance ensuing ballistic power
movements (Seitz & Haff, 2016). Previous literature demonstrated that
low-volume drop-jump PAPE protocols improved subsequent countermovement jumps (CMJ) (e.g., Chen et al., 2013). However, uncertainty
exists whether PAPE provides beneﬁts beyond a warm-up effect (Rappelt
et al., 2024). Here, we compared three protocols: a seated control (CON), a
warm-up consisting of 5 minutes of cycling (WU), and 5 minutes of
cycling with a low-volume (5x) drop-jump PAPE protocol (PAPE) on
CMJ
and
forward
lunge
performance.
Participants
(N = 48;
21.48 ± 2.82 years) signed an informed consent before they completed
a pre-test at Session 1. Session 2 was administered 24 hours after the pretest. During Session 2, participants were randomly assigned to one of three
groups (CON, WU, or PAPE). The post-test was conducted ﬁve minutes
after each exercise protocol. During both sessions, participants performed
three maximal CMJs on a force plate (Bertec FP5060) and three forward
lunge trials per leg on a long force platform (NeuroCom). We assessed
jump height for CMJ and the distance (% height) and contact time (s) for
the forward lunge. A 3 (Group) x 2 (Test) mixed ANOVA with repeated
measures on Test detected a main effect for Test, F(1, 45) = 17.38,
p < .001. Duncan’s MRT further revealed that the PAPE and WU groups
displayed signiﬁcant pre-to-posttest improvement (p < .05). However,
there was no difference between the tests for the control. For the forward
lunge, no differences in the lunge distance or contact time were observed
between groups or tests, p > .05. These results indicate that exercise
protocols facilitate countermovement jump performance; however, a
PAPE protocol consisting of a single bout of ﬁve drop-jumps does not
provide beneﬁt beyond a standardized cycling warm-up.
Do cues that promote an internal focus of attention lead to increased
controlled processing during self-talk implementation? Evidence from
an EEG study
Reese A. L. Tozier, San Francisco State University; Katelin Crutcher,
Boise State University; Jet Taylor, Boise State University; Mariane F. B.
Bacelar, Boise State University; Juliana O. Parma, San Francisco State
University
Instructional self-talk is a psychological strategy often used to enhance
performance, as it probes the performer to attend to crucial task aspects.
Notably, the content of the self-talk cues adopted might direct one’s
focus of attention externally or internally. According to the constrainedaction hypothesis, adopting an internal focus of attention might promote
controlled processing, which can be detrimental to motor performance,
especially for performers with some level of automaticity. To investigate whether self-talk cues that promote internal or external focus of
attention lead to different levels of engagement in controlled processing,
we analyzed a dataset consisting of 10 participants’ brain activity and
extracted the frontocentral electroencephalographic (EEG) measure
theta power (4 - 7Hz). This EEG measure can be used as a proxy
measure of controlled processing, as it is positively correlated with the
use of working memory and action monitoring during a task. EEG data
were collected during a within-subject self-talk study in which participants performed a golf putting task using self-talk with internal
(e.g., “arms-through”) and external (e.g., “putter-through”) focus of
attention cues. After a period of performance stabilization and self-talk
implementation training, each participant performed two blocks of 20
trials of each condition in an interleaved order (e.g., external-internalexternal-internal) that was counterbalanced across participants. Mean
theta power was extracted three seconds prior and one second after
movement initiation for a total of four epochs. Descriptive data analysis
showed that participants had higher theta power during movement
preparation under the internal-focus condition compared to the external-focus condition and that this tendency reversed during movement
execution (last epoch). However, results of a mixed-effects regression
model showed no signiﬁcant differences between conditions (p = .973)
or an interaction with epoch (p = .103). Overall, our ﬁndings do not
support the hypothesis that an internal focus of attention leads to more
controlled processing than an external focus one. It is possible, however, that self-talk cues that promote internal focus shift the timing of
increased working-memory usage and action monitoring from movement execution to preparation as compared to those promoting an
external focus, an effect that might be worth investigating in a larger
sample.
Age-related differences in neural coupling and interference during
bimanual force control
Brock Balthazor, Texas A&M University; Yiyu Wang, Princeton University; Osmar Neto, California State University San Marcos; Madison
Weinrich, Texas A&M University; Jackson Wagner, Texas A&M University; Deanna Kennedy, Texas A&M University
Older adults may experience greater difﬁculty inhibiting or compensating
for neural crosstalk to homologous muscles in the contralateral limb during
bimanual coordination tasks compared to young adults. This study investigated age-related differences in interference between forces generated by
one limb and those produced by the contralateral limb during a bimanual
force production task. Eighteen older adults (Mage = 68.81 years) and 18
young adults (Mage = 21.43 years) performed a task requiring dynamic
force production with one index ﬁnger (dominant or non-dominant) while
maintaining a constant isometric force with the contralateral ﬁnger. Each
trial lasted 65 seconds, with visual feedback for the constant force removed
after 15 seconds and restored at 55 seconds. Muscle activity from the right

--- [Página 40] ---
and left ﬁrst dorsal interosseous (FDI) muscles was recorded. Measures of
mean force and root mean square error (RMSE) were used to assess
accuracy and stability for each limb independently. Time series crosscorrelations between limb forces were analyzed to quantify interference,
and wavelet-based EMG-EMG coherence analysis was conducted to
examine neural coupling mechanisms. Older adults exhibited greater mean
force and RMSE compared to younger adults. Positive time series crosscorrelations were observed in both groups when visual feedback was
removed from the dominant limb, indicating synchronized force variations
between limbs. However, when feedback was removed for the nondominant limb, younger adults exhibited reduced interference in the
contralateral limb, a pattern not observed in older adults. Additionally,
age-related differences in EMG-EMG coherence were found in the alpha
(5–13 Hz) and beta (13–30 Hz) frequency bands, suggesting distinct
cortical and subcortical contributions to bimanual force control. These
ﬁndings highlight the neural mechanisms underlying age-associated decrements in bimanual coordination and provide insights into the complex
interplay between cortical and subcortical systems in regulating motor
control across the lifespan.
The effect of attentional focus on conscious motor control in a
competitive state anxiety context
Young-Joon Kim, University of Tennessee; Jared Porter, University of
Tennessee
Previous research on attentional focus in anxiety-inducing situations has
primarily focused on performance outcomes while giving limited attention to its direct impact on conscious motor control. Despite evidence
suggesting that performance differences linked to attentional focus arise
from variations in conscious control, this relationship remains insufﬁciently explored. Therefore, this study aimed to investigate how attentional focus inﬂuences conscious motor control under competitive state
anxiety. A total of 48 novice performers participated in a golf putting task
and were randomly assigned to one of four conditions: external, holistic,
internal focus, or control. To assess conscious motor control, discrepancies in putting distance and angle between successive trials (e.g., putt-toputt adjustments) were recorded. The putting distance was measured
using a laser distance measurer to determine the distance between the
starting point and the ball’s resting position. The putting angle was
calculated by separately measuring the distances along the target line and
the ball’s actual trajectory, followed by the application of trigonometric
functions. We hypothesized that using an external or holistic attentional
focus would result in smaller putt-to-putt adjustments compared to
participants in the internal focus or control conditions in non-competitive
conditions and that these effects would persist under competition. Participants completed nine pre-test trials followed by 44 practice trials. After
24 hours, they performed nine post-test trials in both non-competitive and
competitive contexts, with competitive state anxiety assessed using the
CSAI-2. Prior to the putting tasks in the competitive condition, participants were informed about the competition and provided details, including the prize, through written and verbal instructions. As expected, results
showed that all groups experienced signiﬁcantly higher levels of competitive state anxiety in the competitive condition compared to the noncompetitive condition. Although no signiﬁcant group differences were
observed in putt-to-putt adjustments overall, participants in the external
focus condition exhibited greater distance and angle adjustments under
competitive pressure compared to the non-competitive condition. These
ﬁndings suggest that adopting an external focus of attention during
acquisition results in greater movement variability during competitive
post-test conditions. This highlights the need to carefully consider
attentional focus strategies when training novice performers to perform
in pressure-ﬁlled contexts.
Performance effects of virtual reality practice in immersive and nonimmersive environments
Joei Velten, University of Tennessee; Jared Porter, University of
Tennessee
While previous studies have demonstrated the positive effects of nonimmersive virtual reality (NIVR) and immersive virtual reality (IVR)
practice independently, few studies have directly compared the two in a
skill performance context. Cost and accessibility can differ greatly between
IVR and NIVR systems, making it critically important for both practitioners and researchers to understand the most effective use of these
technologies for achieving speciﬁc outcome goals. Therefore, the purpose
of this study was to investigate the effects of virtual reality immersion on
behavioral, physiological, and psychological factors using a within-subjects design and a stationary cycling task. It was hypothesized that there
would be no signiﬁcant differences in behavioral measures, but that there
would be signiﬁcant differences in physiological and psychological measures. Participants (N = 29) completed two 8000-meter stationary bike
rides in both a NIVR and IVR environment separated by at least 72 hours.
Each day, participants were ﬁtted with a heart rate monitor and told that
completion time and number of tokens collected would be recorded but
were blind to all other measures. Collecting tokens during the cycling task
served as a behavioral assessment, while RPE served as the psychological
measure. The order in which participants completed each condition was
counterbalanced to minimize order effects. Eleven separate paired-samples
t-tests were conducted and revealed a signiﬁcant main effect of condition
for success rate (i.e., ratio of tokens collected; p = .003) and RPE
(p = .027). However, there were no signiﬁcant differences between conditions for measures of completion time or average and maximum heart
rate, speed, cadence, and power output. Although these results only
partially supported the initial hypotheses, they provide meaningful preliminary data for drawing comparisons between NIVR and IVR training
environments. While VR immersion did not impact motor behavioral or
physiological outcomes as hypothesized, its effect on psychological factors
is critically important for understanding the performer’s experience.
Ultimately, these ﬁndings advance our current understanding of the more
nuanced beneﬁts of IVR, providing valuable insights to help researchers
and practitioners better optimize VR training protocols.
Analysis of triaxial forces in the freestyle swimming pull as predictors
of lap time
Jonny Simoes, Oakland University; Joshua Haworth, Oakland University
Champions in competitive swimming are often determined by hundredths
of a second. The slightest changes in technique can have signiﬁcant
impacts on a race. Previous studies have addressed this by using sensors
on swimmers’ palms to measure force exerted on the water. However, prior
research has been limited to measuring the resultant force rather than the
total effective propulsive force. Recent advancements in swimming sensor
technology now enables the measurement of individual force vectors. With
this technology, swimmers and coaches can better understand how to
minimize swim time by reﬁning stroke patterns. The present study examined the effects of propulsive, lateral, and vertical forces on total swim
time. It was hypothesized that increased propulsive force would result in
reduced swim time. Collegiate swimmers (N = 25, 13 female, 12 male,
Mage = 20.5 ± 1.6 years) completed three maximal effort 50-meter freestyle
trials. Participants were ﬁtted with an omni-directional pressure sensor on
each palm. Swim time was measured from foot push off to hand ﬁnish.
Pearson tests revealed no correlation between hands; therefore, separate
models were created for each hand. Multiple linear regression was used to
evaluate the relationship between swim time and triaxial forces for each
hand. This resulted in a signiﬁcant model for the left hand, F(3, 21) = 5.93,
p < .01, R2 = .38. However, the model for the right hand was not

External focus of attention may not be as beneﬁcial as its reputation on
aesthetic skills
Sophia Winchell, Sonoma State University; Kendall Hernandez, San Jose
State University; Youngmin Chun, Sonoma State University; Yonjoong
Ryuh, Sonoma State University; Kevin Becker, University of Tennessee
Knoxville
The beneﬁts of an external focus of attention over an internal focus are
well-documented (Chua et al., 2021). However, identifying effective
external focus cues for aesthetic skills, such as gymnastics and dance
remains challenging. Recently, a holistic focus (i.e., general feeling of
movement) has been suggested as an alternative, offering similar performance beneﬁts while reducing conscious control of movement (Becker
et al., 2019). Notably, holistic cues should be easier to identify for aesthetic
skills without implements or targets. Thus, this study compared the effects
of different focus cues on pirouette performance in novice and experienced
dancers. With 38 healthy young adults (23 novices, 15 experienced),
pirouette performance was evaluated. A force platform measured the center
of pressure trajectory (predicted ellipse area, PEA), and vertical ground
reaction force (GRF) during pirouette movements. Participants performed
three pirouettes under ﬁve focus conditions: internal (straightening knees
and neck), proximal-external (pushing the ﬂoor), distal-external (focusing
on a dot on the wall), holistic (feeling of power and grace), and control.
Results showed experienced performers had smaller PEA than novices,
F(1, 36) = 4.04, p = .05, ηp
2 = .10. However, no signiﬁcant effects were
found across the ﬁve focus conditions for either the within-subject effect,
F(1.099, 39.566) = 1.13, p = .30, ηp
2 = .03, or the skill x focus interaction,
F(1.099, 39.566) = .93, p = .35, ηp
2 = .03. Vertical ground reaction force
had a signiﬁcant effect across conditions, F(4, 144) = 280.64, p < .001,
ηp
2 = .89, but no signiﬁcance on either the skill x focus interaction, F(4,
144) = 1.75, p = .42, ηp
2 = .05, or skill level main effect, F(1, 36) = 1.13,
p = .30, ηp
2 = .03. Post hoc testing indicated that the vertical GRF in the
proximal-external focus condition was lower than all other cues (p < .001).
Inconsistent with our hypothesis, the PEA ﬁndings suggest that neither
external nor holistic focus cues enhanced performance of the pirouette
skill. A proximal-external focus reduced vertical GRF, but this did not
result in tighter COP trajectories. Further research is necessary to identify
effective focus strategies for aesthetic skills such as a pirouette.
The inﬂuence of brief motivational climate intervention on gaze
control within ROTC shooting training
Taylor Kinney, East Carolina University; Christine M. Habeeb, East
Carolina University; Nicholas Murray, East Carolina University; E.
Whitney G. Moore, East Carolina University
Researchers have consistently demonstrated that visuomotor strategies
(e.g., longer ﬁxation durations) utilized by experts in self-paced tasks, such
as target shooting lead to improved performance (Janelle et al., 2000;
Vickers & Lewinski, 2012). However, it is less known how factors related
to training, like motivational climate (ego-involving or task-involving),
inﬂuence visual motor control. From achievement goal theory, a taskinvolving climate (e.g., feedback emphases self-referenced improvement
and learning) has been associated with more enjoyment, effort, and less
stress
in
sport
contexts
compared
to
an
ego-involving
climate
(e.g., feedback emphases outperforming others and punishing mistakes)
(Nicholls, 1989). The purpose of the current study was to investigate how
motivational climate inﬂuenced visual attention of Reserve Ofﬁcers’
Training Corps cadets (ROTC; N = 64) during a self-paced shooting task.
ROTC cadets completed a shooting task in three positions: prone (lying
down), kneeling, and standing, and at two timepoints (Baseline and
intervention). Dependent measures included ﬁxation duration and search
rate. During the intervention, cadets were randomly assigned to one of two
groups in which they received task-involving (n = 32) or ego-involving
climate (n = 32) instructions from a senior ROTC cadet. The analysis
included three (one for each position) 2-way ANOVA (time x leadership
condition). Results demonstrated a signiﬁcant (p < .05) interaction (time x
condition) for ﬁxation duration in the prone and kneeling positions, but not
in the standing position or search rate in any position. Overall, mean
ﬁxation duration increased for cadets with leaders using task-involving,
climate promoting feedback from baseline to intervention at each position.
Our results suggest that task-involving feedback may have helped ROTC
cadets appropriately allocate their visual attention toward the task at hand,
rather than the performance of others or fear of punishment. The results
will be further discussed in light of how motivation climate inﬂuences
performance-related factors, including visual motor control. Funding
source: AASP Research Grant.
The impact of acute exercise on procedural memory: What we know
and what we don’t know
Jing Chen, Texas A&M University-Texarkana; David Wright, Texas A&M
University
Both chronic and acute exercise have been shown to signiﬁcantly enhance
cognitive function, particularly memory, including declarative and procedural memory. Recent studies have increasingly demonstrated that a
single session of acute exercise can enhance memory performance, with
notable effects on procedural memory—a type of implicit memory
essential for learning motor skills and tasks. Researchers in neuroscience
have begun to unravel the neurophysiological mechanisms underlying the
inﬂuence of acute exercise on procedural memory. Key factors include
the timing of exercise relative to the learning task, exercise intensity,
individual genetic predispositions to exercise response, and neurobiological pathways such as the role of brain-derived neurotrophic factor
(BDNF) in the procedural memory learning process. Despite these
advancements, many aspects of this relationship remain unexplored,
leaving critical questions for future investigation. This narrative review
examines the evidence that acute exercise positively inﬂuences procedural memory through an interplay of optimal timing, intensity, neurobiological mechanisms, and the distinct stages of procedural memory
formation. The ﬁndings suggest that carefully tailored acute exercise
regimens can effectively enhance motor skill acquisition and retention,
making acute exercise a promising intervention for improving procedural
memory across diverse populations. This review lays the groundwork for
future studies and practical implementations in areas like education,
rehabilitation, and sports training.
Evaluation of cognitive workload and motor performance in military
pilots
Jared Porter, University of Tennessee
Military combat helicopter pilots are highly skilled aviators which are
trained to perform and support complex missions in the United States
Military. They are expected to perform at optimized levels in high pressure
situations and to complete difﬁcult areal maneuvers often times in

diminished visual ﬁelds. These external constraints produce great strain on
the cognitive and motor systems by requiring the pilot to coordinate the
movement of all four limbs while tracking multiple visual targets and
follow many lines of communication. The present study investigated
cognitive workload and performance via EEG, reaction time was measured
during a dual task procedure, and Bedford workload while military
helicopter pilots completed many complex maneuvers in a ﬂight simulator
over multiple days of testing. Each participant completed one ﬂight
sequence while wearing a headmounted display (HMD), and one sequence
without the HMD. This allowed for comparison of data to better understand how brain activity and motor behavior changed in response to using
the HMD. Using a dual task procedure, participants were also asked to pull
the communications trigger when they observed the letter “A” in their ﬁeld
of view. Participants were asked to perform several orientation maneuvers
around multistory buildings. Each ﬂight sequence concluded with the
execution of a pinnacle landing atop a multistory building. Following the
completion of each ﬂying sequence, participants completed the Bedford
Workload Rating Scale. The results of the analysis demonstrated several
changes in EEG activity across pilots and across ﬂight sequences. Speciﬁcally, there were clear time points when brain activity was signiﬁcantly
elevated in the frontal and occipital regions. Additionally, there were
elevations in alpha and theta brain wave activation patterns, particularly
while wearing the HMD. The present analysis suggest the cognitive system
of the pilots was working at an elevated level when executing a vertical
maneuver and a pinnacle landing. Moreover, changes in RT revealed pilots
were at or near a point of cognitive saturation when executing a vertical
maneuver and pinnacle landing. Lastly, the ﬁndings generally revealed that
cognitive load was higher while wearing the HMD. Funding source:
United State Department of Defense.
Improving early fall risk detection among persons with type 2 diabetes
Mackenzie Korff, Oakland University; Idalis Anotone, Oakland University; Joshua Haworth, Oakland University
The single-leg stance (SLS) test is widely used as a simple clinical tool to
assess fall risk and identify balance deﬁcits. However, its sensitivity to
detect early and complex balance impairments, particularly in individuals
with Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus (T2D), may be insufﬁcient. This limitation
is signiﬁcant because balance in T2D populations often worsens due to
untreated or undiagnosed complications, including diabetic peripheral
neuropathy, retinopathy, and chronic hyperglycemia, which disrupt proprioception, vestibular function, and vision. A lack of sensitivity in
assessments like SLS could delay interventions and prevention strategies,
leading to more severe and less manageable cases. This study aimed to
evaluate balance performance in individuals with T2D compared to
healthy controls, exploring whether SLS alone adequately captures the
range of balance impairments. Ninety-seven participants were included: 39
with T2D and 60 without T2D. Participants completed timed SLS trials on
each leg, where shorter times indicated poorer balance. A Sway Task (ST)
measured central sway excursion, with higher values indicating worse
balance and a threshold of 0.8 indicating clinical concern. A Falls Risk
(FR) assessment quantiﬁed sway velocity under four conditions: feet
shoulder-width apart with eyes open and closed, and feet together with
eyes open and closed. Independent t-tests were used to analyze SLS and
ST, while a two-way ANOVA assessed FR across groups and stance
conditions (α = 0.05). Results showed that individuals with T2D consistently performed worse across all measures. Notably, SLS alone failed to
capture the full extent of balance impairments, particularly excessive sway
and instability when the base of support was reduced. These ﬁndings
suggest that while SLS provides useful initial insights, it may not be
sensitive enough to identify early or subtle impairments in T2D populations. Incorporating additional assessments like ST and FR could enhance
detection, allowing for earlier interventions to address sway and prevent
falls. This research highlights the need for reﬁned assessment tools tailored
to the unique balance challenges of individuals with T2D.
Exploring the impact of attentional focus and cognitive task engagement on postural control in healthy young adults
Atousa Ebrahimi, University of North Carolina Greensboro; Louisa
Raisbeck, University of North Carolina Greensboro
Postural control is a critical motor skill for maintaining balance during
daily activities, inﬂuenced by attentional focus strategies and cognitive
task engagement. While previous research has demonstrated the individual
effects of external focus and cognitive tasks on postural control, the
combined impact of these factors remains unexplored. This study aimed
to examine how varying levels of cognitive task difﬁculty affect postural
control metrics within two attentional focus strategies: Dual Focus with
Cognitive Task (DFC) and External Focus with Cognitive Task (EFC). A
total of 26 healthy young adults (ages 18–30) were randomly assigned to
either the DFC group (n = 14) or the EFC group (n = 12). Participants
performed Limit of Stability (LOS) tests on the NeuroCom Smart Balance
Master system under two cognitive task conditions: Moderate and Difﬁcult. The DFC group adopted a dual-focus approach, combining internal
focus on ankle movements with external focus on a visual cursor, while the
EFC group utilized exclusively external focus strategies. Primary outcomes included reaction time (RT), movement velocity (MVL), and
directional control (DCL). Paired-samples t-tests revealed no signiﬁcant
differences in RT, MVL, or DCL between the Moderate and Difﬁcult task
conditions within either group (p > 0.05). Speciﬁcally, the DFC group
showed no signiﬁcant variations across cognitive task difﬁculty levels for
any outcome, and the same was observed for the EFC group. These
ﬁndings suggest that the level of cognitive task difﬁculty, as deﬁned in this
study, does not signiﬁcantly affect postural control metrics within either
attentional focus strategy. These results contribute to the growing body of
research on the interplay between attentional focus, cognitive engagement,
and postural stability. They underscore the resilience of postural control
mechanisms under dual-task conditions and provide valuable insights for
designing interventions to enhance balance using attentional focus
strategies.
The effect of attentional focus on rowing performance among experienced individuals
Tatiana Zhuravleva, Southwestern University; Raghav Sharma, Southwestern University; Christopher A Aiken, New Mexico State University
The effects of an internal and external focus on rowing have yielded
ambiguous results in terms of which was more advantageous (Newmann
et al., 2020). Recent research has found a holistic focus to beneﬁt
performance and learning, however, it has not been examined with
continuous tasks such as rowing. The purpose of the present study was
to investigate the effects of various attentional foci, including a holistic
focus on rowing. Sixteen individuals performed three separate rowing
sessions, each lasting six minutes, under an external focus (EF), internal
focus (IF), and holistic focus (HF) in a counterbalanced order, with 24
hours between sessions. The rowing task was performed on a Concept 2
indoor rowing ergometer with LoadSol force insoles in participant’s shoes.
The primary dependent variables collected for analysis were distance
rowed, power, and foot force across three time periods (minutes 1-2, 34, and 5-6). Separate 3 (condition) x 3 (times) repeated measures ANOVA
were used to analyze the data with a Bonferroni post-hoc. A signiﬁcant
main effect of condition was observed for distance (p < .05) and power
(p < .001). The post-hoc revealed that HF rowed signiﬁcantly farther in the
ﬁrst time period than the second and third time period for IF (p’s < .01). HF
also rowed further in the ﬁrst time period than the second or third time
