Neuromotor control differences in the upper extremity between those
with and without rheumatoid arthritis
Yousef Qadumi, East Carolina University; Kaitlyn Wojciechowski, East
Carolina University; Nikole Galman, East Carolina University; Julianna
Ethridge, East Carolina University; Nicholas Murray, East Carolina
University
Rheumatoid arthritis (RA) is an autoimmune disease that causes the
breakdown of the synovial membrane. Individuals with RA often experience joint damage, especially in the hands. Due to decreased hand strength
and dexterity, RA patients typically adopt abnormal movement patterns to
complete activities of daily living. The purpose of this study was to observe
and analyze neuromotor control trends and movement patterns between
those with and without RA. We hypothesize that RA patients will adopt
different movement patterns compared to healthy young adults (H) and
healthy age matched controls (HMA). All participants completed eight
trials manipulating 15 marbles with tweezers. One trial constituted the
manipulation of small marbles (SM), while the other trial utilized large
marbles (LM). Vicon nexus software was used to capture shoulder and
elbow joint displacement using Vicon’s Upper Limb Model. Additionally,
an electroencephalogram (EEG) was used to analyze neuromotor processes and cognitive workload. A series of 3 x 2 (Group x Marble) RMANOVA revealed signiﬁcant main effects of marble size (SM vs. LM) on
shoulder and elbow displacement across both arms in all groups (all
p < .05). Further, the data revealed a signiﬁcant Group x Marble interaction
(F(1, 26) = 3.878, p = .034) for right shoulder displacement (RSD). ANOVA results for Beta activity demonstrated a signiﬁcant main effect for
Region (F(1, 82) = 6.92; p < .01) and Group (F(1, 165) = 9.550; p < .0001).
Likewise, the data revealed a signiﬁcant Group x Region interaction for
Beta activity (F(1, 165) = 10.750; p < .0001). Results indicate SM consistently required greater joint displacement compared to LM. Furthermore,
changes in Beta power indicate higher neural activity in RA patients and
represent increased neural activity during movement initiation which
remains present during movement execution. Future research should focus
on the differences in joint displacement and processing for those with mild
versus severe RA symptoms.
Relationship between Biodex limits of stability and motor control tests
Ben Meyer, Shippensburg University
The purpose of this project was to compare the performance of university
students in limits of stability (LOS) and motor control (MC) tests. Previous
research using the LOS test analyzed results using test completion time as
the variable of interest (Dawson et al., 2018). More recent versions of the
Biodex Balance System utilize the angle of sway as the pertinent measure.
This project aimed to quantify the relationship between LOS and MC tests
using current measures. It was hypothesized that participants’ composite
LOS sway angle values would have a moderate positive relationship with
their composite MC efﬁciency scores. Thirteen males and twelve females
(76 ± 18 kg, 1.71 ± 0.10 m, 21 ± 2 years) performed the LOS and MC
balance tests using a Biodex Balance System SD. Participants stood on
both legs on a static platform in the LOS and MC tests, and they were
required to shift their weight to move a cursor on a screen to all cardinal and
intercardinal directions (presented in a randomized order). The main
difference between the LOS and MC tests is that in the MC test,
participants are required to hold their position at the edge of their base
of support for 0.25 seconds prior to being released back to the center
starting position. For each direction, the maximum angle achieved was
computed in the LOS test, while an efﬁciency score was calculated in the
MC test. A composite value (the average of all tested directions) was
computed that provides an overall indication of performance in both tests.
Results of the Pearson correlation indicated that there is a medium negative
relationship between the LOS and MC composite scores (r = -.361,
p = .076). Mean sway angle for the LOS was 9.7 ± 1.4 degrees. For the
MC test, the composite score of 40 ± 9% was similar to previous results
(Meyer, 2023). The relationship between LOS and MC measures was
negative, which may suggest that participants utilize different strategies for
completing each test. Additional research is required to assess the LOS and
MC tests in clinical and athletic populations.
The inﬂuence of transcranial direct current stimulation (tDCS) on the
primary motor cortex in motor skill learning: A systematic review
Jing Chen, Texas A&M University-Texarkana; Jie Ren, Shanghai University of Sports; David Wright, Texas A&M University
The primary motor cortex (M1) plays a pivotal role in motor skill learning
by supporting the encoding, consolidation, and retention of newly acquired
skills. Transcranial direct current stimulation (tDCS) has gained attention
as a potential approach to improve motor learning by altering cortical
excitability and promoting neuroplasticity. This systematic review evaluates the effects of tDCS on M1 during motor skill learning, synthesizing
ﬁndings to assess its efﬁcacy and underlying mechanisms. The review
examines key factors inﬂuencing tDCS outcomes, including polarity,
intensity, timing of stimulation in relation to practice, the nature of the
motor tasks, and phases of motor skill learning. Additionally, individual
differences, such as genetic factors, age, and ﬁtness levels, are explored as
contributors to variability in tDCS response. Robust evidence demonstrates that Anodal tDCS is consistently associated with improvements in
motor learning, likely due to increased cortical excitability and enhanced
synaptic plasticity. However, Cathodal tDCS shows more variable effects,
often depending on task complexity and individual baseline performance.
Mechanistically, tDCS impacts motor learning through its ability to
enhance neural plasticity, strengthen motor network connectivity, and
inﬂuence neurochemical pathways, such as the regulation of brain-derived
neurotrophic factor (BDNF). These effects may accelerate the acquisition
and retention of motor skills, making tDCS a valuable tool in optimizing
motor learning. Despite its potential, challenges remain, including inconsistencies in stimulation protocols, limited long-term assessments, and
variability in individual responses. This review identiﬁes areas requiring
further investigation and provides guidelines for improving the standardization and application of tDCS in motor skill research. In conclusion, tDCS
represents a promising tool for augmenting motor consolidation through
targeted modulation of the primary motor cortex. Its potential applications
span from improving rehabilitation outcomes in clinical populations to
enhancing performance in athletic and skill-based domains. Further research is essential to establish standardized protocols and maximize its
therapeutic and practical beneﬁts.
Learning a golf chip shot through implicit vs. explicit and error-driven
vs. error-reduced practice
Yonjoong Ryuh, Sonoma State University; Kya Payne, Sonoma State
University; Kevin Cameron, Sonoma State University; Carter Michnevich,
San Diego State University; Dylan Copeland, Sonoma State University
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

Recent research posits that traditional error-driven process is not necessary
to acquire a novel skill; rather, minimizing error with higher expectancy
through errorless learning would promote smoother progression without or
minimal reliance on verbal-analytic knowledge of movement performance
(Maxwell et al., 2001). Errorless or reduced-error practice represents a
speciﬁc technique seeking to minimize errors during practice so learners
develop consistent movement patterns earlier than traditional error-driven
practice (Capio et al., 2017). Previous research by Poolton et al. (2005)
noted that initial exposure to implicit learning with reduced errors in golf
putting minimized hypothesis-testing behaviors during early learning. This
approach decreased reliance on working memory resources and maintained performance under additional cognitive load. To apply these
ﬁndings to a golf chip shot, which is arguably more complex skill, we
divided our 46 health young adult novice golfers (31 females and 15 males)
into four groups: explicit-errorful (EX-EF, n = 12); explicit-errorless (EXER, n = 10); implicit-errorful (IM-EF, n = 11); implicit-errorless (IM-ER,
n = 13). Each participant went through either implicit or explicit acquisition of a golf chip shot skill followed by either errorful (from farthest to
nearest targets) or errorless (from nearest to farthest targets) practice
ranging from 10 to 28 yards. This study assessed the number of declarative
knowledge generation and chip shot error magnitude. Results showed that
explicit acquisition groups generated more declarative knowledge than
implicit groups: F(1, 41) = 352.79, p < .001, ηp
2 = .34. Putting accuracy
indicated a signiﬁcant difference for within-subject effect: F(2.061,
85.560) = 4.25, p = .02, ηp
2 = .09, block x group interaction: F(6.183,
85.560) = 10.295,
p < .001,
ηp
2 = .42,
and
between
groups:
F(3,
42) = 4.49, p < .001, ηp
2 = .79. However, interestingly, post hoc test
showed that, regardless of initial acquisition type (EX vs. IM), errorful
practice elicited lower errors during the whole procedure including practice
and 24-hour retention and transfer phases which contradicts existing
evidence. These ﬁndings suggest that novel learners encountering complex
and difﬁcult skills may still require a traditional error-driven approach.
Future research is necessary to identify appropriate learning strategies for
complex and difﬁcult skills.
Out of sync: Disruptions in neuromuscular synchronization and
bimanual force coordination in Parkinson’s disease
Madison Weinrich, Texas A&M University; Osmar Neto, California State
University; Yiyu Wang, Princeton University; Brock Balthazor, Texas
A&M University; Deanna M. Kennedy, Texas A&M University
Parkinson’s disease (PD) is a progressive neurodegenerative disorder
affecting the dopaminergic cells of the basal ganglia and is marked by
impairments in motor control, including deﬁcits in force modulation, timing,
and coordination. These challenges are particularly evident during bimanual
tasks and contribute to diminished functional independence and ability to
complete activities of daily living. This study explored the role of neural
synchrony in PD-related motor deﬁcits by examining temporal accuracy,
force dynamics, and neuromuscular coordination during a stable synchronous bimanual force task. Thirteen individuals with PD (Mage = 72.5 years; 6
males), 14 healthy older adults (HO; Mage = 72.3 years; 7 males), and 15
healthy young adults (YA; Mage = 20.7 years; 7 males) performed a 1:1 inphase (0°) coordination task. Participants rhythmically generated isometric
forces with both index ﬁngers while muscle activity from the First Dorsal
Interosseus (FDI) was recorded using electromyography (EMG). Each
participant completed 21 trials of 30 seconds each. Temporal accuracy and
stability were assessed using measures of relative phase, while force
dynamics were evaluated through metrics such as force harmonicity,
asymmetry, and peak force. Neural synchronization was analyzed via
coherence measures of force-force and EMG-EMG signals across relevant
frequency bands. Despite comparable temporal accuracy across groups, PD
participants displayed reduced force harmonicity and increased asymmetry,
indicating difﬁculty in producing smooth and balanced bilateral forces.
Coherence analyses revealed signiﬁcantly diminished neural synchronization in the PD group, particularly in the 1–4 Hz and 4–8 Hz bands for force
coordination and in the alpha band for EMG signals. These ﬁndings suggest
that PD-related disruptions in neural synchrony contribute to impaired force
modulation and asymmetry. This study emphasizes that while temporal
coordination remains relatively preserved, PD profoundly affects the neural
mechanisms underlying smooth and coordinated force production. The
observed link between neural synchrony and motor performance highlights
the importance of developing interventions targeting neural coherence to
enhance motor function in individuals with PD. These results provide a
foundation for designing therapies aimed at mitigating motor deﬁcits and
improving quality of life.
Head rotation frequency of patients with a history of concussion is not
predictive of their time since injury
Gustavo Sandri Heidner, Montclair State University; Samantha Gallagher, Montclair State University; Julia Rose Duardo, Montclair State
University; Natalie Mileski, Montclair State University; Samantha Hagenbush, Montclair State University; August Price, Bellapianta Orthopaedics
& Sports Medicine; Robert Horn, Montclair State University; Luis Torres,
Montclair State University
The Vestibular Ocular Motor Screening (VOMS) is a concussion assessment tool typically used in clinics and sports to assess vestibular and ocular
motor function based on the invocation of four symptoms (dizziness,
headache, nausea, and fogginess) immediately after the execution of
different paradigms: baseline, smooth pursuits, horizontal and vertical
saccades, horizontal and vertical vestibulo-ocular reﬂex (VOR), near point
of convergence, and visual motion sensitivity. One of our previous analyses
showed no correlation between time since injury and long-term symptom
reduction. Sample entropy is a variation of approximate entropy used to
investigate the complexity of biological signals. In this study we sought to
investigate if patients completed the horizontal VOR (hVOR) as intended
and if sample entropy (SampEn) applied to accelerometer data of horizontal
head rotation could predict time since injury. Twenty-ﬁve (N = 25, Mdays
since injury = 182.16, SD = 244.29, Range = 27-1152) adult outpatients completed the hVOR using a metronome set at 180 bpm. Data were collected
from accelerometers in the Tobii Pro 3 glasses worn by patients. An OLS
regression revealed that neither SampEn (p = .845, R2 = .002), nor head
rotation frequency (p = .381, R2 = .034) were predictive of Time Since
Injury. The average head rotation frequency was signiﬁcantly lower than
the intended test frequency (z = 1.5 Hz) for each side horizontal rotation,
t(24) = 3.333, p = .002, d = 0.67. Our results suggest that sample entropy and
head rotation frequencies are not predictive of time since injury in patients
that are more than 27 days in the chronic stage of concussion. It also appears
that regardless of the time since injury, there are other factors affecting
patients’ ability to complete the horizontal vestibulo-ocular reﬂex paradigm
at the frequency designed in the VOMS. These results should be interpreted
with caution due to the sample size and wide range of time since injury.
More studies are needed to further resolve these limitations.
Impact of a multi-modal exercise intervention on postural fractal
dynamics in older adults
Scott Ducharme, California State University, Long Beach; Jackie Dawson,
California State University, Long Beach; Ayla Donlin, California State
University, Long Beach; Claudia Huang, California State University,
Long Beach
Various nonlinear analyses are now commonly incorporated into the study
of human posture to assess stability and complexity. One measure, fractal
dynamics, evaluates the statistical relationship between variability
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S41
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS

--- [Página 45] ---
magnitudes across a range of timescales. Fractal dynamics distinguish
young from older adults, as older adults generally display more constrained
behavior. Physical activity may impact an individual’s fractal dynamic
behavior, especially in older adults. Thus, the purpose of this study was to
evaluate changes in fractal dynamics following an 8-week multi-modal
exercise intervention. We hypothesized that participants would display less
constrained fractal dynamics following the intervention. Six older adults
(4W; 70.5 ± 3.4 years) performed three quiet standing postural tasks prior
to and following an exercise intervention, including: eyes open (ES), eyes
closed (EC), and tandem standing (TS), each for 60 seconds. Center-ofpressure data were collected at 1000Hz using a force platform. The data
were then separated into medial-lateral (ML) and anterior-posterior (AP)
directions, low-pass ﬁltered (10Hz), and down sampled to 100Hz. Evenlyspaced detrended ﬂuctuation analysis was used to determine the fractal
scaling exponent ‘alpha’, which is the slope of the linear ﬁt line between
variability magnitude and timescale size in the double logarithmic domain.
An alpha of 1.0 represents 1/f or pink noise and is generally considered
optimal. The exercise intervention entailed two 1-hour sessions/week for 8
weeks, and included strength, cardiovascular, and balance training. Repeated-measures ANOVAs tested for main effects of postural condition
and intervention, and effect size (ES) calculations were used to compare
each condition by task pre vs. post intervention. Overall, alpha values were
above 1.0 and decreased (i.e., improved) following the intervention, albeit
marginally. There was a main effect of condition in the AP direction (F(2,
10) = 37.35, p < .001), but not intervention, and there were no effects for
the ML direction. There was a medium effect of intervention in the AP EO
condition (ES = 0.54), and small effects in the ML EO and TS conditions
(ES = 0.39 and 0.28, respectively). These ﬁndings provide preliminary
support for the efﬁcacy of multi-modal exercise interventions for improving postural fractal dynamics. Subsequent studies should include larger
sample sizes and potentially longer interventions. Funding source: College
of Health and Human Services; Center for Successful Aging, California
State University, Long Beach.
Visual attention and self-efﬁcacy in coed cheerleading
Madison Weeks, East Carolina University; Mikayla Chambers, East
Carolina University; Christine Habeeb, East Carolina University; Nicholas Murray, East Carolina University
The prevalence of collegiate cheerleading has risen in the last few years
despite high injury rates. Most collegiate cheer teams are coed, in which
both male (base) and female (ﬂyer) cheerleaders collaborate to execute
partner stunts. While skills have increased in difﬁculty, little is known
about visual and psychological processes related to safe stunt execution.
The purpose of this study was to understand how self-efﬁcacy relates to
visual ﬁxation duration/frequency in coed stunts, and if gaze behavior
differs between bases and ﬂyers across self-identifying role, skill level, and
roles observed. Hypotheses include: 1) cheerleaders reporting higher selfefﬁcacy will have shorter, more frequent ﬁxations; 2) bases and ﬂyers will
exhibit differences in ﬁxation duration/frequency; 3) as stunt skill level
increases, cheerleaders will have shorter, more frequent ﬁxations; 4)
cheerleaders will tend to ﬁxate on ﬂyer area-of-interest (AOI) longer and
more often than base AOI. Male bases (n = 3) and female ﬂyers (n = 2)
were recruited from a coed cheerleading team to view videos of seven
different skills ranging from basic to elite stunts. Prior to viewing each
skill, participants completed a self-efﬁcacy survey to report self-conﬁdence
in their current ability to execute the skill. Gaze behavior was recorded
with a Gazepoint GP3 eye tracker. Correlations between self-efﬁcacy and
ﬁxation count, and self-efﬁcacy and ﬁxation duration, for bases and ﬂyers
were not signiﬁcant for easy, medium, and hard skills. Flyers ﬁxated in the
ﬂyer AOI (Mfreq = 134.75) less compared to bases (Mfreq = 148.69). No
signiﬁcant differences observed across bases and ﬂyers for ﬁxation count/
duration (t = .34 - 1.92; p = .15 - .75). Signiﬁcant differences observed
across skill level for ﬁxation count (Mfreq = 13.80; p < .001, η2
p = .70) and
duration on the ﬂyer AOI (Mfreq = 19.54; p < .001, η2
p = .77). No signiﬁcant differences observed across base and ﬂyer AOIs except ﬁxation count
during medium skills (t = 2.73, p < .05, d = 1.22). Regardless of base/ﬂyer
role, participants looked at the ﬂyer more often (M = 60.87) than the base
(M = 43.67) during medium skills.
The effects of auditory versus vibrotactile feedback on the control of
goal-directed aiming movements of younger and older adults
Cheryl M Glazebrook, University of Manitoba; Saba Mohammadalinezhad Kolahdouz, University of Manitoba; Quinn Malone, University of
British Columbia: Okanagan; Steven R Passmore, University of Manitoba; Jonathan Marotta, University of Manitoba
Augmented sensory feedback using auditory or vibrotactile stimuli may
offer new possibilities to improve the performance of everyday tasks. The
present study explored the inﬂuence of augmented sensory feedback on
movement performance using the One-Target Advantage (OTA) paradigm
in younger and older adults. The OTA phenomenon occurs when movement time (MT) to reach a single target is faster than when the same
movement is followed by a second target. The OTA is thought to reﬂect
differences in how one and two segment movements are prepared and
executed. Thirty neurotypical, right-handed participants (15 older adults:
66.26 ± 4.02 years; 15 younger adults: 25 ± 5.58 years) performed 20 trials
for one- and two-target movements, each paired with three sensory
feedback conditions: auditory, vibrotactile, or no feedback (for a total of
120 trials). Sensory feedback was provided when participants touched the
ﬁrst target (Target 1) using a 1000 Hz auditory tone (200 ms) or vibrotactile
stimulation (BLDC motor, 200 ms). Movement trajectories were captured
at 300 Hz using an Optotrak 3D Investigator optical motion capture
system. Reaction time (RT), movement time (MT), pause time, constant
error (CE), and variable error (VE), were analyzed using a 2 Group by 2
Target Condition by 3 Sensory Condition mixed ANOVA. Vibrotactile
feedback signiﬁcantly shortened RT and MT compared to both auditory
and no feedback, with the greatest change observed in two-target task. A
signiﬁcant Group by Sensory Condition interaction indicated that vibrotactile feedback led to a larger reduction in MT to Target 1 for the older
adults compared to the younger adults. CE results indicated task and
condition-speciﬁc movement biases: Older adults exhibited overshooting
in one target tasks and undershooting in two-target tasks under no feedback
conditions, while younger adults showed minimal bias across all conditions. Pause time and VE were unaffected by sensory condition or task
type. These ﬁndings underscore the potential for vibrotactile feedback to
enhance motor performance, particularly for older adults, by improving
speed and accuracy in sequential movement tasks. The results provide
valuable insights for designing sensory-based interventions and rehabilitation strategies aimed at improving motor control in aging populations.
Funding source: Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of
Canada.

Tyler Whittier, Montana State University; Brett Fling, Colorado State
University
Skilled motor performance of all types requires an accurate perception of
the involved body parts throughout the performance of each movement.
However, sensory data that informs the central nervous system (CNS) of

model that has been used to explain how the CNS compensates for this
uncertainty is Bayesian inference. While Bayesian inference is wellJSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

established in motor control research, its application across different
populations remains underexplored. The purpose of this study was to
apply the Bayesian inference model of sensorimotor integration to help
elucidate the perceptual and sensorimotor differences that identify high
performers. Such knowledge could be used to identify talent, but also
measure sensorimotor abilities of clinical populations that are otherwise
difﬁcult to measure. In this study, a group of female collegiate athletes
(ATH: N = 23, Mage = 20.7 ± 1.5 years) and a group of healthy age and
gender matched controls (HC: N = 23, Mage = 21.7 ± 1.7 years) performed a
full body visuomotor adaptation task in virtual reality (VR). Participants
attempted to align a pink cursor with various targets, compensating for
cursor shifts under different levels of visual uncertainty. The cursor was
linked to a marker on the participant’s center of mass (CoM), and movements were made via single one-legged steps. We hypothesized that
athletes would align more closely with the Bayesian model, exhibiting
less sensorimotor uncertainty in their CoM position estimates. Our results
showed that the ATH group demonstrated signiﬁcantly less sensorimotor
uncertainty in their CoM positional estimates (p < .05) with a large effect
size (d = -1.1). While metrics of model ﬁt were in the direction of our
hypothesis (ATH > HC), this difference was not signiﬁcant (p = .2). These
ﬁndings suggest elite athletes have an increased ability to compensate for
unavoidable noise in their sensory input and calculate optimal state
estimates. Crucially, this study highlights the value of Bayesian models
in sports and clinical environments by elucidating sensorimotor processes
underlying complex full-body movements, which have previously been
challenging to quantify.
Mastering the stack: How task and ego orientations inﬂuence practice
abandonment
Cabel McCandless, New Mexico State University; Pin-Chen Lin, New
Mexico State University; Kehinde Obidele, New Mexico State University;
Christopher A. Aiken, New Mexico State University; Cheryl Coker, Plymouth State University
Athletes often choose to abort practice trials when performance deviates
from the goal, such as sprinters pulling up on hurdles. Aborting trials
reduces practice volume and decreases the opportunity to mimic competitive conditions (Barris, 2013), however, affording choice in practice has
been shown to facilitate learning (Lewthwaite et al., 2015). Conversely,
completing imperfect trials encourages exploring alternate movement
solutions, enhancing adaptability. One reason a performer may choose
to abort a trial could be their task/ego orientation. Task-oriented individuals are thought to judge success on personal improvement, while egooriented individuals judge success by social comparison (Duda, 1989;
Duda et al., 1995). The present study investigated task and ego orientation
inﬂuence on the choice to abort trials and skill acquisition. Forty-ﬁve adults
aged 18-36 volunteered and were taught a novel 3-6-3 cup stacking task.
Individuals completed the Perception of Success Questionnaire (PQSQ)
prior to participation. Participants were sorted into 2 groups based on
questionnaire responses, High-Task-Low-Ego (HTLE) or Low-TaskHigh-Ego (LTHE). Groups were further separated into a choice or nochoice condition with the choice being to abort one or more trials. The
primary dependent variable was time to complete the up-stack and downstack of the task. A 2 (choice) x 2 (orientation) x 4 (test: pre, post, retention,
transfer) repeated-measures ANOVA was used to analyze performance.
Separate one-way ANOVAs examined the number of total practice trials,
completed trials, aborts, and abort rate in the 20-minute practice phase.
Results indicated that the LTHE had faster retention performance than
HTLE (p < .05). In transfer, the no-choice condition demonstrated better
consistency and faster performance than the aborting choice condition
(p < .05). No other signiﬁcant group/condition differences were observed.
No signiﬁcant interactions between orientation and choice were observed.
While goal orientation did not appear to inﬂuence practice abandonment,
these results suggest a skill acquisition advantage for ego over task oriented
learners. Further, the better performance of the no-choice condition is
contrary to the self-control literature in motor learning. We postulate that
practicing through imperfect trials provided enhanced adaptability which
was necessary in the current task and more meaningful than the notion of
choice.
Physical activity mediates age-related impairment in motor adaptation in adults aged 50-65 years
Victoria Rednoske, University of Texas at Arlington; Shenghai Dai,
Washington State University; Shikha Prashad, University of Texas at
Arlington
Motor adaptation is the process of calibrating our movements in response
to changing stimuli and environments. This process relies on error
feedback to quickly adjust to and learn novel contexts. Older adults may
experience age-related impairments, making it difﬁcult to accurately adjust
and execute movements. Participating in regular physical activity is linked
to many health beneﬁts and elicits positive changes across multiple
cognitive domains. Physical activity may be an inﬂuential mechanism to
preserve motor adaptation in older adults. The purpose of this analysis was
to examine the changes in motor adaptation in young and older adults and
determine whether physical activity has a potential mediating effect on
age-related declines in motor adaptation. We used the Cambridge Centre
for Ageing and Neuroscience (Cam-CAN) dataset to examine the performance of 318 participants (age range: 18-87 years; Mage = 53.9, SD = 18.3;
160 female) on a visuomotor adaptation task. We performed a mediation
analysis to determine if physical activity had a protective effect on motor
adaptation by using participants’ early-exposure, late-exposure, and early
post-exposure trajectory error means and SDs as their motor adaptation
outcomes. The analyses were accomplished using physical activity as a
mediator between age and each outcome. Results from the visuomotor task
suggest that motor adaptation declined with age (p-values < .001). The
mediation analysis revealed that physical activity did not mediate the
relationship between age and motor adaptation in the overall sample (pvalues range: .25-.91). However, when participants were sorted into age
groups (18-35, 35-50, 50-65, and 65+ years), physical activity predicted
motor adaptation in the 50-65-year age group (n = 71; Mage = 58.0, SD =
4.5; 35 female), with physical activity showing small to moderate effect
sizes (standardized path coefﬁcients, i.e., B) and accounting for 19-20% of
variance in early exposure trajectory error mean (B = 0.36, p = .02), early
post-exposure trajectory error mean (B = 0.31, p = .03), and early-postexposure trajectory error SD (B = 0.40, p = .02). These ﬁndings suggest
that engaging in physical activity may be especially important for older
adults to maintain motor adaptation.
Markers of planning in a non-binary grasp predicts choice of binary
end-state comfort strategy
Jesse Hansen, Utah State University; Breanna Studenka, Utah State
University; Brooke VanKampen, Utah State University; Landen Winder,
Utah State University
Motor planning involves the organization of movement sequences to
achieve task goals, requiring integration of motor, cognitive, and sensory
processes. This study investigates whether individuals’ strategy selection
in a binary grasp and place task—favoring either beginning-state comfort
(BSC) or end-state comfort (ESC)—predicts performance on a non-binary
planning task. Prior studies (e.g., Studenka, Gamble, & Hernandez-Zuniga, 2023) suggest that individuals adopt stable strategies despite task
constraints, but the mechanisms behind these preferences remain unclear.
We hypothesized that individuals favoring BSC will exhibit reduced

planning span and ﬂexibility compared to those preferring ESC. Forty-two
participants completed a grasp and object manipulation task under two
conditions: unconstrained and 5-second hold at the initial state. Task order
was counterbalanced. Following this, participants performed a novel nonbinary task that required them to grasp and rotate an object. Joint angles
and reaction times were recorded across all tasks. Preliminary results
replicated prior ﬁndings that most participants consistently adopted either
BSC or ESC strategy. In the binary task, BSC predominated, particularly
under the constrained condition (78% of participants). Joint angle analysis
focused on whether speciﬁc movement patterns predicted group membership (BSC or ESC). Initial results suggest a strong relationship between
binary task strategy and non-binary task performance. Findings indicate
that stable grasp strategies reﬂect underlying cognitive and motor planning
differences. Participants favoring BSC may prioritize immediate ease over
anticipatory adjustment, potentially linked to reduced planning ﬂexibility.
Understanding these relationships could inform interventions for populations with impaired motor planning, such as individuals with ASD. This
study enhances our understanding of motor planning by linking binary task
strategies to broader planning capacities. Insights gained could aid the
development of targeted therapies to improve motor and cognitive function
in clinical populations.
Reach fast or slow? The role of post-stroke fatigue in arm movement
decisions
Mengdi Wang, Texas Woman’s University; Kuan-Chun Liao, Texas
Woman’s University; Jill Stewart, University of South Carolina; Elaine
Trudelle-Jackson, Texas Woman’s University; Wanyi Wang, Texas Woman’s University; Hui-Ting Goh, Texas Woman’s University
Individuals with stroke exhibit impaired reach performance and reduced
use of the affected arm, often attributed to motor impairment. Movement
decisions are partly driven by perceived effort, and many individuals with
stroke experience heightened perceived effort and high fatigue levels
during activities. However, it remains unclear how post-stroke fatigue
(PSF) inﬂuences movement decisions, such as speed selection. This
preliminary analysis of an ongoing clinical trial examines reach performance differences under self-selected and fast-as-possible speed conditions and how PSF inﬂuences these differences. Sixteen individuals with
stroke (9 males; Mage = 57.3 ± 15.4 years; chronicity = 80.9 ± 49.5 months)
performed a 2-dimensional reaching task toward 6- and 12-cm targets
under self-selected then fast-as-possible speed conditions. Reach performance (reaction time, movement time, endpoint error) and perceived effort
(Borg Rate of Perceived Exertion (RPE), Paas Mental Effort Rating Scale
(MERS)) were measured. PSF was assessed using the Fatigue Severity
Scale (FSS), Visual Analog Scale-Fatigue (VAS-F), and Fatigue Scale for
Motor and Cognitive Function (FSMC), and motor impairment with the
Fugl-Meyer Motor Assessment (FM). Repeated measures ANOVA compared performance across arms, distances, and speeds. Stepwise regression
models investigated whether fatigue outcomes and FM predicted reach
performance and perceived effort between speed conditions. Reaction
times and movement times were longer with the affected arm than with the
unaffected arm (p’s < .01) and under self-selected compared to fast speed
conditions (p = .08; p = .01). Perceived effort ratings were higher for the
affected arm compared to the unaffected arm for RPE (p = .02) and for
MERS (p < .01) but were comparable between speed conditions. FM did
not explain reach performance differences for affected arm reaches.
However, fatigue as measured by the VAS-F explained differences in
perceived effort (MERS) and reaction time (both R2 = 36%) between speed
conditions, while fatigue as measured by the FSS accounted for 42% of
variance in the difference in endpoint error between conditions. Our
ﬁndings suggest PSF may explain movement decisions during movements
of the impaired arm. PSF should be considered in future studies on upper
extremity control after stroke, particularly those involving choice. Funding
source: National Institutes of Health (NIH)
Effects of dual-task combined motor and cognitive fatigue on military
marksmanship reaction times
Juliette Jacobi, University of Tennessee/United States Army; Joshua
Springer, University of Tennessee/ United States Army; Jared Porter,
University of Tennessee
Warfare is a complex human endeavor that requires the seamless synchronization of mental and physical capabilities to navigate the high-stakes
dynamic environments of the battleﬁeld. Optimized military performance
in combat requires the successful combination of training, human factors,
and the adoption of immerging technology. As militaries worldwide strive
to enhance their effectiveness, one critical component which remains under
investigated is the nuanced interaction between cognitive and motor
performance, particularly under states of fatigue. Performance degradation
is evident in the physical and mental domains when fatigued. However, the
combined effects of the two types of fatigue in military settings are
underexplored. This study evaluated marksmanship reaction time performance (i.e., precision & accuracy, discriminatory reaction, speed &
accuracy) across three performance conditions to uncover how fatigue
impairs the ability of military personnel to perform optimally in demanding
operational contexts. Participants used a non-immersive virtual reality
InVeris marksmanship simulator with Blueﬁre weapons. Over multiple
testing sessions, volunteers participated in one of the following conditions:
physical fatigue, mental fatigue, or combined mental and physical fatigue.
To explore the effect of fatigue on marksmanship performance, a 2 (Time:
baseline, posttest) x 3 (Condition: physical fatigue, mental fatigue, combined fatigue) repeated measures ANOVA was used to compare performance. Results across the three conditions demonstrated statistically
signiﬁcant
effects
comparing
baseline
to
posttest
performance
(p < .001), indicating a negative, compounding effect of combined physical and cognitive fatigue on marksmanship performance. These effects
were most apparent in the signiﬁcant and meaningful decrease in marksmanship accuracy, prolonged reaction times, and substantial increase in
total time to completion and number of shots required to complete the
course. The ﬁndings of this study suggest that structural or capacity
interference occurred when competing demands (i.e., combined physical
& mental fatigue) exceeded available attentional resources, resulting in
deteriorated marksmanship accuracy, discriminatory reaction control,
reaction time, and marksmanship efﬁciency. The ﬁndings of this study
demonstrate the clear impact that being mentally and physically fatigued
has on a variety of measures associated with marksmanship performance.
Recommendations are made regarding how these data can be used to more
effectively train tactical populations.
Decision-making in one-on-one situations in soccer: Skill level, deception and context affect evidence accumulation
Henrietta Weinberg, Friedrich Schiller University Jena; Robin Jackson,
Loughborough University; Rouwen Ca˜nal-Bruland, Friedrich Schiller
University Jena
Outcome probability impacts the skilled detection of deception in soccer,
as demonstrated by Jackson et al. (2020). In their study, Jackson and
colleagues had high- and low-skilled participants physically respond to
video projections of an upcoming soccer player who took the ball to the left
or right with or without deception and manipulated context with probability information regarding outcome direction. The original study showed
that high-skilled athletes were more accurate than low-skilled athletes as
well as that low-skilled athletes were more prone to deception and more
inﬂuenced by contextual information than their high-skilled counterparts.
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS

However, their original signal detection analysis (SDT) approach does not
allow for conclusions about the dynamic time course of the decisionmaking process. Hence, to gain insight into the dynamic time course of the
decision-making process, we reanalyzed the original data from Jackson
et al. (2020) using a sequential sampling analysis approach, referred to as
hierarchical drift diffusion modeling (HDDM). HDDM accounts for the
dynamic process by dividing decision making into four different parameters: drift rate (i.e., rate of evidence accumulation), boundaries
(i.e., decision threshold), starting point (i.e., bias), and non-decision time
(i.e., perception and motor response). Our ﬁndings indicate that highskilled athletes accumulate evidence faster than low-skilled athletes
(p < .001) and that evidence is accumulated faster in genuine than in
deceptive trials (p = .002). Drift rates of congruent trials (i.e., the ﬁnal
move was towards the higher probability side) are signiﬁcantly higher
compared to incongruent (i.e., the ﬁnal move was towards the lower
probability side) trials (p < .001). Furthermore, high-skilled athletes are
less inﬂuenced by the (in)congruence of contextual information than lowskilled athletes and in deceptive trials drift rates are more strongly
inﬂuenced by contextual information than in genuine trials. On the one
hand, these ﬁndings nicely replicate the conclusions of Jackson and
colleagues. On the other hand, our HDDM analyses signiﬁcantly extend
those ﬁndings, for instance, by specifying that superior decision-making
performance in high-skilled players compared to their less-skilled counterparts is also a result of faster evidence accumulation.
Exploring differences in sensorimotor efﬁciency for two-dimensional
movements in real and virtual environments
Damian M. Manzone, KITE - Toronto Rehabilitation Institute - University
Health Network; Xiaoye Michael Wang, University of Toronto; Gabriela
Oancea, University of Toronto; Sameeha Jafri, University of Toronto;
Joseph X. Manzone, University of Toronto; Timothy N. Welsh, University
of Toronto; Luc Tremblay, University of Toronto
Interactions in virtual environments have become increasingly prevalent
driven by advancements in technology (e.g., virtual reality headsets) and
its potential advantages for training. However, when moving in virtual
environments,
the
representation
of
the
limb
is
often
limited
(e.g., disembodied hand), and the visual feedback latency from rendering
delays can potentially alter spatiotemporal characteristics of the movement
compared to characteristics in real physical environments. Previous work
from our lab (Manzone et al., 2024: PsyArXiv) suggested that movements
in real environments were more efﬁcient (measured using movement time
and endpoint variability) than movements in virtual environments
(i.e., cursor representing the ﬁngertip). Although reduced limb representations and visual feedback delays may account for decreased efﬁciency
when moving in the virtual environments, in our previous work participants performed pointing movements in 3D while the cursor representing
their ﬁnger was presented in 2D. Thus, the mismatch between movement
and proprioceptive feedback in 3D space and visual feedback in 2D space
may have contributed to the decreased movement efﬁciencies when
pointing in virtual environments. In the current study, 30 participants
performed discrete 2D computer mouse movements in a real (i.e., vision of
their limb and hand on the mouse) or a virtual environment (i.e., only the
ﬁngertip was represented by a white cursor). Therefore, movements and
proprioceptive and visual feedback were all in 2D space. Further, vision of
the cursor or the limb was either available prior to movement onset
(i.e., during motor planning) or prior to and throughout the movement
(i.e., during motor planning and online control). In line with our previous
3D pointing work, computer mouse movements were more efﬁcient
overall in real compared to virtual environments. Further, when vision
of the cursor was available throughout the movement, movements were
less efﬁcient than when cursor vision was available only prior to movement
onset. This result suggests that online visual feedback of the cursor was
disadvantageous for spatiotemporal movement processes. Taken together,
representation and delay issues associated with virtual environments can
alter spatiotemporal characteristics of 2D and 3D movements, which
should be considered when training or conducting experiments using
virtual environments. Funding source: NSERC; SSHRC; Ontario Research
Fund.
Interrater reliability of ultrasonic measures of ankle joint health
Amin Mohammadi, Old Dominion University; Ryan McCann, Old Dominion University
Monitoring talar cartilage thickness is potentially valuable for evaluating
ankle joint health, particularly in patients with previous ankle sprains.
Additionally, echointensity (EI) of the talocrural joint space can be used as
a measure of articular cartilage degeneration and may also be a valuable
measure of ankle joint health. While previous studies conﬁrm the intrarater
reliability of these ultrasonic measurements, their interrater reliability has
not been examined. Our purpose was to assess interrater reliability of
ultrasonic measures of ankle joint health between an expert and novice
rater. We recruited 40 university community members aged 20 to 55
(36.78 ± 12.29 yrs, 166.70 ± 18.59cm, 77.37 ± 17.44kg), including those
with (n = 20) and without (n = 20) a previous ankle sprain. Diagnostic
ultrasound images of the talocrural joint were acquired using a Lumify
L12-4 linear-array transducer (Philips, Amsterdam, Netherlands) connected to a 9th Generation iPad (Apple Inc., Cupertino, CA). Images
were analyzed using ImageJ software Version 1.53t (National Institutes of
Health, Bethesda, MD) to measure lateral and medial cross-sectional areas
(CSA) and EI. Both an experienced and a novice rater independently
evaluated each image, calculating mean scores for each variable. We
conducted intraclass correlation coefﬁcients (ICCs) to assess consistency
between raters using IBM SPSS 28. Excellent interrater reliability was
demonstrated across all measurements when all subjects were considered
together, with ICCs showing strong agreement for lateral CSA (ICC = .93,
p < .01), medial CSA (ICC = .92, p < .01), lateral EI (ICC = .93, p < .01),
and medial EI (ICC = .97, p < .01). In the subgroup of participants with
previous ankle sprains, good reliability was observed for lateral CSA
(ICC = .87, p < .01) and medial CSA (ICC = .89, p < .01), and excellent
reliability for lateral EI (ICC = .95, p < .01) and medial EI (ICC = .96,
p < .01). Among those without previous ankle sprains, good reliability was
noted for lateral EI (ICC = .89, p < .01) and excellent reliability for medial
EI (ICC = .97, p < .01), lateral CSA (ICC = .94, p < .01), and medial CSA
(ICC = .94, p < .01). The study conﬁrms high interrater reliability for
ultrasonic measures of ankle joint health between an experienced and a
novice rater. These ﬁndings support the utility of ultrasound as a reliable
tool for assessing talar cartilage CSA and EI in individuals with and
without a history of ankle sprains. Funding source: MAATA.
Acquired action-effect contingencies underlie the beneﬁt of an external focus of attention
William Land, University of Texas at San Antonio; Reza Abdollahipour,
Palacký University Olomouc; Kevin Becker, University of Tennessee,
Knoxville
Effect-based theories of action control suggest that bidirectional associations formed between motor activity and sensory consequences (actioneffect contingencies) play a critical role in the selection, initiation, and
control of action. Based on this framework, the present study investigated
whether action-effect contingencies acquired during motor learning underlie the beneﬁts of an external focus of attention. Speciﬁcally, we
propose that an external focus functions to reactivate corresponding motor
patterns
via
these
bidirectional
links,
thereby
enhancing
motor
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025

performance. Consequently, we hypothesized that training conditions that
restrict the development of such action-effect contingencies would diminish the effectiveness of an external focus. To test this hypothesis, 64
participants (Mage = 22.1 ± 3.6 years) trained over two days on a seated
ball-tossing task, using their non-dominant arm to toss a ball underhand to
a target (160 trials in total). Participants were assigned to either a feedback
or no-feedback training condition. Participants in the feedback condition
received full visual and auditory perceptual feedback during the ball toss,
while participants in the no-feedback condition had visual and auditory
feedback removed immediately after ball release. Knowledge of results
was provided after each toss for the no-feedback condition. On day three,
all participants performed the ball-tossing task with full perceptual feedback across three attentional focus conditions (n = 15 tosses each): control
(no focus instructions), internal (focus on arm movement), and external
(focus on the ball’s ﬂight). Results supported our hypotheses. Participants
who trained with full perceptual feedback exhibited improved accuracy
under external focus compared to internal focus and control conditions
(p < .05). Conversely, no such beneﬁt was observed in participants who
trained without perceptual feedback (p > .05). These ﬁndings support an
effect-based conceptualization of external focus beneﬁts, suggesting that
such beneﬁts depend on the action-effect contingencies developed during
practice. Under conditions that constrain the formation of action-effect
relationships, as with the no-feedback condition, focusing on the anticipated sensory consequences of the action is unable to reactivate the
relevant motor patterns, thereby exerting no inﬂuence on performance.
These ﬁndings have particular relevance for identifying optimal focus of
attention instructions for performers.
Gulliver’s travels in virtual lands: Depth compression in virtual reality
increases perceptual body size
Xiaoye Michael Wang, University of Toronto; Ali Mazalek, Toronto
Metropolitan University; Catherine M. Sabiston, University of Toronto;
Timothy N. Welsh, University of Toronto
Interacting in virtual reality (VR) using a self-controlled avatar is critical to
user experience in VR. While numerous studies have examined the effect
of VR and avatar representation on body image, there is a missing link
between how the perceptual distortions in VR may impact the user’s
perception of body size in VR and the physical environment. The current
study used a “passable aperture” paradigm to investigate this issue. In the
physical and virtual environments, the participants stood 2.5m from two
poles to either physically walk sideways between the poles (action task) or
adjust the distance between the poles so that they could walk sideways
between the poles without touching them (perception task). Performance
thresholds were calculated as the mean of a ﬁtted psychometric curve
(action) or the mean adjusted distance (perception). In VR, participants
embodied avatars with the same (N = 16), 20% less (N = 17), or 20% more
(N = 16) of their physical weight. Participants ﬁrst performed an action
(physical pre-VR action) and a perception task (physical pre-VR perception) in the physical environment before experiencing VR. Then, in VR,
participants completed a perception task immediately after embodying the
avatar (VR pre-action perception), an action task (VR action), and another
perception task (VR post-action perception). Finally, participants completed a perception task in the physical environment (physical post-VR
perception). A mixed design ANOVA showed a signiﬁcant main effect of
task (p < .001, ηp
2 = .70), but not avatar size (p = .88, ηp
2 = .005) or the
interaction (p = .22, ηp
2 = .059). Post-hoc pairwise comparisons showed a
signiﬁcant threshold increase from physical pre-VR perception (M =
31.50 cm, SE = 0.76) to VR pre-action perception (M = 46.86 cm, SE =
1.36, p < .001), indicating the participants perceived their body to be larger
in VR than in the physical world. This effect transferred to the physical
environment where the physical post-VR perception (M = 33.98, SE =
0.85) was greater than its pre-VR counterpart (p < .001). These ﬁndings
indicate that the visual characteristics of VR (e.g., depth compression)
impact the perception of body size therein and when returning to the
physical environment. These changes in perception of body size, however,
were not altered by the body size of the embodied avatar. Funding
source: NFRF.
Stimulating the imagination? Increased incidental movement during
motor imagery following tDCS over the supplementary motor area
Judith Bek, University of Toronto; Xiaoye Michael Wang, University of
Toronto; Timothy N Welsh, University of Toronto
Motor imagery (imagined movement without overt motor execution) and
action observation recruit neural networks that overlap with those involved
in motor planning and execution. The role of the supplementary motor area
(SMA) in imagery and observation is not fully understood, but may
involve the suppression of motor execution during these processes via
inhibitory connections to primary motor cortex (M1). The present study
used transcranial direct current stimulation (tDCS) to elucidate the role of
the SMA in motor execution, motor imagery, and action observation. In
separate blocks of trials, neurologically healthy participants (N = 23)
completed three versions of a reciprocal manual aiming task involving
executed, imagined, and observed movements. The difﬁculty of the
movements was varied across trials by changing target width and movement amplitude in a manner that has previously been found to produce a
speed-accuracy trade-off characteristic of Fitts’ law. The tasks were
completed before and after 20 minutes of anodal, cathodal, or sham
(inactive) stimulation over the SMA. All participants received the three
tDCS conditions in separate sessions, with condition order counterbalanced between participants. Movement time and the expression of Fitts’
law were not signiﬁcantly altered by tDCS in any of the tasks. However,
incidental movement during motor imagery (motor overﬂow) was increased following anodal tDCS compared to sham stimulation. Additional
analysis indicated that the relationship between imagined movement time
and an implicit measure of motor imagery (hand laterality judgement) was
reduced following anodal tDCS. There were no effects of tDCS on the
observation or execution versions of the aiming task. These results suggest
that there may have been an inhibitory effect of anodal tDCS on the SMA.
Alternatively, the ﬁndings may reﬂect excitatory effects of tDCS on
adjacent areas of the motor cortex such as pre-SMA or M1. Further
research should compare the effects of different tDCS targets and parameters on motor imagery and action observation. Funding source: NSERC.
Relative contributions of binocular versus monocular viewing conditions on the visual control of discrete pointing and reciprocal tapping
movements using Fitts’ task in adults
Shirley Wang, University of Waterloo; Xiaoxin Chen, University of
Waterloo; Daphne Maurer, McMaster University; David I. Shore,
McMaster University; Agnes Wong, The Hospital for Sick Children;
Xiaoqing Gao, Zhejiang University; Haotian Lin, Zhongshan Ophthalmic
Center; Benjamin Thompson, University of Waterloo; Ewa NiechwiejSzwedo, University of Waterloo
Motor performance during most daily living activities relies on visual
information. Binocular vision, in particular, produces efﬁcient and accurate discrete reaching movements. The Fitts’ reciprocal tapping task
provides one important measure of motor control: the trade-off between
speed and accuracy. The current study assessed the effects of binocular and
monocular viewing on eye–hand coordination during a Fitts’ task. It was
hypothesized that monocular viewing would reduce motor capacity compared to binocular viewing. Adults (N = 19; Mage = 26.3 ± 5.9 years, 12
females) with normal visual acuity and binocular vision performed two
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S46
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 50] ---
tasks: discrete pointing and reciprocal tapping. For the discrete task,
participants pointed to a visual target presented at 5° or 10° along the
horizontal meridian. For the reciprocal task, participants repeatedly tapped
between two targets of various sizes as fast and accurately as possible
(index of difﬁculty [ID] ranged from 2.28 to 6.97 bits). Eye and hand
movements were recorded at 250Hz with an Eyelink2 eye tracker and the
Optotrak 3D Investigator motion capture system. Participants wore a
translucent eyepatch during monocular conditions. Consistent with previous studies, for the discrete reaching task, binocular viewing led to
signiﬁcantly higher peak velocity (binocular: 0.580 ± 0.126m/s; left monocular: 0.558 ± 0.141m/s; right monocular: 0.530 ± 0.106 m/s; F(2,
36) = 3.37, p = .046) and shorter deceleration (binocular 0.551 ± 0.125s;
left monocular: 0.636 ± 0.142s; right monocular: 0.605 ± 0.155s; F(2,
36) = 6.46, p = .004) with similar endpoint precision across the viewing
conditions. For the reciprocal task, the impact of binocular viewing was
limited to ID level 4.24, where there was a higher peak velocity during
binocular compared to monocular viewing (binocular: 0.698 ± 0.086m/s;
left monocular: 0.675 ± 0.079m/s; right monocular: 0.667 ± 0.070m/s;
F(10, 170) = 2.63, p = .005). Viewing conditions did not alter the temporal
eye–hand coordination pattern in the discrete or reciprocal tasks. These
results suggest that binocular viewing allows for more efﬁcient motor
planning and execution of discrete reaching, but the advantage is not
evident at all ID levels for reciprocal tapping. This study provides
normative values to investigate visuomotor control in patient populations
such as those with amblyopia, which is a neurodevelopmental disorder
characterized by poor vision in one or both eyes. Funding source: CIHR.
Predicting game outcomes in rock-paper-scissors: A kinematic approach to competitive social interactions
Johannes Keck, Friedrich Schiller University Jena; Kazuyuki Sato, Friedrich Schiller University Jena; Rouwen Ca˜nal-Bruland, Friedrich Schiller
University Jena
Humans engage in a multitude of cooperative and competitive interactions,
for instance, when playing games such as rock-paper-scissors (RPS). To
compete in such games the ability to interpret and predict others’ actions
and intentions is crucial. Bodily movements convey informative cues
about the intentions or conﬁdence of the counterpart’s actions (Cavallo
et al., 2016). In the present pilot study, we examined to what degree the
individual and interactive dynamics of the kinematics in dyads playing
RPS allow to predict the outcome of the game. RPS is a hand game and
involves three actions executed as different hand gestures: rock (ﬁst)
crushes scissors (index and middle ﬁnger forming a V-shape), scissors cut
paper (ﬂat hand), and paper covers rock. RPS is widely used to study
competitive behavior, dynamic strategies (win-stay/lose-shift), and decision-making (Garrido-da-Silva & Castro, 2020; Zhang et al., 2020).
However, the role of body movements in RPS remains unexplored, leaving
a gap in understanding how bodily cues may be used to predict the
opponent’s action. In our pilot study four participants each played 100
games of RPS against each other opponent, totaling 600 games. During
each game, participants movements were captured using an OptiTrack
system (operating at 240 Hz) with 16 cameras and markers attached to the
full body. Hand poses were classiﬁed, and outcomes (win, loss, or draw)
were identiﬁed. We analyzed individual and interactive kinematic features,
including hand acceleration, the absolute amount of marker displacement
over time (motion energy), and timing differences between players’ poses.
A transition feature was also included to capture whether players shifted or
stayed with their previous pose. Using a random forest algorithm with kfold cross-validation, we predicted game outcomes and assessed predictor
importance and classiﬁcation accuracy. The classiﬁcation accuracy
(61%, ± 0.2) signiﬁcantly exceeded chance (33%; p < .01). Differences in
hand acceleration between players were the most predictive feature,
followed by motion energy. Interestingly, strategic decision-making (shift
or stay) was the least informative parameter. Our results highlight the
importance of interactive kinematics in competitive games, suggesting that
RPS outcomes are shaped by adaptive body movements unfolding as the
dynamic interplay between opponents. Funding source: Volkswagen
Foundation.
Sport and Exercise Psychology Abstracts
A meta-analysis of eHealth for promoting physical activity in adults
with obesity
Seungmin Lee, Iowa State University; Soyeon Ahn, University of Miami;
Priya Patel, California State University Fullerton; Nicholas Myers,
Michigan State University
Physical activity has consistently been recommended for adults with
obesity due to its independent effect on health-related outcomes, regardless
of weight loss. However, there have been persistent reports that, perhaps,
most adults with obesity do not meet public health guidelines for physical
activity, such as 150 minutes per week of moderate physical activity.
While some studies have utilized eHealth interventions (i.e., the use of
information and communication technology) to promote physical activity
in adults with obesity, there is limited research that has quantitatively
calculated the effects of recent eHealth interventions to promote physical
activity in this population. The purpose of this study was to synthesize the
effects of recent eHealth interventions to promote physical activity in
adults with obesity. This meta-analysis followed the Preferred Reporting
Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses (PRISMA) guidelines.
In the search, 3550 articles were identiﬁed, and 15 studies met all inclusion
criteria. The effects of recent eHealth interventions were analyzed by the
type
of
outcome
variable:
(a)
intensity-based
physical
activity
(e.g., moderate-to-vigorous intensity physical activity) or (b) energy expenditure-based physical activity (e.g., metabolic equivalent of task). The
overall effects of recent eHealth interventions on the physical activity
outcomes in adults with obesity were positive and ranged from small to
medium in size. Ethnicity and weight status moderated the effects of recent
eHealth interventions on physical activity outcomes. Results from this
meta-analysis provided some evidence for both the utility of, and possible
improvements to, eHealth interventions to promote health-enhancing
physical activity in the population. Recommendations are provided for
future eHealth interventions to promote physical activity among adults
with obesity.
Breaking barriers, building biceps: Examining the feasibility of a
culturally tailored strength training program for South Asian Women
Kirina Angrish-Dandora, Brock University; Zaraa Zaman, Brock University; Taranjot K. Dhillon, Brock University; Kimberley L. Gammmage,
Brock University
The 24-Hour Movement Guidelines recommend 150 minutes of moderateto-vigorous physical activity weekly, with strength training done twice
weekly to promote physical and mental health. Despite these beneﬁts,
South Asian women in Canada often fail to meet these recommendations,
often due to socio-cultural barriers (e.g., mixed-sex exercise facilities and
focus on domestic duties). Culturally tailored strength training programs
may address these barriers while leveraging evidence-based facilitators.
Culturally tailored dance programs have successfully promoted physical
activity in this group; no study has explored strength training programs for
South Asian women. This study explored the feasibility and acceptability
of a culturally tailored 6-week strength training program for South Asian
women. The program included a South Asian instructor, a South Asian
dance warmup, Bollywood music, and was exclusive to South Asian
