women for cultural and religious reasons. Indicators of feasibility included
recruitment (e.g., number of eligible participants), adherence, and intervention ﬁdelity. Program feasibility and acceptability were assessed using
validated measures at six weeks and program evaluation measures were
completed as well. Twenty-two participants contacted the research team;
12 were eligible and enrolled, and seven completed the program
(Mage = 20.29 years; SD = 2.75). The average number of classes attended
was 4.86 out of 12 possible classes (M = 40.5%; SD = 4.18), ranging
between one to twelve classes. All 18 classes were delivered as intended,
including the culturally tailored aspects. Participants reported that the
program was implementable and acceptable; however, low interest, enrollment, and poor adherence provide conﬂicting evidence. Program
evaluation data showed that participants appreciated the program’s exclusivity to South Asian women, private group ﬁtness setting, and no cost.
Reasons for continuing attendance included the South Asian dance
warmup, quality of instruction, and group settings. Given the low recruitment rates and poor adherence, running a more extensive study in its
present form is not feasible. There is a need for a further feasibility
assessment of the program and recruitment that can be achieved through
more targeted recruitment strategies and building relationships with
community-based South Asian organizations. Funding source: This research draws on research supported by the Social Sciences and Humanities
Research Council.
Suicidality in Canadian varsity athletes
Burgandy Thiessen, Brock University; Quinten Carfagnini, Brock University; Philip Sullivan, Brock University
Suicide is the second most common cause of death among studentathletes (Whelan et al., 2024). It has been consistently found that the
prevalence of suicidal ideation among student-athletes is similar to
(Anchuri et al., 2020) or lower than non-athletes (Whelan et al.,
2024). However, these studies have focused on the American intercollegiate sport system, which limits the generalizability of results to different
contexts. The Canadian intercollegiate sport system (i.e., USPORTS)
differs from the American one (i.e., NCAA) in ways that may inﬂuence
the ﬁndings noted above (i.e., smaller value scholarships, less time spent
on injury rehabilitation, nutrition counselling, etc. (Comeaux & Harrison,
2011) compared to NCAA student-athletes). The current study examined
the prevalence of suicidal ideation of Canadian student-athletes relative to
non-athletes. Varsity athletes (n = 348) and non-athletes (n = 9,504)
participated in the 2022 Canadian cohort of the American Collegiate
Health Association’s National College Health Assessment, which included the revised Suicide Behaviour Questionnaire (SBQ-R). Student-athletes’ scores on the SBQ-R were signiﬁcantly lower than non-athletes,
indicating signiﬁcantly lower suicidal ideation. Furthermore, while psychological distress and sexual minority status were signiﬁcant predictors
of suicidal ideation status in both populations, stress in the past month
was a signiﬁcant predictor for student-athletes. This ﬁnding is consistent
with current practices and recommendations aimed at addressing stress
and promoting the mental health and well-being of athletes (Breslin et al.,
2017). Additionally, our ﬁnding of a signiﬁcantly lower rate of suicide
ideation among student-athletes is consistent with previous studies and
extends our understanding beyond American samples. While the current
study did not assess deaths by suicide as previous research had, these
results are largely consistent with the results from American samples.
Future research should examine what the optimal focal point for knowledge users should be regarding suicidality (i.e., suicidal thoughts, selfharm, or suicide attempt). Furthermore, future research should aim to
extend our understanding of the Canadian intercollegiate sport system,
given that most research regarding suicide in varsity sport is targeted in
the Unites States of America.
Acute effects of sports on executive functions in youth: The role of
cognitive engagement and social interaction
Marcelo Cabral, Michigan State University; Matthew B. Pontifex, Michigan State University; Spyridoula Vazou, Michigan State University
Sports may impact executive functions through different metabolic
(e.g., dose and intensity through neurotrophic stimulation and cardiovascular ﬁtness hypotheses) and cognitive (e.g., task complexity and
motor coordination through cognitive stimulation hypothesis) processes. However, the role of qualitative aspects of sports, such as cognitive
engagement and social interactions, in developing executive functions
remains unclear. This study examined the acute effects of sports with
different levels of cognitive engagement (simple vs complex motor
tasks) and social interaction (alone vs in group games) on inhibitory
control in youth. A total of 119 college students participated in this
study (Mage = 20.44 ± 0.94 years; 54.6% female). Using a betweensubjects experimental design, four lab sections in an undergraduate
course were randomly assigned to one of four experimental groups
(nG1 = 30, nG2 = 31, nG3 = 30, nG4 = 28). Each condition consisted of 30minute soccer activities varying in cognitive engagement (high or low)
and social interaction (high or low). Exercise intensity, perceived
exertion, cognitive engagement, and social interaction were assessed
as manipulation checks. Inhibitory control was measured before and
immediately after each condition using the Eriksen Flanker Task. Mean
reaction times for correct responses in congruent and incongruent trials
were used to evaluate cognitive task performance. A two-way ANCOVA was conducted to test the effects of cognitive engagement, social
interaction, and their combination on executive functioning performance. Results showed that social interaction signiﬁcantly improved
reaction time in the incongruent trials [F(1, 109) = 6.39, p = .013,
η2
p = .055]. There were no signiﬁcant effects of cognitive engagement
or the combination of cognitive engagement and social interaction on
inhibitory control. These ﬁndings indicate that a single session of sports
activities with high social interaction can acutely improve inhibitory
control in college students. Future research should focus on the quality
of social interaction (e.g., positive or negative) to explore how sports
and physical activities can maximize beneﬁts for executive functions
in youth.
The effect of freediving on mood states
Ruby A Parish, San Francisco State University; Rebecca N. Kirkland, San
Francisco State University; Sierra Dorf-Cardenas, San Francisco State
University; Jacob S. Dinardi, San Francisco State University
Engaging in water-based activities and aquatic immersion can lead to
signiﬁcant changes in mood. For instance, the literature highlights surf
therapy as especially effective for reducing depression and trauma
symptoms, whereas scuba diving has been shown to increase tension
and confusion. However, few studies explore how freediving affects
mood states. Freediving involves swimming underwater for extended
durations while holding one’s breath. This elicits phenomena related to
experiencing the mammalian dive response, hypoxia, and elevated
blood nitrogen levels. These factors lead to lowered heart rate and
calming effects, which aid in freediving and potentially affect mood.
The present study is, to our knowledge, the ﬁrst to investigate the impact
of a single bout of freediving on mood state using a pre-test post-test
design. With permission from the AIDA Go Deep California freediving
competition organizers, eighteen participants (13 male, 4 female, 1 nonbinary; Mage = 36 years) were recruited using snowball methods. Participants completed pre-dive surveys consisting of the Brunel Mood Scale
(BRUMS) and socio-demographic questions within 48-hours of the
competition’s start. Post-dive surveys consisting of the BRUMS and a
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S48
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 52] ---
brief qualitative questionnaire were completed within 24-hours of the
competition’s end. Surveys were administered via Qualtrics. Data was
analyzed using SPSS. Descriptive statistics and paired t-tests were
computed for each BRUMS subscale. Standardized T-scores were
generated to compare freedivers’ mood proﬁles with previously published mood proﬁles for athletes in general. Finally, a thematic analysis
was conducted on the qualitative data. Post-dive, negative affect
signiﬁcantly decreased, while positive affect signiﬁcantly increased
(p ≤.05), each with moderate effect sizes (G ≥.65). The participants’
qualitative responses reﬂected enjoyment of the experience, gratitude
for the opportunity to pursue their goals, and appreciation for their
fellow divers. These results illustrate the mood-enhancing beneﬁts of
freediving. Data collection is ongoing to explore differences between
line diving, pool diving, competitive, and non-competitive settings.
Future studies could expand on this work by examining longitudinal
effects of freediving on overall well-being in adult populations. This
research contributes to a broader understanding of freediving as a novel
method of mood regulation, with potential applications in mental health
interventions.
Empowering new beginnings: A holistic evaluation of a communitydeveloped multi-sports program for new-to-Canada refugee youths
and its impact on physical literacy, mental wellbeing, and social health
Taylor Rowe, Brock University; Nathan Hall, Brock University; Shauna
Pomerantz, Brock University; Sujane Kandasamy, Brock University;
Amanda Koyama, Calgary Catholic Immigration Society; Matthew Kwan,
Brock University
The resettlement process for refugee youth in Canada presents complex
challenges, particularly in integrating into social frameworks such as sports
and physical activity (PA) programs. While these programs are critical for
promoting physical and mental well-being, refugee youth consistently
report lower participation rates compared to their Canadian-born counterparts. This current study was a mixed-methods longitudinal evaluation of a
community-developed multi-sport program aimed at enhancing physical
literacy (PL) development and psychosocial well-being among new refugee youths in Canada. Sixteen refugee youths (Mage = 16.00 years ± 1.75,
n = 14 males) participated in the study, all recent arrivals residing in
government-assisted temporary housing (M = 1.19 months post-arrival).
Pre-post qualitative and quantitative assessments evaluated perceptions of
PL and program impacts. Data from the PLAYbasic tool and PLAYself
questionnaires revealed signiﬁcant improvements in movement competence (M = 58.42 to 61.75, p < .001) among highly engaged participants,
highlighting the program’s success in skill-based aspects of PL. However,
no signiﬁcant changes were observed in conﬁdence, perceived competence, motivation, or knowledge and understanding domains, likely due to
the youths’ high baseline perceptions of these attributes prior to program
entry. This suggests that the program reinforced existing positive selfperceptions rather than transforming them. Qualitative data from focus
group interviews provided deeper insights into the program’s broader
impacts, including enhanced social connections and a sense of belonging,
which were reported as positively inﬂuencing mental well-being. Key
contributors to these outcomes included access to structured programs and
experienced coaches who fostered an inclusive and supportive environment. Youths also identiﬁed logistical constraints, such as competing
schedules and limited program awareness, which limited consistent participation. Findings emphasize the critical role of culturally sensitive,
community-driven initiatives in supporting PL, social integration, and
well-being among newcomer youths. Future research should explore the
long-term impact of such programs and address unique barriers, particularly those affecting female participation, to maximize inclusivity and
effectiveness.
Examining rumination in sport coaches
Martin Dixon, San Francisco State University; Kent Lorenz, San Francisco State University; Sydney Yuen, San Francisco State University;
Martin Turner, Manchester Metropolitan University
The demands of sport coaching can be wide-ranging and pervasive with
coaches often struggling to mentally disengage from their work. Although
qualitative reports link repetitive negative thinking with stress and burnout
(Dixon & Bolter, 2023; Hassmén et al., 2019), research on rumination in
coaches is limited. The current study sought to address this gap by
examining demographic and situational factors of coaches’ rumination.
Participants
were
312
professional
sport
coaches
(Mage = 41.05 ±
13.46 years) with 29% identifying as female and 71% as male. The
coaches worked either full-time or part-time in club, high school, or
college settings across a range of sports with 14.15 ± 10.81 years of
experience. Rumination was measured using the Perseverative Thinking
Questionnaire (PTQ), a content-independent questionnaire that focuses on
the characteristic processes of repetitive negative thinking (Ehring et al.,
2011). The PTQ showed excellent reliability in the coach sample (α = .93).
The coaches reported lower levels of rumination compared to other nonclinical samples such as athletes (Michel-Kröhler et al., 2024) and students
(Miniati et al., 2023). Bivariate correlation analyses revealed signiﬁcant
relationships between rumination and age (r = .21, p < .001), and between
rumination and experience (r = .13, p = .03). These ﬁndings indicate that
coaches are likely to ruminate more as they get older and acquire more
experience. In terms of coaching setting, collegiate coaches demonstrated
the highest rumination scores (M = 21.51 ± 10.24), followed by high
school coaches (M = 20.19 ± 10.24), and club coaches (M = 18.76 ±
7.81). A one-way ANOVA revealed no signiﬁcant differences in rumination between coaching settings, F(2, 200) = 1.00, p = .37. Although these
ﬁndings were non-signiﬁcant, data indicate that rumination is higher in
collegiate coaches, potentially due to greater perceived demands experienced in the collegiate context. No signiﬁcant differences in rumination
scores were found between female and male coaches. The study concludes
with recommendations to further understand coaches’ rumination, with
implications for managing stress and burnout.
Determinants of health-related quality of life in South Korean studentathletes: Uncovering the role of athletic identity
Yeongjun (YJ) Seo, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Erin
Reifsteck, University of North Carolina at Greensboro
Although participating in organized sports can confer mental and
physical health beneﬁts, competitive sport participation can increase
vulnerability to various stressors, mental illness, and injuries that can
have potential long-term health consequences. The demanding culture
of elite sport often prioritizes performance over health, resulting in
maladaptive behaviors and normalizing exposure to sport-related
abuse, all of which can contribute to poor health-related quality of
life (HRQoL). Researchers have emphasized the role of athletic
identity (i.e., the degree to which individuals identify with the athlete
role) on athlete health and well-being, which can serve as both a
protective and detrimental factor for mental and physical health.
However, athletic identity in relation to the unique health challenges
arising from competitive sport participation remains understudied in
the South Korean context. Alarming rates of abuse and compounded
challenges for women underscore the need to examine the factors
inﬂuencing HRQoL. Therefore, this study examined predictors of
HRQoL among 372 South Korean collegiate athletes (women = 140).
Speciﬁcally, the association of athletic identity with mental and
physical HRQoL along with key personal and sport-related factors
(i.e., injury history, verbal, physical abuse) were examined through

hierarchical multiple regression. Injury history was negatively associated with physical HRQoL (β = -.24, p < .001), while exposure to
verbal abuse was found to be related to lower mental HRQoL (β = -
.17, p = .050). The addition of gender, followed by athletic identity,
signiﬁcantly increased the amount of variance explained in the HRQoL
variables. Women reported signiﬁcantly worse mental (β = -.22, p <
.001) and physical HRQoL (β = -.14, p < .01) than men. Additionally,
athletic identity positively predicted both mental (β = .39, p < .001) and
physical HRQoL (β = .20, p < .001). These ﬁndings highlight that
athletic identity is a protective factor of health perception for South
Korean student-athletes, offering cultural insights distinct from prior
research conducted in other global contexts. However, the gender
disparities favoring men, along with the negative impact of injury
history and verbal abuse on HRQoL, highlight the need for culturally
tailored, evidence-based interventions aimed at supporting women and
student-athletes with a history of injury or exposure to abuse in South
Korean sports. Funding source: Association for Applied Sport Psychology 2024, Gualberto Cremades International Research Grant

Effects of affect and emotional regulation on physical activity in
adolescents: An ecological momentary assessment study
Ryan Chen, University of Waterloo; Matthew Y.W. Kwan, Brock University; Sheereen Harris, University of Waterloo
Current guidelines recommend adolescents engage in, on average, at
least 60 minutes of moderate to vigorous physical activity (MVPA)
daily for disease prevention and health promotion; yet, most adolescents
are considered insufﬁciently active. Consistent with dual-process models, affect and emotional regulation (i.e., affect variability) may help
explain physical activity behavior. Although extant research has consistently found positive affect to be related to MVPA, the effects of
affect variability on MVPA requires further exploration. We examined
the role of affect and emotional regulation on daily MVPA among
adolescents using ecological momentary assessment (EMA). Participants (N = 190 grade 11 students; 83 female) completed up to four EMA
surveys daily during the after-school period for seven consecutive days.
EMAs assessed affect and whether the prompt was sent on a weekend or
weekday. Daily MVPA minutes were calculated using a wrist worn
accelerometer. A two-stage modelling analysis was performed using the
program MixWILD. Results from Stage 1 revealed no differences in
affect levels on weekends vs. weekdays (p = .46). There was signiﬁcant
variability among affect scores (coef. = 0.48, p < .001) suggesting students varied in terms of how consistent or erratic their reported affect
was during the after-school period. Results from Stage 2 showed mean
levels of affect were associated with daily MVPA (coef. = 12.35,
p = .01), indicating people with higher affect on average engaged in
more minutes of MVPA. Emotional regulation was not a signiﬁcant
predictor of daily MVPA (coef. = 3.26, p = .50). Exploratory analyses
were subsequently performed to investigate potential interactive effects
of affect and arousal on daily MVPA. Results showed for observations
when participants reported positive arousal, affect was a signiﬁcant
predictor of daily MVPA (p = .02), however, the association between
affect and daily MVPA for observations when students reported negative arousal was non-signiﬁcant (p = .23). Findings suggest affect but
not affect variability predict daily MVPA in students, with some
evidence that arousal may moderate the association between affect and
MVPA. Future research should continue to examine real-time associations between affect, emotional regulation, and arousal on MVPA.
Funding source: Social Sciences and Humanities Research Council (SSHRC)
Canadian university student athletes: Flourishing, languishing, and
everything in-between
Isabella Tremonte, University of Toronto; Catherine Sabiston, University
of Toronto
The mental health of student athletes is often examined in relation to
mental illness. However, the way student athletes experience positive
(i.e., well-being) dimensions of mental health and concomitant negative
(i.e., mental illness) dimensions needs to be explored. This study
(a) identiﬁed latent proﬁles of student athletes based on well-being and
mental illness, and (b) examined personal factors as correlates of latent
proﬁles. Using a cross-sectional design, 247 student athletes from a large
metropolitan university completed the Canadian Campus Wellbeing Survey in 2020 or 2022 (Mage = 20.91 years, SD = 2.39; reporting as women
[68.6%], white [39.1%], heterosexual [79.7%], without disability [69.6%],
and of higher social class based on parent education [66.7%]). Well-being
and mental illness were measured using the Warwick Edinburgh Mental
Well-being Scale (WEMWBS) and the Kessler Psychological Distress
Scale (K10), respectively. Personal variables of gender, ethnicity, sexual
orientation, disability status, and social class (using parent education) were
included. Through an exploratory latent proﬁle analysis, three distinct
proﬁles of mental health (ﬂourishing [30.4%], moderate [57.5%], and
languishing [12.1%]) were identiﬁed. Based on scoring guidelines for the
WEMWBS and K10, ﬂourishing student athletes reported ‘average’ wellbeing and were unlikely to have a mental illness. Those in the moderate
proﬁle reported ‘below average’ well-being and ‘likelihood of moderate
mental illness’. Languishing student athletes reported ‘very low’ wellbeing and ‘likelihood of severe mental illness’. Bisexual/pansexual-identifying participants were more likely to be classiﬁed in the moderate than
ﬂourishing proﬁle. Student athletes with a disability were more likely to be
languishing compared to other proﬁles. No other personal factor was a
unique correlate of proﬁle membership. In conclusion, simultaneously
evaluating positive and negative dimensions of mental health through
latent proﬁles identiﬁed whether student athletes were feeling and functioning well in the absence of mental illness (i.e., ﬂourishing). The majority
of this sample did not report ﬂourishing highlighting a need to further
investigate the multidimensional nature of mental health among student
athletes. Continuing to address both dimensions may augment the development of mental health programming for student athletes speciﬁcally
promoting ﬂourishing while considering personal factors.
How long should athletes savour milestones in sport? An experimental
study
examining
beliefs
about
what
helps
and
hurts
future
performance
Benjamin Schellenberg, University of Manitoba; Jérémie Verner-Filion,
Université du Québec en Outaouais; Patrick Gaudreau, University of
Ottawa
While pursuing important goals in sport (e.g., winning a national championship), athletes often reach milestones along the way (e.g., winning a
divisional championship). Athletes can respond to reaching important
milestones by savouring them – that is, by doing things to enhance or
prolong their good feelings (e.g., sharing an experience with a friend,
thinking about how good it feels to reach a milestone). But people may
think that athletes should restrict how long they savour milestones due to a
belief that too much savouring may negatively impact future goal attainment. In this study, we tested whether people hold this lay belief using a
within-subjects experimental design. One hundred and twenty-ﬁve basketball fans completed an online survey in which they read a description of
savouring and then a hypothetical scenario about a basketball team that has
won an important milestone (a divisional championship) and that will be
preparing for a subsequent competition (the national championship
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S50
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 54] ---
tournament) taking place in seven days. Participants then reported how
long they believed the team should savour the milestone in order to either
help or hurt (order randomized) their performance in the upcoming
national championship tournament. Results showed that participants believed that, on average, the athletes should savour for 1.99 days (SD = 1.95
days) to help their future performance and for 5.12 days (SD = 2.77 days) to
hurt their future performance, a difference that was statistically signiﬁcant,
t(124) = -8.25, p < .001, d = -0.74. These results support the hypothesis that
people hold a lay belief that too much savouring of milestones will
negatively impact future performance. More research is needed to understand why people may hold this belief about savouring, and to test if there
is any evidence to support it. Funding source: Social Sciences and
Humanities Research Council of Canada.
A four-week low-dose digital mindfulness intervention and its effects
on a rowing task
Rebekah Bakken-Gay, Texas Christian University; Sarah Junkersfeld,
Texas Christian University; Robyn Braun-Trocchio, Texas Christian
University
Mindfulness can be deﬁned as paying nonjudgmental attention to the
present moment and observing thoughts without assigning value judgments (Kabat-Zinn, 1994). Previous research has highlighted how
mindfulness administered during an exercise task can be used to
increase exercise enjoyment (Cox et al., 2018; Cox et al., 2020).
Mindfulness has evolved into accessible digital formats, like the Netﬂix
docuseries Headspace Guide to Meditation. Despite widespread use,
limited research exists on how low-dose digital mindfulness interventions administered before physical activity can impact the experience.
The current study investigated the inﬂuence of a low-dose digital
mindfulness intervention on perceived exertion during a rowing task.
Additionally, it examined its impact on exercise enjoyment, remembered pleasure, and both state and trait mindfulness following the task.
Thirty-two participants (Mage = 21.09 ± 3.66) who met World Health
Organization physical activity guidelines and had no prior mindfulness
experience were randomly assigned to either the intervention group
(watching Headspace Guide to Meditation, N = 17) or the control group
(watching Wild Babies, N = 15). Both groups participated in a four-week
intervention, consisting of ten sessions of watching an episode followed
by a 25-minute rowing task. Results found a signiﬁcant decrease in
perceived exertion (p = .01), a signiﬁcant increase in exercise enjoyment
(p = .04), and remembered pleasure (p = .002) from the pre-assessment
to the post-assessment across both groups. While the mindfulness group
showed a greater increase in enjoyment (Δ = +10.76) compared to the
control group (Δ = -0.34), this difference was not statistically signiﬁcant
(p = .05). Mindfulness questionnaires revealed a signiﬁcant decrease in
trait mindfulness (p = .001) across both groups from pre-assessment to
post-assessment, with the control group reporting signiﬁcantly higher
levels of state mindfulness compared to the intervention (p = .04). These
ﬁndings suggest that exercise enjoyment can be inﬂuenced by digital
mindfulness content at a stronger effect, and that mindfulness questionnaires may not accurately reﬂect understanding and application of
mindfulness techniques. This research provides valuable insights for
exercise professionals, highlighting mindfulness as a tool for enhancing
exercise enjoyment. Further research should explore higher-dose interventions and the role of intrinsic motivation in exercise enjoyment.
Exercise addiction through the lens of freediving: A mixed-methods
pilot study
Tyler A. McClure, San Francisco State University; Noah D. Seto, San
Francisco State University; Jacob S. Dinardi, San Francisco State
University
Freedivers swim underwater while holding their breath, in contrast to
scuba divers who breathe using air tanks. Many freedivers report breathholding produces unusual sensations in the body and has additional
effects they describe as therapeutic, euphoric, and “like a drug,” which
prompted this investigation of exercise addiction (EA) within freediving.
Although EA may seem harmless, if individuals have difﬁculty regulating
their involvement with a behavior or activity — including sports and
exercise — it could result in interpersonal, professional, or physical harm.
Therefore, this pilot study sought to learn which aspects of freediving
individuals consider addictive and what their perspectives are on freediving as an addiction. Participants were recruited using snowball
methods from local and online freediving communities (n = 7, 2365 years). First, a content analysis of freediving media was performed.
Next, a questionnaire consisting of the Basic Psychological Needs
Satisfaction Scale, Behavioral Regulation in Sport Questionnaire, Brief
Cope Inventory, and Exercise Addiction Inventory was administered.
Finally, semi-structured interviews were conducted to gather divers’
qualitative accounts. Thematic analysis and descriptive statistics were
used to analyze the data. The content analysis revealed mainstream media
sources often discuss the extreme and risk-taking nature of freediving,
while in freediving groups, divers’ discussions typically center around
support and safety. The quantitative data showed participants were
intrinsically motivated with low levels of reward-seeking motivation,
while exhibiting high levels of problem-focused coping. The group
averaged under the threshold for risk of EA; however, two individuals
did exceed this threshold. Themes from the qualitative data spoke to the
participants’ motives, such as shaping their self-concept through freediving, forming important relationships, and enjoying unique beneﬁts
they receive nowhere else. As a whole, the data portrayed primarily
adaptive relationships with freediving, despite participants acknowledging it as a “positive addiction” that occasionally contributed to ﬁnancial
and interpersonal strains. Similar perspectives could be held by individuals outside of freediving, and may have broader implications in EA.
The results of this pilot study illustrate the need for larger, more focused
studies of EA, using mixed-methodology that gives context to the
quantitative data, and fosters a shared understanding of addiction among
investigators and active individuals.
When the spotlight is on you: Examining individual performance
situations in team sports
V. Vanessa Wergin, The University of Queensland; Andrea Schittenhelm,
Technical University of Munich; Svenja A. Wolf, Florida State University
In team sports, athletes typically rely on their teammates when performing, but certain situations require individual performance, such as penalty
kicks in soccer or free throws in basketball. Research shows that athletes
are more likely to choke under pressure in these individual situations
compared to situations where they perform as a collective (Jordet, 2009;
Jordet & Hartman, 2008). However, the causes behind these individual
underperformances have not been investigated. To understand the causes
and mechanisms behind choking in individual performance situations in
team sport, we interviewed N = 8 (Mage = 23.4 ± 3.1 years, 4 women, 4
men) team sport athletes about moments when they had to perform
individually for their team’s success (e.g., penalty shots, free throws,
serves). Our goal was to identify factors contributing to choking in these
high-pressure situations. Thematic Analysis revealed that athletes felt
more vulnerable to failure in individual performance situations than in
regular team play. The main reasons included heightened individual
responsibility, pressure to perform due to high expectations, the importance and non-repeatability of the game, and being the focal point of
attention. Additionally, choking-facilitating factors like self-doubt,
nervousness, disruption of routine or focus, and an anticipation of failure
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S51
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 55] ---
contributed to the choking experience. Athletes were more likely to choke
when they felt unsupported by teammates or coaches and experienced
more fear of negative judgement and consequences. Emotional responses
to failure depended on the team’s culture of failure. In teams with a
positive failure culture, where mistakes were viewed as part of the game,
players felt frustration or disappointment but were motivated to improve.
In teams with a negative failure culture, where mistakes were ignored or
penalized, players experienced emotions like self-blame, mistrust, and
insecurity, in addition to sadness and anger. Emotional regulation was
easier when athletes felt supported by their team. These ﬁndings underscore the importance of social support and a positive culture of failure in
reducing pressure and preventing choking in individual performance
situations in team sports. Practical recommendations include strengthening team support systems, fostering a healthy attitude toward mistakes,
and preparing athletes for high-pressure moments where they must
perform alone.
Sports team participation associates with better mental health among
trans and gender diverse adolescents, including in the context of biasbased bullying
Sarah M. Kaja, University of Minnesota; Amy L. Gower, University of
Minnesota; Benjamin Parchem, Lurie Children’s Hospital and Northwestern University; Samantha J. Adler, University of Minnesota; Jenifer K.
McGuire, University of Minnesota; G. Nic Rider, University of Minnesota;
Marla E. Eisenberg, University of Minnesota
Adolescents in the U.S. who are trans or gender diverse (TGD;
i.e., gender identity differs from social expectations for assigned sex)
are half as likely to play sports compared to cisgender peers (i.e., gender
identity matches expectations for assigned sex) and experience disproportionate mental health burdens. Multi-level barriers to sport especially affect TGD adolescents holding additional minoritized social positions like low socioeconomic status (SES). Sports team participation
could be a strategy for lowering their risks for depression. However, one
study found the protective effects of general physical activity for mental
health are weakened when TGD adolescents are bullied about their
gender. It is unclear whether protective effects of sport participation for
mental health hold when TGD adolescents are bullied. We explored
whether sport participation associated with elevated depressive symptom (EDS) risk among TGD adolescents, considering their additional
social positions and experiences of bullying. Adolescents (N = 10,545)
in eighth, ninth, or 11th grade self-reported: gender identity; past-month
experiences of bias-based bullying (BBB); sports team participation;
depressive symptoms in the past two weeks; SES; and racial/ethnic
identity. We used Exhaustive Chi-square Automatic Interaction Detections to identify groups of TGD adolescents with the highest prevalences of EDS by BBB, sports team participation, and social positions.
We used post-hoc tests to determine whether EDS prevalence differed
between TGD adolescents sharing social positions who 1) did not report
BBB, 2) played on a sports team, and 3) did not report BBB and played
on a sports team. Each group with high EDS prevalence reported BBB.
Four of seven high prevalence groups reported not playing on a sports
team. Prevalence of EDS was lower for adolescents sharing social
positions who did not experience BBB and who played sports. For
instance, among trans boys with moderate SES, EDS prevalence
(71.3%) was lower among adolescents who did not experience BBB
(49.9%), who played on a sports team (60.4%), and who did not
experience BBB and played on a sports team (34.3%). Decision-makers
should consider these ﬁndings when crafting gender- and sex-based
policies, given the potential beneﬁts of team sports for TGD adolescents. Funding source: NIH NCATS, NIMHD, Williams Institute and
Women’s Sports Foundation.
Within-day time varying associations between movement-related behaviors and affect in older adults
Brynn Hudgins, University of North Carolina Greensboro; Jaclyn Maher,
University of North Carolina Greensboro
Movement-related behaviors, such as moderate-to-vigorous physical
activity (MVPA) and sedentary behavior (SB), are thought to inﬂuence
physical feeling states (i.e., feelings of energy, fatigue) and affective
feeling states (i.e., happy, sad) in older adults. However, little is known
about how the relationship between movement-related behaviors and
physical and affective feeling states may ﬂuctuate over the course of the
day. This study used time-varying effect modeling to determine withinday changes in associations between movement-related behaviors and
subsequent physical and affective feeling states. Older adults (N = 202,
Mage = 69.9 years) completed a 4-day Ecological Momentary Assessment (EMA) protocol with 10, randomly-delivered, EMA prompts/day
on a loaned smartphone. Each prompt assessed feelings of energy and
fatigue and positive and negative affect using an adjective-based
approach. Participants wore two accelerometers during waking hours
to measure movement-related behaviors. Accelerometer-derived minutes of MVPA (i.e., ≥2020 counts/minute via Actigraph) and SB
(i.e., proprietary algorithms via ActivPAL) were captured in the 30
and 60 minutes before the prompt. Eight models were created to
determine associations between SB and MVPA and feelings of energy
and fatigue, and SB and MVPA and positive and negative affect over the
course of the day. All models controlled for grand-mean centered BMI
and age and sex. MVPA in the 30 and 60 minutes before the prompt was
positively associated with feelings of energy, between 7:00am to
12:00pm and again from 3:00pm to 11:00pm, but not at other times
during the day. Additionally, MVPA in the 30 and 60 minutes before the
prompt was negatively associated with feelings of fatigue between
8:00pm and 11:00pm. MVPA in the 30 and 60 minutes before the
prompt was not associated with positive or negative affect over the
course of the day. SB in the 30 and 60 minutes before the prompt was not
associated with feelings of energy or fatigue or positive or negative
affect over the course of the day. The results suggest that the strength of
the relationship between MVPA and feelings of energy and fatigue is
dynamic, changing over the course of the day.
For adolescents impacted by parental incarceration, playing on a
sports team associates with better educational engagement, less emotional distress, and less substance use
Sarah M. Kaja, University of Minnesota; Amy L. Gower, University of
Minnesota; Frederique Corcoran, University of Minnesota; Ingie Osman,
University of Minnesota; Elizabeth Shaver, University of Minnesota;
Samantha J. Adler, University of Minnesota; Rebecca J. Shlafer, University of Minnesota
The U.S. has the world’s highest adult incarceration rate. Millions of
American youth have had a parent arrested. Having a parent in jail or
prison is an adverse childhood experience (ACE) with developmental
consequences. Previous research suggests sports team participation rates
among adolescents impacted by parental incarceration (PI) are low,
though sports team participation seems to attenuate some risks that PI
and other ACEs present. This research is limited and often only gives
cursory attention to sport. Among adolescents who had experienced PI,
we 1) estimated the prevalence of sports team participation, 2) tested
whether prevalence of sports team participation differed by access to
resources (a proxy for socioeconomic status), and 3) examined associations between sports team participation and academic engagement,
emotional distress, and substance use. Eighth, ninth, and 11th grade
students in Minnesota (N = 13,423) self-reported: current or past PI;

absenteeism; depression, anxiety, and suicidality symptoms; and alcohol,
e-cigarette, and cannabis use. We examined frequencies and percentages
of all variables. Then, we used logistic regressions to test whether sports
team participation was signiﬁcantly associated with variables of interest,
controlling for grade, racial and ethnic identity, access to resources, and
whether PI was historical only or current. Thirty-eight percent of adolescents impacted by PI participated on a sports team. Sports team

suicide attempt [.70,.90] and e-cigarette [.82,.99] or cannabis use
[.74,.92]. Schools, community organizations, and philanthropic efforts
should emphasize and expand sports opportunities for adolescents affected by PI and work to limit barriers posed by access to ﬁnancial and
other resources. Funding source: NIH NCATS.
Exploring the developmental networks of professional women team
sport coaches
Rachael Manning, McGill University; Gordon Bloom, McGill University;
Bettina Callary, Cape Breton University; Lindsay Duncan, McGill
University
Despite the increase of elite women athletes over the last decade, the
number of women in coaching positions remains low. The underrepresentation of women in leadership and coaching roles has impacted the
amount of available women role models, decreasing opportunities for
younger, aspiring women in the coaching domain. The developmental
networks perspective offers an understanding of how multiple individuals may impact the personal and professional development of coaches
by considering the constellation of their developmental relationships.
As such, the purpose of this study was to explore the developmental
networks of elite women team sport coaches. Participants were six fulltime North American professional women coaches with an average of
14.2 years of coaching experience in three different team sports (both
men’s and women’s). Data were acquired through individual semistructured interviews with each participant. During the interview,
participants were asked to construct a visual representation of their
developmental network using a relational map, followed by a series of
questions relating to their map guided by the primary researcher. The
data were then analyzed using reﬂexive thematic analysis. The ﬁndings
indicated that coaches acquired a core network of developmental
relationships (e.g., coaches, family, management, mentors) that collectively contributed to their career growth and development as a coach.
Coaches described their networks as a critical part of their professional
development,
providing
them
with
a
foundation
of
career
(e.g., coaching knowledge and development, opportunities, modeling)
and psychosocial support (e.g., encouragement, trust). More speciﬁcally, coaches highlighted the value in the diversity of their networks,
describing how some networks provided them with emotional support,
while others provided insight to the high-performance sport environment. Taken together, the women coaches’ networks helped to alleviate
many of the challenges they faced in the industry and guided them in
their path to becoming a professional coach. These ﬁndings enhance our
understanding of the career experiences and development of women
coaches through the developmental network perspective, providing a
unique theoretical approach to coach learning and progression. Funding
source: SSHRC.
Persuasive messaging: A tool to overcome mental fatigue and enhance
physical activity planning? A HAPA-based investigation
Nicholas Santino, York University; Sepehr Rassi, York University; Sheereen Harris, University of Waterloo; Maggie Toplak, York University; Joe
Baker, University of Toronto; Rebecca Bassett-Gunter, York University
Although planning is often considered the gold-standard self-regulation
strategy to promote physical activity (PA), motivating PA planning can be
difﬁcult. Mental fatigue may further discourage planning by reducing
effort and increasing disengagement. Persuasive messaging could enhance
motivation for PA planning, yet its impact in the context of mental fatigue
remains unknown. Guided by the Health Action Process Approach
(HAPA), this study aimed to determine whether persuasive messages
would: a) inﬂuence the choice to engage in PA planning, and b) affect
psychosocial predictors of HAPA following a mentally fatiguing task. In
addition, this study examined whether changes in these predictors would
predict PA planning under conditions of mental fatigue. Participants
(N = 110) reported baseline mental fatigue and HAPA variables, completed
a mentally fatiguing task, and then reassessed their mental fatigue.
Participants were assigned to receive persuasive messages (intervention)
or not (control) before deciding whether to create a PA plan. HAPA
variables were then reassessed. Participants in the intervention group were
signiﬁcantly more likely to create a PA plan (χ2 = 29.57, df = 2, p < .001,
V = .52). Regarding changes in HAPA variables, a main effect for time
(F(5, 101) = 4.63, p < .001, Wilk’s Λ = .81, η2
p = .19), superseded by a
signiﬁcant time × group interaction (F(10, 204) = 1.95, p = .04, Wilk’s
Λ = 1.99, η2
p = .09), suggesting that psychosocial predictors of PA planning changed over time among participants in the intervention group. After
controlling for baseline scores, HAPA variables explained 23.7 (Cox &
Snell R2) - 31.9% (Nagelkerke R2) of the variance in choice to create a PA
plan (χ2 = 29.27, df = 5, p < .001). Persuasive messaging may be a useful
strategy for promoting PA planning under conditions of mental fatigue.
Future research should explore mechanisms through which messaging
inﬂuences the decision to plan for PA, the quality of the plan, and followthrough with intended PA.
Determinants of older adults’ sedentary behavior in the context of
everyday life: A dual process approach
Alexander Jochim, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Jeffrey D.
Labban, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; Brynn L. Hudgins, University
of North Carolina at Greensboro; Jaclyn P. Maher, University of North
Carolina at Greensboro
Older adults engage in high levels of sedentary behavior (SB). Yet, we lack
understanding of the reﬂective and automatic constructs that predict SB at
different stages of the behavior change continuum. Using a dual process
framework, this study aims to understand the reﬂective and automatic
determinants of SB in older adults’ everyday life. Older adults (≥60 years;
N = 202) participated in a 1-year measurement burst design ecological
momentary assessment (EMA) study with three 14-day waves of data
collection. Each wave, participants wore an accelerometer to measure SB
and completed a 4-day EMA protocol with 10 prompts per day to assess
behavior-speciﬁc reﬂective (i.e., intentions, self-efﬁcacy, plan speciﬁcity -
each to limit SB) and automatic determinants (i.e., affective states, physical
and social context, functional stability). SB reduction adopter and maintainer status (herein referred to as adopter or maintainer) was based on
baseline self-reported SB. Data were analyzed with a two-step multilevel
approach: ﬁrst predicting the likelihood that SB occurred in the 60 min
after the prompt; second, if SB did occur, predicting volume of SB in the
60 min following the prompt. Results predicting volume of SB are
reported. Signiﬁcance tests for maintainer coefﬁcients examined whether
relations differed from adopters. On occasions when SB occurred, adopters

Movement-mediated effects during learning in ADHD: Differences in
subtypes and severity levels
Beverly-Ann Hoy, Western University; Michelle Bi, Western University;
Matthew Lam, Western University; Androu Abdalmalak, Western University; Barbara Fenesi, Western University
Hyperactivity may play a functional role in promoting executive functioning among children with ADHD. This study aimed to elucidate the
neurocognitive impact of movement during executive functioning on
children with ADHD. Twenty-four children with and without ADHD
completed a Stroop task and self-efﬁcacy ratings while remaining stationary (Stationary condition) and while desk cycling (Movement condition).
ADHD subtype (e.g., inattentive, hyperactive-impulsive, combined) and
ADHD severity levels were measured using parent-reported diagnostic
scales. Simultaneous functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) recorded oxygenated and deoxygenated changes in hemoglobin within the
left dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (DLPFC). Among children with ADHD,
the Movement condition produced superior Stroop reaction time compared
to the Stationary condition (p = .046, d = 1.00). Self-efﬁcacy improved in
the Movement condition (p = .033, d = 0.41), whereas it did not in the
Stationary condition (p = .323). Seventy-eight percent of participants
showed greater oxygenation in the left DLPFC during the Movement
condition vs. the Stationary condition. Among children without ADHD,
there were no differences in Stroop or self-efﬁcacy outcomes between
Stationary and Movement conditions (p’s > .085, t’s < 1.45); 60% of
participants showed greater oxygenation in the left DLPFC during the
Movement vs. the Stationary condition. This work provides supportive
evidence that hyperactivity in ADHD may be a compensatory mechanism
to upregulate PFC hypoarousal to support executive functioning and selfefﬁcacy. Analysis of subtype and severity will further delineate moderating
effects of movement on executive functioning within the ADHD group.
Vigorous physical activity relates to maladaptive thought patterns in
college students
Madison Chandler, Elon University; Sean Godfrey, Elon University
Mental health concerns are especially prevalent in college students, with
recent reports suggesting that anxiety and depression are at all-time high
levels. Maladaptive thought patterns such as rumination, perseveration,
and worry are associated with higher levels of anxiety and depression.
Physical activity (PA), on the other hand, is generally associated with
lower levels of anxiety and depression symptoms. The relationship
between PA and maladaptive thought patterns has yet to be thoroughly
explored. Thus, the purpose of this investigation was to determine the
relationship between PA engagement and three maladaptive thought
patterns in college students. Participants in this cross-sectional study were
students at a mid-sized university (N = 133, Mage = 20.1 ± 1.3 years; 33.1%
males). Individuals completed an online survey battery comprising measures of demographics, PA engagement, rumination, perseveration, worry,
anxiety, and depression. Vigorous physical activity (VPA), but not
moderate physical activity or walking, was signiﬁcantly correlated with
all mental health variables such that higher levels of VPA (minutes/week)
were related to lower levels of rumination, perseveration, worry, anxiety,
and depression (rs’s ≤-.278, p’s < .05). Replicating past research, VPA
was a signiﬁcant predictor of both anxiety and depression: higher VPA
levels were associated with lower anxiety and depression scores (Anxiety:
β = -0.21, p = .02, R2adj = .04; Depression: β = -0.29, p = .001, R2adj =
.07). Also consistent with previous literature, rumination, perseveration,
and worry were each signiﬁcant predictors of anxiety and depression in
univariate models (Anxiety: β’s ≥0.66; p’s < .001, R2adj ≤.50; Depression: β’s ≥0.40; p’s < .001, R2adj ≤.58). Novel to the present investigation, in initial univariate models, VPA was a signiﬁcant predictor of
rumination, perseveration, and worry (β’s ≤-0.25, p’s < .001, R2adj ≤.09).
After controlling for gender, a signiﬁcant covariate, VPA remained a
signiﬁcant predictor of rumination and perseveration (β’s ≤-0.20, p’s <
.05, Model R2adj ≤.15). Results suggest that more VPA engagement
relates to lower levels of two maladaptive thought patterns: rumination and
perseveration. Because these thought patterns are related to mental health,
future investigations should determine whether the PA-maladaptive
thought pattern relationship could be leveraged in intervention studies to
mechanistically reduce anxiety and depression symptoms. Future research
should also explore whether this relationship is consistent across other
populations and/or age groups.
The inﬂuence of video projection and immersive virtual environments
on motivation and engagement
Jeremy Praski, University of Tennessee; Joei Velten, University of Tennessee; Jared Porter, University of Tennessee
Virtual reality (VR) potentially enhances motivation and engagement
when compared with traditional physical practice. To our knowledge, no
study has assessed whether a motor task performed in immersive compared
to non-immersive VR inﬂuences intrinsic motivation or engagement
during practice. Thus, the purposes of this study were to compare intrinsic
motivation and engagement when a motor skill is practiced in an immersive VR environment compared to a projected virtual environment. It
was hypothesized that immersive VR practice would lead to greater
intrinsic motivation and engagement following practice compared to
practicing the same task in a projected virtual environment. In this study,
volunteer performance and engagement were compared while completing
a stationary bicycling task in two conditions. Trials occurred on two
different days, at least 72 hours apart. One trial consisted of completing the
task using an Oculus VR headset; the other consisted of completing the
task in a projected virtual environment. Participants were randomly
assigned to either a VR group or projected virtual environment condition.
On the ﬁrst day, participants completed a Physical Activity Readiness
Questionnaire, Motion Sickness Susceptibility Questionnaire, and a pretest intrinsic motivation inventory (IMI). Participants then completed an
8000-meter stationary bike ride in one condition followed by an IMI and
engagement questionnaire. On the second day, participants returned to the
same location and completed the same pre-test IMI followed by the same
8000-meter stationary bike ride in the other counterbalanced condition,
and post-test questionnaire. A paired samples t-test for engagement post
participation revealed no signiﬁcant differences between conditions. The
IMI was administered pre and post participation for each condition. A 2
(pre/post) x 2 (condition) repeated measures ANOVA indicated a signiﬁcant difference (p = .023) and large effect size (η2 = .176) between the two
conditions. Six IMI subscales were examined indicating there were

and the value subscale (p > .05, η2 = .262). There were no differences on
the competency, effort, pressure, or choice subscales. Although the results
do not fully support our hypothesis, they suggest that intrinsic motivation
can be altered based on the immersion level of the virtual practice
environment.
Moral attitudes and behavior in adolescent basketball players: Exploring the inﬂuence of coaches and teammates
Celissa Pena, Texas State University; Lindsay Kipp, Texas State
University
Youth sports are often praised as a platform for fostering life skills and
moral character. However, the outcomes of participation depend heavily
on the environment of the sports programs. This study investigated the
relationships between social contextual factors, including sportspersonship
coaching behaviors and team norms, with athletes’ moral attitudes and
behaviors in 8-9th grade basketball. Grounded in the social learning
theory, the study focuses on a developmental stage critical for identity
and moral development. A total of 197 8-9th grade basketball tournament
participants completed a survey consisting of a modiﬁed version of the
Judgements about Moral Behavior in Youth Sport Questionnaire, featuring
basketball-speciﬁc scenarios: (a) elbowing an opponent, (b) tripping an
opponent, (c) cheating to maintain possession. Additional measures
included the Prosocial and Antisocial Behavior in Sport Scale and the
Sportsmanship Coaching Behavior Scale. The survey assessed perceived
legitimacy of and intention for unsportspersonlike actions (attitude),
teammate approval for unsportspersonlike actions, prosocial and antisocial
behaviors, and coaching behaviors related to sportspersonship. Multiple
regression analyses revealed that perceived teammate approval for unsportspersonlike behaviors positively predicted antisocial behaviors toward teammates and opponents and attitudes endorsing unsportspersonlike
actions (t = 4.80-10.69; p < .05). Notably, perceived teammate approval for
cheating was positively related to prosocial behaviors toward teammates
(t = 3.02; p < .05), which may point to the importance of loyalty to team
goals and norms. Athletes’ perceptions of coaches’ teaching good sportspersonship positively predicted prosocial behavior towards teammates
(t = 2.09; p < .05) and negatively predicted antisocial behaviors toward
opponents (t = -2.57; p < .05), and coaches’ modeling good sportspersonship negatively predicted antisocial behavior toward teammates and
opponents (t = -2.29 and -2.42; p < .05). Effect sizes were medium to large
(10-70% of variance explained). These ﬁndings point to how coach
behaviors and team norms collectively contribute to moral outcomes of
a critical developmental age group, highlighting direct instruction from
coaches and a positive team culture are essential in fostering respect and
integrity in youth sports. Funding source: Texas State University Graduate
College Thesis Research Support Fellowship – Fall (ST6624)
Differential links between mindfulness and interpersonal behaviors
toward teammates and opponents
Cheryl Stuntz, St. Lawrence University; Jack Russo, St. Lawrence
University
Mindfulness involves maintaining awareness of the present moment while
accepting thoughts and feelings as they arise. While mindfulness has been
linked to various positive outcomes, its associations with interpersonal
behaviors, particularly in sports, remain underexplored. Stuntz et al.
(2022) found that college athletes’ mindfulness was associated with
greater respect for rules, ofﬁcials, and social conventions, but not for
opponents. The current study extended prior research by (1) exploring
how different facets of mindfulness relate to behaviors toward teammates
and opponents and (2) examining whether compassion mediates the
relationship between mindfulness and interpersonal behaviors. We hypothesized that mindfulness would enhance compassion, which, in turn,
would promote prosocial and socially mindful behaviors and reduce
antisocial behaviors, with distinct patterns expected for teammates and
opponents. College athletes (N = 172) completed surveys assessing dispositional mindfulness, compassion, prosocial behaviors, antisocial behaviors, and social mindfulness toward teammates and opponents. Results revealed that athletes reported greater compassion toward teammates than opponents, were more likely to leave teammates with choices
than opponents and helped teammates more than opponents. Path models
analyzing the links among mindfulness facets, compassion, and interpersonal behaviors (i.e., prosocial, antisocial, and socially mindful behaviors) revealed distinct patterns for teammates and opponents. For
teammates, greater awareness of thoughts and sensations was associated
with increased compassion, which predicted more prosocial helping
behaviors and greater social mindfulness (i.e., leaving teammates with
choices). In addition, refocusing attention after mind wandering was
directly associated with more helping behaviors towards teammates – not
mediated by compassion. In contrast, for opponents, athletes judging their
thoughts as right or wrong (indicating lower mindfulness) was linked to
greater compassion, which, in turn, was associated with more prosocial
and socially mindful behaviors and fewer antisocial behaviors. These
ﬁndings suggest that different aspects of mindfulness shape interpersonal
behaviors for in-group teammates versus out-group opponents. Speciﬁcally, heightened awareness and stronger ability to refocus foster better
treatment of teammates, whereas stronger judgment fosters better treatment of opponents, with multiple paths operating through increased
compassion.
Parent and coach motivational climate proﬁles: Relationship with
adolescent athletes’ goal orientations, enjoyment, and stress
Stephanie L. K. Fuller, Utah State University; E. Whitney G. Moore, East
Carolina University; Christine M. Habeeb, East Carolina University;
Thomas D. Raedeke, East Carolina University
Parents and coaches play an important role in the quality of adolescent
sport experiences through the motivational climates they create. Although
both parents and coaches play independent roles, the way in which these
motivational climates simultaneously affect young athletes is unknown.
The ﬁrst purpose of this study was to identify proﬁles reﬂecting various
combinations of parent and coach motivational climates using latent proﬁle
analysis. The second purpose was to examine proﬁle differences in
athletes’ goal orientations, stress, and enjoyment. Adolescent athletes
(N = 347, Mage = 12.47 years) completed questionnaires assessing perceived parent and coach motivational climate, goal orientations, stress,
and enjoyment. Overall, athletes perceived moderate-to-high mastery
climates and moderate-to-low performance climates, as well as complementary climates from parents and coaches. Furthermore, athletes reported
high task orientations, moderate ego orientations, moderate stress, and
high enjoyment. Latent proﬁle analyses identiﬁed ﬁve proﬁles based on
mastery and performance climates initiated by parents and coaches.
Athletes who perceived a mastery climate from both parents and coaches
reported higher task orientations and greater enjoyment than athletes who
perceived a performance climate. Athletes who perceived a performance
climate from both parents and coaches reported higher ego orientations and
greater stress than those who did not. Athletes who perceived a climate that
was less achievement driven (moderate mastery and performance climates
from both parents and coaches) reported moderate task/ego orientation and
stress, and lower enjoyment than all other athletes. Finally, athletes who
reported high mastery and performance climates from both parents and
coaches reported high task and ego orientations. These athletes found their
sport experiences to be both enjoyable and stressful. This research ﬁlls a

organizations should provide training for parents and coaches alike,
emphasizing the importance of fostering mastery climates. In this way,
they can facilitate positive sport experiences that are enjoyable and
minimize stress, which ultimately may lead to positive youth development
and sport retention.
How motives moderate spontaneous reactions to non-verbal dominance cues: Recording facial EMG in a soccer penalty scenario
Florian Müller, Friedrich Schiller University Jena; Sophia Zöller, Friedrich Schiller University Jena; Rouwen Canal-Bruland, Friedrich Schiller
University Jena
Body language is an important source of information in competitive
settings. For instance, soccer penalty takers’ dominant vs. submissive
body language affects keepers’ expectancies of success (Furley et al.,
2012) and anticipation performance (Bijlstra et al., 2020). However, less is
known about individual differences in reactions to body language. Following motive disposition theory (McClelland et al., 1989), individuals
with a strong implicit afﬁliation or power motive should be especially
sensitive to such changes because non-verbal signals of dominance or
rejection carry increased incentive value to them. In the current study 40
male participants (Age: Mage = 23.5, SD = 3.4) performed a series of
penalties at a virtual soccer goal. In each trial a goalkeeper entered the
goal and took position. Body language was manipulated between submissive and dominant behavior within participants. In advance, participants’
motives (MMG, UMS) were assessed. To test the motive speciﬁcity of
effects, Big-5 (BFI-K), and chronic regulatory focus (RFQ) were collected
additionally. During each trial both shot placement and EMG activity of
corrugator supercilii and zygomaticus major were recorded, as these
muscles indicate spontaneous affective appraisals (Dimberg & Karlsson,
1997). Dominant body language elicited stronger corrugator activity than
submissive body language, whereas no effects were found for the zygomaticus, F(1, 41) = 5.32, p = .03. Most importantly, the stronger participants’ implicit afﬁliation motive, the stronger their corrugator activation in
reaction to dominant compared to submissive goalkeepers (hope for
afﬁliation: r = .28, p = .04; fear of rejection: r = .33, p = .02). In contrast,
no such relationships emerged for their explicit afﬁliation motive, or their
power motive. The only other personality measure related to corrugator
activity was agreeableness, with higher levels yielding reduced increases in
corrugator activity following dominant vs. submissive keepers. In contrast
to EMG activity, shot placement was not inﬂuenced by goalkeepers body
language. Those ﬁndings illustrate that differences in sensitivity to nonverbal signals such as body language may be explained by individuals’
personality. We discuss implications for future research and applied
settings.
Developing 3D dose-response proﬁles to identify critical windows for
the stimulative effects of training load on psychological variables
across a soccer season
Yannick Hill, Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam; Paula Carmona, Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam
In the domain of sport science, the functional dynamics between
training load and physical development have been well established for
many years. For example, to stimulate muscle growth, training load
should not be too small to provide sufﬁcient stimulation, but also not too
large to avoid injuries. This pattern also follows classical psychological
theories on the stimulative effects of arousal doses like the inverted-U
hypothesis or the individual zones of optimal functioning. However,
these theories lack rigorous empirical testing. Therefore, the ﬁrst aim of
this study was to establish so-called dose-response proﬁles to assess the
relationship between training load and psychological states (i.e., selfefﬁcacy, perceived performance, and mood). Given that training progression necessitates changes in training load, the second aim was to
examine whether these dynamics remain stable or change over the
course of a season. To test these aims, we collected data from 10 players
of the U23 team of a Dutch elite soccer club over the course of one full
season. Players completed a daily questionnaire before and after each
training session and match, rating their perceived exertion (RPE) and
responding to single-question items for each psychological variable. We
calculated the internal load (RPE x duration) for each session and
created personalized 3D dose-response proﬁles by mapping each psychological variable as a function of internal load and time. The 3D doseresponse proﬁles were divided into four periods of equal duration across
the season, allowing us to identify critical windows where the stimulative effects of training load on psychological states change. Our results
show that each player exhibits unique stimulative patterns, and these
patterns change over the course of the season. The 3D dose-response
proﬁles reveal that the same training load can have different effects on
different players and at different times, highlighting the importance of
considering individual differences and temporal dynamics in training
program design. To conclude, the ﬁndings suggest that a one-size-ﬁtsall approach to training may not be effective in optimizing psychological development. Instead, coaches should consider using personalized
and dynamic training programs that account for individual differences
and temporal changes in psychological states.
The impact of athlete status and sex on academic performance
following a concussion
Olivia Spagnoli, Brock University; Philip Sullivan, Brock University
A concussion is classiﬁed as a type of traumatic brain injury and is a major
concern among university student-athletes, as repeated impacts to the
brain can result in decreased cognitive function (Holmes et al., 2020; Hall
et al., 2015). Additionally, negative emotional responses and reduced
academic performance are challenges that can arise in university-level
athletes when suffering from a concussion (Andre-Morin et al., 2017).
Furthermore, previous research has shown that concussions may impact
both academic and athletic performance (Neelakantan et al., 2020).
However, while concussion-related cognitive impairments are wellknown, the intersection of athlete status (varsity athlete vs. non-athlete)
and sex (male vs. female) on the academic challenges faced following a
concussion has not been thoroughly explored. To ﬁll this gap, the current
study investigates the intersection between athlete status and sex as
factors inﬂuencing academic performance in individuals who have
experienced a concussion. Participants were university students and
student-athlete respondents from the Spring 2022 American College
Health Association (ACHA) National College Health Assessment
(NCHA) survey data. The sample included 7,236 female and 2,559 male
university students, with 347 varsity athletes and 9,448 non-athletes.
Included in the analysis was a survey question asking if they perceived
that a concussion or traumatic brain injury was an impediment to their
academic performance within the last 12 months. Results showed a
signiﬁcant interaction between athlete status and sex on academic performance in those who have suffered a concussion or traumatic brain
injury, with female student-athletes facing signiﬁcantly greater academic
challenges than male student-athletes. This sex difference was also
signiﬁcantly larger among student-athletes compared to non-athletes. By
examining the interaction between athlete status and sex on academic
performance, this study addresses a gap in understanding the factors
inﬂuencing academic success in student-athletes who have suffered

athletic departments about the need for sex-speciﬁc concussion treatment

Active esports participation among people with physical disabilities: A
systematic review
Celina H. Shirazipour, Cedars-Sinai Medical Center; Nadia Huynh,
Cedars-Sinai Medical Center; Ciera Torney, University of Southern
California; Tyra Nguyen, Cedars-Sinai Medical Center; Zed Pitts, Community Partner; Andrew D. Shuttleworth, Community Partner; Andrew
Perrin, Community Partner; Sarah-Jeanne Salvy, Cedars-Sinai Medical
Center; Kathleen A. Martin-Ginis, University of British Columbia Okanagan; Amy E. Latimer-Cheung, Queen’s University; Josh Boggi, Invictus
Games Foundation; Samuel Newell, Invictus Games Foundation
Critical participation barriers inﬂuence the availability and accessibility
of adapted sport programming. Active esports, in which a participant
physically moves and competes in sport as part of a virtual community,
may provide an avenue for overcoming these barriers. The purpose of
the current study was to conduct a systematic review of active esports
literature focused on people with physical disabilities to examine
existing evidence and identify directions for future research on active
esports as a means to promote adapted sport participation. Following a
systematic search and screening process, 12 studies met inclusion
criteria. The majority of studies were observational (n = 8), quantitative
(n = 9), focused on adults (n = 7), and recruited participants with diverse
types of disabilities (n = 5). Furthermore, most studies (n = 10) examined participation through commercially available games (e.g., Wii,
PlayStation, Xbox). All studies demonstrated positive participant experiences, the potential of active esports to motivate sport participation,
and/or promote light to moderate intensity physical activity among
adults and youth with physical disabilities. While informative, there are
clear gaps in the research. First, there is a need for consistent use of
terminology relating to active esports. A key reason for study exclusion
was not meeting deﬁnitional criteria for active esports or not providing
clarity regarding the nature of the physical activity. As active esports
grow, clarity in terminology is important for appropriately recognizing
what constitutes sport participation as opposed to exercise, rehabilitation, or sedentary esports. Second, as technology changes and the
options for active esports continue to grow, research must expand
beyond traditional platforms. Research gaps currently include evaluations of active esport participation in virtual active esport leagues and
competitions. Third, more interventional and mechanistic research is
required to understand how to motivate active esport participation, as
well as develop and deliver active esports programming that promotes
fulﬁlling and equitable competitive participation experiences for people
with physical disabilities. Esports, including active esports, are the
world’s fastest growing sport. It is critical that sport psychology
research matches this development so as to ensure the full and equitable
participation of people with physical disabilities. Funding source:
Eunice Kennedy Shriver National Institute of Child Health and Human
Development (R03HD111712)
Physical activity and active recreation practices of LGBTQIA+ individuals in regional Australia: A cross-sectional analysis of 17,401
residents
Chathurani De Silva, Deakin University; Emiliano Mazzoli, Deakin
University; Nicola D. Ridgers, University of South Australia; Lisa M.
Barnett, Deakin University
LGBTQIA+ individuals (Lesbian, Gay, Bisexual, Transgender, Queer,
Intersex, Asexual, and others) face additional stresses due to being
marginalized by society, which can negatively affect their physical and
mental health. According to Meyer’s 2003 minority stress model, these
stressors contribute to poorer health outcomes and can result in lower
physical activity (PA) levels. Studies show that regional populations face
greater barriers to PA than those in metropolitan cities. Existing research
has primarily focused on LGBTQIA+ individuals in metropolitan areas
and on sports rather than active recreation. This study explores PA levels
and active recreation of LGBTQIA+ adults in regional Australia. Residents
of one regional catchment incorporating six local government areas
responded to an Active Living Census survey in May/June 2019. Our
analysis included respondents 16+ years who indicated whether (or not)
they identiﬁed as LGBTQIA+ and completed at least one PA item. A
logistic regression determined the likelihood of LGBTQIA+ residents
meeting PA guidelines (i.e., 150-min of moderate- to vigorous-intensity
PA or 75-min of vigorous-intensity PA per week) compared to nonLGBTQIA+ residents (adjusted for confounders). The frequency of different PA types and barriers was compared for LGBTQIA+ residents and
non-LGBTQIA+ respondents using chi-square tests. Of the 17,401 eligible
respondents, 591 identiﬁed as LGBTQIA+ (3.4%). LGBTQIA+ residents
were 21% less likely to meet PA guidelines (OR = 0.78, 95% CI [0.66,
0.94], p = .007) compared to non-LGBTQIA+ residents. All residents
listed walking as their preferred activity, with no group differences
(LGBTQIA+ = 57.9%; non-LGBTQIA+ = 58.1%). Modiﬁable PA barriers, including lack of support (27.25% vs 17.65%) and cost (24.57%
vs 20.08%), were signiﬁcantly higher among LGBTQIA+ respondents,
despite having more awareness about activities available (15.09% vs
10.29%). The 2019 ALC, a large novel regional survey, found fewer
LGBTQIA+ residents met the PA guidelines compared to non-LGBTQIA
+ residents, which supports existing data mainly from metropolitan locations. Research suggests that creating more welcoming environments with
inclusive signage and policies and offering different memberships can
improve PA in this population. Funding source: Australian Government
HDR Scholarship.
A systematic review of correlates of physical activity in the LGBTQIA
+ community
Chathurani De Silva, Deakin University; Niamh O’Loughlin, Deakin
University; Emiliano Mazzoli, Deakin University; Nicola D Ridgers,
University of South Australia; Lisa M Barnett, Deakin University
LGBTQIA+ individuals (Lesbian, Gay, Bisexual, Transgender, Queer,
Intersex, Asexual, and other sexually or gender-diverse people) are at
risk of poorer health outcomes and lower physical activity (PA).
Understanding PA correlates is crucial for designing targeted interventions. This review is the ﬁrst to synthesize the factors that facilitate or
hinder PA in LGBTQIA+ individuals. Seven online databases were
searched from inception until April 2024 to identify studies that
reported on the association between potential correlates and the PA of
LGBTQIA+ individuals (≥16 years old). Correlates were categorized
using the socio-ecological model – i.e., intrapersonal (including sexuality and gender identity), interpersonal, organizational, and environmental. The percentage of reported tests for each identiﬁed correlate
were classiﬁed as no association (‘0’ 0-33%), inconsistent association
(‘?’ 34-59%), positive association (‘+’ 60-100%) or negative association (‘–’60-100%). When four or more studies indicated an association,
it was coded as ++, –, or ??, to represent the strength of the association.
The search resulted in 10,299 articles, of which 67 studies were
included. Fifty-seven correlates were identiﬁed from these studies.
Twenty-two studies compared lesbians to heterosexual women, while
transgender individuals were included in eight studies. Forty correlates
were investigated at the intrapersonal level. Of these, eight were
investigated by four or more studies. For LGBTQIA+, there was a

age and education had no association with PA. Fifteen interpersonal
