Body emotions, social comparisons, and attunement with exercise: A
three-sample replication study
Samantha J. Adler, Western University; Kristen M. Lucibello, Western
University; Eva Pila, Western University
Attunement with exercise (AWE) reﬂects a mind-body connection during
movement, thought to protect against maladaptive body-related behaviors
and represent shifts away from dysfunctional exercise. This novel construct is particularly important to examine among young populations who
are more susceptible to negative body emotions and social comparisons,
which have been associated with dysfunctional exercise and less (positive)
exercise experiences. To promote adaptive and pleasurable exercise
engagement, we need greater empirical understanding of whether body
emotions and social comparisons thwart AWE. Thus, the purpose of this
study was to examine associations between negative body-related emotions, social comparisons, and AWE among three student samples. We
hypothesized greater body shame, guilt, and body- and exercise-based
comparisons at timepoint 1 (T1) would predict lower AWE at timepoint 3
(T3). Samples were predominantly women and White undergraduate
students recruited from a university Kinesiology course in Fall 2020
(N = 180, Mage = 20.2 ± 1.9 years), 2021 (N = 141, Mage = 20.1 ± 1.6 years),
and 2022 (N = 182, Mage = 20.3 ± 1.6 years). Via a prospective, multi-wave
design, students completed three online surveys over a three-week (Studies 1
& 2) or three-month (Study 3) period. Single items assessed body shame and
guilt, while validated scales captured body and exercise comparisons and
AWE. Controlling for race and gender, Study 1, 2, and 3’s linear regression
models explained 39.2%, 23.4%, and 30.1% of the variance in AWE
(p < .001), respectively. Men reported lower AWE than women across all
studies (β1 = -.32, p1 < .001; β2 = -.34, p2 = .004; β3 = -.35, p3 = .002). Higher
T1 shame was associated with signiﬁcantly lower T3 AWE in Study 1 (β1 = -
.22, p1 < .001) and Study 3 (β3 = -.19, p3 = .001). Higher T1 exercise comparison was signiﬁcantly associated with lower T3 AWE in all studies (β1 = -
.17, p1 < .001; β2 = -.13, p2 = .005; β3 = -.13, p3 = .002). This study addressed
scholarly calls for replication research, enhancing conﬁdence in our preliminary insights. Future research should include more diverse samples when
exploring factors that inﬂuence AWE and subsequent strategies for fostering AWE.
Development of educational resources for social support in adult
physical activity: Feedback from ﬁtness professionals and participants
Bobbie-Ann P. Craig, University of Calgary; Vanessa Paglione, University of Calgary; Kaitlyn Riddell, University of Calgary; Meghan H.
McDonough, University of Calgary; Chloé Drapeau, Université de Montréal; Kristen M. Lucibello, University of Toronto; Corentin Montiel,
Université de Montréal; Regan Thompson, The University of British
Columbia; Erica Bennett, The University of British Columbia; Isabelle
Doré, Université de Montréal; Catherine M. Sabiston, University of
Toronto; S. Nicole Culos-Reed, University of Calgary; Cari Din, University of Calgary; Jennifer Hewson, University of Calgary; Sarah J. Kenny,
University of Calgary; Stephanie Won, City of Calgary; Chantelle Zimmer, University of Calgary; Ann Toohey, University of Calgary; Krista
White, University Heights Dance Program; Amanda Wurz, University of
the Fraser Valley
Social support can enhance physical activity for adults by promoting
connection, belonging, motivation, and adherence. We conducted two
reviews of the literature and identiﬁed nine categories of social support
strategies within adult physical activity settings: feeling welcomed and
included, making physical activity fun, modelling physical activity, providing information, encouragement, mastery feedback, autonomy support,
emotional support, and fostering social connections. We then created a
plan for a training module based on those categories. The objective of this
study was to obtain feedback from those involved in adult physical activity
classes about the development of social support strategies into educational
resources for ﬁtness professionals. Focus groups were conducted with
ﬁtness professionals (n = 25) and adult physical activity participants
(n = 25) across community programs in Calgary, Vancouver, Montreal,
and Toronto. Participants provided feedback on the social support strategies included in the training module plan and ways to make them
acceptable and appealing to ﬁtness professionals, and suggestions for the
development, format, and delivery of the ﬁnal educational resources. This
abstract focuses on results related to considerations for the format, delivery
mode, implementation, and relevance of the social support resources.
Themes centered on recommendations for training ﬁtness professionals
about providing social support in the physical activity context: (1) creating
resources that are clear, comprehensive, and accessible; (2) integrating
active learning through shadowing and modelling, self-reﬂection, and
elements/examples tailored to the learner’s role, physical activity type,
and participant needs; (3) developing practical and actionable resources by
including tangible examples and social support strategies, and when to use
them; and (4) considerations for adapting the resources to different
experience levels, physical activity types, and populations. Our ﬁndings
provide practical recommendations for delivering social support education
in adult physical activity that are accessible and tailored to various ﬁtness
professionals, with actionable elements applicable to their roles that can be
implemented in an adult physical activity setting. Funding source: Social
Sciences
and
Humanities
Research
Council
Partnership
Development Grant.
Cross-lagged associations between social connections and physical
activity in older adults: Understanding synergies and direction of
effects
Sarah A Stephen, University of Calgary; Meghan H McDonough, University of Calgary; Chantelle Zimmer, University of Calgary
Regular physical activity (PA) can bring signiﬁcant health beneﬁts to
people of all ages and can extend years of active independent living and
improve quality of life for older adults. Physical activity and social
connections may also have positive effects on each other, as social
connections can be a source of support for participating in physical
activity, and physical activity contexts can be a source of social connections for older adults. However, there is a lack of longitudinal data that
examine whether these associations are signiﬁcant prospectively. Understanding the long-term relationship between physical activity and social
connections in older adults is essential as results can inform interventions
aimed at promoting both physical health and social well-being. Therefore,
the purpose of this study was to examine (1) whether nine social connection factors (e.g., social network, social contact, dimensions of social
support) predict PA prospectively; and (2) whether PA prospectively

predicts social connection factors. Using data from the Canadian Longitudinal Study on Aging (CLSA) we examined these questions with 14,008
adults who were aged 65 and older (M = 73.02) across two time points
approximately three years apart. To test prospective longitudinal effects
between study variables, a cross-lagged path model was used. Fit for the
ﬁnal model was excellent, CFI = .955, SRMR = .085, and RMSEA = .062.
Results demonstrated that PA at Time 2 was signiﬁcantly predicted by
social participation (β = .04, p < .001), marital status (β = .04, p < .001),
and social network at Time 1 (β = .06, p < .001). Additionally, PA at Time
1 signiﬁcantly predicted all social connection factors (social support
dimensions, social networks, social participation, online communication,
and frequency of social contact; β = .03 - .07, p < .001). In conclusion,
results from this study can inform the development of strategies to enhance
social connectedness and physical activity in older adults, with an emphasis on promoting social participation and strengthening social networks
within this population. Funding source: Canadian Institutes for Health

Social identiﬁcation and relational efﬁcacy: An experimental examination of performance in low self-efﬁcacy contexts
Sarah A Stephen, University of Calgary; Christine M Habeeb, East
Carolina University; Pete Coffee, Heriot-Watt University; Patrick Rider,
East Carolina University
Researchers have provided evidence in support of better performance
outcomes when efﬁcacy beliefs are similar (congruent). However,
athletes often have different efﬁcacy beliefs than their teammates. Lent
and Lopez (2002) suggest that in certain conditions, high other-efﬁcacy
(belief in a teammate’s abilities) may compensate for low self-efﬁcacy
(belief in one’s own abilities) and therefore may reduce negative effects
on performance. The purpose of this study was to investigate the role of
social identity with a teammate in instances of incongruent (high otherefﬁcacy and low self-efﬁcacy) beliefs. Forty individuals participated in
a tug-of-war style task that required them to pull on a rope as hard as
possible for 5 seconds with a research actor. Participants were randomly
assigned to either a high or low social identity condition. The experiment consisted of three trials of rope-pulling: an individual practice
(Trial 1), a team practice (Trial 2), and a team competition. Efﬁcacy
beliefs were manipulated via bogus feedback after Trial 2. Objective
performance was assessed using force measures and EMG signals from
participants’ muscles. Self-efﬁcacy and other-efﬁcacy beliefs were
assessed using questionnaires. Results indicated that for participants
in the high social identity condition, self-efﬁcacy was signiﬁcantly
higher prior to Trial 3 than prior to Trial 1 (MΔ = 8.43), p = .003,
d = .71, but no different prior to Trial 2 (MΔ = 0.01), p = .675. In the low
identity condition, self-efﬁcacy was signiﬁcantly lower prior to Trial 3
(after bogus feedback) compared to prior to Trial 2 (MΔ = 5.68),
p = .046, d = .44. Further, participants in the high identity condition,
had higher mean force pulled and max force pulled on the rope in Trial 3
(Mmean = 29.29, Mmax = 53.30) compared to Trial 2 (Mmean = 26.29,
Mmax = 49.23) while the low identity condition displayed no differences. Finally, mean forearm activation was higher during Trial 3 than
Trial 2 (MΔ = 3.8), p = .043, d = .46 for high identity participants. In
conclusion, results indicate that the negative consequences of incongruent efﬁcacy beliefs on performance may be negated by high levels of
social identity.
Breaking barriers: Insights into physical activity participation among
community-dwelling older adults through the theory of planned
behavior and participatory approaches
Gunay Yildizer, Eskisehir Tech. Uni; Dilara Ebru Ucar, Anadolu
University
Developing effective physical activity interventions for community-dwelling older adults is essential to address the unique barriers they face and
enhance their physical and mental health. Such interventions can promote
independence and reduce the risk of chronic diseases and functional
decline, thereby improving their overall quality of life. Exploratory studies
are important in behavior change interventions designed using the Theory
of Planned Behavior (TPB) to provide a foundation for action plans, obtain
insights, and enhance the effectiveness of intervention strategies. The
research underscores the importance of exploratory studies as a prerequisite for examining physical activity behavior within the TPB framework.
The TPB and participatory approaches synergize effectively in research by
combining TPB’s structured identiﬁcation of behavioral determinants
(attitude, subjective norms, and perceived behavioral control) with the
contextual relevance and empowerment fostered through stakeholder
engagement. Building on this theoretical foundation, this study aimed to
explore these dynamics in practice with 72 community-dwelling older
adults, aged between 65 and 77, who were willing to participate in a
physical activity program. As part of the study, six open-ended questions,
previously used in related research, were presented to the participants, and
they were asked to respond without any restrictions on word count or
space. These questions focused on the themes of attitude toward behavior,
social norms associated with the behavior, and perceived control over the
behavior, which represent the three main components of the TPB. The
responses were categorized into themes, and frequency analyses were
performed for each category. Content analysis revealed several key
themes. Attitudes were shaped by perceived health beneﬁts, such as
managing chronic conditions, maintaining independence, and improving
well-being, alongside concerns about injury and fatigue. Social norms
highlighted the supportive inﬂuence of family, healthcare providers, and
peers, while barriers included social isolation and limited supportive
networks. Perceived control factors emphasized logistical challenges,
including facility accessibility, transportation difﬁculties, ﬁnancial constraints, and a lack of age-appropriate programs. These ﬁndings provide
valuable insights into the facilitators and barriers to physical activity
among older adults, offering a strong foundation for designing tailored
interventions to enhance participation and improve health outcomes in this
population.
Game, set, match: Coaching practices of some of the world’s best
professional tennis coaches
Hugh Clarke, McGill University; Marc Glaude, McGill University; Gordon A. Bloom, McGill University
Tennis ranks among the most widely followed sports globally, with a
fanbase exceeding one billion and media revenues approaching $700
million. However, professional tennis players and coaches face distinctive
challenges inherent to the sport, including extensive travel, social isolation,
and an income structure heavily reliant on performance outcomes. Although there is a large body of literature dedicated to effective coaching
across different contexts (i.e., sport type, geography), little research has
speciﬁcally explored coaching within the professional tennis environment.
Therefore, the purpose of this study was to explore professional tennis
coaches’ perspectives of their unique coaching context, including how this
context inﬂuenced their coaching practices. We conducted individual
semi-structured interviews with ﬁve professional tennis coaches, each of
whom was coaching a top 20 ranked player at the time of the study. On
average, these coaches had over 15 years of tour coaching experience and
had collectively guided male and female players to win four singles grand
slam titles and one grand slam doubles title. Furthermore, this group of
coaches had achieved more than 15 ATP titles with their current players.
Our reﬂexive thematic analysis highlighted coaches’ perceptions of a
unique power-dynamic in which their player was their employer,
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025

FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS

potentially affecting the style and content of their coaching feedback.
Coaches also emphasized the demanding nature of the tour on players,
highlighting the importance of providing social support while also recognizing the importance of taking time apart to avoid relationship burnout.
Furthermore, participants underlined the challenges incoming coaches face
in learning to navigate this unique context, noting that the sport’s competitive nature often makes established coaches hesitant to provide mentorship. Overall, this study is among the ﬁrst to present qualitative data from
an elite sample of professional tennis coaches who shared their perceptions
of coaching within this distinctive coaching context. The ﬁndings provide
valuable information to current and prospective professional individualsport coaches by highlighting how some of the world’s best tennis coaches
navigate their environment, offering coaching strategies to enhance their
coaching effectiveness and success.
A scoping review of antecedents of athlete social identity in youth sport
Justin T. Worley, Utah State University; Alan L. Smith, Utah State
University
Athlete social identity is a multidimensional aspect of an individual’s selfconcept including the subjective importance of being a sport team member
(cognitive centrality), positive feelings about one’s sport team membership
(ingroup affect), and perceived belonging with other sport team members
(ingroup ties; Bruner & Benson, 2018; Cameron, 2004). Because athlete
social identity is identiﬁed as a positive contributor to adaptive youth sport
experiences (Haslam et al., 2020), there is value in understanding antecedents of this construct. This can identify areas of promise for future
research and practice. A scoping review of literature was conducted
accordingly, summarizing antecedents of athlete social identity as well
as the theoretical frameworks and measurement tools used in this research.
Studies were included in the review regardless of participant ages, though
results were organized to delineate trends speciﬁc to youth-based work. A
database (ProQuest Dissertation and Theses Global, PsycINFO, Scopus,
SPORTDiscus) and manual search strategy yielded a ﬁnal sample of 55
articles detailing 60 total studies. Most studies were quantitative (83%),
cross-sectional (58%), and conducted with adult or mixed adult/youth
samples (70%). Antecedents of athlete social identity were consistent
across age groups and organized into seven categories including leadership
factors, environmental factors, personal factors, team factors, interventions, moral factors, and interpersonal factors. Social identity theory and
self-categorization theory (Tajfel & Turner, 1979; Turner et al., 1987), and
Cameron’s (2004) model of social identity, were predominant theories
used in this research. Measurement practices varied, with most studies
using adapted survey items from previous social identity measures with
Likert-type response options. Findings suggested that future research could
beneﬁt from grounding in theoretically and developmentally informed
frameworks, more rigorous measurement and study design, and examining
a broader span of potential antecedents of athlete social identity in youth
sport. For example, researchers could explore whether athlete social
identity varies as a function of normative age-related changes in cognitive
development, along with other physical and social developmental variables of interest. Also, because most studies focused on factors believed to
foster stronger athlete social identity, research on factors inversely linked
to athlete social identity is needed to address maladaptive youth sport
experiences. Funding source: Utah State University Graduate Student
Research Award.
Strengthening
social
competence:
Developing
peer
interactions
through a physical education (PE) intervention
Iris Schüller, Technical University of Munich
Social competence is crucial for children’s development into responsible, socially adapted adults, beneﬁting both individual and societal
needs (Han & Kemple, 2006). This study investigated the effects of a
structured intervention in physical education (PE) on social interaction
among
adolescents.
Seventy-nine
eighth-grade
students
(Mage =
13.45 ± 0.38 years) from a German secondary school participated in
gender-segregated classes. They were randomly assigned to an intervention group (n = 42) or a control group (n = 37). The intervention was
based on Kanning’s Concept of Social Competence (2002) and developed using the Intervention Mapping Approach (Bartholomew et al.,
2016). It aimed to enhance perceptive-cognitive, motivational-emotional, and behavioral dimensions of social competence, using classroom
management techniques, cognitive activities on social behaviour and
cooperative learning approaches. Social competence was evaluated
using self-reports, observations, and peer and teacher evaluations of
behavior. Results showed mixed outcomes. Perceived importance of
social competence increased over time for all participants (F(2,
132) = 3.82, p = .02, η2
p = .05). Additionally, the intervention group
demonstrated an increased use of perceptive-cognitive dimensions in
deﬁning social competence (IG: Pre = 30.5%; Post = 34.2%; Followup = 37.1%) compared to the control group (CG: Pre = 28.1%; Post =
28.1%; Follow-up = 29.8%). Moreover, signiﬁcant effects over time
were observed for popularity votes (non-reciprocated social ties): a)
Success-oriented (i.e., peers want to play with students to win a game):
F(2, 126) = 7.32, p < .001, η2
p = .104; b) Emotion-oriented (i.e., peers
perceive students as good team players): F(2, 126) = 14.13, p < .001,
η2
p = .183; c) Leisure-oriented (i.e., peers want to include students in
after-school activities): F(2, 126) = 6.29, p = .002, η2
p = .091. Emotionoriented reciprocity ties (i.e. both parties identiﬁed the social tie as
equally
beneﬁcial)
also
showed
signiﬁcant
changes:
F(1.7,
107.5) = 10.99, p < .001, η2
p = .148. These results indicate that PE
generally enhances social competence and interaction in adolescents
over time, with the intervention speciﬁcally improving cognitive-perceptive aspects. This has implications for future PE programs fostering
both physical and social development, particularly targeting cognitiveperceptive skills. Further research should investigate the long-term
impacts of such interventions on social competence and physical
activity engagement.
Do I matter? Development of the Mattering Index for Sport
Matt D. Hoffmann, California State University, Fullerton; Zachary Findlay, California State University, Fullerton; Kathleen S. Wilson, California State University, Fullerton; Jingwen Liu, California State University,
Fullerton
Mattering is deﬁned as the personal sense of feeling signiﬁcant in the eyes
of others and is considered a key psychological resource central to the
human condition. Though mattering has been investigated in various
contexts (e.g., workplace, education), it has been relatively neglected as
a psychological construct relative to similar but conceptually distinct
constructs like belongingness and social support. Apart from research
showing that mattering predicted well-being among youth athletes, we are
not aware of any published studies exploring mattering in the sport
psychology literature. The purpose of our study was twofold: 1) assess
the factorial validity of a revised version of the Mattering Index—the
Mattering Index for Sport (MIS)—that would be appropriate for use in a
team sport context, and 2) assess convergent validity by examining
whether mattering scores were associated with theoretically related constructs. Participants were 225 (Mage = 20; 125 males; 99 females; one
‘other’ gender) collegiate team sport athletes (e.g., soccer, volleyball) from
the Western United States who completed a paper-and-pencil survey.
Results of conﬁrmatory factor analysis and exploratory structural equation
modeling (ESEM) provided preliminary factorial validity evidence for the
3-factor structure (i.e., awareness, importance, and reliance) of the 21-item
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

MIS; there was some evidence that ESEM yielded superior model ﬁt:
CFI = .920, SRMR = .037, RMSEA = .074. Convergent validity evidence
was demonstrated through a clear pattern of positive and statistically
signiﬁcant correlations (p < .05) between mattering and global self-esteem,
facets of athlete satisfaction, and social identity, ranging from r = .16 to
r = .59. Overall, the results suggest a) the MIS is a viable new instrument to
assess team sport athletes’ perceptions of mattering on their teams, and b)
collegiate athletes who feel that they matter more on their teams may
experience beneﬁts including greater self-esteem, enhanced satisfaction,
and a stronger identiﬁcation with their teams.
Exploring the equitable impact of Junior Giants, a sport-based youth
development program: Perspectives from multiple stakeholders
Nicole D. Bolter, San Francisco State University; Lindsay E. Kipp, Texas
State University; P. Brian Greenwood, Cal Poly San Luis Obispo
Provided a sport-based youth development program includes the necessary
components, it is assumed that program beneﬁts are available to all youth
who participate, inclusive of age, gender, race/ethnicity, and socioeconomic background. More research is needed to evaluate whether program
impact is differentially experienced by the diversity of participants in these
programs. This study evaluated the psychosocial impact of one sport-based
youth development program, Junior Giants, by including multiple viewpoints and assessing whether perceived program impact varied by demographic characteristics. The sample included 1015 volunteer coaches, 558
team parents, 5943 parents/caregivers, and 3819 youth participants who
completed an online survey at the end of their involvement in the 2024
summer season of Junior Giants, a free, non-competitive 8-week program
using baseball/softball to teach social emotional competencies to 5-14year-old boys and girls. Multivariate analyses explored role, age, binary
gender, race/ethnicity, and activity type as independent predictors of seven
dependent variables (conﬁdence, integrity, teamwork, leadership, respect,
motivation to read, social emotional climate). Mean scores showed positive changes reported in targeted outcomes. MANOVA results revealed a
signiﬁcant main effect of role F(21, 33528) = 6.82, p < .001 and race/
ethnicity F(28, 44708) = 2.82, p < .001, while all other main effects and
interactions were non-signiﬁcant. In terms of role, coaches and team
parents reported signiﬁcantly higher positive changes among program
participants, compared to parents/caregivers and youth participants
(p < .001). Those who identiﬁed as Hispanic/Latino or African American/Black reported signiﬁcantly greater positive changes for youth participants, compared to those who identiﬁed as White or Asian (p < .001).
Subgroup analyses revealed that parents/caregivers who reported lower
socioeconomic status reported signiﬁcantly greater positive change in
conﬁdence and integrity (p < .001) compared to parents/caregivers who
reported higher socioeconomic status. Results conﬁrm that multiple
stakeholders reported a positive impact, and further suggest the program
is equitably serving boys and girls and different age groups. Differences in
perceived program impact by role, race/ethnicity, and socioeconomic
status offer interesting discussion points but should be interpreted cautiously given the small effect sizes. Funding source: Giants Community Fund.
A season long case study investigation on fostering leadership development in a high school lacrosse team
Jedediah Blanton, University of Tennessee; Kayleigh Hart, University of
Tennessee; Scott Pierce, Illinois State University; Mathew Gibs, University of Tennessee; Sophia Walsh, University of Tennessee; Tjaša Pintar,
University of Tennessee
Research examining the development of leadership in youth athletes has
been identiﬁed as an important life skill (Pierce et al., 2020). Past work has
both evaluated online formalized leadership training programs (Walker &
Gould, 2021) and advocated for a formalized process in which online
training can be used to scaffold speciﬁc team-based leadership development (Blanton et al., 2019). Despite coaches agreeing that formalized
leadership training would be valuable for their teams, few actually engage
in such practices (Voelker et al., 2011; 2019). Therefore, the purpose of
this case study was to document the leadership development of selected
athlete leaders and observe the inﬂuence of a formalized leadership
training program on coaches, athletes, and those athlete leaders on a girls’
high school lacrosse team during a single sport season. Perspectives from
the selected leaders (n = 5), coaching staff (n = 4), and teammates (n = 21)
were gathered through focus group interviews and surveys at four timepoints. The athlete leaders completed the online “Student Leadership” twocourse program from the NFHSLearn.com platform, and researchers
conducted a coaches’ workshop to discuss the content and skills their
leaders were exposed to in the online training program. This programming
is based on research in athlete leadership; for example, roles and skills are
described stemming from role differentiation theory including Fransen and
colleagues (2014) four leadership roles. Results showed that at the end of
the season coaches believed the athlete leaders were more precise in their
communications and showed more conﬁdence in modeling appropriate
behaviors after the leadership training. The athlete leaders believed they
focused more intentionally on relationships while providing appropriate
motivational support and challenge. Participants believed that focus group
interviews (i.e., intentionally discussing leadership) were more valuable
than the online training. Therefore, it might be most impactful to equip
coaches with formalized training on how to facilitate and discuss leadership development with their athletes, and have training be conducted
directly between coaches and athletes, within the context of their teams.
The effects of different types of leisure-time physical activity on
positive mental health in general and at-risk adolescents: A mixed
methods systematic review
Lars Lenze, University of Bern; Valentin Benzing, University of Bern; Julia
Schmid, University of Bern; Beatrice Minder, University of Bern; Annika
Frahsa, University of Bern
There is a large and growing prevalence of mental disorders among
adolescents. Leisure-time physical activity (LTPA) can prevent mental
health issues and promote positive mental health (PMH). While most
previous research has focused on the quantitative characteristics of LTPA
(e.g., duration, intensity), less attention has been given to qualitative and
contextual factors, such as the type of LTPA (e.g., yoga, soccer), despite
their critical role in understanding the relationship between LTPA and
PMH. Notably, it remains unclear which types of LTPA are most effective
for enhancing speciﬁc types of PMH in adolescents. This review addresses
this gap by adopting this called-for, differentiated perspective. Using the
PRISMA guidelines for systematic reviews, we searched 7 databases
between 2009 and 2024 with multiple (MeSH) terms aligned with the
PICO framework. After screening, data extraction, and quality assessment,
we applied a convergent segregated approach for the synthesis, following
the Joanna Briggs Institute Manual for Evidence Synthesis. From the initial
7,129 records, 35 articles met the inclusion criteria. The synthesis of the
quantitative studies revealed that the effects from different LTPA types on
(1) general, psychological and emotional well-being, (2) physical wellbeing, and (3) social well-being were inﬂuenced by both the LTPA type
and the speciﬁc PMH outcome assessed. The synthesis of the qualitative
studies complemented or conﬁrmed ﬁndings with evidence from both
methodological approaches showing improved general self-perceptions
(self-esteem/-efﬁcacy) associated with running, sports games, and martial
arts as well as improved physical self-perceptions (e.g., physical appearance) in sports games. Four key facilitators towards perceived better

mental health were identiﬁed in qualitative studies: mastery-related, social,
culture- and climate-related, and affect-related facilitators. These facilitators varied across activities, with a similar conﬁguration of multiple
facilitators observed in dancing and surﬁng. In contrast, only social
facilitators were predominant in sports games and running but less relevant
in martial arts. Our ﬁndings highlight activity-speciﬁc effects and mechanisms for improving different aspects of PMH in adolescents. Therefore,
this review provides new insights in the interplay of physical activity and
PMH, which offers valuable guidance for developing targeted interventions and informing policymaking aimed at promoting adolescent mental
health through LTPA.
Exploring varsity student-athletes’ lived experiences with social media
as it relates to various aspects of mental health: A rapid review
Mikayla McEwan, Brock University; Philip Sullivan, Brock University;
Corliss Bean, Brock University; Nathan Taylor, Brock University
Over the last decade, reliance on social media has signiﬁcantly increased,
with the current generation of young adults being the most frequent social
media users. Varsity student-athletes represent a unique subgroup of young
adults who are exposed to various physical, psychosocial, and sportspeciﬁc stressors; however, there is limited research that examines their
experiences with social media in relation to mental health. The purpose of
this study was to conduct a rapid review of the literature surrounding
varsity student-athletes’ social media use and how it impacts their mental
health. Articles were searched using PsycINFO, Sport Discus, ProQuest
Sociology Collection, CINAHL Complete, open databases and the online
library of the authors’ institution. A total of 224 articles were identiﬁed
through an online database search and 13 articles met the inclusion criteria.
To be included, studies had to be about post-secondary varsity studentathletes, relate to mental health and social media, be peer-reviewed, and be
published in a journal between 2012 and 2024 in English. The review
identiﬁed ﬁve aspects of mental health commonly experienced by studentathletes: social connectedness/social stressors, self-presentation/identity,
psychological distress, inattention and sleep disruptions, and appearancerelated concerns/disordered eating. Findings revealed that social media
plays a signiﬁcant role in the athletic, academic and personal lives of
varsity student-athletes. While student-athletes experienced several negative mental health outcomes, including body image pressure and difﬁculties coping with online performance-related criticism, they also had
positive experiences with social media that enhanced their well-being,
such as feeling a sense of belonging, enhanced personal growth, and
increased conﬁdence. Student-athletes often experienced positive and
negative mental health outcomes simultaneously, suggesting that the
relationship between social media and student-athlete mental health is
dynamic and complex. These ﬁndings have practical implications whereby
athletic departments may wish to implement workshops and other supports
to help student-athletes navigate their social media use and enhance their
well-being, including educating on coping with negative comments,
managing social media usage and fostering positive interactions online.
Embodied self-compassion: A mixed methods exploration and assessment of a self-compassion intervention in a movement-based learning
environment
Paula Mazur, University of Alberta; Jimena Lopez Lamas, University of
Alberta; Emily Noton, University of Alberta; Amber Mosewich, University
of Alberta; Danielle Cormier, University of Alberta
Self-compassion refers to a supportive, positive, and non-judgmental way
of relating to oneself, fostering adaptive responses to suffering, discomfort,
or challenge. Adopting self-compassion has demonstrated several beneﬁts
across various contexts, including health, education, and sport. While
traditional self-compassion interventions rely on cognitive strategies, like
reﬂective writing and guided discussions, they often overlook embodied
aspects of self-compassion, such as emotional expression and somatic
sensations. To address this gap, we aimed to develop and evaluate the
feasibility of a novel, movement-based self-compassion intervention.
Resulting from the development of a research pod (i.e., an interdisciplinary
group of knowledge keepers collaborating and innovating for the beneﬁt of
knowledge users), this intervention was integrated into an existing movement-based undergraduate course that emphasized somatic learning and
mind-body connection principles through an experiential approach. A
convenience sample of 50 students participated in two 50-minute intervention classes. In the ﬁrst class, students received self-compassion
psychoeducation and engaged in movement sequences designed to progressively evoke physical discomfort. During these movements, students
were guided to adopt a self-compassionate approach through verbal
prompts. In the second class, students participated in a guided group
reﬂection to share their experiences and provide feedback on the intervention. Quantitative measures of state self-compassion and state mindfulness
were collected pre- and post-intervention. Paired samples t-tests revealed
signiﬁcant improvements in both self-compassion (pre: M = 3.34, SD =
0.58; post: M = 3.70, SD = 0.55, t[49] = -4.49, p < .001; d = 0.57) and
mindfulness scores (pre: M = 3.46, SD = 1.02; post: M = 2.59, SD = 1.11,
t[49] = 5.81, p < .001; d = 1.05). These results provide preliminary evidence of intervention effectiveness. Using deductive content analysis, key
areas for intervention reﬁnement included: expanding the psychoeducation
to make it longer and more interactive for the users, extending the
intervention to allow more practice time, and enhancing the cohesion of
the verbally articulated movement instruction and self-compassion
prompts. These reﬁnements will guide future iterations of the intervention.
This study highlights the potential of movement-based interventions to
teach self-compassion holistically, integrating embodied and somatosensory experiences to complement traditional cognitive approaches. Funding
source: University of Alberta’s President’s Grant for the Creative and
Performing Arts.
Examining the within-person association between preoccupation with
muscularity and engagement in muscle-building behaviour among
university students
Zach Staffell, University of Western Ontario; Kristen Lucibello, University
of Western Ontario; Eva Pila, University of Western Ontario
Preoccupation with muscularity is an emerging and problematic construct
wherein thoughts about one’s level of muscularity may lead to engagement
in muscle-building behaviours. Understanding muscle-building behaviour
is particularly important as engagement in extreme muscle-building
behaviour is a key risk factor for psychopathology. There has been
increased representation of muscularity for men and women’s sociocultural ideals. While men’s muscularity is characterized by a big and welldeveloped muscular mesomorphic shape, women are pressured to be toned
and athletic with ﬂat and smooth muscles. The current literature lacks
research on muscularity preoccupation and how that inﬂuences individuals
to engage in muscle-building behaviours (e.g., weightlifting) in ecologically valid settings. The present study examines the within-person relationship between preoccupation with muscularity and engagement in
muscle-building behaviour as moderated by sex/gender. Using ecological
momentary assessment, university students (N = 97, Mage = 19.45 ± 2.15,
19.4% cisgender men) received 49 prompts over 7-days to self-report
levels of muscularity preoccupation and engagement in muscle-building
behaviour. Average levels of preoccupation with muscularity and musclebuilding behaviour was signiﬁcantly higher in men than women. Withinperson differences showed that when an individual’s preoccupation with
their muscularity was higher than their own average, they reported greater

p < .001). This relationship was not moderated by gender (b = -0.11, 95%

Equitable teaching in sport, exercise, and performance psychology
Hannah Bennett, Augusta University; Stefanee Maurice, California Polytechnic State University, San Luis Obispo
As educators, fostering student engagement and motivation is essential to
promoting academic success. Research demonstrates that a strong sense
of belonging and validation among students signiﬁcantly increases
emotional and behavioral engagement (Gillen-O’Neel, 2021) and is
associated with improved academic performance (Strayhorn, 2018).
Among college students, a sense of belonging has been linked to higher
retention rates, persistence, motivation, self-conﬁdence, and overall
academic achievement and adjustment (Hausmann et al., 2009; Murphy
& Zirkel, 2015; Walton & Cohen, 2011). While active learning techniques are widely utilized to enhance classroom engagement, educators in
sport and performance psychology can also beneﬁt from incorporating
culturally relevant pedagogy (CRP; Ladson-Billings, 1995) and the
cultural relevance cycle (CRC; Flory & McCaughtry, 2013) to create
equitable and inclusive learning environments. This presentation focuses
on two key objectives. First, it will introduce CRP and CRC as critical
frameworks tailored to the unique contexts of sport and performance
psychology education. Secondly, it will explore practical strategies and
techniques aligned with these frameworks to enhance student engagement in college courses. Examples include bias-resistant grading policies,
professor reﬂections, discourse boundaries, rethinking assignment options, and incorporating multiple learning modalities. By integrating CRP
and CRC, educators can create classrooms that foster a strong sense of
belonging and validation, particularly for diverse student populations.
This approach is especially relevant in sport and performance psychology, where inclusive and engaging teaching methods can signiﬁcantly
impact students’ academic and professional development. Attendees will
leave this presentation equipped with valuable insights and strategies to
enhance engagement, foster inclusivity, and support the success of all
students in their courses.
Exploring differential associations between physical activity intensity
and internalizing problem symptoms in adolescents using network
analysis
Denver Brown, Kansas State University
Research has demonstrated inverse associations between physical activity (PA) and internalizing problems among adolescents. However, relying
on sum scores of internalizing problems obscures the heterogeneity in
symptom presentation and overlooks the potential for PA of varying
intensities to exert differential effects on speciﬁc symptoms. This study
investigated associations between light (LPA), moderate (MPA), and
vigorous (VPA) PA with individual internalizing problem symptoms in
adolescents. This cross-sectional study utilized data from the Year 2
Follow-Up wave (2018-2020) of the Adolescent Brain Cognitive Development Study. A diverse sample of 6,750 adolescents aged 10-13 years
(49% female) wore a Fitbit Charge 2 for three weeks to measure PA and
completed the Brief Problem Monitor-Youth to assess internalizing
problems. A nine-node Gaussian graphical model examined associations
between PA intensities (LPA, MPA, VPA) and six internalizing symptoms. Centrality metrics (strength, closeness) were computed to
characterize the network’s structure. On average, participants engaged
in 324.80 minutes per day of LPA (SD = 98.88), 29.86 minutes of MPA
(SD = 26.73), and 12.30 minutes of VPA (SD = 17.38). The network
included 20 edges, of which four linked MPA and VPA – but not LPA –
with internalizing symptoms. MPA was negatively correlated with “Too
fearful or anxious” (r = -0.05) and “Self-conscious or easily embarrassed”
(r = -0.06). VPA was positively correlated with “Feel too guilty”
(r = 0.05) and negatively correlated with “Unhappy, sad, or depressed”
(r = -0.05). Centrality analysis indicated that “Feel worthless or inferior”
was the strongest node in the network, while “Self-conscious or easily
embarrassed” had the highest closeness centrality, reﬂecting its central
position in the network. Stability tests demonstrated good reliability, and
bootstrapped edge weights showed reasonable accuracy. Collectively,
these ﬁndings suggest that MPA and VPA are differentially associated
with speciﬁc internalizing symptoms, with MPA showing modest protective associations with anxiety and VPA displaying small but mixed
associations with depressive symptoms in adolescents. Null associations
for LPA suggest that the symptom-speciﬁc beneﬁts of PA may only
emerge with higher-intensity activities. Although further research is
needed to establish causal relationships, these ﬁndings highlight the
nuanced role of PA intensity in mental health and emphasize the importance of tailoring PA recommendations to speciﬁc symptoms.
Examining the inﬂuence of control beliefs and setback characteristics
on postsecondary athletes’ goal strategies
Emjai L. S. Deschamps, Thompson Rivers University; Pratham Makol,
Thompson Rivers University; Patti C. Parker, Thompson Rivers University
Sport setbacks such as poor performance or injuries can negatively impact
coping depending on how athletes navigate the setback. Setbacks can
hinder athletes’ pursuit of goals or their ability to adjust to goals that are no
longer feasible. In the present study, we consider important control beliefs
that may be tied to important goal strategies. Primary control (PC) refers to
the belief that one can personally inﬂuence their environment or outcomes
directly; while secondary control (SC) refers to the belief that one can
cognitively align with their environment and accept outcomes. For this
present study, we assessed 199 post-secondary athletes in Canada and the
USA via Proliﬁc (Mage = 23.60; 99 men, 96 women, 4 non-binary) using
secondary data analysis. We conducted multiple regression tests to examine the inﬂuence of PC, SC, and prominent setback characteristics (severity, recovery) on goal strategies (rigid goal persistence, goal adjustment).
Our results suggest that the extent to which athletes felt they were
recovering well from the setback was positively associated with goal
adjustment strategies, β = .32, b = .86, p < .001, 95% CI [0.48, 1.24], as
were SC beliefs, β = .25, b = .07, p < .001, 95% CI [0.03, 0.12]. Furthermore, our results suggest that SC beliefs were negatively linked to athletes’
goal-persistent strategies, β = -.16, b = -.04, p = .038, 95% CI [-0.07, -
0.002]. PC beliefs did not predict either goal strategies in the context of
managing sport setbacks. In conclusion, athletes’ SC beliefs were tied to
greater reports of adjustment to setback-impeded goals and lower rigid
goal-persistence strategies. The ﬁndings align with previous research that
convey the importance of cognitive beliefs concerning coping with various
stressors in sport. Additionally, the ﬁndings generate more questions for
future research regarding what types of goals or length of recovery time
impact cognitive beliefs or goal strategies for handling setbacks in sport.
Funding source: SSHRC IDG.
Motivational proﬁles of doping-related outcomes in gym exercisers: A
latent proﬁle analysis
Ian Boardley, University of Birmingham; Shuge Zhang, Hunan University
of Technology; Scott Gunning, University of Northampton; James Adie,
Birmingham Newman University
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S63
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 67] ---
Research has supported the importance of motivation for doping, with
many researchers drawing upon achievement goal and self-determination
theories (Nicholls, 1989; Ryan & Deci, 2017). Whilst the majority of
researchers have adopted variable-centred approaches, here we adopted a
person-centred approach to compare motivational proﬁles for risk of
doping. 501 UK gym exercisers (Mage = 23.9 ± 7.24 years; 58% male)
responded to a questionnaire measuring goal orientations (task and ego),
motivational regulations (controlled, autonomous and amotivation), doping attitudes (moral and functional), doping moral disengagement, and
self-reported doping use. Latent proﬁle analysis showed a four-proﬁle
model was most representative of the sample based on model ﬁt and proﬁle
accuracy criteria. This four-proﬁle model consisted of autonomous-task
(i.e., high task orientation and autonomous motivation with medium ego
orientation, controlled motivation, and low amotivation; 54.60%) task
(i.e., high task orientation with relatively low or medium scores for the
other four motivation variables; 20.96%), mixture-medium (i.e., medium
or medium-high scores in all ﬁve motivation variables; 19.08%), and
mixture-high (i.e., relatively high scores in all ﬁve motivation variables;
9.50%) proﬁles. The task proﬁle demonstrated lower functional attitudes
than autonomous task (χ2 = 17.45, p = .00), mixture medium (χ2 = 16.03,
p = .00), and mixture high (χ2 = 20.79, p = .00). For moral attitudes,
autonomous task was higher than task (χ2 = 13.92, p = .00), mixture
medium (χ2 = 7.67, p = .00), and mixture high (χ2 = 42.13, p = .00), and
mixture high was lower than task (χ2 = 10.06, p = .00) and mixture medium
(χ2 = 12.79, p = .00). For doping moral disengagement, task was lower
than autonomous task (χ2 = 11.45, p = .00), mixture medium (χ2 = 10.03,
p = .00), and mixture high (χ2 = 5.83, p = .02). No signiﬁcant cross-proﬁle
differences were identiﬁed for prohibited substance use. Our ﬁndings offer
insight on naturally occurring motivational subgroups within UK exercise
communities and how they differ on known doping risk factors.
A qualitative exploration of athlete well-being and cultural shifts in
elite gymnastics
Sara King, Oregon State University; Nazlı Özkoca, Oregon State University; Kim Rogers, Oregon State University
Elite gymnastics is experiencing a notable transformation, with athletes
training and competing well beyond the traditional peak age of 18
(Fédération Internationale de Gymnastique, 2024). Despite this change,
no known studies have examined the factors impacting these extending
careers and their subsequent impact on gymnasts’ well-being. Furthermore, while coaching practices have come under increased scrutiny, no
known studies have explored what gymnasts perceive to be the most
effective coaching practices or areas in need of improvement. This
qualitative study addresses these gaps by investigating two key aims:
(1) Why are we seeing cultural shifts in gymnastics, and how do these shifts
beneﬁt gymnasts? and (2) Which coaching practices do gymnasts ﬁnd most
supportive of their well-being and success, and what areas still need
improvement? Participants included 15 current international elite gymnasts
(18-37 years of age) from various European National Gymnastics teams.
Recruitment began before the start of the 2024 European Championships,
at the event, and continued after via Zoom until data saturation was
reached. Data collection involved semi-structured interviews lasting
20–45 minutes. All interviews were audio-recorded and subsequently
transcribed. The data were analyzed using thematic analysis. Preliminary
ﬁndings for the ﬁrst question revealed three themes: (1) a signiﬁcant
change of mentality in the sport, including the view that 18 is still
considered “young”, athlete’s desires for more autonomy over their
training, and the criticism of past practices; (2) shifts in training methods,
including both psychological and physical changes; and (3) increased
availability of resources, such as ﬁnancial, physical, and mental support.
Two themes emerged in response to the second question: (1) positive
coach–athlete interactions characterized by communication, collaboration, and autonomy; and (2) areas for improvement, notably the desire for
coaches to acknowledge athletes’ lives outside the sport. These ﬁndings
contribute to the literature on coach–athlete relationships and provide
insight into the evolving culture of elite gymnastics, highlighting how
gymnasts’ perspectives can inform both improved coaching practices and
the sustainability of extended athletic careers. Gymnastics governing
bodies can use these ﬁndings to guide policy decisions that support
athletes across multiple Olympic cycles while safeguarding athletes’
well-being.
Exploring the effects of immersive virtual reality exercise on depressive symptoms, heart rate variability, and motivation for physical
activity in young adults
Youngjun Lee, Michigan State University; Rabindra Ratan, Michigan
State University; Spyridoula Vazou, Michigan State University; Sanghoon
Kim, Michigan State University; Youjeung Kim, University of Hawai’i at
Mānoa; Janet Hauck, Michigan State University
The mental health of young adults is a growing global concern. Their
unique challenges, such as adapting to new roles and life-altering circumstances, contribute to a higher risk of mental issues such as depression.
Exercise is a well-established intervention for depression, and immersive
virtual reality (VR) presents an innovative approach that offers an interactive and structured exercise environment to promote exercise. This study
aimed to examine the effects of a six-week VR boxing intervention on
depressive symptoms, heart rate variability (HRV), and motivation for
physical activity in young adults. Ten participants aged 21.1 years (SD =
1.4) engaged in weekly 40-minute VR boxing sessions using the Supernatural Fitness app (exercise group), while eight participants aged
20.8 years (SD = 1.5) continued their regular physical activities (control
group). Depression symptoms were measured pre-and post-intervention
using the Patient Health Questionnaire-9 (PHQ-9). HRV was recorded
with Polar H9 sensors and the Elite HRV app. Physical activity levels were
measured using the GPAQ questionnaire, and body composition was
assessed using the InBody device. Baseline demographics, physical
activity levels, and body composition metrics did not differ signiﬁcantly
between groups. There was a decline in PHQ-9 scores from 6.9 (SD = 6.0)
to 6.1 (SD = 5.2), with a small effect size in the exercise group, and
repeated measures ANOVA found no signiﬁcant differences between
groups (p = 0.526). In addition, there were no signiﬁcant group differences
in HRV. Although our study did not ﬁnd statistically signiﬁcant impacts of
VR exercise on depressive symptoms and HRV, we believe future studies
with larger sample sizes will provide more substantial evidence of such
potential health beneﬁts. However, this virtual exercise improves motivation for physical activity. Most participants stated they exercised more
often or with different types of exercise. Their motivation on an average
scale from 1–10 was 7.6, corresponding to moderate to high motivation.
This study contributes to the literature showing that VR exercise is both
possible and acceptable as a mental health intervention that supports
physical activity and well-being among young adults and affects individual
motivation for physical activity.
Measuring intrateam conﬂict: Content and factorial validity evidence
for a new eight-item measure
Lauren Secaras, Denison University; Andrew Driska, Michigan State
University
Sport teams must work together to compete and achieve goals, but this
process is impeded by intrateam conﬂict which occurs when group
members perceive differences or incompatibilities with team tasks or with
teammates. Interview-based studies with athletes indicate that intrateam
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S64
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 68] ---
conﬂict decreases performance, evokes negative emotions, and adds strain
to teammate relationships (Holt et al., 2012; Paradis et al., 2014; Partridge
& Knapp, 2016). However, quantitative studies of intrateam conﬂict have
not coalesced around an accepted measurement tool. Existing intrateam
conﬂict scales share concerns, including the limited number of items,
double-barreled items, confusing item language, and the lack of sportspeciﬁc language. Given the need for a clear measure of intrateam conﬂict
in sport settings, this research aimed to provide initial validation for a sportspeciﬁc intrateam conﬂict measure. Following a process outlined by Kline
(2006), the researchers ﬁrst developed a preliminary instrument comprised
of 43-items adapted from existing instruments and created by the researchers. Fifty content experts (32 current college athletes, six coaches,
and 12 sport psychology professionals) classiﬁed each item as task or
relationship conﬂict, rated its utility (i.e., essential, useful but not essential,
or not necessary), and provided comments on each item. Descriptive
statistics for each item, within the entire sample and for each expert
group, yielded a shortened survey of 24 items with higher clarity and utility
scores. The 24-item survey was then distributed to 563 current collegiate
athletes participating across all division levels at universities in the Midwest. Exploratory factor analyses were conducted using Mplus. Results
revealed a two-factor structure (e.g., task conﬂict, relationship conﬂict)
which aligned with other intrateam conﬂict measures. Further analyses of
factor loadings, inter-item correlations, and item-to-total correlations
suggested more items could be removed from the survey. An 8-item
version of this survey demonstrated good model data ﬁt (CFI = .99,
TLI = .99, RMSEA = .04, SRMR = .01) and reliability (Factor 1 Cronbach’s α = .81; Factor 2 Cronbach’s α = .73). This initial validity evidence
supports the use of this questionnaire by sport researchers and practitioners
in their attempts to explore team processes and outcomes.
Easing anxiety dysphoria: Effects of Acceptance and Commitment
Therapy on adolescents’ penalty shot performance
Kehinde D. Obidele, New Mexico State University; Michael A. Ajayi,
University of Ibadan; Christopher A. Aiken, New Mexico State University
Adolescence is a period of signiﬁcant physical, emotional, and social
upheaval, resulting in increased anxiety. This difﬁculty is greatly augmented in sporting contexts, where being in competition-related and highpressure situations, such as a penalty shot in soccer, can heighten performance anxiety. Anxiety has been repeatedly shown to impair motor skills,
decision-making, and competitive outcomes (Ford et al., 2017). Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT) is an evidence-based intervention
that enhances psychological ﬂexibility. Through cultivating mindfulness,
acceptance of uncomfortable feelings, and value-based action, ACT
enables individuals to stay focused under pressure, mitigating anxiety’s
effects on performance (Hayes, 2004). While ACT has been studied largely
within clinical populations, its use within sports performance has been
under-researched. The study evaluated the combined effect of ACT
intervention on anxiety dysphoria and penalty kick performance among
adolescent male soccer players in the Ibadan North area of Nigeria. Sixtysix male adolescent players were recruited from public secondary schools,
aged 12–18, and randomly assigned to ACT or a control group (33
participants each). Participants completed 10 penalty shots at baseline
(pre-test), completed a 6-week intervention, and performed a 10-trial
penalty shot post-test. ACT completed weekly 60-minute intervention
sessions which included topics on mindfulness training, values clariﬁcation, and cognitive diffusion. Control received courses on nutrition during
weekly sessions without any psychological intervention. Primary dependent variables were anxiety as scored on the State Trait Anxiety Inventory
(STAI-S), and number of penalty shots made. Separate 2 (group) x 2 (time)
repeated measures ANOVAs revealed a signiﬁcant main of effect time
with STAI-S scores decreasing and penalty performance increasing from
pre- to post-test (p’s < .001). The main effects for group were also
signiﬁcant with ACT having lower reported STAI-S scores and improved
penalty shot performance (p’s < .001). A signiﬁcant group by time interaction was observed for both STAI-S and penalty performance (p’s <
.001). Anxiety reduction and improved performance was greatest for ACT
(p < .001). These ﬁndings support other literature in showing ACT’s
capacity for addressing performance anxiety and enhancing task performance under pressure. For athletes facing high-stakes performance demands, ACT offers promise as an approach for developing psychological
resilience.
Student-athletes’ explanatory thinking about poor academic performance in a two-semester course is mediated by emotions
Matthew R. Tambellini, Thompson Rivers University; Monty Armstrong,
Thompson Rivers University; Madison Clements, Thompson Rivers University; Patti C. Parker, Thompson Rivers University; Raymond P. Perry,
University of Manitoba; Judith G. Chipperﬁeld, University of Manitoba
Student-athletes in postsecondary education must navigate critical life
setbacks that can often be overwhelming and stressful including challenging academic workloads and competitive sport schedules. Our study
examined how student-athletes explain their academic performance based
on Weiner’s (1985, 2018) attribution theory of motivation and emotion.
The theory posits that such explanatory mindsets may lead to better or
worse performance depending on the types of causal ascriptions involved.
Thus, we explored the impact of student-athletes’ attributions for poor
performance on test grades in a two-semester, online introductory course.
We also tested the role of attribution-linked emotions as mediators of this
relationship. Our study consisted of a subsample of 130 student-athletes
(64 female, 66 male) from a larger secondary dataset of students enrolled in
a Canadian research-intensive university (ages = 18-30 years). We measured Semester 1 attributions (ability, effort) for poor academic performance, Semester 2 attribution-linked emotions (helplessness, hope, shame,
and guilt), and collected ﬁnal test grades at the end of the academic year.
Mediation regression analysis revealed that Semester 1 ability attributions
did not directly predict test grades in the two-semester course but did so
indirectly via helplessness (β = -.13, SE = .05, 95% CI [-0.24, -0.03]).
Furthermore, hope also mediated the relationship between Semester 1
ability attributions and ﬁnal test grades (β = -.12, SE = .05, 95% CI [-0.24, -
0.03]). However, shame did not signiﬁcantly mediate the relationship
between ability and grades. When examining Semester 1 effort attributions
for poor performance, effort had a direct negative impact on test grades
(β = -.19, p = .047); however, helplessness, guilt, and hope did not mediate
the effort attribution-performance relationship. All emotions directly predicted performance in predictable ways (β range = -.27 to .40). These
ﬁndings suggest that for student-athletes, relationships between low ability
attributions and maladaptive emotions help to explain lower performance.
The ﬁndings support previous attributional treatment studies that discourage low ability attributions for academically at-risk students. Future
research could explore why effort attributions did not boost academic
performance for these students navigating dual-roles in university and how
attributions might differ in other student populations.
Funding source: SSHRC.
Trust your feelings, aim for the process as the outcome, and accept the
impossibility of certainty: Expert perspectives on group decisionmaking in professional mountaineering
Svenja A. Wolf, Florida State University; Anna Bergauer, Technical
University of Munich; Andrea Schittenhelm, Technical University of
Munich; Benedikt Perl, Technical University of Munich; V. Vanessa
Wergin, The University of Queensland
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S65
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 69] ---
Groups provide their members with substantial beneﬁts such as social
support and shared responsibility (Freeman & Rees, 2010; Karau &
Williams, 1993). However, groups can also have disadvantages, for
example when they lead their members to riskier (i.e., group polarization;
Myers & Lamm, 1976) and less considerate decisions (i.e., groupthink;
Janis, 1972). A context in which individuals work together in groups, need
to make frequent decisions, and in which wrong decisions may have fatal
consequences is mountaineering (Heggie & Jorgenson, 2010). Although
we understand group decisions in a laboratory context and there has been
an increased interest in the psychology of mountaineering (Jackman et al.,
2020), we know little about the process of group decision-making in the
dynamic environment of a mountaineering expedition and which factors
may separate success from failure (Bergauer et al. 2025). Operating from a
paradigm of pragmatic constructionism (Tamminen & Poucher, 2020) and
informed by the structures of the conceptual framework for sport teams
(Eys et al., 2020) and IGLOO levels of contextual nesting (Nielsen et al.,
2018), we interviewed six professional mountaineers and two sport
psychology consultants about their experiences with group decisionmaking on expedition. Through our reﬂexive thematic analysis (Braun
& Clarke, 2019) of these interviews, we carved out themes like Small steps
are adding up, Stay true to your values and feelings, The process is the
outcome, A good structure carries well, and As good as possible, speaking
to the ubiquity of decisions; the reliance on heuristics honed by years of
experience; a democratic and inclusive group focus; the importance of
clear roles, norms, and leadership; and the necessary ability to let go of
certainty as an achievable option. Together, these themes paint a picture of
emotionally and interpersonally intelligent experts who know what they
are doing and know to accept their limitations, factors and skills that may
have safeguarded our participants against fatalities and hence may constitute important aspects to train in aspiring, less experienced mountaineers to
ensure their safety as well.
Funding source: Albrecht von Dewitz Foundation.
Personal trainers’ perceptions of the UWorkItOut UWin program:
An experiential learning opportunity for undergraduate Kinesiology
students
Melissa A. Paré, University of Windsor; Krista J. Munroe-Chandler,
University of Windsor; Aidan M. Kovacs, University of Windsor; Todd
M. Loughead, University of Windsor; Chad A. Sutherland, University of
Windsor
The UWorkItOut UWin program is a six-week exercise training and
counselling intervention aimed to improve the mental health of university
students. Previous research has demonstrated numerous beneﬁts for those
who participate in the program (e.g., decreased anxiety and depression
symptoms; Muir et al., 2020, gained coping skills; Paré et al., 2023,
increased physical activity levels; Paré et al., 2024). Given the vital role of
the trainers in the UWorkItOut UWin program, it is important to understand their perspectives. Therefore, the purpose of the study was to
examine the perceptions and experiences of UWorkItOut UWin personal
trainers. A phenomenological design was used to capture participants’
individualized experiences of nine former trainers (n = 6 females, Mage =
21.77 years old, SD = 2.11 years, all undergraduate kinesiology students)
through two sequential semi-structured interviews. Interviews focused on
identifying skills trainers gained or developed while involved with the
program and were analyzed using Braun and Clarke’s (2019) reﬂexive
thematic analysis. Trainers implemented behaviour change strategies to
increase clients’ motivation characterized through self-determination theory: 1) autonomy (e.g., individualization and adaptation of programming),
2) competence (e.g., increasing conﬁdence, providing feedback), and 3)
relatedness (e.g., communication skills, rapport building, and social
support). In addition, trainers reported gaining “real-world” experience
as a valuable asset of working in the program. Trainers gained a sense of
professionalism through the development of leadership skills, patience,
discipline, and the ability to apply knowledge in various settings. Furthermore, trainers had the opportunity to work with various populations
(e.g., students with mental health challenges and international students).
As such, trainers reported gaining a sense of cultural competence and
mental health awareness. When designing interventions, researchers
should consider incorporating undergraduate students into the process to
enrich their academic learning and provide experiences to prepare students
for their future careers.
Youth athletes’ perceptions of their parents’ motivational orientation
and its impact on their own motivation and athletic competence
Tom Keay, Boise State University; Laura Petranek, Boise State University;
Brian Butki, Boise State University; Eric Martin, Boise State University
In sports, parents play a crucial role in their child’s life by being key
supporters, and providing informational guidance, emotional encouragement, and ﬁnancial resources. These contributions foster social, physical,
and psychological beneﬁts that enhance a child’s overall well-being. Given
the many beneﬁts children gain from participating in sports, understanding
how parents can best support their child’s athletic involvement is essential
for encouraging sustained participation. One relatively understudied area
in this ﬁeld is how children perceive their parents’ motivational orientation
and how these perceptions inﬂuence the child’s own motivational orientation and physical competence. Additionally, little research has explored
whether these perceptions differ between mothers and fathers or whether
they vary based on the child’s sex. Therefore, this study aims to address
these gaps by examining how children’s perceptions of their parents’
motivational orientation impact their own motivational orientation and
athletic competence while also investigating whether these perceptions
differ for mothers and fathers and if they vary between sons and daughters.
Youth who were engaged in summer sports camps (n = 106, Mage =
10.3 ± 1.2 years) completed the Parent Initiated Motivational Climate in
Individuals Sport Competition Questionnaire, Achievement Goal Scale for
Youth Sport, and the Athletic Competence subscale from the Self-Perception Proﬁle for Children. Youth perceived both their mother and father as
promoting high levels of task-oriented climates and low levels of egooriented climates. They also reported high levels of their own task
orientation, low levels of ego orientation, and moderate to high levels of
athletic competence. Interestingly, athletic competence was related to
perceptions of both mother’s and father’s task motivation orientation and
the child’s own ego-orientation. Furthermore, fathers were perceived as
exhibiting higher levels of both task- and ego-oriented behaviors toward
sport compared to mothers. The only signiﬁcant difference between boys’
and girls’ perceptions of their parents’ motivational climate was that boys
perceived their fathers as demonstrating higher levels of task-oriented
behaviors compared to girls. Additional ﬁndings will be discussed, with a
focus on the practical implications of these results.
Different strokes for different folks: Testing for beneﬁts of withinsport diversity during youth (in stroke and distance) among adult male
and female Canadian competitive swimmers
Porter Trevisan, University of British Columbia; Baylee Munro, University of British Columbia; Nicola Hodges, University of British Columbia
There has been much interest in pathways of skill development in sport
related to early specialization and sport-sampling. Most of this research has
been centred around team sports (where skill group differences, rather than
performance times can be compared) or late involvement sports (where
diversity is expected). Moreover, diversity has primarily focused on
involvement in different sports, rather than different events within the
same sport. One study that stands out as a counter-example, was on

German swimmers from an online database of swim-times and events
(Staub et al., 2020). Reliable relationships were shown between withinsport diversity and adult success at age 18. However, the data was from a
relatively old cohort, when specialization may have been the norm,
delimited to 200m freestyle, and with no distinctions between male and
female athletes; despite evidence that males specialize later compared to
female counterparts (Dormehl & Williams, 2016). Our aims were to test
the importance of within sport diversity for later success in Canadian
swimmers; testing for gender differences, across different ages, and in a
larger dataset of ∼150,000 athletes (using www.swimrankings.net). Only
preliminary analysis has been conducted thus far, delimited to overall
number of events and FINA points at age 18 for the top 100 males and
females in 50m freestyle (N = 200). Based on Pearson correlations, we
failed to show relationships between number of events competed from age
12 to 18 (in 2-year intervals) and FINA points at age 18 (rs < .10 for
females, rs < .20 for males, at ages 12 and 14). This was despite the number
of events varying between 2 and 20 competed at age 12 for those that later
reached the top 100. The average number of events was higher for females
(M = 14.1 yr, SD = 3.12) than males (M = 12.5 yr, SD = 3.75) at age 12,
t(200) = 3.28, p < 0.05. We plan to analyze the larger data set to separately
examine correlations between strokes and distances to better test the idea
that practice variability, through within sport diversity, is a positive
predictor of later “success” in swimming. Funding source: This research
