Queens of clutch: Regular season third-set wins in D-I beach volleyball
lead to greater postseason success
Ainsley Patrick, California State University, Northridge; Tasha Mae,
California State University, Northridge; Mark P Otten, California State
University, Northridge
An area of growing focus in sport is the psychology of successful, or
“clutch” performance under pressure. The present study considers the
relationship between success in regular season adverse situations and later
wins in postseason tournament play. Theory suggests that if conditioned to
perform well under situations of importance, a team might see new
pressure conditions as different – less stressful, and more as an opportunity
to demonstrate well-practiced skills facilitated by the added attention. Data
was collected from 64 Division I (D-I) teams competing in NCAA
women’s beach volleyball in 2024. No known studies have examined
the unique format of the beach season in connection with player and team
psychology. Wins and losses in the third set at any pair against conference
opponents during the regular season were counted across 263 duals. Five
pairs play a match within each dual, bringing the total count to 1315
matches. Of these 1315, 376 played to a deciding third set. Wins and losses
in the postseason conference tournament were then collected, across 9
conferences for a total of 113 matches. Win percentages were calculated
for the regular season third set conference matchups and the conference
tournament matches. Linear regression analysis revealed that a team’s win
percentage of regular season third sets signiﬁcantly predicted the team’s
conference tournament performance, Beta = .420, p < .001, R2 = .176. This
relationship remained signiﬁcant (Beta = .217, p = .023) even when controlling for a team’s regular season win percentage across all matches, as
part of a multiple regression to predict conference tournament wins
(R2 = .526). These results suggest that experiencing victory in a third set
may lead to greater success in future similarly pressured situations, as in a
postseason conference tournament – playing for a chance to continue to the
national championships. The ﬁndings can be applied to training, if
simulating pressure situations will enhance one’s ability to perform well
under the circumstances. Future studies of beach volleyball might delve
deeper into the psychology of the third set, to further focus the mental
training of athletes, teams and coaches for success.
Evaluating the Meaning Maintenance Model within athletic and nonathletic populations
Snabu Neupane, Juniata College; Kyle Jaquess, Juniata College
The Meaning Maintenance Model (MMM) posits that an individual’s sense
of meaning in life can be derived from multiple sources, including selfesteem, social connectedness, symbolic immortality, and a sense of closure.
In this present study, we seek to test this model in the context of athletic
(N = 107) and non-athletic (N = 147; Total N = 254) collegiate populations
using the Rosenberg Self-Esteem Scale, the Social Connectedness Scale –
Revised, the Symbolic Immortality Instrument, the short version of the Need
for Closure Scale using two dimensions of meaning in life—presence
(PMiL) and search (SMiL)— from the Meaning in Life Questionnaire.
Relationships were evaluated using multiple linear regression. For PMiL,
the model explained 44.2% of the variance (F(5, 244) = 38.7, p = 0.001,
R2 = 0.442), with signiﬁcant contributions from self-esteem (std β = 0.332,
p < .001), social connectedness (std β = 0.245, p < .001), and symbolic
immortality (std β = 0.232, p < .001). Group membership (athlete vs.
non-athlete) and need for closure did not signiﬁcantly affect the results
(std β = 0.127, p = .203 and std β = -0.023, p = .643, respectively). For
SMiL, the model accounted for 13.7% of the variance (F(5,244) = 7.76,
p < 0.001, R2 = 0.137). Signiﬁcant predictors included self-esteem (std β = -
0.221, p = .009), need for closure (std β = 0.221 p < .001), and symbolic
immortality (std β = 0.184, p = .004). Group membership and social connectedness also did not signiﬁcantly affect the results (std β = -0.138,
p = .265 and std β = -0.02, p = .815, respectively). These ﬁndings highlight
the collective and distinct roles of self-esteem, social connectedness, need
for closure, and symbolic immortality in shaping the presence of and search
for meaning in life in both athletic and non-athletic populations. Importantly, the MMM appears to be equally applicable to both groups. It is of note
that the MMM appears to be most descriptive of PMiL rather than SMiL.
Resilience and athletic team afﬁliation as predictors of meaning in life
Yuanyuan Sunchen, Juniata College; Kyle Jaquess, Juniata College
A sense of meaning in life is linked to a range of positive wellness
outcomes, including increased stress resilience. According to the Meaning
Maintenance Model (MMM), the sense of meaning in life is informed by at
least four psychological needs: self-esteem, closure, social connection, and
symbolic immortality. Although the MMM was proposed for the general
population, its application to athletes remains under-explored. Collegiate
athletics can aid in fulﬁlling key psychological needs as teams foster social
connection and sports provide opportunities to build self-esteem through
displays of competence. In this study, we evaluated whether an individual’s sense of meaning in life is inﬂuenced by afﬁliation to sports teams
with varying levels of performance success and how meaning in life relates
to resilience in an athletic population. It was hypothesized that the sense of
meaning in life is positively associated with the performance success of the
sports team the individual is afﬁliated with. Additionally, it is expected that
meaning-in-life will be positively related to resilience within the athletic
population. A total of 105 college student-athletes completed the Nicholson-McBride Resilience Questionnaire and Meaning in Life Questionnaire. Team win-ratios were extracted from public records. For Meaning in
Life-Presence (PMiL), the model was signiﬁcant (R2 = 0.33, p < .001).
Speciﬁcally, resilience signiﬁcantly predicted PMiL in athletes (std
β = 0.53, p = .005), as expected. The relationship between PMiL and
WinRatio was marginally signiﬁcant and directionally consistent with
expectations (std β = 0.18, p = .056). For Meaning in Life-Search (SMiL),
the model was not signiﬁcant (R2 = 0.01, p = .670). In summary, those with

a present sense of meaning-in-life are signiﬁcantly more resilient and also
tend to be members of relatively high-performing teams. However, the

signiﬁcance of athletic participation across varying levels of team performance as a potential contributor to meaning in life.
Exploring coaches’ capacity to support a self-compassion program for
competitive women’s sport
Karissa Johnson, University of Saskathewan; Margo E. Adam, University
of Alberta; Kent C. Kowalski, University of Saskatchewan; Amber D.
Mosewich, University of Alberta; Lee Schaefer, University of Saskatchewan; Benjamin J. I. Schellenberg, University of Manitoba; Philipp
Rothlin, Swiss Federal Institute of Sport Magglingen; Kate E. Storey,
University of Alberta; Leah J. Ferguson, University of Saskatchewan
To support women athletes’ thriving in sport, where they can both be well
and perform well, it is critical to provide them with resources they see as
valuable to the sport experience. One resource may be sport-integrated
self-compassion programming. Self-compassion is a kind and understanding way of relating to the self, particularly during challenges. In sport,
women athletes face unique challenges and often experience reduced wellbeing compared to men athletes, making it difﬁcult for them to thrive in
sport. Considering these challenges, women athletes view becoming more
self-compassionate as beneﬁcial to their sport experience. With that,
women athletes need support as they become more self-compassionate,
rather than it be a solo endeavor. In considering sport as a complex social
system, there is value in exploring how coaches can support women
athletes in becoming more self-compassionate. Therefore, the purpose of
our qualitative case study was to explore coaches’ capacity to support a
self-compassion program in competitive women’s sport. Data were generated through semi-structured interviews and follow-up focus groups with
experienced coaches of women athletes. A total of 16 coaches, from a
variety of sport backgrounds and competition levels, took part in interviews to discuss their capacity to support self-compassion programming.
Interviews were transcribed verbatim and data were analyzed through
reﬂexive thematic analysis. Eleven coaches returned to discuss initial
ﬁndings in focus groups to reﬁne ﬁnal themes. Four themes were generated: (1) coaches are spread thin, (2) support athletes within Safe Sport
guidelines, (3) taking accountability for team culture, and (4) integration
needs to be (a) simple and easy, and (b) supported by experts and
educational resources. Overall, coaches were supportive of integrating
self-compassion programming into their sport; however, they also acknowledge the challenges of having limited resources and expanding job
descriptions. Future researchers may explore access to support resources
for coaches, and consider requirements for coach training, including
assessing gaps in current available education on supporting women
athletes’ well-being and performance.
Funding source: Social Science and Humanities Research Council of
Canada, Sport Participation Research Initiative.
Resistance training and women’s body image: The impact of contextual factors
Maryam Marashi, University of Toronto; Marianne Rouleau-Tang, University of Toronto; Catherine M. Sabiston, University of Toronto
Resistance training (RT) is known to enhance women’s body image.
However, the inﬂuence of RT-speciﬁc contextual factors—encompassing
physical, environmental, and social elements—on this relationship remains
underexplored.
This
cross-sectional
mixed-methods
study
investigated how RT-speciﬁc contextual factors (e.g., gym setup, equipment, group versus solo settings, trainer/peer interactions, ﬁtness social
media) either facilitate or hinder the body image beneﬁts of RT for women.
The analytic sample encompassed 100 women aged 18–40 (Mage = 29.6,
SD = 5.2) with at least six months of RT experience. Using the dual-model
conceptualization of body image, participants responded to positive
inﬂuences and negative inﬂuences separately using ﬁve-point Likert scales
(ranging from Strongly Disagree to Strongly Agree) for eight unique
contextual factors analyzed as frequencies of endorsement. Additionally,
open-ended responses were captured for perceived positive and negative
inﬂuences of contextual factors on the experiences of RT and body image.
Open-ended responses were coded using qualitative description. The
proportions of participants who agreed or strongly agreed with positive
and negative inﬂuences of the eight contextual factors included: coaching
practices (59% positive, 15% negative), training environments (76%
positive, 26% negative), peers (74% positive, 20% negative), social media
(35% positive, 59% negative), RT programming (79% positive, 15%
negative), RT type (87% positive, 15% negative), RT volume (83%
positive, 21% negative), and “other” (45% positive, 13% negative).
Positive themes included supportive coaching practices, constructive
social media engagement, empowering social environments, and consistent and strength-focused training. Negative themes included unsupportive
and critical coaching practices, harmful media and social inﬂuences, and
intimidating and non-accommodating training environments. One theme,
the compliment conundrum, was identiﬁed as both positive and negative,
and highlighted the complex role of external praise in shaping body image
in RT settings. These ﬁndings highlight the importance of creating
supportive RT environments by fostering constructive coaching, and
empowering social interactions, while addressing barriers such as intimidating environments and harmful social media inﬂuences. Future research
should examine the moderating effects of speciﬁc contextual factors within
targeted interventions to evaluate their role in enhancing body image
outcomes for women engaged in RT settings.
Funding source: SSHRC.
Physical activity self-efﬁcacy online intervention for adults with obesity: An individual randomized feasibility trial
Seungmin Lee, Iowa State University; Kevin Lahoda, Lehigh University;
Nicholas Myers, Michigan State University; Andrew Horowitz, Binghamton University; Monika Freeland, Guthrie Lourdes medical weight
management
Persistent reports suggest that a signiﬁcant portion of adults with obesity
may not meet public health recommendations for physical activity (PA).
As an effort to address this issue, we recently developed a new online selfefﬁcacy-based PA intervention, named the Physical Activity Self-efﬁcacy
(PAS) intervention. PAS consists of six components (i.e., sets of behavior
change techniques), including a total of 30 introductory or post-introductory challenges (e.g., verbal persuasion about capability) based on selfefﬁcacy theory. The objective of this study is to investigate the feasibility
and acceptability of implementing PAS for adults with obesity recruited
from a local weight management center in the United States (US). The
study design is a prospective, double-blind, parallel-group individual
randomized feasibility trial. Three waves of data collection span up to
14 weeks. Thirty participants have been recruited from a local weight
management center in the US and randomly assigned to the PAS group or
the usual care group. The survey battery includes instruments designed to
measure demographic information, anthropometric characteristics, selfefﬁcacy, and acceptability. A research-grade accelerometer is used to
measure free-living PA. Data collection is currently ongoing. As of
December 2024, 73% of the participants (i.e., 22 out of 30) have either
completed the study (n = 19) or withdrawn (n = 3). The remaining

participants (i.e., 8 out of 30) are expected to complete the study by

Regarding feasibility, among the 22 participants, the participation rates
(i.e., the percentage of consented patients who provided at least some
usable data) are: 100% in Wave 1, 91% in Wave 2, and 86% in Wave 3.
Regarding acceptability, the engagement behavior from the PAS group

The participants reported addressable issues with PAS (e.g., need to make

wearing the device on their waist). The results are intended to inform the
plan for a future deﬁnitive randomized controlled trial with PAS.
The effects of different delivery methods of PETTLEP imagery training
on table tennis backhand ﬂick performance and self-conﬁdence
Yun-Shan Han, National Taiwan Normal University, Taiwan; Ting-Hong
Chien, National Taiwan Normal University, Taiwan; Jo-Yun Chen, Tzu
Chi University, Taiwan; Li-Kang Chi, National Taiwan Normal University, Taiwan
Imagery is a psychological process that replicates sensory experiences,
such as visual, tactile, and auditory sensations, to enhance athletic performance. The functional equivalence hypothesis in neuroscience suggests
that imagery supports motor learning by activating neural pathways similar
to those used during actual movement. The PETTLEP imagery model
integrates seven key elements—Physical, Environment, Task, Timing,
Learning, Emotion, and Perspective—offering a more realistic and effective training approach than traditional methods. This study investigates the
effects of different PETTLEP imagery techniques on table tennis backhand
ﬂick performance and self-conﬁdence. Twenty-nine junior high school
table tennis players were divided into three groups: a control group, a
progressive imagery group, and a regressive imagery group, each initially
consisting of 10 participants. One participant from the regressive group
withdrew, leaving 9 participants. All participants completed an imagery
ability assessment, followed by pre- and post-tests measuring backhand
ﬂick performance and self-conﬁdence. The progressive and regressive
groups participated in a four-week training program (three sessions per
week, totaling 12 sessions) combining imagery with physical practice,
while the control group engaged solely in physical practice. A two-way
mixed-design ANOVA (3 groups: progressive imagery, regressive imagery, control × 2 tests: pre-test, post-test) was used for analysis. Results
showed that both the progressive and regressive imagery groups signiﬁcantly improved their backhand ﬂick performance, with the regressive
group outperforming the progressive group. In contrast, the control group
showed no signiﬁcant changes. Regarding self-conﬁdence, only the progressive group demonstrated a signiﬁcant improvement in the post-test,
while the regressive and control groups showed no notable changes. This
study focused on novice athletes, and factors such as age, years of playing
experience, and mental maturity may have inﬂuenced the results. In
conclusion, physical practice alone is insufﬁcient. Combining both progressive and regressive PETTLEP methods enhances performance, with
the regressive method yielding better results. In addition, only the progressive method boosts self-conﬁdence, underscoring the importance of
integrating imagery training into athletic programs. Further research is
needed to explore the application of these methods to other populations.
Move together, improve together? Investigating the role of social
interaction in physical activity for executive functions
Cäcilia Zehnder, University of Bern, Switzerland; Mirko Schmidt, University of Bern, Switzerland; Peter Affolter, University of Applied Sciences,
Switzerland; Valentin Benzing, University of Bern, Switzerland
Acute physical activity beneﬁts executive functions (EF), including inhibition, shifting, and updating. EF are crucial for managing goal-oriented,
adaptive, and ﬂexible behaviors, such as controlling attention, switching
tasks, and mentally updating information. However, most studies have
overlooked that many physical activities occur in social environments,
where social interaction (SoI) may offer additional beneﬁts by pre-activating EF-related brain regions: In SoIs, individuals require EF for
controlling their impulses, regulating emotions, and staying focused
(inhibition). They adapt ﬂexibly to others’ actions, shift their perspectives
(shifting), and continuously update incoming information for coherent
responses (updating). Understanding the role of SoI in physical activity
could provide valuable insights for interventions to maximize cognitive
beneﬁts. Therefore, this study used a 2 x 2 within-subject design to
systematically examine the effects of acute physical activity (physical
activity/no physical activity) and SoI (SoI/no SoI) on young adults’ EF. A
total of 48 young adults between 18 and 24 years (M = 20.31, SD = 1.53,
50% female) underwent four 15-minute conditions, using the cycling app
Zwift. In the physical activity conditions, participants controlled the speed
of their avatar by pedaling on a racing bike connected to a smart trainer,
whereas in the non-physical activity conditions, a gas pedal was used. The
multiplayer mode was employed for the SoI conditions, and the singleplayer mode for the no-SoI conditions. EF performance in inhibition and
shifting (Stroop task) and updating (Letter Memory Task) was assessed
after the intervention using computer-based tests. Factorial repeatedmeasures ANOVAs revealed a signiﬁcant interaction effect of SoI and
physical activity, showing that young adults’ shifting performance was
signiﬁcantly lower after engaging in physical activity with SoI (η2
p =
.096). Shifting performance (η2
ps = .236 – .275) decreased after SoI
(without physical activity), with no signiﬁcant effects found for inhibition
(η2
ps = .001–.096) and updating (η2
ps = .001 – .025). These unexpected
ﬁndings suggest that acute physical activities involving SoI may impair EF
performance. Further research is urgently needed to explore the reasons
behind the detrimental effects and how different aspects of the social
context inﬂuence cognitive performance in the short and long term.
Funding source: This research was funded in part by the Swiss National
Science Foundation (SNSF)

Women coaches often face barriers such as gender bias, challenges in
balancing work and family responsibilities, and limited access to professional networks. As more women take on leadership roles in sport, such
obstacles hinder their long-term involvement in high-performance settings.

goal of improving the retention of women in high-performance coaching

included
self-identifying
women
(N = 11, Mage = 40.5) from diverse coaching backgrounds (e.g., sport type,

the largest amateur multi-sport event in the country. Two focus groups with
two participants (84 and 88 minutes) and seven individual interviews
(Mlength = 99 minutes) were conducted in-person or virtually during the
period of July-August 2022. Using narrative inquiry underpinned by
elements of critical feminism, three composite vignettes were created to
illustrate the shared experiences of the participants: “I’m ﬁghting to make a
better path for the women behind me”, “I’m still ﬁnding my footing as I

Social norms, physical activity self-efﬁcacy, and physical activity
participation among individuals with overweight or obesity
Jeemin Kim, Michigan State University; Nicholas D. Myers, Michigan
State University; Melvin Sangalang, Michigan State University; A. C.
Thayer, Michigan State University; Spyridoula Vazou, Michigan State
University; Bree E. Holtz, Michigan State University; Karin A. Pfeiffer,
Michigan State University
In the US, over 70% of adults are with overweight/obesity, which can lead
to various comorbidities such as cardiovascular diseases and type 2
diabetes. Though physical activity (PA) participation provides numerous
health beneﬁts, individuals who are with overweight/obesity represent one
of the least physically active groups. Among various barriers to PA
participation, individuals with overweight/obesity often feel that they lack
the capabilities to engage in PA (i.e., low PA self-efﬁcacy). One construct
that may help address this barrier is descriptive norms (DN), which are
deﬁned as one’s perceptions of others’ behavior engagement. Although
prior research has found that positive DN perceptions enhance one’s PA
self-efﬁcacy and behavior, little research has focused on individuals who
are with overweight/obesity. This multi-study project aimed to examine
whether DN perceptions regarding others’ PA behavior relate to the PA
self-efﬁcacy, and subsequently PA behavior, of individuals who are with
overweight/obesity. Adults who are with overweight/obesity were surveyed online three times over a two-week period to examine their DN
perceptions, PA self-efﬁcacy, and PA frequency. In Study 1 (N = 223;
Mage = 40.7 years, SD = 12.0), DN perceptions regarding the PA behavior
of the participants’ signiﬁcant others were assessed (i.e., proximal others),
whereas Study 2 (N = 225; Mage = 40.0 years, SD = 11.8) focused on DN
perceptions regarding the PA behavior of others with overweight/obesity
in the US (i.e., distal others). Exploratory structural equation modeling of
the data revealed statistically signiﬁcant indirect effects of DN on individuals’ PA frequency through PA self-efﬁcacy: indirect effect (proximal others) = .02, p = .017; indirect effect (distal others) = .02, p = .005.
These results suggest that among individuals who are with overweight/
obesity, those who perceive that others engage in PA more frequently tend
to have greater conﬁdence to engage in PA and are more likely to
participate in PA themselves, compared to those who believe that others
engage in PA less frequently. Overall, DN may be a viable target for
interventions that aim to empower individuals who are with overweight/
obesity to increase their PA behavior.
Exploring the factor structure of physical activity-related health
competence questionnaire that have undergone translation and cultural adaptation for English-speakers
Jimmy G. Bautista, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; Dimetrius Brandon, University of Tennessee, Knoxville; E. Kipling Webster, University of
Tennessee, Knoxville; Kelley Strohacker, University of Tennessee,
Knoxville
The physical activity-related health competence (PAHCO) model outlines three sub-competencies (workload control of physical load and for
affect-regulation, self-regulation, movement) that support individuals in
integrating physical activity in everyday life. The PAHCO questionnaire
was developed and validated for health promotion research in Germanspeaking populations in 2016 and has since evolved through the addition
and revision of items to broaden each competencies scope. Furthermore,
it was recently translated to English, accounting for both linguistic and
cultural differences. The purpose of this study was to explore dimensionality of the translated PAHCO items. An exploratory factor analysis
(EFA) and supplementary analyses were conducted using the 25-item
PAHCO questionnaire (5-pt Likert scale) completed by young adults
(N = 111, Mage= 22 ± 1.7) in a university setting. Sampling was suitable
based on the Kaiser Meyer Olkin test (.88) and Barlett’s test of sphericity
(p < .0001). Two to ﬁve factors were suggested based on multiple factor
determination methods (scree plot, parallel analyses). A principal axis
factor method was applied (restricted to three factors) with promax
rotation. Factor loading threshold was achieved if items met an absolute
value of .40, (range = .40 - .92) with no evidence of cross-loading. Three
factors explained 50% of the variance in these data. Eigenvalues for
factors 1, 2, and 3 were 8.4, 1.5, and 1.1, respectively. Factor 1 included
seven items speciﬁc to adequate alignment of needs and action
(e.g., ‘what to be aware of with how my body feels, so I don’t over or
under exert myself’). Eight items loaded on factor 2 correspond with
affect- and self-regulation (e.g., ‘can use exercise to relax’). Factor 3
contained ten items mixed from control, self-regulation, and movement
competence (e.g., ‘exercise to increase my endurance’; ‘what to expect
with different types of exercises’). The current set of PAHCO items can be
organized and interpreted as a 3-dimension structure; follow-up research
is needed to conﬁrm model ﬁt to the original sub-competencies. Further,
as generalizability in dimensionality across different populations should
not be assumed, future studies should examine differences in factor
structure in samples varying in age, physical activity level, or health
status.
The daily enactment of behaviour change techniques among physically active university students with physical disabilities and chronic
conditions: An experience sampling method study
Gabrielle D. Bedard, McGill University; Olivia L. Pastore, Algoma University; Jordan D. Herbison, Vancouver Island University; Jennifer R.
Tomasone, Queen’s University; Tayah M. Liska, McGill University; Marley
A.R.S. Mullan, Queen’s University; Alexandra Burns, McGill University;
Alan Jean, Queen’s University; Shane N. Sweet, McGill University
University students with disabilities experience low physical activity
rates, making it important to understand types of strategies used to help
physical activity participation. Behaviour change techniques (BCTs;
e.g., goal setting) are strategies designed to support behaviour change,
yet research regarding their effectiveness in physical activity is inconsistent. These inconsistencies may stem from limited knowledge about what,
when, and how various types of daily BCTs are used (i.e., enactment).
Therefore, this study examined how physically active university students
living with physical disabilities and chronic conditions currently enact
BCTs. To understand the behavioural process of BCT enactment, the
capability, opportunity, and motivation (COM-B) model served as the
guiding framework. Using experience sampling methods, 53 university
students (Mage = 22 years; SDage = 3.93 years) completed a 10-day online
survey via smartphone, covering a checklist of 28 BCTs, a modiﬁed 6item self-reported COM questionnaire, a validated physical activity
questionnaire, and contextual disruption questions. A descriptive analysis
summarized all measured variables. The relationship between demographic
variables,
COM-B
variables,
and
contextual
disruptions

(e.g., commitments) on BCT enactment was examined using a generalized linear mixed model. On average, 11 BCTs were enacted daily
(SD = 5.53, R = 1-27) and 22 distinct BCTs (SD = 5.14, R = 6-28) were
enacted over 10 days. The most frequently enacted BCTs included task
crafting with 52 participants using an average of 6 times over 10 days (M
= 5.78), goal integration (n = 51, M = 5.56), and ﬁnding meaning (n = 50,
M = 5.08). The most frequently reported contextual disruptions included
competing commitments (n = 34, M = 3.83) and ﬂare-ups (n = 35,
M = 3.31). Women were likely to enact 13 BCTs more than men (OR
range = 3.97-15.84, p < .05), and students with physical disabilities were
less likely to enact two BCTs than students with chronic conditions
(OR = 0.30-0.35, p < .05). Motivation and opportunity predicted the
enactment of 15-25 BCTs (OR = 1.03-1.62, p < .05), while capability
predicted the enactment of two BCTs (OR = 0.84 &1.28, p < .05). Results
of this study highlight that individual factors like gender, contextual
disruptions, and motivational and opportunity related factors shaped BCT
enactment. Future research should explore BCTs as distinct behaviours to
support physical activity and behaviour change for individuals with
disabilities. Funding source: SSHRC, CIHR.
Opportunities, challenges, and representation: How being Black intersects with the coach-athlete relationship quality. A qualitative study
Keith McShan, Missouri State University; Tyrus Ayers, Missouri State
University
The quality of the coach-athlete relationship has been studied extensively
using the 3+1C model, but limited research examines how race intersects
with these relationships. This transcendental phenomenological study
explored how the lived experience of being a Black coach inﬂuenced the
quality of coach-athlete relationships. Two research questions guided the
study: the role of race in the overall relationship quality and differences
within the constructs of closeness, commitment, and complementarity
from the 3+1C model.Nine Black high school football coaches with a
mean age of 39.22 years (SD = ±7.48) from the Midwest of the United
States participated in this study. Coaches took part in semi-structured
interviews lasting 60–90 minutes. Interviews were conducted using the
Zoom platform. Three main themes emerged for the ﬁrst research
question: opportunities and responsibilities, challenges, and navigating
identity, history, and difference. Opportunities and responsibilities included subthemes of teaching life skills, being a positive role model,
stewardship and representation, and relationship maintenance strategies.
The theme of challenges included subthemes of biases and exclusion as
well as intersectionality conﬂict. Navigating identity, history, and difference included subthemes of navigating the Black experience, the Black
lived experience, racial differences, and bridging cultures. For the second
research question, all three constructs of the 3+1C model included the
subtheme of no differences, with notable nuances. Closeness included the
subtheme of racial differences, and complementarity featured the subtheme of openness, particularly with Black athletes. The results from both
research questions highlight that the coaches in this study felt a strong
sense of duty, rooted in their racial identity, to serve as positive stewards
and representatives of Black coaches in their relationships with their
athletes. They also described overcoming biases and exclusion in their
relationships and emphasized their role in helping Black and White
athletes understand the lived experiences of interacting with a Black
coach. Findings highlight the unique contributions of Black coaches in
fostering quality coach-athlete relationships, particularly Black athletes.
These insights underscore the importance of representation and cultural
understanding in developing quality coach-athlete relationships. Future
research should examine whether similar patterns emerge in other sports
and among different racialized groups. Funding source: Missouri State
University.
Trait body appreciation and latent class physical activity trajectories
among university students
Kristen M. Lucibello, Western University; Catherine M. Sabiston, University of Toronto; Eva Pila, Western University
Body appreciation is characterized by a love and respect for the body and
its unique features and abilities, as well as a rejection of sociocultural
appearance standards and investment. Importantly, body appreciation has
been associated with higher physical activity levels and positive physical
activity experiences. However, the predominance of cross-sectional evidence cannot account for how body appreciation may relate to the dynamic
and ﬂuctuating nature of physical activity over time. Therefore, the present
study aimed to i) identify physical activity trajectories over eight months
among university students and ii) examine the role of trait body appreciation in predicting latent class trajectory membership. University students
(N = 234, Mage = 19.70 ± 1.98 years, 76.70% women) self-reported baseline measures of demographic information and body appreciation, followed by days of physical activity every other week throughout the 2021
Fall and 2022 Winter semesters. Using latent class growth modeling, four
physical activity trajectory subgroups were identiﬁed: i) low increasing, ii)
moderate stable, iii) high decreasing, and iv) high stable. Greater body
appreciation was associated with higher odds of being classiﬁed as high
stable physical activity relative to high decreasing physical activity, but did
not signiﬁcantly inﬂuence the odds of membership in low increasing or
moderate stable physical activity. Notably, gender was not related to class
membership. These ﬁndings suggest that body appreciation among highly
active men and women may buffer against physical activity declines and
instead support stable physical activity engagement over time. These
ﬁndings also suggest that body appreciation may play a greater role in
sustaining high physical activity engagement rather than initiating or
increasing physical activity among less active subgroups. This has implications for targeted intervention efforts particularly among highly active
groups (e.g., athletes), such that fostering body appreciation may support
sustained and positive engagement long-term. Future research that explores how body appreciation may interact with other body image
(i.e., self-conscious emotions) or identity (i.e., weight status, ethnicity)
characteristics would further elucidate subgroups that may particularly
beneﬁt from high body appreciation as a contributor to high and stable
physical activity behaviour over time. Funding source: KML is supported
through the Western Postdoctoral Fellowship Program.
Division I softball athletes’ perceptions on stress, coping, performance, and mental health
Rhianna Weall, Louisiana State University; Alex Garn, University of
Minnesota - Twin Cities; Ryan Hulteen, Louisiana State University;
Heather Allaway, Louisiana State University
Collegiate athletes in the United States face numerous stressors in both
athletic and academic areas of performance. This study aimed to qualitatively examine the perceived stressors and coping strategies used by a
sample of Division I softball players (N = 5) in the United States. Participants were recruited from one university team, and each completed a semistructured interview. These interviews lasted between 33 to 52 minutes
(M = 41:58, SD = 9.02). Thematic analysis was utilized to interpret and
analyze the transcribed interviews, including line-by-line coding to generate
initial themes. Triangulation methods were employed to ensure the validity
and accuracy of the interpretations of the data. Three main themes were
generated from the data: (1) Pressure is Privilege, (2) Identity Development
and Team Dynamic, and (3) Softball-Centrism. In the ﬁrst theme, pressure
was viewed as a positive opportunity to perform in areas of their sport with
varying levels of conﬁdence. Within the second theme, there was a struggle
with identity outside of softball that presented both challenge and threat
appraisals. The idea of being ‘just a student’ was novel to these athletes and

created a sense of fear toward the future without softball. However, there
were still challenge appraisals (e.g., planning, goal-making) in this theme,
where the athletes looked forward to life outside of college and their sport.
The third theme demonstrated passive coping strategies (e.g., avoidance or
procrastination) were geared toward academics, while active coping strategies (e.g., routines or positive self-talk) were associated with athletics.
Primary appraisals framed as challenges were associated with sport-related
stressors, while secondary appraisals framed as threats were routinely
associated with academic stressors. Practical implications from this study
may guide coaches, sports psychologists, and players to better navigate
stress management and coping strategies for collegiate softball players.
The association between different types of minor life events and
physical activity behavior among midlife and older adults: A repeated
measures investigation over a 12-week period
Heesoo Roh, Purdue University; Steve Amireault, Purdue University;
Kristine Marceau, Purdue University
It is theorized that exposure to minor life events (i.e., everyday situations
that can be perceived as challenging or stressful) can increase people’s
risk of avoiding or giving up physical activity (PA) participation.
Although different types of minor life events may occur simultaneously
and have a combined impact on PA, past studies of midlife and older
adults examined such events either in isolation or without testing
interaction between different types of events. Therefore, the purpose of
this study was to examine the association between different types of
minor life events and PA behavior among midlife and older adults. We
recruited participants aged ≥55 years, without severe cognitive impairment,
who
reported
being
physically
active ≥3
days/week
(N = 128; Mage = 65.79 years) through the ResearchMatch volunteer
registry. Minor life events and PA were measured via weekly online
surveys over a 12-week period. We used the Daily Inventory of Stressful
Events, modiﬁed for weekly reporting, to measure minor life events
(interpersonal-, occupational-, home-, ﬁnancial-, commuting- and healthrelated events). We used a 1-item self-report PA frequency question and
the Physical Activity Scale for Elderly to measure PA; scores from both
instruments were averaged. Multilevel modeling analysis (n = 87), controlling for age, sex, race, and education, revealed that on weeks when a
health-related event occurred participants tended to be less physically
active (within-person effect; b = -.297, 95% CI [-0.460, -0.134];
p = .0004). Moreover, the within-person effect of occupational-related
events interacted with the between-person effect of health-related events
(b = -.164, 95% CI [-0.270, -0.058]; p = .0025). On weeks when an
occupational-related event occurred, participants tended to be less physically active, and this relation was stronger for those who reported higher
occurrence of health-related events across the 12-week study period.
Health-related events (having an illness or injury) may not only relate to
PA behavior by having distinct and immediate negative effect on PA
participation, but also by piling up over time and interacting with
occupational-related events (facing an unexpected deadline at work).
Replication studies are needed to assess the reliability of the reported
ﬁndings.
Investigating experienced female USPORT coaches’ process of creating positive team culture
Mia Landry, Brock University; Phil Sullivan, Brock University; Erin
Sharpe, Brock University
It is generally accepted that culture is present in all sports domains, whether
it has a positive or negative impact. Coaches can employ several strategies
to cultivate a strong culture, but this development is not instantaneous; it
requires gradual growth and continuous nurturing by the head coach
(Martens et al., 2004). Positive organizational culture prioritizes recognition and fosters an environment where individuals are empowered to
develop, ﬂourish, and perform at their highest capabilities (Radu, 2023). In
sports environments, positive culture can related as an atmosphere that
enables athletes, coaches, and staff to feel empowered to develop and
perform at their highest potential. Women are notoriously under-represented in coaching, particularly at the highest competitive levels (Division
for the Advancement of Women of the United Nations Secretariat, 2008;
Fasting & Pﬁster, 2000). This under-representation naturally results in a
lack of literature focusing speciﬁcally on female coaches and their unique
experiences. The current study aims to illuminate the strategies employed
by USPORT female head coaches to build and maintain a positive team
culture given their experience and prior achievements in coaching. Two
centralizing questions guiding this research are: (1) How do experienced
female USPORTS head coaches deﬁne and understand positive team
culture, and what are its core characteristics? (2) How do these coaches
create and implement this culture within their programs? A total of 10
female intercollegiate coaches will participate in this study. To analyze the
interview data, a thematic analysis approach (Braun & Clarke, 2006) is
employed, organizing codes and themes in response to the research
questions, interpreting the data through a relativist ontological position.
Based on four individual interviews conducted so far, several key themes
have emerged among the participants that promote a positive team culture
within sports programs. These include Learning and Development, CoachAthlete Relationships, Player Characteristics/Recruitment, and Safe Environments. Upon completion, the research will provide a deeper understanding of the deﬁnition and processes that shape and sustain positive
team culture from experienced female USPORT coaches’ perspectives.
Teamwork and well-being in motorsport: Insights from a season-long
case study
Christopher R. Hill, California State University, San Bernardino; Christine E.
Pacewicz, Miami University; David P. Ferguson, Michigan State University
Professional motorsport has unique psychological demands that can
impact the health and well-being of athletes involved. To date, the
connections between mental health outcomes and teamwork in this unique
context have yet to be examined. Utilizing real-world data collected from
two professional drivers with over a decade of experience, this mixedmethods case study investigated changes in teamwork perceptions and
mental health outcomes across a professional motorsport season. The
Athlete Burnout Questionnaire and BBC Well-Being Scale were administered at three key points during the season (pre-season, mid-season, and
post-season) and in-depth interviews were conducted at the end of the
season. Quantitative results indicated that teamwork perceptions increased
from pre-season to mid-season but decreased from mid-season to postseason. Scores for burnout were relatively low across the season for both
drivers while perceived well-being was relatively high across the season.
Thematic analysis of qualitative data revealed links between perceived
teamwork dynamics and the drivers’ mental health, highlighting higher
perceptions of effective teamwork led drivers to experience fewer negative
mental health outcomes across the season. This study contributes to the
limited body of research on the mental health of motorsport athletes by
providing insights derived from real-world, longitudinal data. It underscores the importance of psychological support strategies tailored to the
motorsport context, where teamwork is both a performance enhancer and a
potential stressor. Findings offer valuable implications for sports practitioners seeking to optimize mental health and team cohesion within highpressure competitive environments. Finally, the study illustrates how
mixed-methods can be used to capture the complex interplay between
psychological outcomes in elite sport, while minimizing research demands
placed on high-performance athletes.

Early experiences with sport and gender in the southeast US
Kayleigh Hart, University of Tennessee; Jedediah E. Blanton, University
of Tennessee; Leia Cain, University of Tennessee
As research aims to understand potential for youth development through
sport, it is important to consider gender in exploring experiential
differences. Previous literature has found that gender stereotype beliefs
were consistently related to sport participation in youth athletes (Cormack & Hand, 2020) and girls often have a narrower window of
opportunity to participate in sports (Sabo, 2009). While these studies
illuminate important relationships, quantitative measures alone fail to
describe mechanisms behind gendered experiences in detail. Qualitative
interviews may provide more thorough insight to sport and gender
experiences. The purpose of this study was to understand common
themes from young adult stories discussing sport and gender while
exploring the culture of youth sport in the southeast US. By localizing
stories to a particular region, participants can situate their experiences
within the nuances of southeast culture. Participants in this study were
18-30 years old and had participated in youth sport (age 7-18) in one of
the 13 southeast states in the US. Results from 24 interviews (14
women, 1 non-binary, 9 men) were analyzed using Feminist Standpoint
Theory as the guiding framework, focusing on elevating the voices of
those underrepresented in sport spaces (i.e., non-men) by inductively
analyzing their interviews ﬁrst. Once themes were ﬁnalized for those 15
participants, the 9 men’s interviews were deductively coded using that
framework. Results are reported in a narrative style describing how the
athletes view their sport experiences globally (e.g., intensity, cost,
status), through relationships (e.g., with coaches, family, peers) and
in the mirror (i.e., self-reﬂection). Within each of these themes, codes
were separated into gendered vs. general experiences and discussed by
ﬁrst describing experiences of the marginalized group and then supplementing with storytelling from those who typically beneﬁt from sport
power structures. Moving forward, it is critical to consider the intersection of gender with social, developmental, and experiential aspects of
youth sport to better amplify the voices of marginalized groups underrepresented in sports.
Investigating the effects of a novel therapeutic program administered
remotely on lower back pain and measures of locus of control
Susannah L. Reiner, Aletha Health/University of Kentucky; Lydia Roos,
Evolve Well Research Partners; Rachelle A. Reed, Reed Science & Health;
Christine Koth, Aletha Health
Lower back pain (LBP) affects up to 84% of adults, with 23% experiencing
chronic symptoms. Effective and accessible interventions promoting
autonomy are crucial to reduce pain and improve physical function. This
study evaluates the effects of Aletha Health’s “The Mark” on LBP
remotely, focusing on pain reduction and locus of control. This singleblind, randomized controlled trial included 25 participants (52.0% women,
Mage= 39 years, SD = 6.8). The experimental group used Aletha Health’s
“The Mark”, a novel muscle tension self-release tool (SRT) on the psoas
and iliacus muscles for 90 seconds, 3 times per week for 4 weeks. The
control group performed standard stretching exercises (SSE) targeting the
same muscles using the same duration and frequency. Outcomes were
measured at baseline, post-intervention, and after a 2-week washout
period, using the numeric pain rating scale and the Multidimensional
Health Locus of Control instrument. The SRT group reported acute
reduction in pain, with a signiﬁcant 27% decrease in pain from baseline
(M = 2.6, SD = 1.6) to post-session (M = 1.9, SD=1.2), (t(13) = -2.62,
p = 0.021, Cohen’s d = -0.70). Chronic changes showed a 19% sustained
pain reduction (p = 0.046), lasting into the washout period, not observed in
the SSE group. For the SRT group, there was a statistically signiﬁcant
increase of internal locus of control from baseline (M = 4.5, SD = 0.9) to
follow-up (M = 4.8, SD = 0.8), t(12) = 2.38, p = .035, Cohen’s d = 0.66,
95% CI [0.05, 1.25], with no signiﬁcant change observed in the SSE group.
The novel device demonstrated both acute and long-term beneﬁts for
individuals with LBP, including signiﬁcant pain reduction and increased
back pain-related internal locus of control. These ﬁndings suggest that The
Mark may be an effective tool for managing LBP, helping participants feel
more empowered and in control in their LBP-reduction program.
Funding source: Aletha Health.
Go with the ﬂow: Exploring ﬂow state experiences of male and female
mountain biking, river sport, and snow sport athletes
Chris Wing, Appalachian State University; Kimberly Fasczewski, Appalachian State University; Kelly Okonek, Appalachian State University;
Andrey Sanko, Appalachian State University; Abi Cameron, Appalachian
State University
Flow is a state of consciousness associated with enhanced performance and
accelerated learning; both attributes are critical to progressing in action
sports. An individual in a “ﬂow state” is automatically performing
physically and cognitively at the peak of their ability. It is well understood
that physiological sex differences exist for various aspects of human
performance. However, the impact of gender on how an individual
experiences ﬂow is not well understood. This study aimed to investigate
how female-identifying and male-identifying athletes experienced ﬂow in
three male-dominated adventure sports: mountain biking, river sports, and
snow sports. Data were collected from 644 athletes (340 mountain bike,
159 river sport, 145 snow sports) using a self-report survey consisting of
established measures. Results indicated that overall, female athletes reported attaining higher levels of ﬂow state than male athletes (t = - 8.568,
p < .001). There was no difference among sports in ﬂow state experiences
(F(2) = 1.209, p < .299). Examining gender by sport, there was a signiﬁcant
difference in ﬂow state in mountain biking (F(2,337) = 7.332, p < .001;
male = 16.6042, female = 18.1728), and in snow sports (F(2,142) =
84.608, p = .003; male = 13.2805, female = 15.3548). There was no difference between male and female ﬂow states in river sports (F(3,155) =
22.138, p = .078). These results suggest that gender differences in ﬂow do
exist in mountain bike and snow sport athletes, but these differences do not
extend to river sport athletes. Interestingly, contrary to previous ﬁndings
suggesting that male athletes in other extreme sports experience ﬂow at
higher levels than female athletes, the female athletes in the current study
reported higher levels of ﬂow. It is probable that these differences are not
solely due to gender but might also result from additional contributing
factors such as social and collaborative contexts, goal orientation, motivation, and speciﬁc environmental antecedents. Future research should
explore these additional factors to better understand the underlying
mechanisms that contribute to gender differences in ﬂow experiences
within adventure sports.
An update on the association between sports self-image and performance: A mixed-methods assessment of adult athletes
Andrew Gavigan, California State University, Northridge; Anna Chiara
Di berardino, California State University, Northridge; Mark P Otten,
California State University, Northridge; Jessica Lewis, California State
University, Northridge
A history of literature exists on the relationships between body image,
athlete psychology, and sport or exercise performance. Previous results
from our lab suggest that among college student-athletes, self-image and
performance may be connected even more strongly for males than for
females. The following study intended to test whether these ﬁndings would
hold in a sample of amateur adult athletes and ﬁtness participants, from the
general population. A sample of U.S. adults (N = 57; 31 female, 26 male;

Mage = 44.30; SD = 11.47) were recruited to take an online, mixed-methods
survey. Participants were asked to self-assess current performance in their
primary sport or ﬁtness modality. Self-image was assessed using the 9item, short version of the Body Self-Image Questionnaire. Respondents
also completed the Traditional Masculinity-Femininity Scale. For our
sample, three self-image items correlated signiﬁcantly with performance
for females (“My body looks good”, r = .699, p < .001; “My body is
healthy”, r = .481, p = .006; “I’m usually well-dressed”, r = .424, p = .018).
Only one self-image item was related signiﬁcantly to performance for
males (“My body is healthy”, r = .434, p = .027). Thus, results suggest a
different pattern of results for adult athletes than previously found for
college student-athletes. For adult females, those who felt more feminine
reported better performance (r = .507, p = .004). For adult males, feelings
of masculinity and femininity did not relate signiﬁcantly to performance.
Qualitative responses to two open-ended prompts largely corroborated the
above quantitative ﬁndings. Females cited feeling good about their body as
a way of boosting their conﬁdence, for the purpose of performing better.
Meanwhile, one 47-year-old male said, “I can’t just look strong : : : I have
to actually BE strong!” Overall, the results of this survey conﬁrm that
perceptions of one’s own body image and body health often relate to
performance. For adult females, this relationship may have been stronger,
whereas previously among student-athletes, we had found it to be more
important for males. Thus, practitioners are encouraged to tailor their
health, wellness and performance services to participants’ age, gender and
situation.
Weight-related body image & physical activity engagement in adolescent girls: A latent proﬁle analysis
Megan Sutton, Western University; Kristen M. Lucibello, Western University; Eva Pila, Western University
Various negative body image (BI) constructs (i.e., body- and weightrelated self-conscious emotions, self-objectiﬁcation, weight bias internalization) are salient predictors of physical activity (PA) engagement and
often act as a barrier to PA participation. Empirical evidence suggests that
weight stigmatizing experiences can negatively impact these negative BI
constructs, which contribute to poor PA outcomes. Notably, adolescent
girls encounter signiﬁcantly more weight-stigmatizing experiences and
report disproportionately lower PA engagement than boys. Taken together,
adolescent girls are at an elevated risk for developing negative BI outcomes
and worse PA behaviours. While negative BI indicators often co-occur,
person-centered approaches identify distinct patterns among these indicators, providing more nuanced understanding of how negative BI may
inﬂuence PA behaviours. The present study aimed to i.) identify unique
negative BI proﬁles among adolescent girls and ii.) determine whether PA
levels differ between proﬁles. Adolescent females (N = 485; Mage= 16.14,
SDage = 1.61) completed a self-report survey assessing weight-related selfconscious emotions (i.e., shame, embarrassment, envy, and guilt), selfobjectiﬁcation, weight bias internalization, and physical activity. Latent
Proﬁle Analysis revealed a 6-proﬁle solution (Entropy = .81) with individuals characterized as; i) severe negative BI (16.3%), ii) high negative BI
(15.7%), iii) average negative BI-high guilt (11.5%), iv) average negative
BI (22.3%), v) low negative BI-high guilt (7.6%), and vi) low negative BI
(26.6%). Participants in the severe negative BI, high negative BI, and low
negative BI-high guilt proﬁles reported signiﬁcantly lower levels of PA
than those in the average negative BI-high guilt, negative BI emotions, and
low negative BI proﬁles. These results support the notion that high levels
of negative BI are associated with poorer PA engagement. Further, this
analysis highlighted the complexities of weight-related self-conscious
emotions, namely weight-related guilt, where unique emotional interactions related to distinct PA behaviours. Negative BI constructs tend to
coexist,
though
understanding
patterns
allows
one
to
more
comprehensively evaluate key emotional predictors of PA outcomes. This
study emphasizes the intricacies of negative BI indicators in adolescent
girls and may inform the development of interventions, programs, and
promotional material to improve PA outcomes in this population.
Challenge accepted? The relationship between readiness to engage in
challenging practice and practice quality for female competitive youth
soccer players
Jost Hausendorf, University of British Columbia; Carrie M. Peters,
University of British Columbia; Porter Trevisan, University of British
Columbia; Nicola J. Hodges, University of British Columbia
Two major motor skill development and learning frameworks; Deliberate
Practice Theory (Ericsson et al., 1993) and the Challenge Point Framework
(Guadagnoli & Lee, 2004), highlight the importance of optimizing challenge during practice to promote skill acquisition. In a study examining
developmental activities of female soccer players, hours in childhood
practice rated as moderate-to-high in challenge was the only variable to
distinguish between adult National and University athletes (Hendry et al.,
2019). Yet, it remains unclear how factors such as skill level, developmental period, and individual readiness and engagement in deliberate/
challenging practice, are related to perceptions of challenge throughout
sport development. In this study, female youth soccer players (N = 204,
MAge= 15.4, SD = 1.4 yr) completed a newly developed, Challenge, Effort
and Purposeful practice questionnaire (CEPP), comprised of 8 subscales
(Ma =.89). They also provided their soccer participation history, including
annual practice challenge ratings. We ﬁrst conﬁrmed that the CEPP scores
were sensitive to skill, with the higher skill groups scoring higher than
lower skill groups (ps < .01). We then compared historical practice challenge ratings in a linear mixed model, with skill level (National, Premier,
Competitive, Grassroots), developmental period (Elementary: gr1-6; Secondary: gr8-12) and CEPP scores as ﬁxed effects. There were main effects
of skill and CEPP score (ps < .05), where the higher skill groups and those
with higher CEPP scores generally rated practice as more challenging than
the lower skill groups and lower CEPP scores. There were also interactions
with skill and developmental period, due to increases in challenge perceptions from Elementary to Secondary years, but mostly for the lower skill
athletes. Although these data partially conform with evidence that individuals competing at a higher skill level experience more challenge during
practice than those in less competitive levels; the conclusion was dependent on current challenge readiness and developmental period. Further
analysis is underway to explore these relationships and investigation is
needed to validate if and how challenge perceptions match the degree of
difﬁculty chosen/perceived by a coach and whether this relates to continued participation or successful transitions into adult levels.
Funding source: This research was supported by a SSHRC (Social
Sciences and Humanities Research Council of Canada) insight grant
awarded to NJH and the Sport Participation Research Initiative (SPRI;
Sport Canada).
What’s happening with gym culture? Exploring emerging adults’

Alex Engel, University of Lethbridge; Alexa Fowler, University of Lethbridge; Scott Rathwell, University of Lethbridge; Paige Pope, University
of Lethbridge

establishing enduring health behaviours, there have been continued reports
of an alarming number of individuals who do not meet recommended

for exercise, where social dynamics, including the presence of other

patrons, can shape perceptions and signiﬁcantly inﬂuence exercise engagement. Guided by Social Cognitive Theory the purpose of this study
was to understand what factors contribute to the debilitative presence of
others at the gym among emerging adult women. To be included in this
study, individuals had to identify as women between the ages of 18 and 25
who had exercised within a public ﬁtness facility within the 6-months prior
to recruitment. All participants (n = 10) completed a brief eligibility survey
before continuing to their own one-on-one semi-structured interview.
During the interviews, participants were asked about their exercise habits,
as well as their values, expectations, and experiences involving others in a
gym. AI-generated images of potential gym-goers were used to facilitate
the conversation and help participants identify factors they perceived to
hinder their exercise experience. Through the lens of a critical realist, a
thematic analysis of the interviews was conducted to inductively produce
the following preliminary themes: (a) Stereotyping; (b) Debilitative coping
strategies; (c) Insecurities; (d) Personal standards; and (e) Judgment from
limited understanding. This study demonstrated that the debilitating
presence of others in a gym environment is multifaceted, extending from
one’s own internal biases and emotional capacity to external impacts of the
social world. Moreover, implications of this study include proposed
adjustments to various aspects of the public gym environment (ex. gym
staff, layout, resources, safety), potential personal strategies supportive to
one’s exercise experience, suggested ways for gym patrons to be facilitative and not debilitative to other’s experiences, and future research
directions. Funding source: SSHRC; AGES.
Coaching behaviour change techniques to university students with
physical disabilities and chronic health conditions: Preliminary results
of a single-subject design intervention
Natara J. Ng, McGill University; Gabrielle D. Bedard, McGill University;
Marley A. R. S. Mullan, Queen’s University; Tayah M. Liska, McGill
University; Olivia L. Pastore, McGill University; Cindy Li, McGill
University; Audrey C. Wong, Queen’s University; Jennifer R. Tomasone,
Queen’s University; Jordan D. Herbison, Vancouver Island University;
Shane N. Sweet, McGill University
University students with physical disabilities and chronic conditions are
not meeting recommended physical activity guidelines. Behaviour change
technique (BCT) enactment (i.e., ability to use a BCT in daily life) is
emerging as a key determinant of physical activity intervention effectiveness, yet is often overlooked during the implementation and evaluation of
interventions. To improve BCT enactment, a physical activity intervention
that shifts the focus from guiding participants through BCTs (e.g., helping
a participant set a goal) to coaching participants how to use BCTs
(e.g., teaching a participant the mechanics of goal setting) is needed. The
purpose of this study is to assess whether inactive university students with
physical disabilities and chronic conditions report changes in BCT enactment during and after a BCT coaching-focused physical activity intervention. The study followed a two phase, single-subject design across 10
weeks: baseline (2 weeks) and weekly BCT coaching sessions (8 weeks; 1
BCT per week). Participants (n = 2 completed, n = 3 in-progress) reported
the extent to which they used 16 BCTs via weekly questionnaires using an
experiential sampling software application. The attrition rate was 0%
(n = 2) and the survey completion rate was 100%. A total of 12 BCTs
were coached, 10 of which were coached to both participants. Using visual
analysis for completed participants, enactment of 9 BCTs increased from
baseline in the weeks following being coached and higher levels of
enactment remained for 5 BCTs at week 10. For example, “goal setting”
enactment improved in participants 1 and 2, who each reported no
enactment at baseline, but enactment 1-2 times/week and enactment about
every second day at week 10. Enactment of “re-structuring the social
environment” also improved in participants 1 and 2, who both reported no
enactment at baseline and enacting it 1-2 times/week (as of week 10). The
ﬁndings provide preliminary insight into the beneﬁcial effects of coaching
BCTs on BCT enactment within physical activity behaviour change
interventions. This work contributes towards generating strategies to
support individuals in self-regulating health behaviours by focusing on
BCT enactment within intervention delivery. Funding source: Social
Sciences and Humanities Research Council of Canada (SSHRC); Fonds
de recherche du Québec – Société et culture (FRQSC)
Stakeholders’ perspectives on the development and implementation of
an embodiment-based wellness program
Maryam Nourollahimoghadam, Washington State University; Sarah Ullrich-French, Washington State University; Amanda McMahon, Washington State University; Anne Cox, Washington State University
Adolescent girls continue to face disparities in access to equitable
physical activity opportunities compared to boys. This remains a signiﬁcant public health concern as lower physical activity levels, along with
common health challenges such as body dissatisfaction and disordered
eating behavior, put this population at higher risk for a lower quality of
life. The Find What Moves You (FWMY) program was created to address
inequitable physical activity opportunities for adolescent girls’, as well as
the need for prevention programming that supports physical, mental, and
social well-being through an embodiment-based approach. Recognizing
the critical role of stakeholder perspectives in creating effective and
sustainable programs, we aimed to inform the development and implementation of FWMY with input from key stakeholders. Therefore, the
purpose of this study was to seek feedback on the need for FWMY,
curriculum and communication materials, and barriers and facilitators to
successful implementation. Four focus groups were conducted with a
total of twenty-four professionals (2 male, 22 female; Mage = 42.75 ±
10.89) working in youth prevention programming across the state of
Washington. Focus groups were conducted over Zoom, with research
questions guided by the Quality Implementation Framework (QIF). The
transcripts were thematically analyzed using deductive and inductive
coding. Stakeholders’ responses were categorized into three domains:
visual and written communication, perceived value and need, and factors
inﬂuencing implementation. Overall, communication materials were
perceived as inclusive, with suggestions to enhance their relevance and
clarity for different targeted audiences. All stakeholders emphasized a
lack of programs addressing FWMY’s four main outcomes in their
communities, viewed current sport opportunities as non-inclusive practices and prioritized common humanity, belonging, and body appreciation as priority needs. Common barriers to implementation included
transportation and funding, while key facilitators involved collaboration
with existing programs, schools in particular, and the involvement of a
safe, and inclusive instructor. Conducting this study guided by a systematic framework like QIF was valuable for the continued development and
implementation of FWMY. It enabled researchers to obtain buy-in from
key stakeholders, develop community partnerships, revise program materials based on community needs, and highlight potential barriers and
facilitators to successful real-world implementation.
A RE-AIM evaluation of an Exercise is Medicine On-Campus healthcare intervention
Gabriella G. C. Senior, University of North Florida; Jessie N. Stapleton,
University of North Florida
Recent national data indicates that less than half of university students meet
national physical activity (PA) guidelines for optimal health beneﬁts.
Exercise is Medicine-On Campus (EIM-OC) is a global initiative that
urges university healthcare providers to measure PA as a vital sign and
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S75
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 79] ---
refer insufﬁciently active (IA) students to qualiﬁed ﬁtness professionals for
PA counseling. This intervention, when successfully implemented, may
positively impact students’ physical, mental, social, and academic wellbeing. Accordingly, the purpose of this study was to evaluate an EIM-OC
intervention using the RE-AIM framework. Reach was assessed by
evaluating the number of students who utilized campus healthcare and
reported their PA levels via the Physical Activity Vital Sign (PAVS).
Effectiveness was measured by comparing the percentage of students
referred to PA counseling to the number of IA students. Adoption was
addressed by the representativeness of healthcare sites that agreed to
measure PAVS and refer IA students to PA counseling. Implementation
included how healthcare sites measured PAVS and referral protocols.
Maintenance involved tracking the sustainability of interventions at
healthcare sites. PA and referral data were collected retrospectively from
electronic medical records from one healthcare site. Students not meeting
the national PA guidelines were categorized as IA. Within the ﬁrst and last
semesters of intervention, 2,534 students (Mage = 21.67 ± 4.32 years) visited campus healthcare services, and, of these students, 57.34% were IA.
Referrals of IA students for PA counseling signiﬁcantly increased from
1.4% in the ﬁrst semester to 13.9% in the latest semester (z = -8.32,
p < .001). Both campus physical and mental healthcare sites adopted the
PAVS and referral intervention. However, the implementation of PA
measurement varied between the sites. One site incorporated PAVS into
their online intake, and the second conducted PAVS via interviews. The
second site reported inadequate data, leading to incomplete records and
referrals. These sites have maintained the intervention for three and two
years, respectively. The future objectives are to standardize PA measurement procedures across healthcare settings and increase referrals of IA
students to PA counseling by healthcare providers, fostering a healthier,
more active campus.
Insights from a Canadian youth sport research collective initiative:
Recruitment and evaluation
Vanessa Coulbeck, University of Toronto; Sasha Gollish, University of
Toronto; Catherine Sabiston, University of Toronto
Engaging youth through advisory groups is essential to integrating their
diverse perspectives into decision-making processes. Despite increasing
recognition of youth advisories, evidence-based practices tailored to youth
engagement in sport remain scarce. To address this gap, this study focused
on the process of establishing a youth collective focused on transforming
sport knowledge, innovating practices, enhancing access, improving participation, and positively inﬂuencing sport experiences for girls in Canada.
The resulting collective (N = 8, consisting of 6 girls and 2 boys, aged 1014, with diverse backgrounds, plays a vital role in shaping the initiative by
providing insights into the unique needs and preferences of Canadian
youth. Recruitment and selection procedures were informed by equity,
diversity, inclusion, and accessibility best practices and guided by a
diversity skills matrix that highlighted representation across Canadian
geographic regions, varied lived experiences (e.g., cultural, linguistic, and
socio-economic diversity), and a balance of skill sets such as leadership,
collaboration, and creative problem-solving. This approach led to the
successful onboarding of the core diverse youth collective, and a broader
youth champions group. Onboarding procedures led to the co-creation of
group values, and approaches guiding collaborative processes. Over the
ﬁrst year, evaluation of the youth collective has identiﬁed best practices for
sustained engagement. Five meetings were conducted to gain feedback on
the research objectives, methods, and knowledge translation activities. A
comprehensive data collection included notes, audio and video recordings,
observation, and character canvases with data coded using content analysis. Key factors for youth collective engagement were identiﬁed as: (1)
time for rapport building; (2) adaptable and ﬂexible meeting materials and
activities; (3) opportunities for skill development, and (4) youth-centered
approach to support communication and participation. Early outcomes
show the youth collective’s signiﬁcant contributions to designing policies
and programs that are inclusive, equitable, and reﬂective of youth lived
experiences. Funding source: SSHRC Partnership Grant.
Taking the United Soccer League by storm: A comprehensive and
evidence-based performance program for professional soccer
Brandonn Harris, Georgia Southern University; Megan Byrd, Georgia
Southern University
The United Soccer League (USL) is a soccer governing body operating
men’s and women’s professional soccer. There are four men’s leagues
(Championship, League 1, League 2, and Youth League) and one
women’s league (W League). League 1 was developed to focus on
smaller markets (bringing professional soccer to approximately 75 million people previously without access to professional soccer; USL, 2025)
and player development. In 2019, a League 1 team was formed in
Statesboro, Georgia called Tormenta FC. The Head Coach and Ownership team of Tormenta FC recognized that player development consisted
of multiple facets, including mental training and well-being, sports
science, sports medicine, and nutrition. Thus, a performance team was
developed to provide comprehensive and evidence-based services to the
organization, which has continued to evolve over the last six years.
Mental training was developed by two Certiﬁed Mental Performance
Consultants (CMPC) ® via a multimodal package approach (Hanton &
Jones, 1999) after completing a needs assessment with the staff and
players. The program was created based on the feedback from the needs
assessment and recommendations from Thelwell and colleagues on using
psychological skills training with soccer players (2007). Mental training
components included team and individual sessions, practice and game
observations, and collaboration with the rest of the performance team.
Through this collaboration, the sport science component included daily
questionnaires that assessed sleep, stress, diet, and training load (DeJohn
et al., 2021). Other physiological metrics assessed markers of recovery,
such as vertical jump, heart rate variability, and perceived exertion.
Previous research has suggested the use of case studies as an effective
means for learning (see Anderson et al., 2002). Therefore, this poster
presentation will detail (1) the systematic implementation of the mental
training program, (2) the rapport and relationship building with stakeholders, (3) collaboration with the sport science and medical staff, and (4)
suggestions for best practices to implement such a program within a
professional sport organization. Additionally, recommendations for training program structure, ethical best practices when working in an organizational setting, and suggestions for working effectively with culturally
diverse groups of athletes (Quartiroli et al., 2020) will also be offered.
Bat ﬂips in the MLB postseason: What happens in the next at-bat?
Juan Alcala, California State University, Northridge; Diana Ruano,
California State University, Northridge; Mark P Otten, California State
University, Northridge
Outward displays of emotion in sport may be viewed by an opponent as
offensive, if taken personally. Sport psychology theory suggests such
offense taken may lead to an increase in arousal for the opponent, which
might translate to inspired performance by them if interpreted adaptively, or ﬂustered performance if not. Over time, post-home run (HR)
celebrations by a hitter in Major League Baseball (MLB) have been
accepted more as part of the game. Historically, however, pitchers often
saw these as violations of baseball ethics. The current study explored
what happens directly after a HR celebration, to see if changes in pitcher
effectiveness could be detected. Footage was viewed of each of the
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
