S76
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 80] ---
home runs hit in the 2023 MLB postseason (N = 110). Post-HR hitter
celebrations were rated on a scale from 1 (no visible reaction) to 5
(maximum celebration), and results from the next at-bat after the HR
were recorded. Of the HR that were not celebrated visibly (n = 51), the
following batters hit .238, with 2 HR and 8 walks. Of those celebrations
rated as 2 or higher on the scale (n = 59), the next batters hit .212, with 3
HR and 6 walks. Delving further, we coded a celebration as “unnecessary” if 1) it occurred in a game prior to the 6th inning, and/or 2) if one
team was leading by more than 4 runs, and 3) it was rated as a 4 or 5 on
the celebration scale. For our sample, there were only two HR that ﬁt
these more stringent criteria, and the batters that followed these outbursts went 0-for-2. Thus, we did not see any signiﬁcant changes in
pitcher effectiveness based on the size of the preceding post-HR
celebration. It is possible that these celebrations are not inducing boosts
in arousal for pitchers, at least not in the MLB postseason. Therefore, it
may be interesting to look also at regular season games, and for changes
across MLB history, especially since a larger sample of unnecessary
celebrations could be studied.
Aging Well: A comprehensive assessment of older adults’ needs and
experiences related to physical activity and healthy aging
Kimberly Fasczewski, Appalachian State University; Samantha DuBois,
Appalachian State University; Jared Skinner, Appalachian State University; Rebecca Kappus, Appalachian State University
Physical activity (PA) is crucial for older adults as it plays a pivotal role in
maintaining overall health and well-being. Unfortunately, most older
adults do not meet the PA recommendations set by the American College
of Sports Medicine. In recent years, community programming in many
areas has begun to address this lack of PA by developing programming for
older adults. The Appalachian Institute for Health and Wellness (Institute),
connects Appalachian State University to the community by offering an
Aging Well program that conducts age-based health assessments. Currently, once a patient completes their Aging Well assessment, they are
provided general recommendations for PA and other health behaviors, but
do not have access to any provided follow-up programming. The purpose
of this study was to perform a comprehensive needs assessment with the
current Aging Well patients to determine speciﬁc needs, with the goal of
creating a future sustainable PA program that addresses the unique
challenges faced by a rural population. Nine individuals who had previously participated in the Aging Well program (6 females, 3 males; aged 5978 years) participated in semi-structured focus group interviews, designed
to explore participants’ experiences and needs related to PA and healthy
aging. Results reﬂected a range of concerns, priorities, and insights from
participants regarding their health and well-being, highlighting the multifaceted nature of aging and the need for a comprehensive approach in PA
program development. The following themes emerged: health concerns
surrounding aging, social connections, barriers to PA, importance of PA -
including aerobic endurance, strength, and ﬂexibility, and feedback on the
Aging Well program, with each theme consisting of multiple subthemes.
Overall participants appreciated the Aging Well program but wanted more
options for supportive follow up programs. Given the suggestions provided by participants, future directions in program development will include
targeted exercise education classes, developing group activities to address
social concerns, and incorporating student engagement for multigenerational interactions.
Athlete burnout proﬁles: The role of goal orientations and affective
experiences
Alice Valdesalici, University of Padua; Erika Borella, University of
Padua; Marta Ghisi, University of Padua; Stephanie Fuller, Utah State
University; Alan L. Smith, Utah State University
Athlete burnout is a concern in the sport context because it can signiﬁcantly
undermine performance, well-being, and long-term engagement in sport.
Improved understanding of this phenomenon may result from examining
patterns of athlete burnout perceptions as they co-occur within individuals.
Relatively little work has adopted this strategy to date, with most work
instead examining athlete burnout perceptions as variables in traditional
analytic frameworks. In the present study we: (a) identiﬁed athlete burnout
proﬁles based on perceptions of physical and emotional exhaustion,
reduced accomplishment, and sport devaluation, and (b) examined how
these speciﬁc burnout patterns relate to athletes’ goal orientations and
affective experiences. High-level athletes (n = 178), aged 18-37 years
(M = 23.2, SD = 3.8), competing in individual or team sports completed
reliable and valid online questionnaires assessing athlete burnout perceptions, goal orientations (ego and task), distress and resilience. Latent proﬁle
analysis was conducted using mean scores on the three burnout dimensions
as the latent class indicators. MANOVA and multivariate logistic regression analyses were performed to investigate how motivational and affective factors varied across proﬁles. Latent proﬁle analysis identiﬁed three
patterns: a universally Low burnout proﬁle (n = 51), a Moderate burnout
proﬁle (n = 78), and a universally High burnout proﬁle (n = 47). Results
showed signiﬁcant differences in ego goal orientation (p = .006) and
distress levels (p < .001) across proﬁles, but no differences in task goal
orientation and resilience. Athletes with the High burnout proﬁle reported
lower ego goal orientation compared to athletes exhibiting the other
proﬁles, while athletes with the Low burnout proﬁle experienced lower
distress levels than athletes exhibiting the other proﬁles. Moreover, athletes
with higher ego goal orientation were 53% less likely to belong to the High
burnout group, whereas athletes with higher distress were 12% more likely
to fall into the High or Moderate burnout proﬁles. These ﬁndings show that
athletes reporting lower ego goal orientation and higher distress symptoms
might be more susceptible to experiencing universally higher burnout
perceptions. This suggests that coaches and sport psychologists should
support athletes in developing balanced achievement goal proﬁles and
should cultivate a supportive sport environment that mitigates distress to
prevent burnout and safeguard athletes’ well-being.
Identifying implicit biases of kinesiology students related to special
populations in physical activity settings
Kimberly Fasczewski, Appalachian State University; Sara Powell, California State University at Monterey Bay; Caitlyn Hauff, University of
South Alabama
Implicit biases are beliefs that subconsciously inﬂuence individuals’
thoughts and behaviors. Kinesiology professionals often interact with
special populations (individuals that require additional consideration based
on physical or cognitive characteristics) in physical activity (PA) settings,
and thus, it is vital to recognize and understand these biases for equitable
and culturally competent practice. As future professionals in the ﬁeld, it is
crucial for Kinesiology students to be aware of their bias and how it might
impact practice. Therefore, the purpose of this study was to examine the
implicit biases of current Kinesiology students regarding special populations in PA settings. Participants (N = 106, 72% female, 54% White, 39%
Hispanic or Latinx) from three midsize university Kinesiology/Exercise
Science departments responded to three qualitative case studies about
interacting in PA settings with individuals in special populations (an
overweight individual n = 106, a disabled athlete n = 102, and an older
adult n = 101) indicating affective responses, descriptions of characters,
and how they would respond in that given situation. Results indicated in
case study one, most students described the character as an overweight
(n = 68, 64.2%), White (n = 36, 34%), female (n = 53, 50%). In case study
two, most students described the character as a disabled (n = 54, 51%),
White (n = 34, 33%) male (n = 53, 52%), and in case study three, most

Physical activity, adjustment to college, and mental health among
ﬁrst-year college women
Ling Beisecker, The University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill; Patrick
Harrison, The University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill; Erin Reifsteck,
The University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Johna Register-Mihalik,
The University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill; Shelby Baez, The
University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill; J.D. DeFreese, The University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill
The transition to college is a critical life stage characterized by psychological and physical challenges. Women, in particular, experience signiﬁcant declines in physical activity (PA) levels and increases in adverse
mental health (MH) outcomes, including depression, anxiety, and stress,
during the ﬁrst six months of college. Adjustment to college, measured by
four key domains—academic, social, personal-emotional, and attachment
—is recognized as a key factor related to college women’s PA and MH
outcomes, yet its relationship with both PA and MH remained underexplored. This study investigated the relationship between PA and MH
through four key domains and total college adjustment score. We
hypothesized adjustment during the fall semester would signiﬁcantly
mediate the relationship between PA and MH outcomes, with adaptive
adjustment linked to lower depression, anxiety, and stress. Recruitment
goals were met with 204 ﬁrst-year college women (Mage= 18.8 ± 1.2)
completing the initial survey and 49.5% (n = 101) completing the followup survey. The survey included the International Physical Activity
Questionnaire-Short Form (IPAQ-SF) to assess PA, the Student Adaptation to College Questionnaire (SACQ) to evaluate the domains of
adjustment, and the Depression, Anxiety, and Stress Scale (DASS-21)
to measure MH outcomes. Participants experienced comorbid anxiety and
depression with prevalences of 29.95% at Time 1 and 28.7% at Time 2. A
two-model structural equation modeling (SEM) approach indicated a
simpler, holistic measure of adjustment, as measured by the total adjustment to college score (p = 0.02), provided comparable or superior explanatory power for MH outcomes compared to the four individual
subscales. Analysis of the four subscales indicated signiﬁcant mediation
paths from PA through personal-emotional adjustment to depression
(p = 0.03), anxiety (p = 0.03), and stress (p = 0.03). These mediation
effects were only observed during the initial transition into college and
were not present later in the semester. This pattern raises important
theoretical and methodological questions about the evolving role of PA in
sustaining MH and adjustment over time, suggesting the need for
longitudinal studies that follow students throughout college. Future
research should also aim to categorize and predict changes in PA and
MH during the transition into college, particularly examining whether
personal-emotional adjustment serves as a predictor for change.
Funding source: NASPSPA Graduate Student Research Grant.
Development and implementation of an educational workshop to
reduce weight bias in undergraduate Exercise Science students
Andrey A, Sanko Posada, Appalachian State University; Kimberly S.
Fasczewski, Appalachian State University; Sara M Powell, California
State University, Monterrey Bay; Rebecca M. Kappus, Appalachian State
University
Implicit weight biases are unconscious negative attitudes and beliefs about
individuals perceived to carry excess weight. Healthcare professionals
often exhibit implicit weight bias toward clients and patients, resulting in
treatment disparities. Educational strategies for undergraduate pre-professional health sciences students have proven largely ineffective in combating implicit weight bias. This gap in knowledge leaves students who aspire
to become future healthcare professionals vulnerable to holding and
enacting unconscious biases that might impact their care for patients and
clients in the future. Little research has been done speciﬁcally tethering an
intervention towards students majoring in Exercise Science. Therefore, the
purpose of this study was to develop and implement a 3-hour weight bias
reduction workshop using established weight bias reduction strategies
reported in past healthcare education literature. Twenty undergraduate
Exercise Science majors (8 male, 12 female) were randomly assigned to
either the weight bias workshop or a control scenario. All participants were
assessed on implicit preference for thin or fat people, and completed a
survey pre- and post- participation examining their attitudes and beliefs
about obese people. Results indicated a majority of participants demonstrated an implicit preference for thin individuals (n = 10), or no preference
(n = 6). Workshop participants demonstrated changes in the Attitudes
Towards Obese People scale, (t(9) = 3.26, p = .005), the Anti-fat Attitudes
Scale (AFA) total, (t(9) = 5.343, p < .001), AFA Fear of Fat sub-scale,
(t(9) = 1.993, p = .039), and AFA Willpower sub-scale, (t(9) = 5.588,
p < .001). Post intervention differences between the workshop and control
group participants demonstrated differences only for the AFA Willpower,
(t(18) = – 3.688, p < .001). The workshop proved effective in reducing
beliefs on the controllability of weight, consistent with limited existing
literature in a similar population. Future workshops should consider
expanding on “hands-on” practical experience where students might
experience real life scenarios and apply learned concepts, include a larger,
more diverse student population, and explore the long-term impacts of
workshops targeted at reducing implicit bias. Funding source: Beaver
College of Health Science, Appalachian State University - $500 Graduate
Research Grant.
Beyond the ball carrier: Peripheral vision in VR football defense
Božo Vukojevi´c, University of Bern; Svitlana Pinchuk, University of Bern;
Christian Vater, University of Bern
Peripheral vision plays a crucial role in team sports, allowing players to
monitor the ball and multiple opponents simultaneously (Vater et al.,
2019). This study investigated how varying distances between a direct
opponent and the participant affect visual behavior and decision-making in
virtual reality (VR) football scenarios. Eighteen experienced football
players participated in 3 vs. 3 VR counter-attack situations from a central
defender’s perspective. Participants were tasked with detecting wing
striker overruns while monitoring a direct opponent preparing to pass.
The distance between the direct opponent and the participant was systematically varied (10, 15, and 20 m). Decision accuracy and timing as well as
ﬁxation characteristics were examined. Head orientation and body movements were captured with a 14-camera Optitrack system to assess if
participants moved before or after the pass. Gaze behavior was differentiated between the monitoring (before overrun) and the decision-making
phase (after overrun). It was predicted that the closer the distance, the more
likely participants would ﬁxate on the direct opponent and use peripheral
vision instead of foveal vision to avoid the costs of late saccades. Results
revealed ﬁxation distance signiﬁcantly increased with direct opponent
distance, F(1.585, 53.889) = 181.428, p < .001, ηp
2 = 0.842, with signiﬁcant differences between all distances (p < .001). Gaze behavior differed
markedly between the monitoring and execution phases, F(1, 34) = 49.194,
p < .001, ηp
2 = 0.591, with ﬁxation distance decreasing from the monitoring to the decision-making phase. The percentage of ﬁxations on the direct

training to optimize gaze anchoring and peripheral vision. Funding source:

A phenomenological examination of the impact of social media on the
youth sport experience and motivation
Justin Morris, Louisiana State University; Melinda Solmon, Louisiana
State University
A phenomenological examination of the impact of social media on the
youth sport experience and motivation Justin T. Morris and Melinda A.
Solmon, Louisiana State University Youth sport athletes experience a wide
range of social inﬂuences that impact their motivation and overall experience in athletic endeavors. The relationships among these inﬂuences are
complex and interact with one another. Parent, coach, and peer relationships are all personal relationships that are commonly explored, but how
social media affects youth sport athlete motivation has rarely been examined. Social media differs from other relationships because athletes can be
inﬂuenced by people with whom they may or may not have a direct
relationship, and the use of social media has increased considerably in
recent years. Using a phenomenological approach, the purpose of this
study was to examine former youth sport athletes’ perceptions of how
social media inﬂuenced their experiences. Ten participants (5 male, 5
female) participated in interviews about various social inﬂuences during
their youth sport experience. Two higher order themes were identiﬁed in an
inductive content analysis: social media as a form of recognition, and
social media as a form of comparison. Former youth sport athletes
commonly used social media to gain recognition for their performance,
style, and knowledge, both at the team and individual levels. Social media
was also used as a form of comparison with other athletes, validating or
diminishing young athletes’ perceptions of their abilities. Athletes felt
validated when they saw athletes like themselves getting accolades and
attention. Social media use also contributed to a decreased sense of
competence and led to attrition when the athletes realized they were not
as talented or physically gifted as those they were watching on social
media. As the presence of social media expands and is further ingrained in
people’s lives, it is important to have a foundational understanding of the
role it plays in youth sport experiences. These results suggest the inﬂuence
of social media on perceptions of competence can facilitate or constrain
decisions to continue participation. Ways social media can be used to foster
perceptions of competence need to be further explored.
Care partners’ experiences of social support and resilience in a
dementia-friendly golf program
Kaitlyn Riddell, University of Calgary; Meghan H McDonough, University of Calgary; Pamela Roach, University of Calgary; Chantelle Zimmer,
University of Calgary; Cindy Barha, University of Calgary; Dallas Seitz,
University of Calgary
Caring for someone with dementia can impose signiﬁcant physical and
emotional stress, which can negatively affect care partners’ health, ability
to cope with adversity, and well-being. Regular physical activity (PA) can
reduce stress and improve well-being, and meaningful social connections
and support in group PA programs can serve as resources to enhance
resilience and support coping. Fore! The Love of Golf is a dementiafriendly, golf-based program for individuals with dementia and their care
partners to engage in together. Similar programs have demonstrated
positive impacts on participants; however, research on psychosocial outcomes for care partners is limited. This study addressed two research
questions: (1) how do care partners of individuals with dementia experience social support related to participating in the Fore! The Love of Golf
program? and (2) how did participating in the program impact their
resilience, coping, and quality of life? Qualitative interviews exploring
the lived experiences of care partners (N = 6; Mage = 72 years) and ethnographic ﬁeld notes were analyzed using reﬂexive thematic analysis. Three
themes were identiﬁed: (1) a place for us with the subthemes an authentically friendly space and together in the journey, (2) ﬁlling my cup with the
subtheme “I get to be the wife again”, and (3) “it’s ok”. Staff and
volunteers created a physically and emotionally safe environment by
providing authentic and meaningful support. This environment cultivated
a sense of community and fostered positive social connections, which
provided care partners, who often experience isolation in their role, a
meaningful source of social support and belonging. The demands on care
partners can be depleting. However, staff and volunteers provided instrumental support that alleviated care partners’ responsibilities and allowed
them to temporarily step out of their care-providing role and reconnect with
their partner in a more romantic dynamic. By observing other participant
pairs and seeing that fellow care partners were experiencing similar
feelings and challenges, care partners gained insight into the progression
of dementia and found validation for their own experiences. Dementiafriendly PA programs may effectively bolster care partners’ resilience
while providing a practical opportunity to receive social support. Funding
source: VPR Catalyst Grant.
Social exclusion, inclusion and physical activity decision making
Emily Brown, McMaster University; Caleb Zigby, McMaster University;
Steven Bray, McMaster University; Sheereen Harris, McMaster University
Social exclusion (SE) refers to experiences where people feel physically or
emotionally separated from others. Social inclusion (SI) relates to experiences where people feel belonging or connection with others. SE can
diminish self-regulation and motivation to engage in effortful physical
activity (PA), whereas SI can increase motivation to exert physical effort.
We examined effects of recalled SE and SI on affect and motivation to
engage in PA. We hypothesized recalled SE would cause negative changes
in affect and lower motivation to engage in PA, while recalled SI would
cause positive changes in affect and greater PA motivation. Undergraduates (N = 449) were randomized to one of three groups: SI (n = 158), SE
(n = 151), or control group CON (n = 140) and completed online measures
of affect separated by an autobiographical recall task where they wrote
about a previous experience of SE, SI, or a control task. Following the
second affect measure, participants completed an exercise decision questionnaire (EDQ) in which they made a series of hypothetical choices
between engaging in PA of three intensities [light, moderate, vigorous]
crossed with six durations [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60 minutes] for a ﬁxed
reward ($20) or a sedentary task for varying reward amounts ($2-$20).
Motivation for PA was measured indirectly for each intensity-duration
combination by calculating a point of indifference (POI), representing the
monetary value at which the choice to complete the sedentary and PA
options were equal. Recalling SE or SI did not change affect (p > .05).
Consistent with previous research the sample reported lower POI scores for
increasing durations and intensities of PA (p < .001). A signiﬁcant group X
intensity interaction (p < .05) was observed for motivation to engage in
moderate intensity PA. The SI group reported greater motivation for
moderate intensity PA. Findings indicate recalled SE does not de-motivate
PA, but recalled SI may increase PA motivation. Although it’s unclear why
only moderate-intensity PA was affected by SI, the recall manipulation

Cognitive outcomes of a dual-task intervention to support exercise
adherence: Findings from a randomized controlled trial
Sean Mullen, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign; Yan Luo, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign; D. David Thomas, University of
Illinois Urbana-Champaign; Xuan Lin, University of Illinois UrbanaChampaign; Maxime Lussier, Université de Montréal; Edward McAuley,
University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign; Arthur F. Kramer, Northeastern University
This study evaluated the cognitive outcomes of a novel intervention
designed to enhance dual-task performance and support physical activity
behavior change through multimodal cognitive-motor training. Addressing
the trial’s secondary aims, we examined the intervention’s effects on dualtask performance, a hypothesized mechanism of behavior change. We
conducted a randomized controlled trial involving 233 middle-aged adults
(M = 46.7, predominantly non-Hispanic [92.3%], White [81.1%], female
[65.6%], and college-educated [74.7%]), recruited across six waves.
Participants were randomized to either a Gaming group, which engaged
in interactive cognitive-motor tasks emphasizing executive functioning
and dual-task performance, or a Video group, which completed health
educational videos. Dual-task performance was assessed pre- and postintervention using an iPad-delivered dual-task paradigm. Missing data
(8.6%) were excluded, and one statistical outlier was removed to enhance
interpretability. Repeated measures ANOVA revealed a signiﬁcant SESSION × GROUP interaction, F(1,212) = 14.35, p < .001, η2 = .06. Groupspeciﬁc analyses showed that dual-task cost (DT cost) improved signiﬁcantly in the Gaming group, F(1,109) = 10.58, p = .002, η2 = .09, but not in
the Video group, F(1,102) = 3.68, p = .058, η2 = .04. No signiﬁcant
changes were observed in task-set cost (Video: F(1,102) = 0.006,
p = .937; Gaming: F(1,109) = 0.244, p = .622). Accuracy remained consistently high (98.87%), with stable coefﬁcients of variability, suggesting the
improvements in DT cost were genuine and not due to speed-accuracy
trade-offs. These ﬁndings support the theoretical basis of the intervention,
which emphasized general and exercise-speciﬁc dual-task training as a
mechanism to facilitate behavior change. The presentation will discuss the
cognitive outcomes of this intervention, the role of dual-task performance
in supporting physical activity engagement, and implications for scaling
this approach to diverse populations. Future research should examine the
long-term effects and scalability of dual-task training for improving
cognitive and physical health outcomes. Funding source: National Institute
on Aging.
Less snow, more problems? Youth cross-country ski coaches’ navigation of challenging seasons
Samantha J. Adler, Western University; Hans-Peter B. de Ruiter, University of Minnesota; Sarah M. Kaja, University of Minnesota
In 2023-2024, high temperatures and low snowfall disrupted climatedependent winter sports like cross-country skiing in the Upper Midwest.
Our previous survey study suggested youth coaches worry about these
climate-related threats, and some reported associated burnout symptoms.
However, many used various strategies to maintain their own and their
teams’ resilience throughout the season. Expanding on these quantitative
ﬁndings, we qualitatively examined youth cross-country ski coaches’
experiences in the 2023-2024 season to understand how climate concerns
impacted coaching responsibilities, team training, skier performance, and
coach and skier wellbeing. Recruited from the preceding survey, eighteen
coaches participated in individual, semi-structured interviews. Coaches
worked in rural and metropolitan areas of Michigan, Minnesota, and
Wisconsin. They coached a range of competitive levels (e.g., learn-to-ski
programs, high school teams, college clubs), had varying levels of access
to resources, and shared different opinions regarding the severity of
disruption to the season. The interview guide addressed coaches’ experiences with training adaptations, manufactured snow sites, and ski waxing,
as well as coaches’ perceptions of skiers’ mindsets and performances, their
feelings toward coaching, and their expectations for the future of the sport.
The primary researcher analyzed interview data using reﬂexive thematic
analysis, with other team members engaging in dialogue as critical friends.
Preliminary themes included: the necessity of adaptability; increased coach
and family contributions (e.g., costs, time); challenges with relying on
manufactured snow (e.g., safety, longer travel); threats to skiers’ enjoyment,
mindsets,
and
ﬁtness;
the
importance
of
team
culture
(e.g., prioritizing social connection over performance); silver linings.
Findings suggest that without snow and sufﬁcient resources, youth cannot
“do much of the sport they’re supposed to be doing,” which has implications for the growth of skiing. Despite fatigue and frustrations, coaches
remained committed to the sport and were proud of what they were able to
accomplish with youth skiers, in regard to skiers’ performances, skier
retention, and skiers’ ability to navigate adverse conditions. Factors
inﬂuencing nuance in coaches’ perceptions included their team’s performance levels (e.g., beginner, elite), funding, location, and culture. Our
ﬁndings warrant development of strategies to support cross-country ski
teams who feel like “boiling frogs” as climate change continues.
The effect of brief motivational climate intervention on self-efﬁcacy
within a high-performance military training setting
Mary Green, East Carolina University; Madison Kennedy, East Carolina
University; Christine M. Habeeb, East Carolina University; Nicholas
Murray, East Carolina University; E. Whitney G. Moore, East Carolina
University
Sport research has demonstrated consistent consequences of the egoinvolving
(i.e.,
abilityand
outcome
focused)
and
task-involving
(i.e., process- and improvement-focused) climates described in achievement
goal theory. For example, athletes’ self-efﬁcacy, a key predictor of performance, tends to be greater while experiencing a task-involving climate
compared to an ego-involving climate. Despite the similarities between elite
sport and military contexts (e.g., often performance-driven and high stress),
little is known about the effects of military leaders’ motivational climate
promotion on followers’ self-efﬁcacy beliefs during a job-related task. The
purpose of this study was to examine how efﬁcacy changes across time,
shooting position, and leadership condition during a Reserve Ofﬁcers’
Training Corps (ROTC) shooting task. Sixty-four ROTC cadets performed
a shooting task in three positions: prone (laying down), kneeling, and
standing. In Timepoint 1 participants completed the shooting task without
feedback from a leader. Approximately one month later (Timepoint 2),
participants completed the same shooting task while receiving feedback
from a senior cadet enacting either a task-involving (n = 32) or egoinvolving climate (n = 32). Self-efﬁcacy was measured immediately before
cadets began shooting in each position using a single-item measure from 0
(no conﬁdence) to 10 (complete conﬁdence). Analyses included a 3-way
ANOVA (time by shooting position by leadership condition), and this was
not signiﬁcant. Follow up tests indicated that for all participants at both time
points, self-efﬁcacy signiﬁcantly decreased from the ﬁrst shooting position
(Mprone = 7.86) to the second shooting position (Mkneel = 6.12), and from the
second shooting position to the ﬁnal shooting position (Mstand = 5.83). Also,
a signiﬁcant Time by Condition ANOVA indicated that self-efﬁcacy
changed from Timepoint 1 to Timepoint 2 in different directions for the
task-involving and ego-involving groups. Speciﬁcally, self-efﬁcacy increased for the task-involving group (Δ M = + 0.65) and decreased for the
ego-involving group (Δ M = - 0.18). However, the two groups essentially

reported the same level of self-efﬁcacy within each timepoint. In a highperformance military training setting, the presence of a leader can have an
effect on an individual’s self-efﬁcacy during performance, but it is important
to consider the motivational climate being fostered. Funding source: AASP

Mental health and ofﬁciating performance: A qualitative study
Nathan Taylor, Brock University; Philip Sullivan, Brock University; Paul
Gorczynski, University of Greenwich; Ian Cunningham, Edinburgh Napier University; Duncan Mascarenhas, Edinburgh Napier University;
Tom Webb, Coventry University; Lori Livingston, Ontario Tech University; David Hancock, Memorial University of Newfoundland
Referees are essential to sports, ensuring fair play by enforcing rules and
prioritizing player safety, but their responsibilities often come with unique
challenges. Referees frequently encounter abuse from players, coaches,
and spectators which has been shown to elevate stress levels and negatively
impact their mental health (Brick et al., 2022; Downward et al., 2023; Dell
et al., 2016; Webb et al., 2021). Moreover, 90% of football ofﬁcials believe
that common mental disorders can negatively inﬂuence refereeing performances (Gouttebarge et al., 2017). Despite the growing body of research
on the mental health challenges faced by referees, there remains a
signiﬁcant gap in understanding how referees’ mental health experiences
impact their performance. To address this gap, my speciﬁc research
question is: How do referees interpret the relationship between their
mental health experiences and ofﬁciating performance? This study utilizes
an interpretivist paradigm which understands that the social world is
shaped by individual perceptions and interpretations (Mayan, 2023). Semi
structured interviews (N = 5) were conducted with American football,
baseball, and ice hockey referees. Participants ofﬁciated at either a
recreational, competitive, provincial, national, or international level. Interviews were transcribed verbatim and analyzed using thematic analysis.
Findings revealed that referees face signiﬁcant stressors, including verbal
abuse and the fear of making performance errors. Both factors were shown
to negatively impact mental health and ofﬁciating performance. Sleep
disturbances, often caused by late-night games and rumination over past
calls, were prevalent and further impaired performance. Participants agreed
that mental health disorders inﬂuence ofﬁciating performances by reducing
focus, making unreasonable calls due to impatience, and impaired conﬂict
management because of heightened emotional reactivity. Additionally,
referees demonstrated an inconsistent understanding of mental health and
reported limited access to mental health resources. In conclusion, verbal
abuse, performance anxiety, and sleep disturbances are prevalent stressors
that contribute to mental health struggles, often leading to impaired focus,
emotional reactivity, and compromised decision-making. This study highlights the urgent need for tailored mental health support and education.
Addressing these challenges may enhance the well-being of referees and
ensure successful performances, in turn maintaining the integrity of sports
competitions. Funding source: The Social Sciences and Humanities Research Council (SSHRC)
It is all about control: The impact of affect-improving interpersonal
emotion regulation on regulators’ perception of control in an interdependent golf task
Rebecca Foti, Florida State University; Svenja Wolf, Florida State University; Katherine Tamminen, University of Toronto
Emotions in sport are common and impact psychological processes and
performance (Robazza & Ruiz, 2022). Therefore, athletes often engage not
only in personal but also interpersonal emotion regulation, IER, to elicit
emotions in teammates that align with their performance goals (Tamminen
et al., 2019). However, IER also seems to inﬂuence the regulator’s
emotional development and perceived control (Foti et al., 2022, 2023).
Cognitive-motivational-relational theory (Lazarus, 1999) suggests emotions develop from the appraisal of a situation and IER may inﬂuence this
process by adding a coping resource for the regulator. In this study, our aim
was to test whether the ability versus inability to provide affect-improving
IER, paired with the presence or absence of affective change in the target
caused greater perceived control in the regulator. We conducted a 2
(provision of affect-improving IER) x 2 (target pleasant affective change)
within-subjects experiment with n = 54 undergraduate sport psychology
students (n = 27 male, n = 27 female; Mage = 19.87) in which pairs of
participants completed an interdependent golf putting task. The ability to
provide IER to the target had signiﬁcant impacts on the perceived control
of the regulator F (1, 198) = 123.64, p < .001, ηp
2 = .38, with mean
comparisons showing greater perceived control in the ability to provide
IER conditions (M = 5.27; M = 5.38) than the inability to provide IER
conditions (M = 3.29; M = 3.47). However, the target’s affective change
(i.e., whether they actually expressed more pleasant emotions) had no
impact on the perceived control of the regulator F (1, 198) = 0.71, p =.402,
ηp
2 = .004. Follow-up analyses showed that this may be due to the
regulator not accurately perceiving the target’s emotions (although they
were convincingly displayed). Therefore, it seems as if athletes may be
using IER as a form of self-regulation through others, instead of to beneﬁt
their teammates and the key factor may be the regulator’s active engagement in IER, rather than the target’s affective change. Thus, to beneﬁt both
the self and others, practitioners should train emotion regulation, awareness, and intelligence (Pool & Qualter, 2012).
Older adults’ experiences transitioning to an independent living home:
The role of physical activity and social connections
Ravija Ramsamugh, University of Calgary; Meghan H. McDonough,
University of Calgary
Transitioning into an independent living home results in major life changes
for older adults. Their participation in physical activity may provide them
with opportunities to socialize with other residents; however, they may
encounter social barriers, which affects their engagement with physical
activity. The present study examined older adults’ experiences with
transitioning into independent living and their understanding of the effect
that transition had on their physical activity participation. We also investigated their perceived social support and social barriers related to physical
activity participation. The study population included residents (N = 8;
Mage = 87.6 years) of an independent living home, who had transitioned
into the residence in the last 5 years. Participants were interviewed and the
data was thematically analyzed. The transition into a congregate living
setting required participants to become adjusted to the organizational
culture which consequently inﬂuenced their physical activity participation
as well as their social connections. Transitioning into independent living
allowed participants to implement physical activity into their daily routines
due to the convenience of the residence providing regularly scheduled PA
programs. The opportunity to form meaningful social connections was
achieved by participating in physical activity programs at the residence.
The social support received from other residents and staff during physical
activity encourage participants to maintain their participation and engagement in positive health behaviours. The person-environment ﬁt also
inﬂuenced participants’ engagement in physical activity after they transitioned into independent living. Instructors of physical activity programs in
independent living homes should be aware of both the social and physical
barriers older adults may face when transitioning into independent living
homes, and how they interact during the major life transition of moving to
an independent living home. Further research examining the role that
physical activity programs in independent living homes play in addressing
the social support needs of older adults would be useful to better

A pilot study of social emotional learning-based school physical
activity intervention for underserved adolescents: Testing feasibility
KeonYoung Chung, MICHIGAN STATE UNIVERSITY; Chelsi Ricketts,
University of Toronto; Marcelo Cabral, Michigan State University;
Aarushi Lokhande, Michigan State University; Mahya Ardestani, Michigan State University; Leapetswe Malete, Michigan State University;
Spyridoula Vazou, Michigan State University
Social-emotional learning (SEL) competencies are essential skills that can
help children succeed academically. While physical activity in schools can
effectively facilitate SEL, there is a notable lack of interventions integrating SEL into physical activity programs for underserved adolescents. This
pilot study explored the feasibility of a SEL-based after-school physical
activity program for middle and high school students. Grounded in the
CASEL framework, the program focused on the ﬁve core SEL skills of
self-awareness, self-management, social awareness, relationship skills, and
responsible decision-making. Discussion prompts were designed to connect these skills to the physical activities practiced during the lesson. The
program was implemented 1–2 times per week over two sessions in 2024:
spring (3 weeks) and summer (4 weeks), totaling 13 days. Activities
included group-based games, sports, and tinikling dance. Feasibility data
were gathered using instructor logs completed after each 45-minute
session. Forty-eight students participated in the program, which featured
SEL discussions during 12 of the 20 sessions. Although all SEL skills were
discussed, emphasis was placed on relationship skills (5 sessions), decision-making (4 sessions), and self-awareness (3 sessions). Lessons were
rated as “OK” (65%), “Great” (15%), or “Difﬁcult” (20%). Challenges
were identiﬁed regarding acceptability and practicality. Students often
struggled to participate in discussions held at the end of sessions, as they
were eager to leave, leading to missed opportunities for reﬂection. A
potential modiﬁcation to improve feasibility is incorporating discussions
during activities, rather than after. Despite these challenges, implementation was good as the discussion prompts required minimal preparation and
were easily adaptable to students’ developmental levels. This highlights
the practicality and low cost of implementing SEL in physical activity
programs. From the perspectives of researchers and educators, integrating
SEL into school-based physical activity programs shows promise in
supporting the holistic development of socio-economically underserved
adolescents. Further reﬁnements to the program structure could enhance its
effectiveness and sustainability.
Expectancy-value constructs predict goal achievement and persistence
in one-off, project-based physical activities
Vista Beasley, State University of New York - Brockport; Katharina Neissl,
State University of New York – Brockport
One-off, project-based physical activities (e.g., long-distance backpacking
trip, sail around-the-world, summit Mount Everest, law enforcement/
military training academies, Ironman) involve goals to ﬁnish projects in
a set time period. Failure to achieve goals can induce negative effects
(e.g., atychiphobia, depressive symptoms, shame). The purpose of this
longitudinal study was to identify factors which contribute to goal outcomes in project-based physical activity. Per situated expectancy-value
theory, context-speciﬁc choices relevant to body composition, ﬁnancial
resources, and injury susceptibility were hypothesized to be proximal
predictors of goal outcomes. Self-concept of ability beliefs (e.g., mental
toughness, ability to accomplish goal) and subjective task values
(e.g., accomplishment, enjoyment, social interaction) were hypothesized
to predict choices. Participants (N = 157; 32.5% women; 53.5% overweight/obese; Mage = 36.68) were thru-hikers who set the goal of hiking ≥
2,000 miles of the Appalachian Trail in less than a year. Past thru-hikers
cited injury as a primary reason for failing to achieve this goal. Participants
completed pre-hike measures of choices, values, and expectancy beliefs
along with post-hike measures of goal achievement (i.e., did/did not
achieve goal) and persistence (i.e., miles hiked before quitting); 55.4%
achieved the goal. Choices signiﬁcantly predicted goal achievement,
χ2(3) = 17.5, p < .001, accounting for 16.2% of variance. Values and
expectancy beliefs signiﬁcantly predicted choices which increase injury
susceptibility, F(7, 149) = 3.70, p = .001, accounting for 14.8% variance.
Mental toughness was a signiﬁcant predictor of injury susceptibility
(β = .27), potentially explaining its lack of relationship with goal achievement. Survival analyses of goal persistence revealed a signiﬁcant effect of
body composition, χ2(2) = 7.21, p = .03, such that 32.7% of overweight
and 37.9% of obese hikers quit by the half-way point, compared to 22.2%
of hikers with normal weight. Choices that increase injury susceptibility
were signiﬁcantly related to quitting in the ﬁrst 400 miles, χ2(1) = 4.65,
p = .03. Future researchers may examine expectancy-value constructs not
included herein (e.g., cultural) to account for unexplained variance.
Findings may inform interventions and preparation to prevent negative
effects of failing to achieve goals in one-off, project-based physical
activity.
Losing streak vs. performance crisis: A case study on the (non-)
experience of a performance crisis in an interdependent sports team
during a losing streak
Stephanie Buenemann, University of Muenster; Charlotte Behlau, TU
University of Dortmund; Katherine Tamminen, University of Toronto;
Maike Tietjens, University of Muenster; Bernd Strauss, University of
Muenster
The term “crisis” is widely discussed—and the sports context is no
exception (for a demonstration of the relevance of crises in sports, see
the book edition by Strauss et al., 2024). From a scientiﬁc perspective,
performance crises in teams are deﬁned as lasting poor performance,
perceived as threatening, which cannot be adequately managed, and lead to
dysfunctional team processes (Buenemann et al., 2023). In contrast to a
mere losing streak, the evaluation of performance and the associated intraand interpersonal psychological processes are central. Investigating a crisis
is challenging due to the team dynamics and internal processes involved.
Using a case study approach, the research question addresses why a team
that is objectively experiencing a losing streak subjectively perceives it as a
crisis—or not. Case studies are suitable for questions that address “how” or
“why” phenomena occur, where the behavior of those involved cannot be
manipulated, and where context is relevant to the phenomenon (Yin,
2003). Semi-structured interviews were conducted with youth female elite
athletes (N = 12), aged 15 to 19, of an interdependent team sport. The
interviews took place between the end of February 2024 and the end of
April 2024. The season concluded at the end of April with 21 losses out of
24 games. The interviews addressed various aspects of team performance
crises with the interview guide and deductive categories developed based
on Buenemann et al. (2023). Questions focused on expectations, changes
over time, evaluation regarding challenge and threat, emotions, intra- and
interpersonal coping and emotion regulation processes, and team functioning. The interviews are being coded using a deductive-inductive
category system and analyzed through content analysis (Mayring,
2019). Currently, the results indicate that come athletes on this team
experienced a performance crisis that aligns with the deﬁnition by Buenemann et al. (2023). Initial insights suggest that in addition to competition
performance, a decline in training performance occurs. Furthermore,
inductive categories distinct to the youth context were developed. Results

Cheers! The dual role of post-sports gathering culture in Korean
university sports clubs: Its impact on identity and cohesion
Min jae kim, Yonsei University; Soo-jung Yang, Yonsei University; YongJin Yoon, Yonsei University; Sibak Sung, Florida State University; SeongKwan Cho, Texas A&M International University
Alcohol acts as a social lubricant, facilitating communication, maintaining
social functions, and symbolizing group cohesion and bonding. Recreational sports clubs are voluntary groups centered on sports and various
activities, where relationships among club members signiﬁcantly impact
participation continuity. To sustain these relationships, after-parties and
drinking activities play a crucial role in strengthening cohesion, enhancing
belonging, and shaping sports identity. While excessive drinking can harm
group cohesion, moderate alcohol consumption can positively inﬂuence
members’ sense of belonging, unity, and cohesion. Despite the longstanding tradition of after-parties and drinking culture in group activities,
limited research has examined their impact on recreational sports club
members. Understanding the dual nature of drinking culture—its potential
to foster harmony and its risks of weakening cohesion—requires an indepth exploration of club members’ experiences. This study investigates
the drinking culture within South Korean university sports clubs, focusing
on how students negotiate team belonging and identity through this
culture. Thirteen university students from a recreational basketball club
in Seoul participated in the study. Using qualitative research methods,
particularly a case study approach, data were collected through nonparticipant observation and in-depth interviews. The ﬁndings of the study
are as follows: First, the background and motivations for after-parties and
drinking activities were found to naturally and habitually create a group
atmosphere, enhancing camaraderie. Second, personal experiences of
drinking culture revealed both beneﬁts and drawbacks. Members shared
personal stories and freely exchanged opinions about matches, but concerns were raised regarding reduced athletic performance due to drinking.
Third, in terms of social cohesion, the drinking culture of after-parties
played a role in fostering shared understanding, strengthening social
bonds, and enhancing interpersonal relationships. Fourth, the inﬂuence
of drinking culture on sports team identity emphasized its importance in
helping members understand their reasons for participation and reinforcing
their roles and sense of identity within the team. In summary, the drinking
culture in recreational sports clubs serves as both a mechanism for bonding
and a potential risk factor, underscoring the importance of balanced and
thoughtful approaches to its integration into group activities.
“I’m engaged because everyone else is so engaged”: A multilevel
randomized control trial to show that autonomy supportive teaching
in PE enhances class wide engagement
Yong-Gwan Song, Pukyong National University; Chang-Ha Im, Korea
University; Sung Hyeon Cheon, Korea University; Johnmarshall Reeve,
Australian Catholic University; Hue-Ryen Jang, Australian Catholic
University
Autonomy-supportive teaching increases student engagement at the individual level. In the present study, we tested the multilevel hypothesis that
manipulated autonomy-supportive teaching also increases student engagement at the classroom (L2) level and that the development and enhancement of this classroom norm would increase student engagement at the
individual (L1) level. Using a randomized control trial research design with
longitudinally assessed dependent measures, we randomly assigned 52
Korean PE teachers and their 1,794 secondary grade level students to
participate (experimental group) or not (control group) in an autonomysupportive teaching intervention at the beginning of the school year.
Students completed a questionnaire assessing perceived autonomy-supportive, perceived autonomy-supportive classroom climate, classmates’ engagement, and individual engagement four times over the course of a 10-month
academic year. A doubly-latent multilevel structural equation modeling
analysis showed that the intervention increased both perceived autonomysupportive teaching (B = .62, p < .001) and perceived autonomy-supportive
peer-to-peer classroom climate (B = .55, p < .001). Both experimental condition (B = .72, p < .001) and perceived autonomy-supportive classroom
climate (B = .29, p < .001) predicted longitudinal gains in classroom (L2)
level engagement. Finally, when students perceived that their classmates
were highly engaged, students themselves reported very strong longitudinal
gains (over baseline) in their own individual classroom engagement
(B = .81, p < .001), a social contagion effect. This study addresses the “unit
of analysis” issue (L1 vs. L2) to show that teachers trained to be highly
autonomy-supportive increased student engagement at the group- or classlevel, which in turn produced engagement beneﬁts for the individual
students in these classes. Funding source: Ministry of Education of the
Republic of Korea and the National Research Foundation of Korea.
The impact of competition experience on sport commitment, sport
satisfaction and sport endurance intention in the sport for all: Moderation effect of types of sport
Ye-ji Choi, Yonsei University; Yu-jin Park, Yonsei University; Si-on Lee,
Yonsei University; Ryeong-Hyeon Park, Yonsei University; Jae-Gu Hong,
Yonsei University; Sibak Sung, Florida State University; Yong-Jin Yoon,
Yonsei University; Seong-Kwan Cho, Texas A&M International University
This study aims to examine the differences in sport commitment based on
experiences in participating in lifestyle sport competitions and the moderating effect of sport types on this relationship. It also aims to clarify the
mediating effects of sport satisfaction and sport endurance intention, using
sport commitment inﬂuenced by experiences in participating in lifestyle
sport competitions as an independent variable. To achieve this, a survey was
conducted on participants in lifestyle sport competitions across 43 different
sports, excluding three surveys out of the 258 collected ones due to invalid
responses, resulting in a total of 255 surveys used for analysis. Independent
sample t-test, two-way ANOVA, SPSS PROCESS Macro Model 4 were
analyzed using SPSS 26.0 and AMOS 20.0, yielding the following research
ﬁndings. Firstly, sport commitment was higher in groups with experience in
participating in lifestyle sport competitions compared to those without such
experience. Secondly, there was a difference in the inﬂuence of participation
experience in lifestyle sport competitions on sport commitment according to
sport types. Thirdly, there was a signiﬁcant relationship between sport
commitment, sport satisfaction, and sport endurance intention. Fourthly, a
partial mediating effect of sport satisfaction was observed in the relationship
between sport commitment and sport endurance intention. Through the
results of this study, it was found that sport commitment and sport
satisfaction contribute to increasing sport endurance intention, and it
suggests that participation in lifestyle sport competitions may have a
signiﬁcant impact on sport endurance intention.
The impact of physical activity on quality of life in women: A metaanalysis in Korea
Ye-Ji Choi, Yonsei University; Yu-Jin Park, Yonsei University; Si-on Lee,
Yonsei University; Ryeong-Hyeon Park, Yonsei University; Sibak Sung,
Florida State University; Yong-Jin Yoon, Yonsei University; Seong-Kwan
Cho, Texas A&M International University
Physical activity is an essential factor for both physical and mental
health, and it has been identiﬁed as a signiﬁcant contributor to improving quality of life (QoL). Researchers have explored various factors,
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S83
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 87] ---
such as satisfaction and self-esteem, to understand how physical activity
affects QoL. However, few studies have comprehensively reviewed the
overall impact of physical activity on QoL. Additionally, women’s
levels of physical activity vary depending on their life stages (adolescence, adulthood, and old age) and social roles (academic, professional,
caregiving), necessitating further research in this area. This study aims
to identify the factors inﬂuencing QoL and assess the strength of their
impact on QoL in women. Following the PICOS framework, data were
selected from four major Korean databases (RISS, KISS, National
Assembly Library, and DBpia) and a meta-analysis was conducted
using R software on 8 selected studies. Initially, 188 studies were
identiﬁed using keywords such as “women,” “physical activity,”
“sport,” “exercise,” and “quality of life.” After a ﬁrst screening based
on titles and abstracts, 106 studies were excluded. A second screening
removed 10 duplicates, 42 studies lacking relevant variables, 4 studies
with mismatched research purposes, and 18 studies with missing
statistical data, leaving 8 studies for ﬁnal inclusion. Fisher’s Z transformation was used to standardize the correlation coefﬁcients, and the
overall effect size was calculated. The overall effect size indicated a
moderate positive effect of physical activity on QoL (ES = 0.424, 95%
CI [0.347, 0.502]), which was statistically signiﬁcant, as the conﬁdence
interval did not include zero. Funnel plot analysis suggested a low
likelihood of publication bias, conﬁrming the reliability of the ﬁndings.
Heterogeneity was very high (I2 = 97.27%), indicating considerable
variability among studies. Subgroup analysis revealed that self-care
and physically-focused activities had stronger effects on QoL. The
ﬁndings of this study are signiﬁcant in integrating previous research.
Notably, many studies focused on middle-aged (15) and older adults
(34). Therefore, future studies should focus on adolescents and younger
women. Expanding keyword searches and including more studies in
future meta-analyses may yield more meaningful results.
Sedentary behavior and physical activity in adolescent girls: The
moderating role of social media encouragement
Berta Vidal Carnero, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Jaclyn
Maher, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Jessica Dollar,
University of North Carolina at Greensboro
Adolescents spend several hours per day engaged in screen-based sedentary behavior (SB) during their leisure time which can negatively impact
opportunities for physical activity (PA). However, the impact of screenbased SB on PA likely depends on the domain or type of SB adolescents
are engaged in. Social media is a dominant presence in adolescents’ daily
lives. In the context of PA, social media could promote PA engagement as
it may provide resources, encouragement, and modelling of behavior.
Alternatively social media may provoke feelings of shame or insecurity, or
create unrealistic expectations related to body size and shape which may
cause adolescents to disengage from PA. This study was designed to
explore relations between screen-based SB, social media, and PA. We
conducted a secondary analysis from a 28-day daily diary study involving
adolescent girls (n = 66; ages 12-18 years). Each evening, girls reported
their minutes of moderate to vigorous intensity PA and domain-speciﬁc SB
that day. Of the domains of SB assessed, we focus on the item assessing
sitting while using electronics for entertainment purposes. On days when
girls reported PA, they were asked to report facilitators that helped them
engage in PA including encouragement from social media (scored as yes or
no). Our ﬁndings indicated that on days when girls spent more time than
was typical for them engaging in SB while using electronics for entertainment purposes, they engaged in signiﬁcantly less PA on those days (β = -
0.12, SE = 0.03, p < 0.001). But social media encouragement moderated
this relationship (β = 0.32, SE = 0.15, p = 0.03). Speciﬁcally, on days when
girls reported being encouraged to engage in PA on social media, there was
a slight positive relationship SB and PA, but on days when girls did not
report being encouraged to engage in PA on social media, there was a
negative relationship between SB and PA. This suggests that although
sitting while using electronics for entertainment purposes may result in
lower PA, there may be a protective effect of being exposed to social media
encouragement to engage in PA while using those electronics.
Head impact accumulation and P3 even-related potential (ERP) in
female athletes during a sport season
Megan O’Brokta, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Praveen
Pasupathi, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Andrew Cornwall, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Bryan Montero Herrara, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Eric Drollette, University of North Carolina at Greensboro
Recent research has delved deeper into the complexities of concussion
and explored repetitive head impacts (RHI), revealing that RHI may pose
similar risks to neurological health as concussions. Furthermore, the
negative effects of RHI may be more pronounced in female athletes than
their male counterparts. Thus, the present study aimed to investigate the
change in behavior and brain indices of attentional allocation (i.e., P3
event-related potential; ERP) in female athletes across a sports season as
head impacts are expected to accumulate. Participants (n = 7) completed
three laboratory visits at preseason, midseason, and postseason. Visits
consisted of completing the dot-probe expectancy (DPX) task, during
which EEG data were recorded while wearing an EEG cap (64 Ag/AgCl
electrodes). Participant number of head impacts (i.e., direct blow or hit to
the head via head to head, head to ground, head to body, head to elbow,
head to ball, or head to knee impact or indirect impact with another player,
the ground, or whiplash) were collected via self-report and objective
observations throughout participant seasons (Mself-report = 91; Mobserved =
32). Paired sample t-tests for the DPX task across BX trials revealed
faster reaction time from preseason to postseason (p = .01) and from
midseason to postseason (p ≤.01), suggesting a learning effect. There
were no signiﬁcant differences in response accuracy (p’s ≥.06) for any
trials across time. Finally, P3 results revealed reduced amplitude for BX
trials at postseason (2.1 ± 3.7 μV) compared to midseason (5.2 ± 2.1 μV;
p = .02; site FCz). Results also showed increased amplitude on BX trials
at midseason (5.2 ± 2.1 μV) compared to preseason (2.4 ± 1.5 μV;
p = .03; site FCz). Together, these ﬁndings align with prior research
showing initial increases in P3 mean amplitude as a compensatory
response to brain injury but eventual decline as head impacts accumulate
further across a season.
Trends in team sport participation and depression across adolescence
Ross Murray, University of Toronto; Catherine Sabiston, University of
Toronto;
Robert
Wellman,
University
of
Massachusetts;
Jennifer
O’Loughlin, University of Montreal
There is a bidirectional relationship between team sport participation and
depression symptoms, wherein team sports can protect against adolescent
depression symptoms, while adolescents with fewer depression symptoms are more likely to engage in team sports. Moreover, these associations may be inﬂuenced by gender, as boys and girls often experience
sports differently. This study examines the bidirectional associations
between team sport participation and depression symptoms during adolescence using data from the Nicotine Dependence in Teens (NDIT)
study, a longitudinal dataset surveying students aged 11–18 across 20
time points, spaced 3–4 months apart. Team sport participation was
assessed using a sports checklist, and depression symptoms were measured with the Depressive Symptoms Scale for Adolescents. Linear
mixed models with random effects for individuals and surveys were
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 88] ---
employed to evaluate trends over time and lagged associations, with
gender as a potential moderator. Descriptive analyses revealed divergent
patterns in team sport participation and depression symptoms over time.
Depression symptoms increased throughout adolescence, particularly
among girls, while team sport participation steadily declined. When
disaggregated by gender, girls consistently reported higher levels of
depression and lower team sport participation compared to boys. In the
model predicting team sport participation, depression symptoms were not
signiﬁcantly associated with subsequent team sport participation for
either gender (b = -0.011, p = 0.10). Conversely, in the model predicting
depression symptoms, a signiﬁcant interaction between gender and team
sport participation (b = -0.097, p = 0.05) suggested a protective effect of
team sports for girls. These ﬁndings underscore the importance of
promoting team sport opportunities for girls as a targeted strategy to
reduce depression symptoms during adolescence. Future research should
explore contextual factors, such as social support and identity processes,
that may enhance the mental health beneﬁts of team sports for
adolescents.
Divisional variations in athletic identity and achievement goal orientation in collegiate sports: Insights from a mixed-methods approach
Marissa Markey, Pitzer College; Mark Otten, Cal State University of
Northridge
Although extensive research has highlighted that athletes competing at
different levels display distinct psychological proﬁles, limited studies
have explored this within the divisions of collegiate sports. Therefore,
the present study aimed to identify psychological variations among
collegiate student-athletes (SAs) in different divisions, focusing on
athletic identity, team cohesion, and achievement goal orientation. An
online mixed-methods survey was conducted with collegiate tennis
players from Division I (DI; n = 26), Division III (DIII; n = 30), and
club teams (n = 15) in the US. Participants were asked to complete the
Athletic Identity Index, the Group Environment Questionnaire, and the
Perception of Success Questionnaire at two time points: the beginning
and end of the 2023 spring season. An independent-samples t-test
revealed that, at the start of the season, DI SAs (M = 5.972, SD = 0.805)
had higher athletic identity than DIII SAs (M = 4.767, SD = 1.193),
t(20) = 3.668, p < .002. This pattern persisted at the end of the season.
Moreover, at the season’s end, DI SAs (M = 1.638, SD = 0.425) had
signiﬁcantly lower task orientation than DIII SAs (M = 2.350, SD =
0.821), t(17) = -2.413, p = .027. Qualitative analysis revealed that almost every female DI SA emphasized the importance of others recognizing their athletic identity, associating it with their personality and
accomplishments. Across all divisions, female SAs were more likely
than males to value external recognition of their athletic status. Additionally, DIII SAs and club tennis players were consistently taskoriented, highlighting that they felt successful when they improved,
played their best, or had fun. DI tennis players also displayed high task
orientation at the beginning of the season but reported the largest shift
towards ego orientation at the end of the season. Finally, DI SAs
reported the highest feelings of success from recovering after injury.
These ﬁndings suggest variations in the psychological experiences of
SAs competing in different divisions. This emphasizes the need for
tailored practice plans, mental health interventions, coaching styles, and
support systems across each level.
A randomized control trial demonstrating that autonomy-supportive
teaching facilitates PE students life skills development
Chang-Ha Im, Korea Univeristy; Bo-Gyeong Lee, Korea University; Sung
Hyeon Cheon, Korea University; Johnmarshall Reeve, Australian Catholic University; Yong-Gwan Song, Pukyong National University
According to self-determination theory, recurring classroom experiences
of psychological need satisfaction empower adolescents to develop healthy
and productive life skills, such as teamwork, goal setting, and interpersonal
communication. The present study conducted a randomized control trial
with longitudinally assessed dependent measures to test a mediation model
in which manipulated autonomy-supportive teaching in the physical
education (PE) context increased students’ need satisfaction which, in
turn, helped students develop personal and social life skills. At the
beginning of the academic year, 50 secondary school PE teachers in
Korea and their 2,679 students were randomly assigned to either an
experimental group (autonomy-supportive intervention; n = 1,325) or a
delayed-treatment control group (n = 1,324), while students completed the
same questionnaire assessing perceived teacher autonomy support, psychological need satisfaction, and 10 life skills (5 personal, 5 social) at four
time points over one year. A multilevel structural equation modeling
analysis showed that the intervention worked to increase perceived
autonomy-supportive teaching, and that the students of teachers in the
experimental group reported higher levels of psychological need satisfaction and greater development of the full range of life skills over two
semesters. These ﬁndings demonstrate that PE teachers help students
develop their important life skills because they ﬁrst provide students with
recurring classroom experiences of psychological need satisfaction.
Funding source: This work was supported by the Ministry of Education of
the Republic of Korea and the National Research Foundation of Korea.
Bad memories, broken motivation? Unraveling the link between PE
memories and adult inactivity
Ralf Brand, University of Potsdam; Panteleimon Ekkekakis, Michigan
State University
Recent research has shown that physically inactive adults tend to report
negative memories of school physical education (PE), with some even
citing these experiences as a contributing factor to their lifelong physical
inactivity. This study examined the relationship between memories of
school PE and hedonic motivation to exercise (“antipathy-attraction” as
measured by the Affective Exercise Experiences Questionnaire, AFFEXX)
in adulthood. An online cross-sectional survey, conducted using broader
community convenience sampling, included 1,189 German adults. The
survey assessed the study participants’ PE memories, current exercise
behavior, and hedonic motivation, and identiﬁed three subgroups of
participants with different exercise histories: lifelong exercisers (active
or inactive in the past 4 weeks; n = 628 and n = 236, respectively) and those
who stopped exercising after high school (n = 93). Linear mixed-effects
modeling revealed that individuals with positive PE memories reported
signiﬁcantly greater attraction to exercise compared to those with mixed
(p = .023, b = -0.23, 95% CI [-0.44, -0.03]) or negative (p < .001, b = -0.73,
95% CI [-0.96, -0.49]) memories. This effect was relatively independent of
exercise history, suggesting that PE memories exerted a lasting inﬂuence
on attraction regardless of the participants’ distinct biographies. The
ﬁndings underscore the potentially critical role of early exercise experiences in fostering lifelong engagement with physical activity and highlight
the need for positive, inclusive school PE environments. Identifying
patterns of how individuals’ appraisals and core affective experiences
inﬂuence hedonic motivation should be a focus of further modeling and
analysis.
Grip strength, APOE-e4, and memory in middle-aged women at
increased risk of Alzheimer’s disease
Chadsley M. Wessinger, University of North Carolina at Greensboro;
Alexis B. Slutsky-Ganesh, Emory University School of Medicine, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Kyoung Shin Park, Emory
