University School of Medicine, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Samantha L. DuBois, Appalachian State University; Samuel L.
Kibildis, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Hadassah SomPimpong, Meharry Medical College; Jarod C. Vance, Randolph College;
Brittany D. Armstrong, University of North Carolina at Greensboro;
Christopher N. Wahlheim, University of North Carolina at Greensboro;
Laurie Wideman, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Jennifer L.
Etnier, University of North Carolina at Greensboro
Poor muscle health and carrying the Apolipoprotein-E ε4 allele (APOE-e4)
are independent risk factors for Alzheimer’s disease (AD). Handgrip
strength (HGS), a muscle health assessment, is associated with episodic
memory (epMem), the hallmark cognitive symptom of AD; however, it is
unknown if APOE-e4 inﬂuences this relationship during midlife. Therefore,
cross-sectional data from an ongoing clinical trial (NIH-R01AG058919)
were analyzed to explore the relationship between HGS and epMem relative
to APOE-e4 status in cognitively normal, sedentary, middle-aged female
adults with a family history of AD (FH+). Participants (N = 40,
Mage = 55.4 ± 6.6 years) provided saliva samples to determine APOE-e4
status (carriers, n = 21; non-carriers, n = 19), completed a HGS assessment
via handgrip dynamometer, and underwent epMem tests, including Auditory Verbal Learning Test (AVLT), Paired Associates (PAS), Logical
Memory (LM), and Complex Figure Task (CFT). Moderated regression
models were ﬁtted to test whether the interaction term HGS*APOE-e4
predicted epMem performance while controlling for age, education, and
body mass index. Simple slope analyses were conducted to probe the
conditional effects of HGS based on APOE-e4 status. Based on American
College of Sports Medicine (ACSM) combined HGS norms, non-carriers
(51.7 kg) were classiﬁed as good while carriers (46.7 kg) were classiﬁed as
fair. APOE-e4 moderated the relationship between HGS and AVLT
Summary score (AVLT-S; p = .007), PAS Delayed Recall (PAS-DR;
p = .019), PAS Summary score (PAS-S; p=.004), LM Summary score
(LM-S; p = .012), and LM Delayed Recall (LM-DR; p = .010). Conditional
simple slope effects revealed greater HGS predicted better AVLT-S
(p = .010), PAS-S (p = .015), LM-S (p = .044), and LM-DR (p = .038)
performance in APOE-e4 non-carriers but there was no relationship in
carriers (all p > .19). Greater HGS reliably predicted better epMem performance in APOE-ε4 non-carriers while there was no relationship in carriers,
with non-carriers also demonstrating higher HGS values than carriers, per
ACSM norms. These ﬁndings suggest that early, targeted interventions to
improve muscle health, particularly in APOE-ε4 carriers, may be necessary
to preserve epMem at midlife. Future research should investigate whether
such interventions can longitudinally improve epMem and muscle health
outcomes while exploring other cognitive domains (e.g., executive function)
and potential relationships in men. Funding source: NIH.
Behavioral adjustments and emergent efﬁcacy beliefs in team performance during a rope-pulling task
Bishop Guempel, East Carolina University; Christine Habeeb, East
Carolina University; Sarah Stephen, University of Calgary; Patrick Rider,
East Carolina University
Within input-process-output models of team dynamics, efﬁcacy beliefs
(i.e., conﬁdence) are described as emergent states that arise during
performance and interact with behavioral changes. However, few studies
incorporate both psychological and kinematic observations during team
performance. An interdisciplinary approach would advance understanding
of teams because observing how individuals move to coordinate with a
teammate can reveal mechanisms in teamwork that psychology researchers
cannot effectively test using surveys alone. The purpose of this study was
to examine the presence of behavioral changes (i.e., kinematics) associated
with emerging efﬁcacy beliefs. Participants completed 5-second max effort
tug-of-war style pulls on a rope as an individual, with a partner, and with a
partner after bogus performance feedback. As part of a larger research
study, bogus performance feedback was provided to participants to elicit
low self-efﬁcacy (i.e., conﬁdence in oneself) and high other-efﬁcacy
(i.e., conﬁdence in one’s partner). All performances were video recorded.
Video analysis of 2D body kinematics for trunk angle, elbow angle, heelto-heel distance, and center of mass (distance measured from the ﬂoor)
were observed via Kinovea for a subsample of 16 participants that faced
towards the camera and could be compared. A repeated measures ANOVA
indicated a signiﬁcant difference for elbow joint angle F(2, 30) = 3.42,
p = .046 across conditions. Speciﬁcally, differences were observed between the individual pull (M = 15.69) and pull with partner after bogus
feedback (M = 9.80). No other differences were observed to be signiﬁcant.
While these results did not demonstrate a holistic change in body position
across all conditions, there was evidence for behavioral changes immediately after emergent efﬁcacy beliefs. In addition, this study provides a
foundation for interdisciplinary research in team dynamics. Speciﬁcally, an
ability to integrate biomechanical information and survey-data to better test
input-process-output models of team dynamics.
The role of attitudes and subjective norms in predicting mental health
help-seeking intentions among collegiate student-athletes: A pilot study
Marissa Markey, Pitzer College
Collegiate student-athletes (SAs) deal with similar levels of mental health
challenges as their non-athlete (NA) peers. Despite this, SAs have signiﬁcantly lower intentions to seek professional mental health support. The
purpose of the current pilot study was to utilize the Theory of Planned
Behavior (TPB) to examine factors that predict a SAs professional mental
health help-seeking intentions. An online survey was conducted with
Division III SAs (N = 47, Mage = 19.745 ± 1.206) from liberal arts colleges
in the US. Participants completed measures assessing their intentions,
attitudes, perceived behavioral control, team subjective norms, and coach
subjective norms regarding seeking professional psychological help. The
SAs’ level of identiﬁcation to their teams was also assessed. It was
hypothesized that attitudes, perceived control, team subjective norms, and
coach subjective norms would signiﬁcantly predict help-seeking intentions, with team identiﬁcation moderating the inﬂuence of both team and
coach norms. A multiple linear regression analysis indicated that only
attitudes (β = 0.685, CI: 0.306, 1.063, p < 0.01) and team subjective norms
(β = 0.488, CI [0.099, 0.876], p = 0.0152) signiﬁcantly predicted help
seeking intentions. Therefore, SAs with more positive attitudes towards,
and stronger team norms supporting, professional mental health services
were more likely to intend to seek help if they were in need. Furthermore,
an exploratory analysis investigated gender differences in help-seeking
intentions. An independent-samples t-test revealed that male SAs
(M = 3.697, SD = 1.416) had signiﬁcantly lower intentions to seek professional mental health help compared to female SAs (M = 4.963, SD =
1.433), t(45) = 2.588, p = 0.013, 95% CI [0.281, 2.251], d = 0.891. These
ﬁndings suggest that interventions aimed at encouraging SAs to seek
mental health support should focus heavily on improving attitudes.
Moreover, coaches and athletic leaders should recognize the importance
of normalizing mental health help-seeking within team settings to foster
positive team norms. Along with this, future research should further
investigate the underlying causes of gender differences in SAs’ helpseeking intentions and replicate this study with a larger sample size.
Effect of 10-week online ﬁtness intervention on body composition and
self-perception in older female ofﬁce workers
Gunay Yildizer, Eskisehir Tech. Uni; Dilara Ebru Ucar, Anadolu
University
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS

Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 90] ---
This research examines the effect of an online physical ﬁtness intervention
plan on body composition and physical self-perception among older female
ofﬁce employees. A total of 32 individuals participated in the study, and 27
completed the protocol. Fat and muscle mass in the arms, legs, trunk,
android, and gynoid regions and total body with DEXA. Health, body fat,
strength, ﬂexibility sub-dimensions of the Marsh Physical Self-Description
Inventory were used for self-perception. Participants actively contributed to
the program’s development by providing input on the types of exercises,
scheduling preferences, and methods of online engagement to ensure the
intervention addressed their speciﬁc needs. The exercise program was
developed by 3 ﬁeld experts, uploaded to the system 2 days a week, and
the participants were followed up regularly with an online communication
tool. Dependent t-test results indicated that the muscle mass increased in
various regions of the body over the 10-week period, although this increase
was not statistically signiﬁcant (p > 0.05). On the other hand, total fat mass
signiﬁcantly decreased over time (pre-test: X = 27.32 ± 6.32, post-test:
X = 24.68 ± 5.89, p = 0.042). Moreover, there was an improvement in body
fat (pre-test: X = 21.90 ± 6.00, post-test: X = 23.94 ± 4.88), strength (pretest: X = 21.02 ± 1.98, post-test: X = 21.80 ± 2.02), ﬂexibility (pre-test: X =
20.22 ± 3.15, post-test: X = 20.98 ± 3.52), and health (pre-test: X = 37.72
± 5.81, post-test: X = 38.98 ± 4.55) subscales of physical self-perception;
however, these differences were not statistically signiﬁcant (p > 0.05).The
ﬁndings suggest that an online physical ﬁtness intervention plan can lead to
positive changes in body composition and self-perception, even in a shortterm period. While the increase in muscle mass and improvements in selfperception metrics were not statistically signiﬁcant, the signiﬁcant reduction
in total fat mass indicates the potential effectiveness of such programs in
improving health outcomes for older female workers. The signiﬁcant
reduction in total fat mass underscores the importance of consistent physical
activity in combating age-related changes in body composition. However,
the non-signiﬁcant changes in muscle mass and self-perception dimensions
suggest that longer intervention durations or more intensive exercise regimens may be necessary to achieve statistically meaningful improvements.
Future research should explore the long-term effects of similar interventions,
the role of individual adherence, and the impact of personalized feedback
mechanisms to further optimize outcomes.
The changing methodological landscape of judgment and decision
making in sports: Historical insights and future prospects
Maša Iskra, German Sport University Cologne; Laura Voigt, German
Sport University Cologne; Markus Raab, German Sport University
Cologne
Judgment and decision making (JDM) research in sports has produced
thousands of publications, dating back to the 1980s. In this relatively short
history, the ﬁeld has undergone profound methodological changes, inspiring developments in disciplines beyond sport psychology. Examining this
evolution through a historical lens offers valuable insights into recurring
trends, paradigm shifts, and contextual inﬂuences, including theoretical
and technological advancements. The present study explores the historical
development of decision-making tasks involving sports-related stimuli
through a scoping review of 430 studies. Task characteristics, including
stimuli, response types, and their interaction were extracted and categorized into four epochs based on the publication year. Results indicate rapid
growth in JDM research, from 30 studies published before 2003 to 188
studies in the last six years. The prevalence of individual theoretical
approaches in different epochs is reﬂected in the trends of stimuli and
response types. Videos have consistently been the most common stimuli,
often presented using the temporal occlusion paradigm rooted in cognitive
theoretical approaches. Virtual reality technologies, introduced in JDM
tasks after 2011, enabled more advanced and standardized manipulation of
task demands. Discrete response types were the most common prior to
2010, with micro movements and written responses found in approximately half of all studies. In the last decade this however changed, as
continuous, whole-body movements became the most prevalent, inspired
by the ecological and embodied choices approaches. Greater attempts to
implement representative movements, further enabled through cross-disciplinary inﬂuences and advances in wearable sensors, shaped the methodological landscape. Nevertheless, the lack of dynamic and time-sensitive
stimulus presentation has hindered the approximation of cognitive demands experienced in sports contexts. This historical perspective highlights the shift in prioritizing representative response patterns and sensitive
measurement thereof, at times at the cost of overly simplistic stimulus
presentation. In light of recent theoretical and technological advancements
such as augmented reality environments, future task development should
prioritize both cognitive and motor demands equally to capture the
interplay of cognition and action in JDM processes.
Pride in body image: Unpacking the dual facets of self-conscious
emotions
Sarah E. Ryan, University of Toronto; Catherine M. Sabiston, University
of Toronto
Self-conscious emotions (SCE) inﬂuence how individuals perceive, evaluate, and accept their bodies. Several SCEs have been contextualized to the
body, with pride as the only positive emotion. Body-related pride is a
multifaceted SCE with authentic and hubristic facets. Authentic pride (AP)
stems from controllable achievements and feelings of self-worth, while
hubristic pride (HP) results from global and uncontrollable aspects of the
self. Due to the differences in causal attributions and divergent outcomes,
there remain questions of whether body-related pride is distinguishable
into two facets, and if body-related pride is operationalized and experienced as positive body image. This study aimed to bridge gaps in the
literature by investigating the relationship between the two facets of bodyrelated pride and key body image correlates through a secondary analysis
of data collated from three unique datasets. The combined sample
(N = 678) included active adolescents and emerging adults (13 to 30 years)
who completed a series of validated self-report measures with pride
assessed using the Body and Appearance Self-Conscious Emotions Scale.
Correlation analyses were used to test the associations between facets of
appearance-related pride and body image correlates. AP was signiﬁcantly
(p < 0.01) positively correlated with self-esteem (r = 0.18), positive affect
(r = 0.42), physical self-worth (r = 0.55), and body appreciation (r = 0.57),
and HP followed similar trends. There were no signiﬁcant differences in
the strength of correlations based on Z-scores. AP and HP were positively
correlated (r = 0.68 to 0.73). In combined regression models, AP was a
signiﬁcant positive predictor of positive affect (β = 0.09) and physical selfworth (β = 0.36), while HP was a signiﬁcant negative predictor of social
physique anxiety (β = -0.35). Both AP (β = 0.31) and HP (β = 0.35)
signiﬁcantly contributed to body appreciation. Findings align with previous research highlighting the strong correlation between facets of pride.
These results suggest the unique yet complementary roles of AP and HP in
shaping body image outcomes. Fostering body-related AP may be an
effective strategy for promoting positive self-perceptions within physical
activity contexts, while HP may have broader implications for reducing
anxieties in appearance-focused settings. Further research is needed to
continue probing at the nuances between facets of body-related pride and
their distinct contributions to body image.
Positive body image development in girl and women athletes
Taylor Unger, The University of British Columbia; Erica V. Bennett, The
University of British Columbia; Desmond McEwan, The University of
British Columbia; Katherine A. Tamminen, University of Toronto
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025

--- [Página 91] ---
Women athletes experience sport-speciﬁc and general sociocultural pressures about body size, shape, weight, and/or appearance. Body talk,
discussions around the body’s function and physical appearance, verbal
pressures to change the body, and unsolicited negative commentary, is
pervasive for girl and women athletes. Notably, body image concerns often
arise during adolescence and these concerns may explain why girls drop
out of sport at a higher rate than boys. Body image theory, research, and
practice has predominantly focused on negative aspects. Thus, a comprehensive understanding of body image, including positive body image, and
how to promote experiences of positive body image in girl athletes may
help keep them in sport and address this drop-out gap. The purpose of this
research was to understand late adolescent girls and young women
athletes’ experiences of positive body image development. Seven late
adolescent girls and young women athletes aged 16 to 24 (M =19) who
self-identiﬁed as experiencing positive body image participated in a semistructured interview followed by a collage session where they depicted
their positive body image experiences by gluing images and other artsupplies on paper. Two themes were generated from reﬂexive thematic
analysis of the data. Theme one titled Body Image Climates: Coaches’
Dual Roles pertained to coaches’ pivotal role in supporting and/or
thwarting positive body image development. Some coaches’ comments
created subcultural team climates fostering negative body image and
hindered positive body image development. Other coaches, particularly
women who were previously athletes and who embodied positive body
image themselves, facilitated positive body image development through
representation and role-modeling. Theme two, Empathy as a Catalyst for
Body Acceptance, referred to the interaction of body acceptance and
perspective-taking as a driver of positive body image development.
Participants expressed body acceptance and concurrent recognition that
everyone’s body is unique by using stickers such as “no wrong way to have
a body”, which contributed to their agency to retain positive body image.
These ﬁndings contribute theoretically to our understanding of positive
body image in sport, which is essential in equipping clinicians to promote
health and well-being and prevent and treat body image disturbances.
Funding source: SSHRC CGS-M.
Drive for exercise, perfectionism, and body-related self-conscious
emotions: Elucidating internal processes in maladaptive movement
Avery Hinchcliffe, University of Toronto; Danika Quesnel, University of
Toronto; Catherine Sabiston, University of Toronto
Perfectionism and self-conscious emotions contribute to the onset and
maintenance of eating disorders (ED) and disordered eating (DE). Indeed,
internal appraisals link self-conscious emotions, speciﬁcally, body related
self-conscious emotions (BRSE; i.e., shame, guilt, authentic pride, hubristic pride, envy, and embarrassment) and perfectionism to DE. Less is
understood about how drive for exercise, a facet of maladaptive movement
and predictor of ED onset, interplays with perfectionism and BRSE.
Further, knowledge about the mechanisms through which perfectionism
and BRSE function together in the context of drive for exercise is limited.
This project explored the relationship between perfectionism and drive for
exercise through BRSE. Women students (N = 176; Mage =19.11,
SD=1.29; 38.1% East/Southeast Asian, 21.6% South Asian, 17% White)
from a Canadian university completed measures of perfectionism (perceived discrepancy between personal standards and performance), BRSE,
and exercise drive (the extent to which one feels the need to exercise every
day). Pearson bivariate correlations and a parallel mediation model were
used to explore relationships between perfectionism, BRSE, and exercise
drive. Perfectionism was signiﬁcantly (p < .05) correlated with bodyrelated shame (r = .50), guilt (r = .45), and hubristic pride (r=-.23). Exercise drive was signiﬁcantly correlated with perfectionism (r=.15), bodyrelated shame (r = .21), and guilt (r = .15). The parallel mediation revealed
a total effect of perfectionism on exercise drive (c =.13, p <.05), and
signiﬁcant pathways to body-related shame (a1=.34, p<.001), guilt (a2
=.30, p <.001), hubristic pride (a4 = -.14, p < .01), envy (a5 =.31, p <.001),
and embarrassment (a6 =.31, p < .001). Direct effect and b pathways were
nonsigniﬁcant. Unsurprisingly, perfectionism inﬂuences BRSE and drive
for exercise. However, BRSE did not appear to have a direct effect on
exercise drive. Results highlight the complexity of internal cognitive
processes that inﬂuence the engagement in behaviors that can become
pathological in the context of an ED. Ongoing efforts are needed to better
align prevention and intervention efforts with these complex internal
processes.
National physical activity guidelines and mental health: Gender’s
inﬂuence on the relationship between physical activity and wellbeing
Avery Hinchcliffe, University of Toronto; Danika Quesnel, University of
Toronto; Catherine Sabiston, University of Toronto
Students experience stressors (e.g., transitions, academic expectations) in
university that impact their mental health. Further, mental health challenges often appear during emerging adulthood, when individuals typically start university. Physical activity (PA) is protective of both physical
and mental health. Meeting national PA guidelines (getting at least 150
minutes of moderate-vigorous PA/week) is associated with improved
mental health. Few university students meet these guidelines, yet little is
known about how gender differences impact this lack of PA engagement,
nor how it inﬂuences their mental health. This project explored how
gender moderates the relationship between meeting PA guidelines and
mental health among university students, using multiple mental health
indicators. Students from a large Canadian university [N = 4566, 69.1%
women, 25.4% men, 5.9% gender diverse individuals (i.e., non-binary,
two-spirit); 59.7% getting at least 150 minutes of moderate-vigorous PA/
week] completed a self-report survey. Mental health was examined
through measures of wellbeing, psychological distress and loneliness.
Campus support and awareness of mental health resources on and off
campus were also considered. Group differences by achievement of PA
guidelines and gender on mental health factors were explored using
multivariate analysis of variance. Men (65.2%) were more likely to meet
PA guidelines compared to women (58.0%; p < .001) and gender diverse
individuals (56.5%; p = .001). Women and gender diverse individuals
reported poorer mental health indicators [e.g., lower wellbeing (p <.001),
higher psychological distress (p <.001), higher loneliness (p < .001)].
Women reported better mental help seeking online than gender diverse
individuals (p < .05). Those who were active reported better mental health
outcomes [e.g., higher wellbeing (p <.001), lower psychological distress
(p <.05), lower loneliness (p <.001), and higher access to support (p
<.05)]. Interaction effects were nonsigniﬁcant. Findings highlight the
protective role of PA on mental health across genders, which may indicate
that in university PA serves as a valuable avenue for connecting students
with mental health resources. However, mental health disparities persist,
affecting women and gender minorities regardless of PA engagement.
Recognizing and addressing these trends can enhance the design and
effectiveness of mental health prevention and intervention strategies
tailored to the needs of young adults.
Physical activity, sedentary time and their association with psychological functioning: Insights from a feasibility study among posttreatment cancer patients
Arianne Côté, Université du Québec à Trois-Rivières; Claudia TrudelFitzgerald, Université du Québec à Trois-Rivières; Pier-Olivier Caron,
TÉLUQ University; Benoit Plante, Université du Québec à Trois-Rivières;
Paule Miquelon, Université du Québec à Trois-Rivières

The post-treatment period is a phase during which individuals with cancer
are particularly vulnerable to the side effects (physical and psychological)
of treatments, where the adoption of healthy lifestyle habits is crucial to
counteract these effects. The objective of this study was to determine the
acceptability and feasibility of a daily survey protocol to clarify the roles of
physical activity (PA) and sedentary time (ST) in the post-treatment
psychological functioning of adults with cancer. Fourteen adults who
received oncological treatment in the past year were recruited. They ﬁrst
ﬁlled out a questionnaire documenting their socio-demographic characteristics and information about their cancer (type, stage, treatment(s) received). Then, daily surveys were sent out for seven consecutive days to
measure their anxiety/depressive symptoms and positive/negative emotions. PA and ST were measured by accelerometers during the same
period. Descriptive analyses regarding the acceptability/feasibility rates of
the protocol were conducted, and structural equation analyses were
performed to estimate inter-individual associations between PA, ST,
positive/negative emotions and anxiety/depressive symptoms. Overall,
participants reported a positive experience, indicating that they would
participate in a study of this type again and that wearing the accelerometer
was not restrictive. No signiﬁcant association was found between PA, ST,
positive/negative emotions, and anxiety/depressive symptoms, although
marginal negative trends emerged between ST and positive emotions (β = -
0.001, p = .102) as well as between PA and anxiety symptoms (β = -0.004,
p = .055). More speciﬁcally, it seems that the more participants practice
PA, the fewer anxiety symptoms they experience and the higher their ST,
the fewer positive emotions they feel. These results, although based on a
small sample size, show the potential of the upcoming larger study and
suggest that higher levels of PA and lower levels of TS in adults with
cancer in the post-treatment period are associated with better psychological
functioning.
Cycling to the (heart)beat: How auditory cues shape pacing decisions
Maša Iskra, German Sport University Cologne; Eva Crone, University of
Cologne; Carla Alfonso, Universitat Autònoma de Barcelona; Sylvain
Laborde, German Sport University Cologne; Uirassu Borges, German
Sport University Cologne; Markus Raab, German Sport University Cologne; Laura Voigt, German Sport University Cologne
In endurance sports, performance beneﬁts are attained through efﬁcient
pacing. Pacing refers to the distribution of speed, power, and energy over
time to prevent excessive bodily strain. Drawing on the theoretical
approach of embodied choices, the body’s physiological state plays a
constitutive role in perceptual and decision processes. Although cardiac
activity has been depicted in several pacing models, the causal relationship between heart rate and pacing decisions remains to be speciﬁed. The
present study investigated how manipulating the auditory heartbeat signal
affects pacing decisions in a dynamic cycling task. It was hypothesized
that hearing faster rather than congruent heartbeat stimulus would lead to
more frequent and earlier pacing down decisions, resulting in shorter
distances covered. Physically active students (N = 40, 19 female) completed three sessions: an incremental cycling test to determine 80% heart
rate reserve (baseline intensity), followed by two 24-minute simulated
cycling competitions. During the competitions, participants listened to
either a congruent or faster (+10 bpm) auditory heartbeat stimulus in a
randomized order. They began cycling at their individual baseline
intensity and every three minutes they were given an option to either
maintain the same intensity or pace down by reducing the intensity by 20
Watts. To assess the pacing down decisions, the changes in exercise
intensity over the 24 minutes were examined. Preliminary results suggest
that beyond the second interval/decision point, participants cycled at a
lower intensity in the faster compared to the congruent condition, reaching the largest difference in intensity during the ﬁnal 3-minute interval.
Paired-samples t-test indicated signiﬁcantly longer distances covered in
the congruent condition compared to the faster condition (p = .014,
d = 0.38). These results suggest that the faster auditory heartbeat stimulus
served as a pacing cue to prevent excessive exertion and enable task
completion, however at a reduced performance level. Future studies
should examine the predictive power of heartbeat signals in pacing and
further explore how understanding bodily cues can help athletes make
more adaptive decisions.
Physical activity goals and well-being: Examining the role of social
support
Kathleen S. Wilson, California State University, Fullerton; Isabella Gore
Rivero, California State University, Fullerton; Andy Jerome, California
State University, Fullerton; Lily Haeberle, California State University,
Fullerton
Ones’ social network is instrumental in helping deal with both life’s
challenges as well as opportunities, which in turn may support ones’
well-being (Brooke & Feeney, 2015). This is illustrated in the importance of social support when pursing physical activity-related goals
(Berli et al., 2018). This study examined the role of social support in
relation to progress towards a physical activity goal and psychological
well-being. Participants (N = 88; 64.8% women) were students who had
a physical activity goal that was new or that they were interested in
expanding upon. They also needed to have identiﬁed someone who
would support them in reaching their goal. In an in-person meeting,
participants completed questionnaires and engaged in a discussion/
reﬂection of their goal. For this meeting, participants were assigned
to either come with a partner they knew, participated alone or were
assigned a partner who had some training in motivational interviewing.
At three weeks, participants completed an online survey that assessed
social support using the Physical Activity Social Support Scale (PASSS;
Golaszewski & Bartholomew, 2019) and a secure base questionnaire
(Feeney & Thrush, 2010). Six months later, participants (n = 42)
completed an online survey that assessed goal progress (Milyavskaya
et al., 2012) and psychological well-being (Ryff & Keyes, 1995). All
groups were combined for the analyses as there were no differences in
social support at 3 weeks based on the group. Regression models were
used to predict goal progress and well-being at 6 months with secure
base and types of social support from the PASSS. In predicting goal
progress, social support accounted for 30.5% of the variance in goal
progress (p = .037), with companionship support being the only signiﬁcant predictor (b = 0.8, SE = 0.3, p = .013). In predicting overall wellbeing, social support accounted for 35.8% of the variance (p = .012),
with informational support being negatively related (b = -0.2, SE = 0.07,
p = .003) and validation support being positively related (b = 0.2, SE =
0.01, p = .064). The ﬁndings will be discussed in context of the role of
physical activity-speciﬁc measures versus general measures of support
as well as differences based on type of social support.
Lifestyle patterns and mental health problems in Swiss university
students: A latent proﬁle analysis
Markus Gerber, University of Basel; Jan Hochuli, University of Basel
Mental health is a critical component of life, essential to public health,
and recognized as a fundamental human right. Around the globe, mental
health issues are alarmingly high among university students. Effective
prevention and treatment options are mostly known and available. Yet,
most individuals with mental disorders do not receive the care they need,
potentially worsening the problem. In this study, we examined whether
Swiss ﬁrst-year university students with distinct lifestyle proﬁles experience varying mental health problems. The data come from a larger
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S89
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 93] ---
international study, in which more than 50 universities from over 20
countries are involved (www.unilifem.com). We used a cross-sectional
design with self-selection sampling. We examined sociodemographic,
lifestyle behavior, and mental health in 352 students (age=20.8±2.56,
women: 66.5%) who were enrolled in different disciplines at the University of Basel. We used logistic regression analyses to examine associations between lifestyle behaviors and mental health problems. Additionally, we performed latent proﬁle analysis to identify classes. We used chisquare and multinomial logistic regression analysis, adjusting for covariates, to examine between-class differences. A healthier lifestyle score
was associated with reduced prevalences rates for different psychiatric
disorders, including depression, anxiety, sleep disorder, obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), substance use, and attention-deﬁcit/hyperactivity disorder. We further observed an inverse relationship between a
healthier lifestyle and bipolar disorder. Improved sleep, physical activity,
social connections, and lower use of substance use, stress-coping, and
screen time were linked to fewer mental health problems. Latent proﬁle
analysis identiﬁed three distinct lifestyle classes: one healthier and two
less healthy (high substance use and isolated). University students who
were categorized in the healthiest class exhibited fewer mental disorders.
Employment status and BMI category inﬂuenced class membership,
whereas age and sex did not. Within two classes, female university
students were more likely to screen positive for depression, anxiety, sleep
disorder, OCD, and suicidal ideation, whereas male university students
exhibited higher substance use. Overall, our ﬁndings suggest that healthier lifestyle behaviors are associated with a reduced prevalence of
multiple mental health issues. Taking into account knowledge from
lifestyle medicine seems essential for the prevention and treatment of
mental health issues among university students.
Assessing motivation in structured exercise classes for older adults:
Links with attendance and psychological well-being
Amber Shearer, California State University, Fullerton; Kathleen S. Wilson, California State University, Fullerton; Hikari Kitano, California
State University, Fullerton; Koren L. Fisher, California State University,
Fullerton
Adherence is a challenge in physical activity (PA) programs with the need
to understand factors that may contribute to engagement in these programs (de Lacy-Vawdon et al., 2018). Self-determination theory suggests
individuals with higher autonomous motivation for a behavior are more
likely to be participate in that behavior (Ryan & Deci, 2000). Among
older adults, motivation is linked to PA participation (Palombi et al.,
2023) and psychological well-being (Tang et al., 2021). Few studies have
examined autonomous motivation in relation to attendance in structured
programs in older adults. This study examined motivation in older adults
and its relationship to attendance and well-being, using a 6-item motivation scale (BREQ-3-6; Rocchi et al., 2023). This motivation scale is a new
adaptation of a measure so evaluating its use with older adults is
important. Participants included older adults (N = 45, Mean age = 74.6;
71.1% female) registered in a twice weekly, group-based multicomponent exercise program. Each exercise session included aerobic endurance,
functional strength training, and neuromotor activities targeting balance,
agility, coordination, and problem-solving. The class format consisted of
a group warm-up, individualized strength and endurance programming,
and a ﬂexibility-focused cool-down. Attendance was recorded for each
session. At the start of the program, participants completed a questionnaire that included demographic questions and the BREQ-3-6 (Rocchi
et al., 2023). A relative autonomy index was calculated to assess
motivation. There was a positive non-signiﬁcant relationship between
the motivation and attendance (r = .249, p = .150) and signiﬁcant positive
relationship with psychological well-being (r = .442, p = .007). In the
individual items reﬂecting the different types of motivation, the expected
patterns were seen with the intrinsic, integrated, and identiﬁed motivation
being positively related and introjected, external regulation and amotivation being negatively related to wellbeing and attendance. Although
under powered, these ﬁndings provide support for the use of 6 item
BREQ with older adults as well as the link between motivation and
psychological well-being.
An examination of mindfulness proﬁles in recreational surfers
Garrett Pilgrim, Springﬁeld College; Kathleen Mellano, Springﬁeld College; Jasmin Hutchinson, Springﬁeld College; Daniel Smith, Springﬁeld
College
Sport is a performance context where mindfulness-based interventions
(MBIs) have been widely implemented and effective for performance and
mental wellbeing (Wang et al., 2023). Research has suggested that surﬁng
may be an inherently “mindful” sport that can enhance one’s mindfulness
and provide similar beneﬁts to MBIs (Amrhein et al., 2016; Levin &
Taylor, 2011). The present study aimed to 1) identify existing mindfulness
proﬁles within recreational surfers and 2) determine if these proﬁles are
unique to the surﬁng population compared to recreational exercisers.
Surfers and non-surfers (N = 286, Mage = 33.01 years, SD = 13.42) completed the Five Facet Mindfulness Questionnaire (FFMQ; Baer et al., 2006)
and a demographic questionnaire. Non-surfers were recreational exercisers
with varying levels of exercise experience. Nonhierarchical k-means
clustering
yielded
a
three-cluster
solution:
1)
High
Mindfulness
(n = 128, n surf = 74), 2) Low Mindfulness (n = 146, n surf = 88), and 3)
Non-Judgmentally Aware (n = 12, n surf = 4). Three clusters produced the
highest number of nonredundant proﬁles while showing similarities to
previous proﬁle research with non-surfer populations (Bravo et al., 2018).
However, further analysis showed no signiﬁcant difference in the distribution of surfers and non-surfers among proﬁles, X2(2) = 3.31, p = .191.
Therefore, mindfulness proﬁles are independent of surﬁng participation,
suggesting non-surfers demonstrate similar mindfulness to surfers despite
having no experience surﬁng. High Mindfulness participants had higher
average scores on all subscales of the FFMQ than other participants, with
the inverse being true for the Low Mindfulness proﬁle. The mixed proﬁle,
Non-Judgmentally Aware, is characterized by those high in Nonjudgement
and Acting with Awareness but low in Observing, Describing, and
Nonreactivity, suggesting increased self-awareness and self-acceptance.
The mindfulness proﬁles that emerged in this study suggest that surfers are
not characterized by a unique combination of mindfulness dimensions
relative to non-surf populations. More research is needed to explore the
mindful properties of surﬁng to determine if it has the potential as a future
intervention tool. Funding source: AASP.
Psychological distress proﬁles of college student-athletes
Justin T. Worley, The Ohio State University; Samuel Davis, The Ohio State
University; Sydney Mack, The Ohio State University; James Houle, The
Ohio State University
Routine mental health screening may help identify college student-athletes
who experience elevated levels of psychological distress. These assessments are often evaluated through a variable-centered framework, such as
creating symptom-speciﬁc (e.g., depression, anxiety) or global distress
scores. Such approaches highlight symptom endorsement but may overlook qualitatively unique patterns of psychological distress. A personcentered data-analytic approach may identify subpopulations of college
student-athletes characterized by unique patterns of mental health experiences. We tested this using latent proﬁle analysis, and examined whether
sex (i.e., male versus female), sport type (i.e., team versus individual), and
class standing (i.e., underclassman versus upperclassman) predicted proﬁle
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S90
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 94] ---
membership. Data were derived from two independent samples of college
student-athletes at a large Midwestern university collected during Fall
2020 (N1 = 383, 66% female, 34% team sports, 31% underclassman) and
Fall 2024 (N2 = 423, 37% female, 73% team sports, 59% underclassman).
Participants completed the Counseling Center Assessment of Psychological Symptoms-Screen (CCAPS-Screen) which measures eight domains of
perceived psychological distress symptoms (i.e., depression, generalized
anxiety, social anxiety, family distress, academic distress, eating concerns,
frustration, alcohol use) during the previous two weeks. Latent proﬁle
analysis was conducted separately in each sample to test the reproducibility
of proﬁles and multinomial logistic regression was used to test predictors
of proﬁle membership. We found support for three proﬁles in both samples
that were characterized by low (N1 = 156, N2=161), average (N1 = 176,
N2 = 206), and high (N1 = 51, N2 = 56) psychological distress symptoms. In
the 2020 sample only, female student-athletes were more likely to be
represented in the high psychological distress proﬁle compared to male
student-athletes (OR = 5.87, β = 1.77, SE = 0.43, p < .001) when using the
low psychological distress proﬁle as the reference group. No signiﬁcant
differences were found for sport type or class standing in either sample.
Results consistently supported quantitative (i.e., difference in severity)
versus qualitative (i.e., difference in conﬁguration) differences in perceived psychological distress symptoms. Practitioners using the CCAPSScreen to monitor mental health of college student-athletes may ﬁnd more
clinical value in interpreting overall levels of psychological distress rather
than accounting for unique combinations of psychological distress
symptoms.
Measuring stability and change in response patterns to a set of
hierarchical physical activity self-efﬁcacy scales in a randomized
intervention study: An innovative application of latent transition
analysis
Nicholas D. Myers, Michigan State University; Isaac Prilleltensky, University of Miami; Seungmin Lee, Iowa State University
Latent transition analysis (LTA) is a mixture model that typically takes an
auto-regressive approach to describe stability and change in categorical
states (i.e., classes) across time via transition probabilities. While applications of LTA have recently increased, multiple-group LTA where the
grouping variable is randomization to an intervention within a randomized
controlled trial (RCT) is relatively rare. Multiple-group LTA where the
observed grouping variable is randomization to an intervention within a
RCT has the potential to answer an important research question: do
transition probabilities for latent classes of a theory-driven malleable
intervention target differ by intervention groups? Answering this question
has the potential to identify for whom an intervention may be effective.
Knowing for whom an intervention is likely effective has implications for
optimizing delivery of an intervention. Focusing on “for whom” research
questions may be well-served by person-centered models, where key data
may be person-level instead of a more common focus on variable-level
data. For whom research questions often are exploratory aims of an RCT
that are investigated in secondary analyses after evidence for variablecentered models investigating primary variable-level research questions
has been provided. The objective of this study was to measure stability and
change in response patterns to a set of hierarchical Physical Activity SelfEfﬁcacy (PASE) scales with LTA. To accomplish this objective a multiplegroup LTA modeled binary responses to six ordered items within each
PASE scale. Data (Nbaseline = 461 and N30 days post-baseline = 428) from the
Well-Being and Physical Activity (WBPA; ClinicalTrials.gov, identiﬁer:
NCT03194854) study were analyzed. A four-class solution with interpretable parameter restrictions explained response patterns to each PASE scale
at baseline. Evidence for temporal measurement invariance of this fourclass solution was provided. Stability of latent class membership from
baseline (i.e., pre-intervention) to 30 days post-baseline (i.e., post-intervention) was modest, consistent with substantive theory. Desirable differences in LTA probabilities (e.g., transitioning from conﬁdence to engage in
10 min of weekly physical activity at baseline to conﬁdence to engage in
90 min of weekly physical activity at 30 days post-baseline) by intervention group were observed, consistent with objectives of the WBPA study.
Funding source: Erwin and Barbara Mautner Charitable Foundation.
Exploring the relationship between coach feedback style and female
youth athlete self-efﬁcacy perception
Katherine Eldred, Springﬁeld College; Kathleen Mellano, Springﬁeld
College; Elizabeth Mullin, Springﬁeld College; Thaddeus France, Springﬁeld College
Exploring the relationship between coach feedback style and female
youth athlete self-efﬁcacy perceptions Katherine Eldred, Dr. Kathleen
T. Mellano, Dr. Elizabeth M. Mullin, Dr. Thaddeus J. France Springﬁeld College Athlete self-efﬁcacy beliefs have been linked to performance as well as the quality of sport experience (Fransen et al., 2017;
Heazlewood & Burke, 2011; Moritz et al., 2000; Reverdito et al., 2022).
Higher self-efﬁcacy perceptions tend to have a more positive impact on
sport-related outcomes. A series of sources, such as performance
accomplishments, vicarious experiences, physiological state, and verbal
persuasion shape self-efﬁcacy beliefs (Bandura, 1977). Coaches can
serve as a key verbal persuasion source for athletes (Carpentier &
Mageau, 2013). The present study examined the relationship between
coaches’ feedback style and self-efﬁcacy perceptions in female youth
basketball players. In Phase I of the study, a self-efﬁcacy in youth
basketball measure was created following the steps outlined in Jackson
and colleagues (2011, 2014) previous youth sports study. In Phase II of
the study, female youth basketball players (N = 84, Mage = 15.25 ± 1.53)
completed the Youth Basketball Self-Efﬁcacy Scale (YBSES) developed for this study and the Coach Feedback Questionnaire (CFQ;
Amorose & Horn, 2000). There was a signiﬁcant relationship between
all three feedback types and self-efﬁcacy perceptions in theoretically
expected directions. Regression analyses revealed that positive instructional feedback (p < .001, b = .067, 95% CI [0.118, 0.383]) and punishment-oriented feedback predicted self-efﬁcacy perceptions (p = <.001,
b = .044, 95% CI [-0.238, -0.063]). Speciﬁcally, self-efﬁcacy is higher
when athletes are provided positive instructional based feedback and
lower when provided punishment oriented feedback or nonreinforcement and ignoring of mistakes. The ﬁndings differ from a previous study
by Horn (1985), which found that athletes may perceive frequent
positive feedback to mean they had lower ability, creating a negative
association with praise. The results of this study suggest that young
female athletes’ self-efﬁcacy beliefs are shaped by the feedback coaches
provide, and coaches should provide athletes with more positive feedback combined with instruction to improve their self-perception as a
youth female basketball player.
Supporting self-identiﬁed girls in sport: Assessing the feasibility of a
self-compassion intervention
Danielle L. Cormier, University of Alberta; Amber D. Mosewich, University of Alberta
This study assesses the feasibility of a self-compassion intervention
tailored for self-identiﬁed girls participating in youth sports. Participation
in youth sport provides signiﬁcant physical, emotional, and developmental beneﬁts; however, Canadian girls drop out at disproportionately higher
rates than boys, with one-third leaving during adolescence. In an earlier
needs assessment conducted during the initial stage of this project,
athletes, coaches, and parents identiﬁed harsh self-criticism as a
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S91
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 95] ---
signiﬁcant barrier to girls’ enjoyment and retention in sport. In response, a
psychoeducational workshop on self-compassion (i.e., a balanced approach towards and a desire to alleviate one’s experience of suffering, and
a known antidote to harsh self-criticism; Neff, 2003) was developed and
delivered to self-identiﬁed girls participating in week-long Canadian
university-afﬁliated summer camps. Measures of self-compassion and
related constructs were administered pre- and post-workshop, with
signiﬁcant improvements noted in the Self-Compassion Scale for Youth
(Neff et al., 2021; pre: M = 3.06, SD = 0.58; post: M = 3.37, SD = 0.28;
pre-post: t = -2.38, p = .02) and the Sussex-Oxford Compassion for the
Self Scale (Gu et al., 2019; pre: M = 72.29, SD = 10.96; post: M = 76.51,
SD = 10.81; pre-post: t = -2.08, p = .03). Participants rated the workshop
as enjoyable (M = 7.27/10), useful (M = 6.9/10), and indicated conﬁdence
in applying self-compassion in future sport challenges (M = 7.1/10). Inperson focus groups and online open-ended written feedback suggest
participants valued the interactive and varied teaching approaches used in
the session and perceived self-compassion as a fundamental skill to
counteract sport-related challenges. Constructive feedback included
breaking the single 90-minute session into smaller segments, further
integrating self-compassion into everyday sport environments, and aligning resources provided to athletes, coaches, and parents to create a
uniﬁed, supportive performance environment. These ﬁndings suggest
the intervention is feasible and holds promise in enhancing self-compassion and sustained sport participation among self-identiﬁed girls. Insights
gained from this phase will inform adjustments to the intervention, with
plans for larger-scale implementation to further evaluate its impact on
self-identiﬁed girl athletes. Funding source: Funding for this project
comes from D. L. Cormier’s Izaak Walton Killam & Grant Notley
Memorial Postdoctoral Fellowship.
Identifying conceptual attributes of physical activity maintenance
among adults with physical disability: Preliminary results
Tayah M. Liska, Department of Kinesiology and Physical Education,
McGill University; Shane N. Sweet, Department of Kinesiology and
Physical Education, McGill University
Evidence of physical activity (PA) maintenance remains limited due to the
inconsistent conceptualization of the term maintenance. Moreover, understanding the constructs that support PA maintenance speciﬁcally in a
physical disability context remains limited. Conceptual attributes associated with PA maintenance among adults with physical disability have been
proposed by a conﬁgurative review. These attributes are grouped in three
categories: (1) Attention towards PA which includes, supportive physical
environment, social support, self-monitoring, self-efﬁcacy, and grit; (2)
Inﬂuences towards PA participation which includes, health outcomes,
enjoyment, values PA, and gratiﬁcation/satisfaction; and (3) Achieving
maintenance which includes, consistency and achieving PA recommendations. The purpose of this preliminary analysis was to examine the
relationship of the attributes with participants PA levels and consistency
using baseline data of a 12-month longitudinal study currently being
conducted. Participants (n = 41) recruited by a disability service organization, were adults with physical disability who reported at least 30 min of
PA in the last month. Participants answered self-reported questionnaires
associated with identiﬁed PA maintenance attributes. Multivariate regression analyses were conducted separately for each category of attributes and
for each outcome: PA participation and consistency of PA participation.
Participants reported a mean PA participation of 155min/week (SD = 223)
and 76% (SD = 22) consistency of PA participation over 3-months.
Attributes within the Attention Towards PA category showed a positive
relationship towards PA participation, but none demonstrated a signiﬁcant
relationship. Only self-efﬁcacy was found as a signiﬁcant predictor of
consistency of PA maintenance (β = 0.78, p = .001). Attributes within the
Inﬂuences Towards PA category were also positively related, although
non-signiﬁcant with PA participation. Satisfaction with PA outcomes had a
signiﬁcant positive relationship with participants’ consistency of PA
maintenance over the 3-month period (β = 0.54, p = .05). These baseline
results provide initial outcomes of the identiﬁed PA maintenance attributes
among adults with physical disability. The frequency/strength of certain
attributes occurrences suggests these attributes could be relevant for PA
maintainers with physical disability. Continued analysis to predict PA and
consistency at future timepoints will provide clearer estimates of the
relationship of these attributes with PA maintenance among adults with
physical disability. Funding source: Social Sciences and Humanities
Research Council Doctoral Fellowship.
“For women, it very much is a community building sport”: Experiences of social support in women mountain bikers
Isabella Gore Rivero, California State University Fullerton; Kathleen S.
Wilson, California State University Fullerton
Mountain biking is a male-dominated action sport (Sharman, et al., 2023)
with differences in engagement reported between men and women (Roberts, et al., 2018). Within action sports, like mountain biking, social
aspects have been described as playing a role in motivation (Sharman et al.,
2023). Social support is one such aspect that has been linked to participation in physical activity (Scarapicchia et al., 2016). However, the role of
social support within action sports has received little attention. One model
that may guide our understanding of social support in this context is the
Thriving through Relationships model (Feeney & Collins, 2015). In this
model, social support can help individuals take advantage of life opportunities in addition to managing challenges. The purpose of this study was
to explore women mountain bikers’ experiences of social support within
the mountain bike community, focusing on goal exploration, connections with other riders, and gender speciﬁc experiences. Twenty-two
women mountain bikers (ages 29-61) participated in semi-structured
virtual interviews lasting on average 51 minutes. Participants had
varying mountain bike experience (beginner to expert) and were all
from North America. Interviews were transcribed and then analyzed. A
thematic analysis was used to identify the main themes that reﬂected the
women’s experiences (Braun & Clarke, 2006). Participants described
mountain biking as “a community unlike anything I’ve ever been a part
of” with two main themes identiﬁed. Within the theme of the women’s
community in a male dominated sport, participants discussed three
subthemes: a) the sport as male dominated with a lack of investment in
women, b) the creation of the women’s subcommunity, and c) the
importance of giving back to the women’s community. In the second
theme of ﬁnding the right group, three subthemes were identiﬁed: a)
social support as function of the community, b) riding group considerations, and c) it’s not just about mountain biking. Participants described
that they may not be where they are without receiving social support
within the sport and discussed how integral support has been to their
mountain biking experience. Overall, the ﬁndings emphasize the need to
foster a supportive community for women mountain bikers. Funding
source: CSUF EG-RSCA.
High-performance Canadian ice hockey coaches’ perceptions of creating a positive and inclusive team culture
Madison M. Fraser, McGill University; Danielle Alexander-Urquhart,
University of Ottawa; Gordon A. Bloom, McGill University
Hockey is one of the most dominant and inﬂuential sports in Canada with
approximately half a million Canadians participating in the sport each
year and 7.55 million viewers tuning in to watch the Stanley Cup ﬁnals in
2024. However, hockey culture has also been associated with negative
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S92
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 96] ---
behaviours (e.g., unhealthy representations of masculinity, excessively
aggressive play) that inﬂuence team culture, both on and off the ice. With
the understanding that coaches play a critical role in shaping team culture,
there is a need to explore how ice hockey coaches create and facilitate a
positive and inclusive team culture. Thus, this study explored Canadian
Hockey League (CHL) coaches’ perceptions of creating and maintaining
a positive and inclusive sport culture for elite 15–20-year-old male
hockey players. Participants were six male CHL hockey coaches across
Canada with an average of 15 years of coaching experience. All coaches
completed individual, virtual semi-structured interviews that were analyzed using a reﬂexive thematic analysis. Interestingly, coaches largely
drew from their own athletic experiences when building their team
culture, and at times, questioned whether they were approaching culture
in a way that would yield effective results. Coaches described several
challenges in creating a positive and inclusive team culture, including
managing athlete egos, acquiring parental buy-in, dealing with misbehaviour, improving poor communication, and managing high-performance expectations. The coaches worked to address these challenges
by modelling expected behaviours, holding athletes and staff accountable, fostering effective leadership, and developing trusting and respectful relationships among the team. Looking forward, coaches consistently
recommended an increase in coach education on how to build and sustain
a culture of excellence as well as ongoing support from provincial and
national sport organizations to succeed in this pursuit. These ﬁndings will
inform sport and coaching organizations on effective coaching strategies
and behaviours that have the potential to inﬂuence team satisfaction, wellbeing, and performance among athletes and coaches, both in Canada, and
around the world.
Impact of enjoyment on sustained swimming propulsion and athletic
performance
Lauren Koppelmann, John Carroll University; Jonny Simoes, Oakland
University; Joshua Haworth, Oakland University; Gregory Farnell, John
Carroll University
Participation in team sports can help to relieve stress, enhance socialization, and improve general welfare. Unfortunately, this is not the case
for all athletes, where performance in team sports can produce pressure
and anxiety. Currently, there is a lack of research addressing the effect
of sports enjoyment on athletic performance, particularly when enjoyment is diminished. When an athlete loses enjoyment of their sport, they
are at risk for burnout. With increasing pressures on elite athletes,
coaches, and athletes must understand the tangible effects of burnout.
This study examined how diminished enjoyment impacts physical
performance during swim training by measuring declines in performance. Collegiate swimmers (N = 25, 13 female, 12 male, Mage =
20.5 ± 1.6 years) completed a questionnaire reporting demographics,
injuries, and emotional relationship with the sport. Subjects then
completed three swimming trials wearing palmar force sensors which
measured triaxial forces. Pearson tests revealed no correlation between
hands; therefore, separate models were created for each hand. Participants were categorized into two groups: those who reported higher
enjoyment and those who reported lower enjoyment, derived from their
Likert Scale responses on the questionnaire. An independent-sample ttest was conducted to compare the slope of mean propulsive force in
high and low enjoyment conditions. The left hand showed a signiﬁcant
difference in scores for high enjoyment (M= .021, SD= .012) and low
enjoyment (M = .032, SD = .016) conditions; t(23) =-.1922, p = .034.
However, the right hand showed a non-signiﬁcant difference in scores
for high enjoyment (M= .025, SD= .020) and low enjoyment (M = .025,
SD = .014) conditions; t(23) = -.015, p = .494). This could support the
hypothesis that higher enjoyment enhances fatigue resistance, as
indicated by the slope of sustained propulsion in swimming trials.
These ﬁndings could suggest that swimmers who enjoy practice maintain stronger propulsion, while those with lower enjoyment exhibit
weaker propulsion throughout trials. This could support the idea that
burnout, often associated with low enjoyment, can lead to observable
declines in performance. More testing is needed to determine if the
right-hand exhibits similar trends to the left hand.
The perfect body is athletic: exploring perfectionism, disordered
eating and internalization of body ideals among health professionals
Yannick Rudolph, University of the Bundeswehr Munich; Avery Hinchcliffe, University of Toronto; Maryam Marashi, University of Toronto;
Catherine Sabiston, University of Toronto
Disordered eating (DE) is a growing concern, particularly among health
professionals who are frequently engaged in work focused on eating,
exercise, and weight. Self-oriented perfectionism (SOP), deﬁned as the
tendency to set unrealistic and often unattainable standards for oneself, is
a signiﬁcant predictor of DE behaviors and may also be prevalent among
health professionals. Theoretical mechanisms linking SOP to DE behaviors highlight body image factors. Speciﬁcally, the internalization of
appearance and body ideals may help explain the relationship between
SOP and DE. As part of a broad study examining DE among health
professionals, the current study examined the relationship between SOP
and DE, with the internalization of athletic body ideals (IABI; adopting
culturally valued standards of a lean, muscular physique) as a potential
mediator. Health professionals (N = 125, 44% exercise health professionals) completed an online survey that included measures of perfectionism (Multidimensional Perfectionism Scale; MPS), internalization of
body ideals (Sociocultural Attitudes Towards Appearance Questionnaire4; SATAQ-4) and disordered eating behaviors (Eating Attitudes Test-26;
EAT-26). Bivariate correlations and a mediation analysis were conducted
to explore the relationship between SOP and DE, with IABI included as a
mediator. Variables were moderately inter-related (SOP and DE, r = .48,
p < .001; SOP and IABI, r = .42, p < .001; DE and IABI, r = .53, p < .001).
The mediation analysis revealed a signiﬁcant overall model (R2 = 36%,
p < .001). The total effect of SOP and DE was signiﬁcant (c = 1.15,
SE = .20, p < .001). SOP was also signiﬁcantly directly associated with
IABI (a = .29, SE = .06, p < .001) and IABI was signiﬁcantly directly
associated with DE (b = 1.38, SE = .29, p < .001). The direct effect of SOP
on DE was weaker when internalization of athletic body ideals was
accounted for (c’= .745, SE = .20, p < .001). The indirect effect through
IABI was signiﬁcant (a*b = .403, 95% CI [0.198, 0.648]). Findings
support understandings of SOP as a contributing factor to DE among
health professionals. Furthermore, ﬁndings suggest that IABI may help
explain the relationship between SOP and DE among health professionals. Future education and training should be tailored to mitigate
these risks.
Daily perceptions of the built environment and physical activity in
adolescent girls: Role of motivation
Diamond McKelvey, University of North Carolina-Greensboro; Jessica
M. Dollar, University of North Carolina- Greensboro; Jaclyn P. Maher,
University of North Carolina- Greensboro
Research underscores the signiﬁcant inﬂuence of the built environment on
adolescent girls’ physical activity (PA). Even though the places girls go
change from one day to the next, almost no research has addressed how
daily ﬂuctuations in environmental perceptions (e.g., walkability) are
associated with girls’ daily PA. Further, it is unclear how girls own
motivation (i.e., the extent to which girls perceive their engagement in
PA as self-determined or not) may moderate these daily associations
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S93
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 97] ---
between the built environment and PA. This study explored whether
within-person (daily) changes in perceived built environment predicted
moderate-to-vigorous PA (MVPA) in adolescent girls, and whether motivation moderated this relationship. Over a 28-day study, adolescent girls
completed evening surveys assessing their daily perceptions of the built
environment and MVPA. At the study’s onset, girls reported the extent to
which their motivation to engage in MVPA was self-determined
(i.e., autonomous vs controlled). Multilevel modeling was employed to
(a) identify within-person (daily) associations between perceptions of the
built environment and MVPA, (b) identify between-person (average)
associations between perceptions of the built environment and MVPA,
and (c) test self-determined motivation as a potential moderator of those
associations. On days when girls perceived the environment as more
supportive of PA than usual, they reported signiﬁcantly higher MVPA
(β = 1.00, SE = 0.36, p < .01). By contrast, typical perceptions of the built
environment were not associated with differences in PA across individuals
(p = .43). Self-determined motivation was correlated with MVPA, but it
did not signiﬁcantly moderate built environment–MVPA associations
(p = .24). Findings suggest that girls’ perceptions of the built environment
on a given day are associated with MVPA. Further, associations between
the built environment and MVPA did not differ depending on the extent to
which girls perceived their PA engagement as self-determined. These
ﬁndings contradict theoretical frameworks and empirical work that suggest
individual’s motivation and built environment may interact to inﬂuence PA
engagement. Future research should include more diverse samples and
continue exploring how daily ﬂuctuations in individual motivation and
perceptions of the built environment shape PA in adolescent girls.
How coaches teach sportspersonship: Observations, coach perceptions, and player perceptions in adolescent competitive soccer
Trinity Rosado, Texas State University; Neal Ellis, Soccer Central;
Lindsay Kipp, Texas State University; Nicole Bolter, San Francisco State
University
Coaches can signiﬁcantly inﬂuence athletes’ sportspersonship, yet they
may adopt a neutral stance or “silent observation” during instances of poor
sportspersonship. The purposes of this study were to (a) develop an
observational tool for sportspersonship coaching behaviors and athletes’
prosocial and antisocial behaviors, and (b) compare observed behaviors
and self-reports by coaches and athletes. Five U14-U18 competitive soccer
teams (2 girls teams; 3 boys teams) participated, including 60 players
(M = 16.1 years, SD = .5) and 5 coaches (4 men; M = 35.0 yrs, SD = 5.0) An
observational tool was created based on past research on sportspersonship
coaching behaviors (e.g., modeling, instructing, and reinforcing good and
poor sportspersonship) and players’ prosocial and antisocial behaviors
(e.g., high-ﬁves, hard foul). For surveys, coaches and players reported on
sportspersonship coaching behaviors. Players also reported their prosocial
and antisocial behaviors in sport. Observations (10 practices and 3 games
total) revealed coaches’ most frequent behavior in practice was teaching
good sportspersonship and, in games, was disrespecting referees (modeling). Players’ most frequent behavior in practices was prosocial behavior
toward teammates and, in games, was prosocial behavior toward teammates followed by antisocial behavior toward opponents. A chi-square
analysis indicated unsportspersonlike behavior was signiﬁcantly more
likely to occur during games compared to practices (p < .05). Surveys
showed that coaches reported punishing poor sportspersonship (M = 3.60,
SD = .45) more often than players reported (M = 2.99, SD = 1.02), and
coaches
reported
teaching
and
reinforcing
good
sportspersonship
(M = 3.90, SD = .58; M = 3.60, SD = 1.1) more often than players reported
(M = 3.56, SD = .15; M = 3.03, SD = .12, respectively). T-tests were not
signiﬁcant (p > .05), but effect sizes showed a large effect for punish
(d = 1.05) and medium effects for teaches and reinforces (d = .72, .57),
indicating practically meaningful differences. In comparing observations
to surveys, self-reports demonstrated both players and coaches felt coaches
often modeled good sportspersonship, yet game observations showed
frequent modeling of unsportspersonlike behavior. Results point to a
possible coach-athlete communication gap as well as an intention-behavior
gap for coaches.
The generation effect: Collegiate coaches’ view of student-athletes
then and now
Caitlin Murphy, Florida State University; John Protzko, Central Connecticut State University
The “Kids These Days” theory (KTD) states that older generations tend to
see themselves as more favorable or superior compared to younger
generations (Protzko & Schooler, 2019). These attitudes from older
generations affect different facets of life, including athletics. Speciﬁcally,
the coach-athlete relationship can be affected based on generational clash
points that exist between coaches and athletes from different generations.
The current study aims to provide coaches with information about how
they view their current players compared to their former players to decrease
the generational barriers that could be negatively inﬂuencing the coachathlete relationship. A sample of Division I, II, and III collegiate coaches
who are at an NCAA afﬁliated institution (N = 173) completed an online
survey that measured the coaches’ attitudes towards their current versus
their former athletes, their perceptions about young adults, and their level
of coaching efﬁcacy. It was predicted that coaches would look more
favorably on their former athletes compared to their current athletes.
Additionally, the age of the coach, the number of years of coaching
experience, and self-reported level of coaching efﬁcacy were predicted
to inﬂuence the extent to which coaches look more favorably on their
former versus their current athletes. The coaches completed the CoachAthlete Relationship Questionnaire (CART-Q) two times to measure
their attitudes towards their current players and their attitudes towards
their former players. Difference scores were created for each subscale
on the CART-Q and one-sample t-tests revealed there was a nonsigniﬁcant difference between the coaches’ attitudes towards their current
versus their former players on all subscales. Further, multiple regression
analyses using clustered robust standard errors revealed age, years of
coaching experience, and level of coaching efﬁcacy did not predict the
difference in preferring past over current athletes. Exploratory analyses
revealed that older coaches tended to report feeling closer to and have
more similarities with their current athletes compared to younger
coaches. Future research should explore new variables that could
inﬂuence the attitudes of collegiate coaches towards their past and
current players.
Does like attract like within a team-based, technology-delivered physical activity intervention among adults?
Courtney Monroe, University of South Carolina; Amanda Rebar, University of South Carolina; Chelsea Carpenter, University of South Carolina;
Gabrielle Turner-McGrievy, University of South Carolina
Technology-based physical activity (PA) interventions seeking to leverage
support within self-selected teams of adults with existing social ties
(e.g., friends, family) via gamiﬁcation are increasingly being evaluated.
Homophily (similarity-based connection) is often present within one’s
existing social circle concerning sociodemographic characteristics and has
been linked with the social contagion of health behaviors. Yet, little is
known about similarities within self-selected teams recruited into technology-delivered PA interventions. This study explored the extent of similarities characterizing team formation within a randomized controlled trial
evaluating the efﬁcacy of Columbia Moves, a technology-delivered,
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S94
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 98] ---
gamiﬁed PA intervention. Self-selected teams (n = 24) of 3-8 insufﬁciently
active adults (N = 116) were recruited. Using baseline Columbia Moves
data, the current study evaluated whether participants were more similar to
their teammates versus nonteammates regarding age, sex, body mass index
(BMI), perceived social support (SS; Sallis SS Survey for Exercise; score
range: 1[low]-5[high]), accelerometer-measured moderate-to-vigorous PA
(MVPA), and afﬁnity for competition (Hypercompetitive Attitude Scale;
score range: 26[low]-130[high]) and cooperation (Cooperative Strategy
subscale; score range: 1[low]-40[high]). Intraclass correlations with 95%
conﬁdence intervals were calculated as a homophily proxy, testing for
signiﬁcant within-team variability. Participant characteristics include: 78%
female; Means: age=40.1 ± 10.3 yrs; BMI = 33.1 ± 6.9 kg/m2; MVPA=
15.1 ± 9.4 min/d; SS = 2.5 ± 0.6; Competitiveness = 62.4 ± 13.2; Cooperativeness = 31.7 ± 3.3). Individuals were signiﬁcantly more likely to be like
their teammates versus nonteammates regarding age (ICC = 0.37, 95% CI:
[0.12 to 0.56]), BMI (ICC = 0.19, 95% CI: [0.03 to 0.39]), and SS
(ICC = 0.15, 95% CI: [0.02 to 0.36]). Individuals did not tend to have
more similarities with their teammates versus nonteammates regarding sex,
MVPA, and afﬁnity for competition and cooperation (ICCs < 0.08, 95%
CIs: < 0 to 0.23). These results reveal the complexity of team composition
within self-selected teams of adults with existing social ties in a technology-delivered PA intervention, highlighting factors that inﬂuence team
similarity and guiding recruitment efforts to reach more participants in
need by targeting individuals with certain characteristic proﬁles for team
formation. Future research should test whether homophily within selfselected teams enhances PA intervention efﬁcacy via social contagion.
