Virtual and in-person physical activity programming for post-secondary students: Associations between therapeutic alliance, social connectedness, and depression symptoms
Melissa L. deJonge, University of Toronto; Catherine M. Sabiston,
University of Toronto
As distance-based learning increases across North American post-secondary campuses, offering both virtual and in-person physical activity (PA)
programs is crucial for supporting student well-being and enhancing
program accessibility. Therapeutic alliance (i.e., the collaborative and
supportive relationship between the PA trainer and program participant)
can inﬂuence the effectiveness of PA programs for promoting student wellbeing. Yet, limited research has explored how alliance factors (task, goal,
and bond alliance) in PA interventions impact well-being outcomes across
virtual and in-person delivery models. Using path analysis, the current
study explored the direct effects of task, goal, and bond alliance on social
connectedness and depression symptoms, and the indirect effects of task,
goal, and bond alliance on depression symptoms through social connectedness. Students (N = 90; Mage = 23.69 ± 4.80 years; 82% women; 30%
international student) experiencing poor mental health participated in a 6week supervised virtual (N = 62) or in-person (N = 28) PA program and
completed pre-post intervention self-report assessments. Controlling for
PA program delivery style and baseline characteristics, there were signiﬁcant (ps < .05) direct associations between task (β = .21) and bond alliance
(β = .22) on social connectedness, and a direct association between social
connectedness (β = -.67) on depression symptoms. There was also a
signiﬁcant indirect effect of task alliance on depression symptoms through
social connectedness (β = -.14). These ﬁndings support social connectedness as a mechanism through which task alliance may reduce depression
symptom severity in PA programs among post-secondary students. Further
research is needed to explore how program delivery characteristics
(e.g., motivational interviewing) and PA trainer training can enhance task
and bond alliance in intervention delivery. Targeted efforts to strengthen
therapeutic alliance may improve the effectiveness of both virtual and inperson PA programs in promoting social connectedness and alleviating
depression symptoms among post-secondary students.
Meta-Analysis of athlete ratings of coach–athlete relationship via the
coach-athlete relationship questionnaire (CART-Q)
Kat V Adams, Utah State University; Diana J. Meter, Utah State University; Travis E. Dorsch, Utah State University
The purpose of this study was to conduct a meta-analysis to determine what
factors inﬂuence an athlete’s rating of coach-athlete relationship quality, as
assessed by the Coach-Athlete Relationship Questionnaire (CART-Q)
(Jowett & Ntoumanis, 2004). Three databases were searched for studies
that reported means for the athlete version of the CART-Q, resulting in 53
articles with a total of 72 means from January 2000 – December 2023 from
15,196 total individuals. The country of the sample was reliably reported
across studies (n = 48), and there was a balanced gender distribution across
samples (57% male). Means for the full scale and subscales ranges from
5.43 to 5.75 on a 7-point scale. The studies included had narrow range of
ages, with mean ages for all but two studies falling between 12 and 30.
Furthermore, studies frequently aggregated scores for sex/gender; competitive level, and sport type, leading to insufﬁcient data to conduct
moderation analyses. Of the moderation analyses possible, the percent of
males included in a study’s sample did not have a moderating effect on the
full CART-Q scale, or any of the three subscales. Meanwhile, country of
sample was signiﬁcant for at least one subscale, but no moderation effect
was seen for the full CART-Q scale. Complementarity was the subscale
with the most moderation effects, followed by the closeness subscale. This
study highlighted the limited insighted currently provided by the CART-Q
and calls for more purposive, methodologically coherent sampling to better
examine how the coach-athlete relationship is inﬂuenced by athlete
characteristics.
The emotional and behavioral costs of body surveillance
Catherine Sabiston, University of Toronto; Edina Bijvoet, University of
Toronto; Timothy Welsh, University of Toronto; Luc Tremblay, University
of Toronto
Heightened body surveillance is associated with poor psychosocial wellbeing and motor and cognitive performance outcomes that are integral to
sport and exercise. However, the mechanisms that explain these associations are not well-elucidated. The current quasi-experimental study tested
the effects of a body surveillance manipulation on arousal, expressed
emotion, and motor performance. Participants [n = 73; M = 22.3 (SD = 4.3)
years of age; 73% female; 42% Asian] completed a measure of trait body
surveillance and a visuo-motor performance and adaptation task. Bodyfocused measurements (e.g., skin caliper assessments and surface area
measurements of the chest, waist, and upper arm, and thigh, and two full
body photos) were taken as the body surveillance manipulation. Galvanic
skin response (GSR; biosensor indicator of arousal) and facial expression
analysis (FEA; biosensor indicator of expressed emotion) were measured
throughout the experiment. Data were analyzed using correlations, t-tests,
and repeated measures ANOVA models. Trait body surveillance was
signiﬁcantly (p < .01) correlated with longer movement time (r = .27-
.29). Higher body surveillance scores were also associated with more
negative expressed emotion, r = .24. There was a signiﬁcant difference in
the movement times for pre- and post-manipulation, t(71) = 1.96; p = .03,
d = .11. Similar results were not observed for radial error, t(71) =.61, p=.27,
d = .06 or angular error t(71) = .84, p = .17, d = .11. There were signiﬁcant
(p < .01) time effects comparing pre-manipulation, during manipulation,
and post-manipulation for GSR peaks/min and emotions of sadness,

emotion valence but not anger (p = .07), surprise (p = .38), fear (p =

with an increase between pre- and during manipulation followed by a
decrease post-manipulation except for attention where scores decreased
during the manipulation. Generally, the ﬁndings indicated heightened
arousal and emotional distress during the body surveillance manipulation.

surveillance, motor performance and adaptation, arousal, and emotional
responses. Further research is needed to understand the ecological replication and validity of these ﬁndings in contexts such as sport and exercise.

Intermittent fasting protocols and their acute psychological effects in
young females: A randomized crossover study
Kristan Amendola, McMaster University; Laura Harris, McGill University; Rivka Ohayon, Tel Aviv University; Anthea Christoforou, McMaster
University; Alison K. Shea, McMaster University; Antony D. Karelis,
Université du Québec à Montréal; Jennifer J. Heisz, McMaster University
Intermittent fasting (IF), characterized by alternating periods of eating and
fasting, has gained popularity for its metabolic beneﬁts. However, the
psychological effects of IF are not well understood, with limited studies
reporting on its acute psychological impact and no comparison across
different fasting protocols. To address this gap, the present study utilized a
randomized crossover design to compare the acute psychological effects of
two popular fasting protocols—time-restricted feeding (TRF; 16-hour fast)
and alternate-date fasting (ADF; 24-hour fast). Twenty-two female participants (Mage = 18.3 ± 0.4 years) completed three days of TRF and three
days of ADF, separated by a four-day washout period. The fasting order
was counterbalanced across participants. On the day before fasting and on
each fasting day, participants completed online questionnaires assessing
hunger (Hunger-Satiety scale), diet satisfaction (Likert scale from 1, “I am
not enjoying this diet at all” to 5, “I am really enjoying this diet”), mood,
(Proﬁle of Mood States 2) and sleep quality (Consensus Sleep Diary).
Responses were analyzed using separate repeated-measures ANOVAs
with within-subject factors of condition (TRF, ADF) and time (baseline,
1, 2, 3). Preliminary results indicated that hunger increased to a lesser
extent during TRF compared to ADF [condition x time interaction: F(3,
57) = 21.02, p < 0.001] and diet satisfaction was signiﬁcantly higher during
TRF than ADF [main effect of condition: F(1, 20) = 18.17, p < 0.001].
Neither fasting protocol signiﬁcantly affected total mood disturbance or
sleep quality. Overall, the acute psychological effects of IF were minimal
in this sample of young females. However, TRF resulted in lower hunger
levels and greater diet satisfaction compared to ADF, suggesting it might
be a more sustainable protocol for long-term adherence. Funding: NSERC.
Effortful self-control mediates the relationships between autonomous
motivation and habit with physical activity
Jonathon Bourque, Rutgers University; Anthony Delli Paoli, Rutgers
University; Hannah Perdue, Rutgers University; Luke Poole, Rutgers
University; Amber Sarwani, Rutgers University; Brandon Alderman,
Rutgers University
Long-term maintenance of physical activity is needed to achieve many of its
associated health beneﬁts. Although the development and application of
theory-based interventions aimed at promoting physical activity have
resulted in moderate success, the observed effects are mostly short-term.
Research efforts that focus on the long-term maintenance of physical
activity are needed to extend current understanding. Autonomous motivation and habits are two widely studied constructs implicated with physical
activity maintenance. However, there is a need to investigate potential
mediators that explain how autonomous motivation and habits facilitate the
maintenance of physical activity. One characteristic associated with greater
autonomous motivation and stronger habits that may explain how these
constructs work is behavioral engagement that requires little to no effortful
self-control. Thus, one potential reason autonomous motivation and habits
may promote physical activity behavior and maintenance is by reducing the
amount of effortful self-control needed to engage in physical activity. The
present study examined whether effortful self-control mediates the relationships between autonomous motivation and habit with moderate-to-vigorous
physical activity (MVPA). Six hundred three participants (327 females;
Mage=38.6 ± 12.0 years) completed an online survey assessing autonomous
motivation, habit, perceived amount of effortful self-control needed to
engage in physical activity, and MVPA via the International Physical
Activity Questionnaire Short-Form. A path model with bias-corrected
95% conﬁdence intervals based on 5,000 bootstrapped samples was used
to test mediation effects. The effect of autonomous motivation on MVPA
was fully mediated by effortful self-control, βdirect = .04, p = .38, βindirect =
.06, p = .002, 95% CI [0.03, 0.10]. The effect of habit on MVPA was
partially mediated by effortful self-control, βdirect = .15, p = .007, βindirect =
.11, p = .001, 95% CI [0.05, 0.19]. These results suggest that reduced
amounts of effortful self-control needed to engage in physical activity may
explain how greater autonomous motivation and stronger habits facilitate
physical activity behavior. These results can help extend current theories to
better inform interventions to promote physical activity maintenance.
More kids, less fun? A test of the resource dilution model in organized
youth sport
Emily DeLisle, Utah State University; Travis Dorsch, Utah State
University
The resource dilution model (RDM) posits that families have ﬁnite resources
and that the resources available to each child are therefore diminished as the
number of children in a family grows (Blake, 1981, 1989). There is evidence
supporting RDM in several educational settings; however, it is unknown
whether RDM applies to the processes families use to allocate their
resources in youth sport settings. The present study examined the impact
the number of siblings participating in youth sport had on the time and
ﬁnancial resources families allocated to their oldest child’s participation in
sport. It was hypothesized that the number of athletes in a family would be
inversely associated with the amount of time an athlete participated in sport
activities (e.g., training and competition) as well as the amount of family
spending (e.g., equipment, travel, coaching, team fees) toward that participation. Participants were derived from a national sample of 2485 parents
(Mage = 39.1, SDage = 8.2) with youth who were active participants in
organized sport (Mage = 12.2, SDage = 3.1). Counter to our hypotheses,
results of linear regression models indicated that the number of athletes
in the family was positively and signiﬁcantly related to both the amount of
time (b = 2.51, CI [1.66-3.36], p < .001) and the amount of spending on the
oldest child’s sport participation (b = 276.5, CI [66.4 – 486.6], p = .01).
Findings suggest that as the number of athletes in a family increases, both
the amount of time and spending on the oldest child’s sport participation
also increases. This provides evidence counter to a primary tenet of RDM,
suggesting that sport may be a unique context of family resource allocation.
Future research should explore the processes families engage in when
determining resource allocation among siblings in sport.
Bridging policy and practice: Exploring mental health assets in varsity
athletes and non-athletes in Canada
Jessica Malcolm, Brock University; Phillip Sullivan, Brock University
Promoting mental health in elite athletic environments has become a
priority, as reﬂected in the growing prevalence of mental health policies
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

--- [Página 100] ---
in sports. However, a critical gap exists between high-level sport policies
and the empirical evidence required to support their effective implementation. Traditional mental health interventions and policies have predominantly focused on addressing individual-level deﬁcits and mental illness.
Recent research advocates for promoting the development of mental health
assets—individual-level resources that maximize the ability of individuals
to attain and maintain mental health and well-being (e.g., help-seeking). To
address this gap, our study investigates the mental health assets of
resilience, social support, and mental health service utilization among
varsity and non-athletes, focusing on potential gender differences. Using
data from the 2023 deployment of the Canadian Campus Wellbeing Survey
(CCWS), which included responses from 1,151 athletes (468 male and 690
female) and 24,468 non-athletes (6,710 males and 12,758 females), we
analyzed differences in mental health assets. Custom questions developed
by CCWS measured resiliency, social support, and mental health service
utilization. Results revealed that athletes reported higher levels of social
support compared to non-athletes (F(1, 25,615) = 21.85, p < .001). Additionally, signiﬁcant interaction effects were observed for resiliency and
help-seeking, with athletes scoring higher than non-athletes on both
measures
(F(1,
25,740) = 111.74,
p
<.001);
F(1,
25,705) = 23.87,
p < .001, respectively). Male athletes demonstrated superior mental health
assets on two of the three measures. These results suggest that female
athletes may require more targeted support within mental health policies.
Our ﬁndings provide knowledge users with the evidence and information
required to implement mental health policies meaningfully. By advancing
the understanding of the mental health assets of athletes, this research aims
to bridge the gap between policy intentions, empirical evidence, and
actionable strategies. Ultimately, this work seeks to enhance the mental
well-being of athletes in Canada, fostering a more informed and supportive
athletic environment.
Is it you, me, or the environment? The inﬂuence of motivational
climate, hierarchy, and pride displays on teammates’ perceptions and
emotions
Jordan Smith, Florida State University; Svenja Wolf, Florida State
University; Jens Lange, Universitat Hamburg; Jon Maner, Florida State
University; Robert Eklund, Florida State University; Jeannine Turner,
Florida State University
Individuals rely on others’ emotions to interpret their surroundings (Hess
& Hareli, 2015), but distinguishing between emotional expressions and
individual interpretations within situational contexts is complex. In teams,
pride displays elicit varying responses based on whether they are perceived
as authentic or hubristic (Lange et al., 2018; Lange & Crusius, 2015).
Authentic pride fosters positive appraisals and emotions that beneﬁt teams,
while hubristic pride often triggers detrimental reactions (Smith et al.,
2022, 2024). This present study examined how motivational climate and
hierarchical structure shape interpretations of pride displays and emotional
responses. Using a 2 (motivational climate: task vs. ego) × 2 (hierarchy:
prestige vs. dominance) × 3 (pride displays: authentic, hubristic, or embarrassment) mixed-design vignette experiment, 169 undergraduates
(nmale = 42, nfemale = 122) completed a survey assessing perceptions of
pride and emotional responses (e.g., benign envy, malicious envy, admiration; Tracy & Matsumoto, 2008; van de Ven et al., 2011) while
experiencing all motivational climate and hierarchy types. Results revealed
signiﬁcant main effects for motivational climate, F(6, 610) = 9.44,
p < .001, ηp
2 = .085), hierarchy, (2.13, p = .049, ηp
2 = .020), and expression of pride, (77.44, p < .001, ηp
2 = .432) on dependent variables. Hubristic pride and malicious envy were associated with ego climates, while
admiration was stronger in task climates. Malicious envy was more
prevalent in dominance hierarchies, whereas admiration was greater in
prestige hierarchies. Authentic pride expressions were often misperceived
as hubristic, leading to increased malicious envy; however, when perceived as authentic, admiration increased. A signiﬁcant interaction effect
between hierarchy and pride display, F(12, 1222) = 5.75, p < .001, ηp
2 =
.053) showed responses varied by context: authentic pride was highest in
prestige hierarchies with hubristic expressions and in dominance hierarchies with authentic expressions. Benign envy was higher in dominance
hierarchies and authentic pride, malicious envy peaked with dominance
hierarchies and hubristic pride, and admiration was strongest in prestige
hierarchies with hubristic pride or dominance hierarchies with authentic
pride. These ﬁndings highlight how team environments shape pride
interpretations and emotional responses, underscoring the complexity of
emotion perception in team settings.
The temporal dynamics of cognitive enhancement following acute
physical activity: Beneﬁts for episodic recognition memory, not inhibitory control or working memory
Praveen Pasupathi, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Megan
O’Brokta, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Andrew Cornwall,
University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Bryan Montero-Herrera,
University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Eric Drollette, University of
North Carolina at Greensboro
Existing research underscores the positive inﬂuence of acute physical
activity (PA) on cognition. Yet, it remains unclear whether these enhancements are domain-speciﬁc or universal cognitive improvements
following acute PA. Further, there is limited understanding of the
temporal dynamics of cognitive changes after acute PA, particularly in
emerging adults. This study aimed to evaluate the effects of acute PA on
separate cognitive domains (recognition memory, working memory, and
inhibitory control) and time windows (immediate, short, and long delays)
that are optimal for improving performance for emerging adults. Using a
within-participants cross-over design, thirty emerging adults (n = 30;
Mage = 22.4 ± 1.6 years; 21 females) visited the lab on two separate
days engaging in either a 30-minute moderate-intensity PA or seated rest
(watching educational video) control session. Participants completed
cognitive tasks pre- and post-acute PA and seated rest. Recognition
memory included measures of primacy (ﬁrst 10 words), recency (last
10 words), and the overall accuracy of words. Working memory (change
detection dot task; set sizes from 2-5) and inhibitory control (modiﬁed
ﬂanker task) were evaluated after a short (working memory task started at
5 minutes post) and long delay (ﬂanker task started at 25 mins post).
Results revealed maintenance in inhibitory control and working memory
outcomes [reaction time (p’s > .48) and response accuracy (p’s > .75)]
after acute PA compared to rest and across time. Additionally, participants improved overall primacy and recency accuracy, following acute
PA (after PA: 77.3 ± 3.2 %, before PA: 69.2 ± 4.0 %; t (29) = 2.23,
p ≤.03), and a signiﬁcant trend (p = .08) for overall word recognition
accuracy revealing higher accuracy after PA. Together, results indicate
that acute PA offers selective ﬁndings in cognition such that inhibitory
control and working memory performance were maintained after short
and long delays following acute PA, while, recognition memory, improved when assessed immediately after PA. These results collectively
highlight that acute PA offers temporal domain-speciﬁc improvements in
cognition.
One of these bodies is not like the others: Contrast effects emerge when
rating body sizes of individuals presented in groups
April Karlinsky, California State University, San Bernardino; Priscilla
Jussicha, California State University, San Bernardino; Angela Haven A.
Barandino, California State University, San Bernardino; Ashley Colstock
Horn, California State University, San Bernardino; Catherine M. Sabiston, University of Toronto; Timothy N. Welsh, University of Toronto
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S97

--- [Página 101] ---
The “Cheerleader Effect” describes the ﬁnding where individuals are
perceived as more attractive when they are presented in groups compared
to alone. Previous research has tested whether a similar pattern of effects
emerges for perceptions of body size. Although the Cheerleader Effect was
not observed because ratings of body size were not inﬂuenced by whether
the body was presented alone or in a group of similar-sized peers, the
ratings were inﬂuenced by differences in body size across groupmates.
Speciﬁcally, contrast effects emerged such that a centrally-presented
model was rated as thinner when ﬂanked by heavier bodies, and vice
versa. These effects increased as the difference in body size between the
model and groupmates increased. In the previous study, all images were
presented on a neutral white background. The current study was designed
to test whether or not the context (physical activity vs. non-physical
activity setting) in which models were presented was a modulator of
perceptions of body size. Participants (n = 13 women, Mage = 22 years)
rated the body size of women models, presented alone or in groups of three.
When presented in groups, the central (to-be-judged) model had either a
larger, similar, or smaller body size than the other 2 models. These
differences could be small, medium, or large. Models were presented
wearing similar athleisure clothing either in a gym or ofﬁce context.
Participants rated the body size of the alone/central model on a continuous
scale from “very thin” to “very heavy”. The data replicated previous
results. There was no overall Cheerleader Effect because there was no
difference in body size ratings of models presented alone or between
groupmates of similar body size (p > .05). Contrast effects emerged,
however, with bodies being rated as thinner when presented alongside
heavier groupmates and vice versa (ps < .05). There were trends for these
contrast effects to increase as the differences between groupmates increased, and to be exacerbated in the gym vs ofﬁce context. These ﬁndings
provide further support that perceptions of body size are inﬂuenced by the
body size of groupmates and may indicate that the context can inﬂuence
perceptions of body size.
Family inﬂuences on NCAA Division 1 athletes’ emotion regulation
Jared Vance, Utah State University; Shannon Pynn, Utah State University
Student-athletes face unique mental health challenges as they navigate the
demands of balancing athletic, academic, and social responsibilities
(Brown et al., 2022). Emotion regulation (ER) plays a pivotal role in
managing these challenges, as it is closely linked to academic and athletic
success as well as psychological and physical well-being. Initiatives to
support student-athlete mental health have emphasized the importance of
engaging families in fostering environments that promote the mental health
and well-being of student-athletes (NCAA Sport Science Institute, 2024).
Additionally, research highlights the parent-child relationship as a signiﬁcant inﬂuence on the development of children’s ER strategies (Kiel &
Kalomiris, 2015). However, there is limited research examining the
speciﬁc ways family dynamics may shape the ER strategies of collegiate
athletes. This study aims to explore how families, particularly parents,
inﬂuence the ER of NCAA student-athletes. Using an interpretative
phenomenological approach (Smith et al., 2022), we conducted semistructured interviews with ten NCAA Division I student-athletes (ﬁve men,
ﬁve women). Participants reﬂected on their relationships with their parents,
their emotional experiences as student-athletes, and the strategies they
employ to regulate emotions in response to sport-related stressors. Guided
by Morris et al.’s (2007) tripartite model of family impact on emotion
regulation and adjustment, we identiﬁed three higher-order themes: (1)
observing the emotionality of parents and siblings, (2) speciﬁc parenting
practices related to emotion socialization, and (3) the emotional climate
and cultural values of the family. This research provides valuable insights
into the role of the family context in shaping student-athletes’ ER, with
implications for future sport parent interventions and family-based support
strategies. Practical recommendations for fostering environments that
support student-athlete mental health and well-being will be discussed.
Associations among variables representing Newell’s model of constraints and the season-long performance of adolescent cross-country
runners
Thatcher Coleman, University of South Carolina; Sally Miedema, University of South Carolina; Jeremy Lewis, University of South Carolina;
Skylar Allen, University of South Carolina; Hayes Bennett, Kennesaw
State University; Heather Holder, University of South Carolina; Eva
Monsma, University of South Carolina
A challenging aspect of coaching adolescent athletes is tailoring the
training environment to facilitate sport-speciﬁc development constrained
by an athlete’s current physical and psychological characteristics. This
season-long case study aimed to 1) explore cross-country athletes’ physical
and psychological characteristics relative to their performance, and 2)
uncover themes underlying their reﬂections of training and performance.
Utilizing variables representing subdomains of Newell’s (1986) model of
interacting individual, task and environmental constraints, purposeful
network sampling was used to recruit eight middle (n = 4; 2 males, 2
females) and high school aged (n = 4; 2 males, 2 females) cross-country
athletes. Anthropometrics were collected. Based on population-referenced
somatotype, BMI and leg length data, runners were further categorized as
physically resembling elite-cross country runners (elite prototypes (EP),
n = 4) or not (non-elite prototypes (NPT), n = 4). Across 4 races, conﬁdence and anxieties were assessed using CSAI-2R collected in tandem
with post-race reﬂections respective of race importance and performance
results. Post-season, athletes participated in semi-structured interviews
illuminating their perceptions of their body type, anxieties and conﬁdence,
training experiences and performance. Deductive analysis informed the
alignment of variables representing Newell’s model. Four interdependent
themes emerged from inductive thematic analysis: prior experience,
interpersonal interactions, goal alignment, and personal values. For example, athletes described adverse conﬁdence, season-long satisfaction, and/or
anxiety when perceptions of success involved rigid, ﬁxed expectations to
meet performance or outcome goals. Overall, EPs across age-groups and
sex, ran faster, reported lower anxieties, found cognitive anxiety facilitative, and reported higher conﬁdence than NPTs. Future research should
explore interactions among anthropometric and psychological variables as
they relate to performance in larger samples, with the goal of informing
individualized coaching practices for adolescent athletes not only for
directing elite training opportunities but maintaining life-long sport
participation.
Physical activity, cigarette smoking, and depression in people
with HIV
Nicholas SantaBarbara, Merrimack College; Melissa St. Hilaire, Merrimack College; Deborah Konkle-Parker, University of Mississippi; Warren
Scott Comulada, University of California, Los Angeles; Jennifer Cantrell,
New York University; Amber D’Souza, Johns Hopkins University; Robert
Foronjy, Downstate Health Sciences University; Susan Koletar, The Ohio
State University; Matthew Mimiaga, University of California, Los Angeles; Frank Palella Jr, Northwestern University; Daniel Merenstein,
Georgetown University; Anna Rubtsova, Emory University; Sarath Raju,
Johns Hopkins University; Claudia Martinez, University of Miami; Kristine Erlandson, University of Colorado Denver
People with HIV (PWH) are 2-3 times more likely to smoke cigarettes and
3 times more likely to have depression than people without HIV (PWoH).
PWH with depression are twice as likely to smoke compared to PWH
without depression. Physical activity (PA) can aid in smoking cessation in

PWoH, and reduce depression in people with and without HIV, but its
role in smoking cessation among PWH is unknown. Data collected from
the Multicenter AIDS Cohort Study (MACS)/Women Interagency HIV
Study (WIHS) Combined Cohort Study (MWCCS) were analyzed using
logistic regression and structural equation modeling to examine associations between cigarette smoking, PA and depression. Demographics and
smoking status (current smoker/non-smoker) were collected by selfreport. Height and weight were collected to calculate body mass index
(BMI). PA (International Physical Activity Questionnaire- Short Form
[IPAQ-SF]) was categorized as sufﬁciently active (metabolic activity of
task score [MET] min/week > 500) or insufﬁciently active (MET-min/
week ≤500). Depressive symptoms were dichotomized as at-risk (Center
for Epidemiological Studies Depression [CES-D] score ≥16) or not atrisk (<16). The sample consisted of 1,584 PWH (54.8 ± 10.4 years),
including 979 (62%) female sex at birth, 999 (63%) Black/African
American, 1,349 (85%) Non-Hispanic or Latino. 400 (25%) were smokers, 505 (32%) were insufﬁciently active, and 420 (27%) were at risk for
depression. The odds of being a current smoker were higher among PWH
who were insufﬁciently active compared to sufﬁciently active PWH
(OR = 1.42 [95% CI: 1.12-1.80], p = .004), even after adjusting for age,
sex, BMI, race and ethnicity. Similarly, the odds of being a current
smoker were higher among PWH at-risk for depression compared to
PWH not at risk (OR = 1.77 [95% CI: 1.38-2.25], p < .001); this relationship remained after adjusting for age, sex, BMI, and race and ethnicity. In
mediation analysis, PA and depression had independent yet direct effects
on smoking among PWH.PA, depression, and cigarette smoking are
related in adult PWH. Speciﬁcally, these results demonstrate that physical
inactivity and depression are each strongly associated with smoking in
PWH. Longitudinal studies are needed to explore PA as a tool in smoking
cessation for PWH.
Establishing a multidisciplinary preventative student-athlete wellbeing strategy
Michael Jorgensen, University of Toronto; Danika Quesnel, University of
Toronto; Isabella Tremonte, University of Toronto; Catherine Sabiston,
University of Toronto
There is a need for improved mental health support for student-athletes. As
university budgets continue to be stretched, there is a marked gap in the
resources provided to student-athletes relative to the demand for support as
they confront the complex challenges associated with competing as a
varsity athlete. Moreover, many resources are reactive and treatmentfocused rather than proactive and prevention-oriented. The ﬁrst step to
addressing this need is understanding the range of support structures for
mental health across the Canadian university landscape. Therefore, the
purpose of this study was to describe university mental health strategies
and support for student-athletes. This study was conducted using a
bilingual purpose-built survey to gain an understanding of the existing
mental health services for student-athletes, barriers to mental health
support, and facilitators to mental health programming. The survey was
emailed to university athletics directors across Canada. Ongoing data
collection has captured 18 participant responses to date. Notably, 44% of
participants reported receiving no funding for student-athlete mental
health, while 89% reported that less than 10% of their budget was
dedicated toward student-athlete mental health. The majority of participants (61%) also reported that they had no processes in place to identify
athletes who might have mental health concerns, and only half of the
participants conﬁrmed that there were mental health prevention programs
available for student-athletes at their institutions. Furthermore, 39% of
participants noted that the current strategic plan for athletics at their
institution did not include a focus on student-athlete mental health, despite
broad recognition that mental health challenges affect the athletic and
academic performance of student-athletes. Findings will further be delineated across institution-speciﬁc demographics and depict the current
mental health service provision landscape across Canadian universities.
There is a clear need for low-cost prevention strategies that can be
sustainably implemented across the Canadian university system. In response to our emerging ﬁndings, we will also present the preliminary steps
for an athlete-centred, multidisciplinary approach to designing a strengthbased and prevention-oriented mental well-being program.
Funding source: Tanenbaum Institute for Science in Sport (TISS)
Seed Grant.
Pickleballers’ sense of community, belonging & well-being across 1888-year-olds
Darshan Patel, East Carolina University; Stacy Warner, East Carolina
University; E. Whitney G. Moore, East Carolina University
Social community connections are associated with decreased mortality and
higher quality of life; unfortunately, individuals tend to become isolated from
their local communities as they age (Tiernan, Lysack, Neufeld, & Lichtenberg, 2013). Therefore, it is important to ﬁnd ways to increase community
engagement across the lifespan. The purpose of this study was to examine
pickleballers’ sense of community (SoC) with belonging and well-being
across three age groups (< 55, 55-64, and 65+). Of the 340 participants who
started the online survey about their pickleball experience, including sense of
community, life satisfaction, mental and physical health, and belonging to the
community, 298 provided their age – 18-54 (n = 78, 26%), 55-64 (n = 83,
28%), and 65+ (n = 137, 46%) – and were included in the subsequent
analyses. The majority identiﬁed as White (88%), and women (61%), with
38% identifying as men. Other identities – African American, Asian, Latinx,
biracial, prefer to not specify – had low representation (n < 10). The SoC
scores were reliably measured (α = .93, 95%CI [0.918, 0.942]). One-way
ANOVAs were used to test for age group differences. Correlations with 95%
CI were compared across the age groups. Participants reported having a good
SoC (M = 4.06 out of 5), physically active due to pickleball (M = 3.56 out of
5), and increased interest in their overall health (M = 5.59 out of 7); all of
which were not signiﬁcant moderated by age. Those 65+ reported signiﬁcantly greater belonging to their local community (M = 8.17) than those <55
(M = 7.44). Compared to older adults, those <55 reported signiﬁcantly lower
well-being: life satisfaction (M<55 = 8.06; M55-64 = 8.63; M64+ = 8.58); physical
health
(M<55 = 7.50;
M55-64 = 8.21;
M64+ = 8.16);
mental
health
(M<55 = 7.85; M55-64 = 8.66; M64+ = 9.10). Five of the six SoC correlations
were signiﬁcantly moderated by age. Overall, pickleballers’ SoC and belonging were signiﬁcantly, positively correlated with their well-being. Interestingly, results suggest playing pickleball promotes a SoC, which can facilitate
belonging and well-being for 18–88-year-olds with the potential impact
increasing with age.
Students’ Super Bowl experiences: Beyond learning to belonging
Stacy Warner, East Carolina University; Andrea Buenano, East Carolina
University; E. Whitney G. Moore, East Carolina University; Rachel
Madsen, Ithaca University
Hands-on learning is fundamental to preparing students to work in the
sport industry. This experiential learning can take many forms from
internships to volunteer experiences. Regardless of the experience the
goal stays the same—to enhance student learning and outcomes. Volunteer
mega-sporting event experiences are becoming increasingly popular to
help give students hands-on experience while exposing them to high-level
sporting environment (Warner & Buena˜no, 2024). To date, few researchers
(cf. Buena˜no et al., 2024) have considered the student gains from these
experiences compared to expectations. Using Experiential Learning and
Situated Learning theories as a framework, we explore student outcomes
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S99
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 103] ---
related to their mega sporting event experience. Experiential Learning
Theory contends it is necessary to connect education with experience as
students retain more when they are actively engaged in the learning
(e.g., Freeman et al., 2014; Theobald et al., 2020), while Situated Learning
Theory posits learning as “social coparticipation” (Lave & Wenger, 1991,
p.14) and argues student learning is elevated when students engage in
practical and collaborative tasks with others. Lave and Wenger also
describe such environments as communities of practice. Using open-ended
questions and content analysis of survey answer (Elo et al., 2014; Schreier,
2014), data from 82 students working Super Bowl LVII collected before
and after the event were analyzed. Results indicate three common themes
regarding the anticipated outcomes for the experience: Networking Experiences, Professional Development, and Mega-Event Experience. These
identical themes also were reported as post-event outcomes along with an
additional theme, Community of Practice. Although students’ expectations
aligned with their reported gains, the emergence of Community of Practice
as a post-event outcome is noteworthy. Simply, students did not anticipate
the sense of community that they would experience by taking part in the
mega-event experience. As loneliness has become recognized as a major
health concern (U.S. Surgeon General Report, 2023) and 70% of college
students report mental health struggles with only 37% seeking college
resources (Wood, 2024), it is important to explore how Community of
Practices and student experiential learning experiences can play an important role in providing social connections and community for college
students. Funding source: East Carolina University.
“The best teammate I’ve ever had”: A grounded theory of professional
athletes and successful marriage
Stephen Fife, Texas Tech University/Associate Professor; Jaclyn Cravens
Pickens, Texas Tech University/Associate Professor; Allison Bonner,
Texas Tech University/Master’s student; Karla Piccio, Texas Tech University/Master’s student; Monica Osterbauer, Texas Tech University/PhD
student; Shi’Anne Swift, Texas Tech University/PhD student; Michael
White, Texas Tech University/MS student; Nicole Smalley, Texas Tech
University/PhD student
Undoubtedly, professional athletes encounter unique demands that create
threats to their marriages. Indeed, the spouses of professional baseball
players have shared that the prioritization that must be given to the
athletes’ career over their marriage leaves spouses feeling powerless
(Ortiz, 2020). With a divorce rate of 80% (Lester & Gunn, 2013), a
percentage exceedingly higher than the national average, there is a need to
understand how professional athletes navigate the challenges of maintaining successful marriages. Constructivist grounded theory methods
(Charmaz, 2014) were utilized to analyze 20 semi-structured interviews
with current and retired professional athletes from the NFL, NBA, and
MLB and their spouses. A team of eight researchers utilized Charmaz’s
(2014) constructivist grounded theory, meeting weekly throughout the
analysis process. Couples who participated had an average marriage
length of 17 years and ages ranging from 25 – 69 (athletes) and 25 – 68
(spouses). Our grounded theory identiﬁes important actions that athletes
and their spouses take to protect and strengthen their marriage while
offsetting the unique challenges associated with professional athletics.
Challenges related to being a professional athlete included signiﬁcant
time away from their partner, performance pressure, living with career
uncertainty, and opportunities to engage in activities harmful to a
committed partnership. Foundational to participants withstanding these
challenges was remaining true to their values, with a key value being
prioritizing their marriage. Prioritizing their marriage led to establishing
boundaries, regularly communicating, and building communities of
support. Couples also protected their marriage from the demands of a
professional athletic career by adopting an attitude of ‘we-ness’ and
providing mutual support. These actions helped couples maintain trust
and connection. The results of our study provide a model of how
professional athletes and their spouses can maintain a successful marriage, illustrating not only how to avoid pitfalls but also what intentional
actions they can engage in to enhance their marriage. Our ﬁndings offer
empirical evidence that can be used to develop strategies for reducing the
high divorce rates of professional athletes. This includes guidance for
mental health professionals, as well as resources that professional sports
leagues could provide in their athlete education programing.
The Mindful Pain Reappraisal Scale: Initial validity evidence for use
during isometric exercise
Sara Thompson, University of Toronto; McKenna Zanelli, Washington
State University; Reese Small, Washington State University; Sarah Ullrich-French, Washington State University
Pain is a barrier to exercise continuation (Rice et al., 2019). A scale was
recently developed to assess the general ability to mindfully reappraise pain
(MPRS: Garland et al., 2023) and could help us understand how to manage
exercise induced pain. This study used the MPRS to assess state mindful pain
reappraisal during isometric exercises (plank and wall sit). The purpose of this
study was to test the psychometric properties of the state MPRS during
isometric exercises and explore correlations for initial construct validity.
Participants (N = 127, 50% female, Mage = 22.01, SD = 4.47) completed
assessments of trait mindfulness, level of pain, and level of pain interfering
with life over the last four weeks. Participants were asked to hold a plank and
wall sit to exhaustion and completed state measures of mindfulness, mindful
pain reappraisal, and attentional focus after each exercise. We tested a one
factor CFA model for state MPRS during plank and wall sit, respectively,
using MPlus. Aligned with Garland, we let the errors correlate between
similar items. Model ﬁt for plank MPRS was (CFI = .92, TLI = .88,
RMSEA = .10, SRMR = .05). Factor loadings (.53 - .78) were signiﬁcant
(p < .001). Model ﬁt for wall sit MPRS when errors were allowed to correlate
showed good ﬁt (CFI = .95, TLI = .92, RMSEA = .08, SRMR = .04). Factor
loadings (.50 - .80) were signiﬁcant (p < .001). The average score was
calculated for the plank and wall sit exercise experience. The internal
consistency reliability was good for plank (α = .88) and wall sit (α = .90)
MPRS. State MPRS was correlated with trait mindfulness (plank r = .33, wall
sit r = .38), state mindfulness (plank r = .44, wall sit r = .50), external
attentional focus (plank r = -.22, wall sit r = -.12ns), and general level of
pain experienced in the last 4 weeks (plank r = .11ns, wall sit r = .18). Results
are preliminary but show promise for the MPRS to be a useful measure to
explore how pain may be managed during exercise.
Examining the jingle-jangle of leadership and motivational climate
constructs
E. Whitney G. Moore, East Carolina University; Joan L. Duda, University
of Birmingham
Jingle refers to seemingly the same constructs having different correlations
with extrinsic convergent validity (ECV) constructs; thus, they should not
be treated as the same construct. Jangle refers to seemingly different
constructs having the same/similar correlations with ECV constructs; thus,
these different constructs are acting the same or are overlapping. The
purpose of this study was to examine the jingle-jangle in regard to the
multitude of leadership styles and motivational climate scales in sport. 466
British athletes (Mage = 20.88 yrs, SD = 2.42; 44% Women, 56% Men)
across a wide variety of sports completed a planned missing data designed
online survey including measures of leadership styles (authentic, servant,
transformational, and transactional), motivational climates (empowering,
disempowering, and caring) as well as measures of psychological safety,
and
the
coach-athlete
relationship,
and
a
number
of
outcomes
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S100
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 104] ---
(commitment, dropout, positive and negative affect). We tested the jinglejangle with SEM using subscales or parcels as indicators to assess internal
reliability (composite reliability), modiﬁcation indices supporting signiﬁcant and meaningful cross-loadings (i.e., potential jangle), and correlations
between similar measures (all leadership and psychosocial motivational
climate constructs) as well as ECV testing of correlations with outcomes
for signiﬁcant differences (e.g., distinguish jingle from jangle). All the
constructs were reliably measured. Regarding the shared variance amongst
the leadership and motivational climate constructs, correlations emerged
between the scales assessing leadership styles, motivational climates, and
the coach-athlete relationship (CAR) representing at least 50% variance
overlap. This ﬁnding raises questions about whether all of these constructs
(as assessed) are distinct (i.e., jangle). Furthering support for a ‘jangle’
fallacy the majority of the correlations with the outcomes (positive affect,
negative affect, enjoyment, and dropout) were non-signiﬁcantly different
across the leadership style, motivational climates, and CAR. This is
notable as both positive and negative outcomes were considered. This
work points to: 1) the importance of comparing both the theoretical
underpinning of these constructs and their item content, and 2) before
additional measures of leadership are introduced in the ﬁeld, it is critical to
examine the measures already in existence in terms of what aspect of
leadership (and leadership-related processes) they are capturing.
The sleep gap: Comparing sleep quality and quantity in university
athletes and non-athletes across academic years
Emma Shea, Brock University; Philip Sullivan, Brock University
Sleep is a critical component of health and performance, particularly for
university students navigating the demands of academics, social life, and,
for some, athletic commitments. Research indicates that university students frequently experience insufﬁcient sleep and poor sleep quality due to
irregular schedules, high stress levels, and lifestyle factors (Hirshkowitz
et al., 2015). Student athletes face additional challenges, including early
training sessions, travel for competitions, and recovery demands, which
can further disrupt sleep patterns (Watson, 2017). Comparatively, nonathlete students may have fewer structured demands but often report
irregular sleep-wake cycles and sleep deprivation due to academic pressures (Gomes et al., 2011). While much of the existing literature focuses on
the general sleep patterns of university students and athletes, less is known
about how sleep quality and quantity vary across years of study, particularly comparing student athletes and non-athletes. This study aims to
address this gap by comparing sleep metrics from student-athletes and nonathlete students across different years of study from ﬁrst year to fourth year
students. Data were collected from the 2024 American College Health
Association’s National College Health Assessment Canadian cohort
(n = 277 varsity athletes; n = 7147 non-athletes). Factorial ANOVAs were
conducted to examine differences between year of study and athlete status
on the NCHA’s measure of sleep quality and sleep quantity. Analyses
show that there were no signiﬁcant interactions, but there were signiﬁcant
main effects whereby student athletes reported signiﬁcantly greater quality
and quantity of sleep across all four years of undergrad. Future research
should investigate a four-year longitudinal study with one cohort of
undergraduate student-athletes and student non-athletes to assess potential
changes in quality and quantity of sleep over academic progression.
Parental support as a transition resource in elite youth soccer academies — A cross-cultural investigation into youth athletes’ experiences
and preferences
Valeria C. Eckardt, University of Muenster; Anton Bechtloff, German
Sport University Cologne; Rawa, University of Muenster; James Newman,
Shefﬁeld Hallam University; Connie Wragg, Shefﬁeld Hallam University;
Travis E. Dorsch, Utah State University
Transitioning to a competitive youth sports pathway presents distinct
challenges and requires signiﬁcant adaptation for youth athletes. In this
context, a supportive environment can serve as an important social
resource to personal and athletic development, well-being, and success
in sports. One of the primary social resources for children is parents.
Although the effects of parental support have been well-documented
across various career stages, less is known about how parents can facilitate
an early transition into a specialized sports program. Further, an increased
internationalization of youth sports warrants the consideration of sociocultural practices and contexts for talent development and athletes’ wellbeing. The present study, therefore, sought to address this gap by examining youth athletes’ experiences and preferences regarding parental support
during their transition to a professional youth soccer academy from a crosscultural perspective. Semi-structured interviews were conducted with male
soccer players aged 7 to 11 years who were participating in professional
academies in Germany (n = 12), England (n = 13), and the United States
(n = 8). Data were initially analyzed separately within each country using
thematic analysis and subsequently integrated using a culturally sensitive
approach. This process provided nuanced insights into parent-athlete
relationships during athletic transitions and revealed how these interactions
are shaped by the elite soccer pathways speciﬁc to each country. Key crosscultural themes identiﬁed were parental presence, parental coaching,
emotional support, and performance expectations. While the ﬁndings
largely afﬁrm the role of parents as a critical resource during athletic
transitions, some children demonstrated limited awareness of their parents’
overall academy involvement and in facilitating their transition into the
academy. Speciﬁcally, children highlighted the importance of their parents
being physically present and expressed a need for increased communication and reﬂection with their parents to better understand and navigate their
athletic transitions. Cultural differences became apparent in academy
policies regulating and thus limiting parental involvement, which was
criticized by youth athletes. This cross-cultural study highlights unique and
interconnected opportunities to enhance parental roles and transition
experiences of youth athletes such as tailoring parental support to the
emotional needs of young athletes.
We’re a team for today, but not for long: Investigating the effects of
transience on group dynamics
Jared Hrabcak, Florida State University; Svenja Wolf, Florida State
University; Blair Evans, Western University
Teamwork, collaboration, and the desire to belong with others in teams are
fundamental social experiences among people (Deci & Ryan, 2000).
Nearly all teams fall within a standard set of deﬁning characteristics:
members share a common fate, beneﬁt mutually from the team’s existence,
have a social structure, engage in quality interactions, and self-categorize
as a team (Eys et al., 2020). While this informs the investigation of welldeﬁned, more archetypal teams, there is a gap in understanding the group
dynamics of teams that are interdependent and entitative but exist with
short-duration membership (i.e., weeks or months), ﬂuid membership
(i.e., regular changes in membership), or occasional membership
(i.e., brief periods as a group separated by long-duration breaks. These
are so-called transient teams (Hancock et al., 2018). Due to their transience, group dynamics likely develop and play out differently in comparison to more traditional, archetypal teams (Hrabcak, 2025). In this study, we
are applying a grounded theory method (Holt, 2016) to develop a
framework for group dynamics in transient teams. To this end, we are
conducting semi-structured interviews with an object/photo elicitation
strategy (Harper, 2002) with 12 performers with ﬁrst-hand experience
participating on transient teams at an elite level (i.e., USA soccer, beach
volleyball, swimming, gymnastics). We are coding these interviews
iteratively, using participant descriptions to construct categories of coded

data with theoretical integration into existing group dynamics frameworks
(e.g., Eys et al., 2020). We will be presenting the resultant ﬁrst version of
this new, grounded theory on group dynamics in transient teams and
discuss the extent to which it generates unique ﬁndings and implications
for the further study and support of transient teams.
Associations between physical exercise, alcohol craving and inhibitory
control in young adult binge drinkers
Luke Poole, Rutgers University; Jonathon Bourque, Rutgers University;
Hannah Perdue, Rutgers University; Amber Sarwani, Rutgers University;
Andrew Ude, Rutgers University; Marsha Bates, Rutgers University;
Brandon Alderman, Rutgers University
Hazardous substance use behaviors, such as binge drinking, have been
explained using dual process frameworks; use behaviors result from an
imbalance between enhanced automatic approach bias towards alcohol
cues coupled with impaired inhibitory control in the presence of such cues.
Physical exercise has been utilized as treatment for alcohol use disorders
(AUDs) based on emerging evidence linking exercise with reduced alcohol
craving and enhanced inhibitory control among individuals recovering
from AUD. However, few studies have examined these relationships
among individuals at-risk for future AUDs; i.e., young adult binge
drinkers. Because emerging adulthood is a critical developmental period
when chronic health behaviors become established, examining these
interactive associations between exercise, craving, and inhibitory control
during college years in those at-risk may point towards physical exercise as
a potential preventative strategy as related to later-in-life AUD. Young
adults (n = 65, 40 females, Mage= 20.6 ± 1.7 years) who endorsed 2+ binge
drinking episodes within the last month were recruited as part of a parent
study. Self-reported alcohol craving and physical exercise were assessed
via the Penn Alcohol Craving Scale and Simple Physical Activity Questionnaire. Inhibitory control was assessed via an alcohol-cued Go/NoGo
task and electroencephalography (EEG). Participants were presented with
alcohol or non-alcohol beverage cues, which changed color (i.e., the target)
to indicate whether a response was required. Target-locked NoGo and
(NoGo-minus-Go) difference waveform N2 event-related potential (ERP)
components were reduced and quantiﬁed as mean amplitude within a
localizer window. Linear regression models with interaction terms assessed associations between exercise, craving, and inhibitory control.
Alcohol-cued NoGo and N2 difference waveform amplitudes were associated with craving (β’s > 0.5, p’s < .05). Greater craving predicted attenuated (less-negative) N2 amplitudes. N2 amplitudes were associated with
the craving-exercise interaction term (β’s > -0.7, p’s < .05). Among highercraving binge drinkers, more exercise was associated with enhanced
(more-negative) N2 amplitudes whereas less exercise was associated with
attenuated N2 amplitudes. These ﬁndings extend relationships between
exercise, craving, and inhibitory control from those recovering from AUD
to those at-risk. While ﬁndings indicate an inﬂuence of exercise on craving
and inhibitory control relations, follow-up studies are necessary to establish causality and impact on alcohol use behaviors. Funding Source:
NIAAA.
Maximizing cognitive gains in middle-aged adults: The role of ﬁtness,
motivation, and engagement in multimodal exergaming training — A
randomized controlled trial
Xuan Lin, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign; Yan Luo, Shanghai
Jiao Tong University; Kamila Makhembetova, University of Illinois
Urbana-Champaign; David Thomas, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign; Maxime Lussier, Université de Montréal; Arthur F. Kramer,
Northeastern University; Edward McAuley, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign; Sean Mullen, University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign
Cognitive training interventions that integrate physical activity offer
promising strategies to enhance emotional and cognitive health across
the lifespan. This randomized controlled trial investigated whether individual differences in physical ﬁtness, exertion, and motivational factors
inﬂuenced cognitive gains in middle-aged adults (Mage=46.73 ± 8.20,
N = 233). Participants were randomly assigned to either a Games group
(n = 118) or a Videos control group (n = 115). The Games group completed
20 hours of multimodal training, alternating daily between motion-controlled exergaming with a verbal dual-task element and stationary computerized dual-task training. The training paradigms rotated between two
stimulus types: Vehicle:Fruit and Number:Letter. The Videos group served
as an attention-control condition, watching health-related educational
content. Exploratory analyses using Mplus v.8 identiﬁed task-speciﬁc
predictors of cognitive improvements from the Games training. In the
Vehicle:Fruit paradigm, which involved verbal and associative memory
stimuli, greater baseline cardiorespiratory ﬁtness (β = 1.413, SE = 0.505,
z = 2.795, p = .005) and lower perceived exertion during training
(β = −0.497, SE = 0.140, z = −3.546, p < .001) predicted larger cognition
gains. Additionally, participants with higher expectations of cognitive
beneﬁts from the intervention (β = 4.250, SE = 1.938, z = 2.193, p = .028)
achieved greater cognition improvements, suggesting a motivational
priming effect. In contrast, gains in the Number:Letter paradigm, which
required cognitive ﬂexibility with numeric and verbal stimuli, were driven
by greater prior exercise consistency (β = 2.622, SE = 1.013, z = 2.588,
p = .010) and stronger early intentions to complete the program (β = 4.127,
SE = 1.562, z = 2.642, p = .008), while injury or illness negatively impacted performance (β = −9.725, SE = 3.547, z = −2.741, p = .006). These
ﬁndings highlight the importance of physical ﬁtness, motivational priming,
and sustained engagement in optimizing cognitive training outcomes.
Multimodal interventions, such as exergaming combined with dual-task
training, offer an effective means of enhancing cognitive health in middleaged adults and may be tailored to individual needs for maximal impact.
Funding source: National Institute on Aging.
Can collegiate physical education instructors enhance students’ imagery abilities by supporting their autonomy?
Chang-Ha Im, Korea Univeristy
This study tested the effects of a Self-Determination Theory (SDT)-based,
autonomy-supportive intervention program designed to help collegiate
physical education instructors support students’ autonomy. Additionally,
the study examined its impact on students’ psychological needs and
imagery abilities over the course of a semester. Four physical education
instructors (2 females, 2 males) were randomly assigned to either an
autonomy-supportive (AS) intervention group or a control group. Instructors in the AS group (1 female, 1 male) participated in a workshop that
included SDT principles, scientiﬁc evidence, practical applications, and
group discussions, while those in the control group received a delayedtreatment workshop. A total of 71 students (37 in the AS group, 34 in the
control group) completed a four-page questionnaire measuring perceived
autonomy-supportive teaching, psychological need satisfaction and frustration, and imagery skills at two time points during the ﬁrst semester.
Results from ANCOVA-based repeated measures analyses indicated that
students taught by autonomy-supportive instructors reported signiﬁcantly
greater perceived autonomy support compared to the control group, F(1,
68) = 21.42, p < .01, eta2 = .24. These students also experienced higher
psychological need satisfaction (autonomy, competence, and relatedness),
F(1, 68) = 16.48, p < .01, eta2 = .20, and lower psychological need frustration, F(1, 68) = 16.29, p < .01, eta2 = .19. Furthermore, students in the
AS group demonstrated signiﬁcantly greater improvements in imagery
abilities compared to the control group, F(1, 68) = 17.09, p < .01, eta2 =
.20. These ﬁndings suggest that the intervention was effective and that
