“I’ve never had anyone listen to my story like that”: Exploring the
social validity of biographical mapping as a foundation for personalized exercise programming
Adam H. Ibrahim, The University of Tennessee; Jannika M. John, University of Tübingen; Cory T. Beaumont, Baldwin Wallace University;
Jedediah E. Blanton, The University of Tennessee; Dawn P. Coe, The
University of Tennessee; Kelley Strohacker, The University of Tennessee
Promoting exercise as a sustainable behavior is challenging due to its
dynamic, multifaceted, and person-speciﬁc nature. For exercise promotion
to lead to successful exercise adoption, unique and tailored strategies are
required. Biographical mapping (bioMAP) can provide a comprehensive
and a detailed visual representation of an individual’s life by combining an
interactive drawing activity with a narrative interview. Thus, the bioMAP
approach could be used to help understand ﬁtness behaviors across the
lifespan helping to uncover personal insights that can inform effective,
personalized exercise interventions. This study explored the method’s social
validity to understand exercise history. Twenty-four participants (67%
female, 34 ± 10 years, 79% White) each completed a bioMAP session
prior to designing a personalized exercise program. Following a 14-day trial
period of their resultant program, participants completed virtual semistructured interviews designed to understand participant perceived utility
of the bioMAP. Using reﬂexive thematic analysis, the ﬁndings were
organized into ﬁve themes: Theme one, ‘We’re humans, we have a lot of
life events, you start to forget after a while’ highlighted the complexity and
challenges of recalling life events tied to exercise. Theme two, ‘It was really
cool! I had never done anything like that before’ emphasized the method’s
novelty and its ability to generate unique insights. Theme three, ‘Even
though it was a vulnerable time, it was deﬁnitely worth it’ and theme four
‘You made the connection. So, it’s easy to open up when you think people
are going to support you’ reﬂected the vulnerability of sharing personal
stories. Theme ﬁve, ‘Sharing it as a story draws a lot more of an emotional
connection to everything, which I think informs the motivation piece later in
the actual programming’ emphasized how reﬂection strengthened emotional connections, illuminated exercise patterns, and inspired future goals.
These ﬁndings suggest that bioMAP may serve as a helpful tool for gaining
an in-depth understanding of an individual’s exercise history, which can
guide satisfactory program development. However, due to the intensive
collaboration required, facilitators should be trained to manage interviews
and handle the processed and shared information ethically.
Youth sports in Africa: A scoping review across ﬁve geopolitical
regions
Ayomiposi O. Aribasoye, Utah State University; Travis E. Dorsch, Utah
State University
Much is known about youth sports participation in Western cultures, but the
same cannot be said about the African continent. Despite the growing
popularity of youth sports, limited research has been designed to document
its impact in Africa. This is an important gap, as youth sport holds signiﬁcant
potential as a tool for empowerment and social development. Further,
anecdotal reports suggest that socioeconomic, cultural, and infrastructure
barriers may be leading to disparities in access, participation, and outcomes.
The purpose of the present scoping review was to consolidate existing
knowledge by comprehensively examining the youth sports literature across
Africa’s ﬁve geopolitical regions. Initially, a systematic search of peerreviewed papers was conducted utilizing PRISMA guidelines. Two inclusion criteria guided the search: (a) studies focused on youth sport
participation within the African context, and (b) studies were published in
peer-reviewed, English-language academic journals. An initial search in
October 2024 yielded 40 original research articles meeting these criteria.
Data from studies were tabled, synthesized, and analyzed, highlighting
multiple shared themes across Africa’s ﬁve geopolitical regions: (a) socioeconomic, gender, and community-level barriers to participation (b) the
inﬂuence of globalization, and (c) sport’s role in promoting health and social
cohesion. Region-speciﬁc ﬁndings highlight North Africa’s focus on elite
athletic development, West Africa’s connection between sport and socioeconomic mobility, East Africa’s dominance in distance running, Central
Africa’s challenges around infrastructure and opportunity, and South
Africa’s unique blend of indigenous practices and colonial legacies. The
study frames what is currently known about youth sports in Africa and
provides directions for scholars who may wish to leverage the potential of
sport to address social challenges in Africa. Moreover, ﬁndings suggest that
region-speciﬁc strategies may best ensure equitable access and the achievement of positive developmental outcomes for youth.
Frontal brain alpha asymmetry predicts physical activity intensity
and duration using wrist-worn accelerometry
Bryan Montero Herrera, University of North Carolina at Greensboro;
Megan M. O´Brokta, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Praveen A. Pasupathi, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Andrew S.
Cornwall, University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Jennifer L. Etnier,
University of North Carolina at Greensboro; Eric S. Drollette, University
of North Carolina at Greensboro
Frontal alpha asymmetry (FAA), a neural marker (measured using electroencephalography; EEG) linked to motivation and affect, represents the
difference in alpha frequency power between the right and left frontal
regions of the scalp. Recent evidence reveals that FAA power may serve as
a predictor of physical activity (PA) behavior. However, prior studies
predominantly rely on self-report data and traditional levels of PA
(e.g., total activity time, moderate-to-vigorous physical activity) which
may fail to capture the most active periods of the day. This study explored
the relation of FAA to the most physically active periods of the day using
accelerometry. Fifty-nine young adults (41 females; average age:
21.8 ± 2.9 years) completed a resting EEG assessment (eyes closed for
5-min) during a single visit to the lab. FAA was measured and calculated at
frontal electrode sites (mean[F4,P4]–mean[F3,P3]). Next, participants
wore a wrist accelerometer for 7 consecutive days during waking hours.
Accelerometry data was processed utilizing the MX method at different
durations. The ﬁrst step in the MX method calculates average PA intensity
during all equal-duration windows of time during the day (e.g., all 5minute windows of time within a 24-hour period). The next step identiﬁed
the intensity of the activity during the most active window for that speciﬁc
time duration. This provides a sensitive measure of low to high-intensity
PA patterns. Separate regression models were performed with FAA as
independent variable and each MX interval (2-, 5-, 10-, 15-, 30-, 60-, and
120-min) as dependent variable. Signiﬁcant models for MX15, MX10, and
MX5, revealed greater FAA power for increase exercise intensity (β’s ≥
0.23, t’s ≥1.08, p’s ≤.05). These results suggest that FAA reliably predicts
time spent in PA bouts of 5-, 10-, and 15-minutes. Given that FAA reﬂects
motivation and affect, these ﬁndings suggest that FAA may play a role in
inﬂuencing behavioral patterns of PA with respect to duration and
intensity, warranting further investigation into mechanistic pathways.
Intricate steps: A reﬂective conceptual framework for developing a
coaching philosophy statement
Manal Beydoun, York University; Jessica Fraser-Thomas, York University
Coaching philosophies have been proposed to offer a purposeful means for
coaches to facilitate youth sport outcomes such as performance success and
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
S103
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 107] ---
personal development (Côté & Fraser-Thomas, 2021; Nash et al., 2008).
Despite this, most coaches do not hold well developed coaching philosophies (Nash et al., 2008), suggesting coaches could beneﬁt from assistance
in developing their philosophies (Adams et al., 2016). The purpose of this
study was to advance understanding on the processes employed by youth
sport coaches to craft a coaching philosophy statement. Thirteen youth
sport coaches (n = 5 women, 8 men; aged 26-61 years) having explicit
coaching philosophies, engaged in two semi-structured interviews. Data
were recorded, transcribed, and analysed drawing upon reﬂexive thematic
analysis (Braun & Clarke, 2023). Several themes captured the interrelated
considerations involved in developing a coaching philosophy, including:
(a) contextually and situationally driven, (b) athletic community-informed,
(c) bridging retrospective and current experiences, (d) unpacking core
foundations, and (e) sincere and strategic reﬂection as a driver of development. Themes were synthesized into a conceptual framework illustrating
the varied and complex processes coaches followed over time, in developing their statements. The framework integrates reﬂections that coaches
considered at each phase of development such as (a) key characteristics and
achievements they aimed to facilitate in athletes, (b) their expectations of
themselves and of their athletes (c) their own background, and (d) their
decision-making processes. The framework is completed by facilitators
that participants highlighted in the development process, including resources (e.g., social media, teaching education materials) and “trigger
points” (e.g., inspiring philosophies by sport leaders). Participants’ perspectives varied regarding the demands of developing their own statements, with some suggesting it was a “straightforward” process, while
others expressed discomfort, considering it an uncommonly practiced
activity. Participants were unanimous in voicing the beneﬁts of developing
their statement, suggesting this conceptual framework could serve as a
preliminary practical tool to prompt and guide coaches through different
reﬂective stages, in turn, supporting them in crafting their own unique and
authentic operational coaching philosophies.
Acceptability and efﬁcacy of a student-delivered health coaching
intervention for rural adults
Alison Phillips Reichter, Louisiana Tech University
Health coaching is a theory-based, client-centered approach to improving
individual lifestyle behaviors through conversation, goal setting, and
accountability. Health coaching has demonstrated effectiveness in advancing health behavior change and preventing the onset or development
of chronic disease (Sforzo et al., 2017, 2019). However, the majority of
interventions have been evaluated in healthcare settings, rather than
community or workplace settings, speciﬁcally in rural communities. This
pilot study evaluated the acceptability and efﬁcacy of a 9-week health
coaching intervention on physical activity, other lifestyle behaviors, and
psychosocial outcomes among rural adults. Participants met weekly with
a trained undergraduate student health coach either in-person or via video
call. Intervention acceptability, psychosocial outcomes, and lifestyle
behaviors were measured with an online questionnaire, and physical
activity was measured objectively with an ActiGraph LEAP for 5 days
before and after the intervention. Participants (N = 18, 89% female,
Mage = 45.5 years) attended 92% of coaching sessions and indicated
relatively high levels of satisfaction with their health coach, speciﬁcally
the accountability provided. Paired t-tests indicated participants showed
non-signiﬁcant increases in daily steps (11,132 to 11,959, t(17) = 1.1,
p = .278) and MVPA minutes (156.4 to 164.3, t(17) = 0.6, p = .568),
though participants reported exercising more days per week (1.4 to
3.2, t(17) = 3.3, p = .004) and eating more servings of fruits and vegetables (1.6 to 2.3, t(17) = 2.1, p = .049) after the intervention. Participants
also reported increases in several psychosocial outcomes, including
affective attitude toward physical activity (t(17) = 5.3, p < .001), PA
capability (t(17) = 3.0, p = .009), PA behavioral regulation (t(17) = 6.9,
p < .001), PA identity (t(17) = 3.3, p = .004), exercise competence
(t(17) = 2.7, p = .015), exercise autonomy (t(17) = 2.3, p = .032), and
exercise relatedness (t(17) = 2.2, p = .038). Findings provide preliminary
evidence of participant acceptability of the intervention. Further reﬁnement of intervention features to support both psychosocial and behavioral
outcomes and the inclusion of a comparison group is warranted.
Parental involvement proﬁles and child well-being in youth sports
Hailey Muehleisen, Louisiana Tech University; Jordan Blazo, Louisiana
Tech University; Travis Dorsch, Utah State University
As youth sport has become more professionalized and competitive,
parental investment in children’s sports has steadily increased (AOSSM,
2024; Hardiman et al., 2024). Parental pressure can negatively impact child
physical, mental, and social well-being as the emphasis on fun in youth
sport shifts to pressure for athletic success (Bonavolonta et al., 2021,
Dorsch et al., 2015). Fostering a positive environment through sport is
essential for child well-being and development. To this end, the ﬁrst
purpose of this study was to characterize distinct family proﬁles based on
parental involvement variables (i.e., total money spent on child’s sport, and
minutes per week dedicated to child’s sport). A second purpose was to
explore potential proﬁle differences by parent reports of child well-being
during a sport season (i.e., mental health, physical ﬁtness, emotional
control, and social well-being). We hypothesized that higher levels of
parental involvement, marked by ﬁnancial investment and time commitment, would be associated with improved youth well-being. Participant
data came from a nationally representative sample of youth sport parents
from across the United States (N = 1848, Mage= 40.9, SDage = 10.6) in Fall
of 2024. Participants completed measures assessing how much was spent
on a child’s sport over the previous year, minutes per week spent on a
child’s sport, and markers of child well-being. Cluster analysis based on
ﬁnancial commitment and minutes per week dedicated to a child’s sport
yielded a 4-cluster solution. Analysis of the proﬁles and markers of wellbeing supported our hypothesis. Proﬁles exhibiting moderate to high forms
of parental engagement were associated with signiﬁcantly higher scores of
youth well-being in sport. Alternatively, a single proﬁle representing
relatively low parental engagement was associated with signiﬁcantly lower
well-being. Collectively, ﬁndings support the importance of encouraging
balanced (e.g., not excessive and not deﬁcient) and child-centered approach to parental involvement in youth sport for the physical, mental, and
social development of young athletes. Future scholars may consider
examining the independent inﬂuences of money and time spent on youth
sports, as well as exploring the implications for well-being among children
from families who do not invest signiﬁcant time or ﬁnancial resources.
Funding source: The Aspen Institute - Project Play.
Examining the relationships between team ﬂow, group cohesion, and
emotional synchrony
Zack Bryant, Menlo College; Sean Pradhan, Menlo College
Scholars and practitioners have long been interested in optimizing team
dynamics and performance, examining concepts like ﬂow, group cohesion, and emotional synchrony (contagion). More recently, van den Hout
et al. (2018) developed a comprehensive model of team ﬂow to explain
high-performing groups. Speciﬁcally, the authors proposed a connection
between group cohesion and team ﬂow. Similarly, other literature has
noted a meaningful relationship between group ﬂow and emotional
synchrony. However, many of these studies did not employ psychometrically validated scales designed for measuring team ﬂow. According to
van den Hout et al. (2018), group cohesion is an integral part of team ﬂow,
and this study marks the ﬁrst empirical validation of this theoretical

TFM to explore the relationships between team ﬂow, group cohesion, and
emotional synchrony. The present study examined these relationships
using a sample of 510 collegiate athletes (301 women, 209 men; Mage
19.73, SD = 1.43) from a variety of sports. Participants were recruited to
complete the Team Flow Monitor (TFM; van den Hout et al., 2019),
Group Environment Questionnaire (GEQ; Carron et al., 1985), and
Perceived Emotional Synchrony Scale (PESS; Zumeta et al., 2015). A
series of Pearson product-moment correlations were performed to examine the relationships between team ﬂow, group cohesion, and emotional
synchrony. Results revealed signiﬁcant relationships across all main
outcomes and subscales. Speciﬁcally, overall team ﬂow was strongly
and positively associated with group cohesion, r(357) = .73, p < .001,
95% CI [0.68, 0.78], and moderately and positively related to emotional
synchrony, r(356) = .65, p < .001, 95% CI [0.59, 0.71]. Among the
correlations involving the 11 dimensions of team ﬂow and the main

cohesion, r(357) = .71, p < .001, 95% CI [0.66, 0.76], while sense of unity
was

and
positively

to
emotional
synchrony,
r(356) = .66, p < .001, 95% CI [0.60, 0.72]. Findings from the current
study demonstrate the utility of the TFM in assessing group dynamics
within an athletic population.
Differences in parental satisfaction for youth sport programming
between families with and without a child with disability
Andrew C. Parks, Louisiana Tech University; Jordan A. Blazo, Louisiana
Tech University; Travis E. Dorsch, Utah State University
Youth sport participation provides a unique opportunity for children
to engage in skill development designed to enhance physical, emotional, social, and cognitive health (Bartko & Eccles, 2003; Corning,
Ketcham, & Hall, 2020). However, experiences additionally designed
to include children with disabilities are limited despite the potentially
ampliﬁed beneﬁt of sport participation to this population. Furthermore, many families struggle to ﬁnd youth sport programs within their
communities that support the needs of children with disabilities,
despite evidence supporting signiﬁcant intrinsic motivation from
these youth to participate (Wong et al., 2024). To better understand
the expectations of these families, the present study sought to examine
how parental satisfaction associated with youth sport participation for
families with a child with disabilities. Participants included 1695
parents (Mage = 41.3 ± 10.4 years, 58.6% female; Mchildage = 11.7 ±
3.7 years, 38.7% female). 379 parents reported that their child was
diagnosed with a disability (e.g., Autism Spectrum Disorder, Attention Deﬁcit Hyperactivity Disorder, Vision Impairment). Parental
satisfaction with youth sport was determined using a 5-point Likert
scale (1 = Very Dissatisﬁed; 5 = Very Satisﬁed). Satisfaction was
assessed relative to their involvement as a sport parent/caregiver, the
child’s coach, the youth sport organization, the community infrastructure for youth sport, the facilities available, and the overall sport
culture in the United States. Due to the nature of the survey scale,
Mann-Whitney U analyses were conducted to determine if there were
any differences in parental satisfaction between the families with and
without a child with disability. Findings indicate no differences in
satisfaction between the groups for any of the variables of interest (U’s
>= 234462.5, z’s <= -1.92, p >= .06), with all variables averaging
between 3.92 – 4.21 on the scales indicating near satisﬁed or satisﬁed
reports. These results align with prior research indicating that parents
of children with disabilities are typically satisﬁed with the programs
their children are participating it for youth sport. However, future
research should examine the context of satisfaction the parents are
seeking when enrolling their child in these programs, as what
constitutes ‘satisﬁed’ may be a subjective lens for each family.
Funding source: The Aspen Institute - Project Play.
The impact of physical activity on the relationship between perceived
effectiveness of the healthcare system and burnout in medical students
Adam J. Lavelle, Texas Tech University Health Sciences Center El Paso;
Alison Phillips Reichter, Louisiana Tech University; Jordan A. Blazo,
Louisiana Tech University
Burnout in medical students detrimentally impacts mental health, increases drop-out rates, and negatively impacts overall academic performance and patient treatment. The Conservation of Resources Theory
explains burnout through the lens of balancing resources and demands.
When resources are not sufﬁcient to meet demands, stress and eventual
burnout ensues (Halbesleben, 2014). Within this framework, physical
activity has been shown increase resources and mitigate resource strain,
ultimately resulting in decreased burnout symptoms (Freire et al., 2020).
With an upcoming physician shortage and 49.6% of medical students
exhibiting severe symptoms of the condition, burnout in medical
students has become a critical issue to understand and alleviate (Dyrbye
et al., 2014). Physical activity has been associated with reduced burnout
and improved quality of life in medical students (Taylor et al., 2022).
However, limited attention has been given to a potential moderating
inﬂuence of physical activity on the relationship between perceptions of
the medical system and burnout. The purpose of this study was to
examine if exercise behavior moderates the relationship between medical students’ perceptions of the current healthcare system and burnout
experiences. Third- and fourth-year medical students in the United
States (n = 64, Mage = 26, 67% female) completed the Maslach Burnout
Inventory, Godin Leisure Time Questionnaire, and People’s Voice
Survey to examine burnout symptoms, typical weekly exercise behavior, and perceptions of the healthcare system, respectively. Multiple
regression analyses showed no signiﬁcant relationship between perceived quality of the healthcare system and burnout symptoms. However, conﬁdence in patient accessibility to quality care signiﬁcantly
predicted the depersonalization symptom of burnout (p < .01, β = -0.60,
r2 = 10.7%). Medical students who perceived less conﬁdence that
patients can access quality healthcare displayed higher depersonalization symptoms of burnout. Exercise was not a signiﬁcant moderator of
this relationship (p > .05). Medical students’ negative perceptions about
future patient access may contribute to burnout, potentially impacting
patient care. Investigating preventative interventions within medical
education and further examining the role of self-care practices, such as
different modes of physical activity, in this population could yield
valuable insights to address burnout among medical students.
Stigma, shame, and dropout: Investigating predictors of program
attrition in a physical activity behaviour change initiative for postsecondary students
Samira Sunderji, University of Toronto; Melissa deJonge, University of
Toronto; Catherine M. Sabiston, University of Toronto
Students experiencing mental health challenges often face negative
perceptions and stigmatization, which can exacerbate mental health
challenges. Addressing mental health-related stigma and providing
effective supports for students experiencing mental health concerns,
is thus a critical priority for post-secondary institutions. A six-week
physical activity (PA) behavior change program at the University of
Toronto,
was
designed
to
support
physically
inactive
students
experiencing poor mental health. Despite the demonstrated effectiveness and acceptability of the program, predictors of dropout and the
inﬂuence of the program on attitudes towards mental health problems

remain underexplored. This study examined whether perceived stigma
(PS), internal shame (IS), and external shame (ES) inﬂuenced drop-out
from the program, and whether program participation inﬂuenced these
mental health-related stigma outcomes. A logistic regression model to
predict dropout among students [N = 85, Mage (SD) = 25.43(5.43)] based
on mental health-related stigma outcomes was not statistically signiﬁcant [X2(8) = 6.40, p = .60]. PS [OR = 2.12, CI [64, 6.99], χ2 = 1.53, p

t(58) = 2.93, p <.001] but not for PS [PSPRE =1.09 ± .71; PSPOST
=1.02 ± .75; t(60)=1.16, p =.25]. These ﬁndings suggest that participation in PA behaviour change programs can reduce internal and external
shame related to mental health problems for students. Programs that
facilitate change in mental health-related stigma outcomes have implications for reducing help-seeking barriers and supporting student
well-being. Future research is needed to examine PA program delivery
components and mechanisms of change that help to reduce mental
health-related stigma within tailored PA behaviour change programs
focused on supporting post-secondary student mental health and
well-being.
Respiration-heart rate coupling and its role in performance optimization for elite shooters
Seiji Matsumura, NTT Communication Science Laboratories; Sorato
Minami, NTT Communication Science Laboratories; Naoki Saijo, NTT
Communication Science Laboratories; Makio Kashino, NTT Communication Science Laboratories
Elite riﬂe shooting demands exceptional precision and stability, relying
on the ability to regulate physiological states under competitive pressure. This study investigated dynamics of respiration and heart rate in 10
elite riﬂe shooters during simulated trials, aiming to understand their
role in performance optimization. Continuous recordings of respiration
and electrocardiographic activity were analyzed, focusing on physiological changes leading up to shot release and their relationship with
shooting performance. Our ﬁndings revealed signiﬁcant physiological
adjustments in preparation for shooting. Respiration cycles showed a
marked reduction, with temporary cessation observed immediately
before the shot. Simultaneously, heart rates decreased below resting
levels during the shooting phase. This reduction was closely related to
suppressed respiration, possibly reﬂecting a psychophysiological adjustment occurring during the preparation phase. The synchronized
decrease in respiration and heart rate was effectively visualized using
Hilbert transformation and polar coordinate mapping. These results
indicate that elite shooters may stabilize their physiological state
through psychophysiological adjustments, potentially enhancing focus
and minimizing disruptions. However, in the present analysis, no
consistent relationship was found between shooting performance and
physiological variables, such as heart rate at the time of the shot or
respiration patterns during the shot within and between the shooters.
While these adjustments appear to be integral to the preparatory process,
their direct impact on performance outcomes could not be determined
and remains unclear. These ﬁndings underscore the importance of
understanding the precise physiological adjustments that elite shooters
employ during preparation. The observed synchronization of respiration
and heart rate suggests a psychophysiological process that may enhance
stability and focus under competitive conditions. While the direct
impact of these adjustments on performance remains uncertain, their
role in creating a stable physiological state is evident. This emphasizes
the signiﬁcance of psychophysiological regulation as a key element of
elite-level shooting performance. Funding source: JST [Moonshot
R&D][Grant Number JPMJMS2295]
How coaches can foster quality sport experiences for adult paraathletes living in Nova Scotia
Ueli Albert, Acadia University; Emily Bremer, Acadia University
This study examines the quality of sport experiences among adult paraathletes in Nova Scotia, with a particular focus on the role of coaches in
fostering quality participation. Using a cross-sectional mixed-method
design, the research applies a social-relational lens to investigate the
experiential aspects of participation. In this ongoing study, participants
(target N = 40) complete an online survey measuring demographic
characteristics, the Measurement of Experiential Aspects of Participation (MeEAP), and open-ended questions about coaching strategies to
foster quality participation. Data collection will be complete by March
2025 and full results will be presented at the conference. As of abstract
submission, 10 participants (Mage = 37, 30% female) have completed the
survey. Preliminary quantitative analyses revealed an average MeEAP
score of 5.9 (out of 7), with mean scores for each dimension as follows:
autonomy (6.0), belonging (6.4), challenge (5.6), engagement (5.7),
mastery (5.8), and meaning (6.0). The highest scores reﬂected participants’ strong sense of belonging, while the lowest scores highlighted
challenges in feeling appropriately challenged. Independent t-tests and
correlations will explore differences in quality experiences based on
demographic and sport-speciﬁc factors, such as years of participation
and level of competition (local, provincial, national, international).
Qualitative thematic analysis of the open-ended responses will provide
deeper insights into key factors inﬂuencing quality participation. So far,
participants have emphasized the critical roles of coach behaviors—
such as effective communication and tailored adaptations—as well as
organizational structures, including opportunities for inclusive practices
and accessible competition formats. The interplay of these factors was
seen as instrumental in creating environments that promote meaningful
and engaging sport experiences for para-athletes. We anticipate that this
study will underscore the importance of individualized coaching strategies and systemic adaptations to enhance engagement, satisfaction, and
long-term participation in parasport. The discussion will offer actionable recommendations for coaches and program leaders, contributing to
the broader literature on adaptive sports. This research also serves as the
foundation for the development of evidence-based workshops designed
to improve the quality of parasport programs in Nova Scotia. Funding
source: Mitacs.
Validity evidence for responses to the Coach Precompetitive Communication Questionnaire – Helpfulness using an exploratory bifactor
approach at the athlete-level
Corinne T. Zimmerman, Michigan State University; Nicholas D. Myers,
Michigan State University; Robin S. Vealey, Miami University; Christine
E. Pacewicz, Miami University; Andrew P. Driska, Michigan State
University; Jeemin Kim, Michigan State University
Pregame speech is a well-known tool used by coaches to help prepare
athletes before competition. Pregame speech can be categorized by three
components: tactical content (i.e., the verbal description of speciﬁc
skills or strategies that are relevant to the upcoming competition),
emotional content (i.e., the verbal expression of emotional and motivational information), and delivery (i.e., the nonverbal and paraverbal
messages demonstrated by coaches during pregame speech). These
components have primarily been studied in isolation; however this
study seeks to study these components simultaneously because
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS

Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 110] ---
communication is a complex and interconnected experience in which
such components are often used in tandem. The Coach Precompetitive
Communication Questionnaire-Helpfulness (CPCQ-H) was developed
to investigate the multifaceted construct of pregame speech as it relates
to athlete perceptions of helpfulness to their own performance
(i.e., which components of pregame speech do athletes ﬁnd helpful to
their competitive performance?). The purpose of this study was to
provide initial validity evidence for responses to the CPCQ-H using
an exploratory bifactor approach (EBFA) at the athlete-level. Participants included athletes (N = 258) from NCAA varsity level interactive
teams (G = 28). Main analyses controlled for dependency due to the
nested nature of the data (i.e., athletes nested within teams). A fourfactor measurement model exhibited better model-data ﬁt than models
with less factors. The four-factor model exhibited close to adequate ﬁt to
the data. Pattern coefﬁcients support the existence of a general factor
(i.e., pregame speech) and three grouping factors (i.e., tactical content,
emotional content, and delivery). Cross-loadings were present and
provided additional information regarding the factor structure of the
CPCQ-H. EBFA results provided initial evidence for the validity of
responses to the CPCQ-H. Overall, ﬁndings contribute to the psychometric and practical understanding of pregame speech.
Afraid to fall, or excited to climb? Qualitative observations of rock
climbing and mindset
Sienna Ludka, California State University, Northridge; Stella Pe˜na,
California State University, Northridge; Isaac Bouchard, California State
University, Northridge; Mark P Otten, California State University,
Northridge
From traditional climbing emerging in the mid-20th century to sport
climbing making its debut in the Tokyo 2020 Summer Olympics, both
the physical and psychological aspects of the sport have been a focus
of scientiﬁc inquiry. In pursuit of understanding the psychology of
rock climbing, we collected and analyzed qualitative data from 36
climbers (18 female, 18 male) from a variety of backgrounds. Data was
gathered systematically through YouTube with search statements
including “professional climber mental health”, “professional climber
psychology”, “competitive climbing mindset”, and “rock climber
interview”. The following requirements were kept in mind: 1) the
video was at least ﬁve minutes in length, 2) the climber could be
categorized as professional, competitive, or pioneer, 3) all types of
rock climbing were considered, with the exception of mountaineering
and alpinism, and 4) themes related to mental health or mindset were
discussed for a minimum of 15 seconds. The most frequent formats of
the videos were narrations, interviews, and presentations. Common
themes included the power of positive thinking. Olympian Miho
Nonaka said, “I have a couple sayings that are really important to
me: ‘just you wait’ and ‘keep smiling.’ Those words remind me to
keep my head up.” Another emerging premise was the confrontation of
fear. As ice and mixed climber Will Gadd explained, “How you get
difﬁcult, complicated high risk things done is, ﬁgure out everything
that can go wrong and think about those consequences and probabilities. Dig through it and listen to your fear.” Resilience arose as another
theme, as discussed by extreme climber Jonathan Siegrist: “I look
back at quote-unquote failures that have happened in my climbing like
they were all learning experiences, so years down the line you’re going
to look back and it’s just going to be a small piece of the overall puzzle.
It’s you moving towards the eventual best version of yourself.” Taken
together, results mold into a set of sport psychology tips and strategies
by way of an organized collection of rock climber recommendations.
Findings may be synthesized to inspire one’s own training or consulting work, particularly in the climbing world.
The interpersonal and intrapersonal correlates of youth athletes’
perceived sport competence: A meta-analytic review
Liam O’Neil, Utah State University; Alan L. Smith, Utah State University
Over the past four decades, perceived sport competence has been central
to our understanding of youth sport motivation and participation (Weiss,
2019). Grounded in several cognitivist motivation theories (e.g., Deci &
Ryan, 1985; Harter, 1978; Nicholls, 1984; Parsons et al., 1983),
considerable research has tied this competence-related appraisal to
various interpersonal (i.e., social) and intrapersonal (i.e., behavioral
and psychological) experiences of youth athletes. To advance science
(i.e., hypothesis generation) and practice (i.e., intervention utility) on
this competence-related appraisal, our meta-analytic review quantitatively reviewed the interpersonal and intrapersonal correlates of perceived sport competence in youth sport. Generally, we expected theoretically adaptive interpersonal (e.g., approval) and intrapersonal
(e.g., satisfaction) variables to positively associate, and theoretically
maladaptive
interpersonal
(e.g.,
rejection)
and
intrapersonal
(e.g., dissatisfaction) variables to negatively associate, with perceived
sport competence. A literature search was conducted of ﬁve electronic
databases: ERIC, PsychINFO, PubMed, Scopus, and SPORTDiscus.
After screening records identiﬁed from the literature search (N = 3979),
70 records (i.e., journal articles and dissertations/theses) were included
in the review. Our meta-analytic strategy involved pooling correlation
coefﬁcients of perceived sport competence and interpersonal or intrapersonal variables using random-effects models in R. Overall, 8 of 13
interpersonal variables were signiﬁcant correlates of perceived sport
competence – the strongest interpersonal correlate being perceived
social competence (r = .28; 95% CI [0.22, 0.34]). Similarly, 19 of 21
intrapersonal variables were signiﬁcant correlates of perceived sport
competence – the strongest intrapersonal correlate being perceived sport
commitment (r = .46; 95% CI [0.30, 0.60]). Despite substantial between-study heterogeneity, none of the coded study characteristics
(i.e., demographic and methodological) consistently and signiﬁcantly
explained the variation in signiﬁcant pooled associations via subgroup
analyses. This meta-analytic review demonstrated that perceived sport
competence is tied to a range of psychosocial experiences among youth
athletes. There was a shortage of behavioral correlates of perceived
sport competence, however, which limits our understanding of the
participation outcomes of this competence-related appraisal in youth
sport. This work has far-reaching implications for the development and
reﬁnement of both theory and intervention.
Perceived body acceptance by teammates in sport: Psychometric
evaluation of the teammate-adapted body acceptance by Others
Scale-2 in Botswana athletes
Chelsi Ricketts, University of Toronto; Leapetswe Malete, Michigan State
University; Nicholas D. Myers, Michigan State University; Catherine M.
Sabiston, University of Toronto
Perceiving that one’s body is accepted by others in sport contributes to
developing a positive body image. However, current evidence on this
relationship is limited by theoretical and methodological challenges to the
conceptualization and measurement of perceived body acceptance. To
address these limitations, the 13-item Body Acceptance by Others Scale-2
(BAOS-2; Swami et al., 2021) was developed as a generalized unidimensional measure of perceived body acceptance by others. While the BAOS-2
shows promise for evaluating perceived body acceptance by interpersonal
agents in sport, the scale’s factor structure requires validation in the sport
context. As such, this study adapted the BAOS-2 to assess perceived body
acceptance by teammates and examined the factor structure in a unique
cultural sample of athletes. Cross-sectional data from 508 Botswana
athletes (Mage = 25.32, SD = 7.10) who completed the modiﬁed BAOSJSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025

2 items (e.g., “I believe that important others respect my body” -> “I
believe that my teammates respect my body”) were analyzed. Exploratory
structural equation modeling supported a two-dimensional structure for the
teammate-adapted BAOS-2 (χ2(53) = 112.713, p < .001; RMSEA [90%
CI] = .049 [.037, .062], CFI = .959, TLI = .939, SRMR = .031), with dimensions representing perceived unconditional body acceptance (trust and
acceptance of the body without conditions) and perceived positive body
regard (reassurance, respect, and appreciation for the body). All but two
items loaded signiﬁcantly on their respective factors, with negligible crossloadings and adequate latent factor reliabilities. Contrary to the unidimensional structure of the BAOS-2 identiﬁed in prior research, these ﬁndings
suggest that two factors better explain responses to the teammate-adapted
BAOS-2 among Botswana athletes. Pressures to conform to sport-speciﬁc
body ideals may heighten the need for differentiated forms of body
acceptance from teammates. Unconditional body acceptance may fulﬁll
athletes’ desire for acceptance of their physical self, while positive body
regard may signal that the physical self is valued by teammates, fostering a
sense of belongingness within Botswana’s collectivistic context. Additional research is needed to explore these dimensions in athletic populations and their broader applicability in sport contexts. Funding source: This
work was funded by the College of Education, Department of Kinesiology,
Alliance for African Partnerships, African Studies, and Graduate School at
Michigan State University.
The effects of augmented reality and traditional practice conditions on
learning, motivation and engagement
Andrew Shaw, University of Tennessee; Jared Porter, University of
Tennessee
Previous studies utilizing augmented reality (AR) in practice have demonstrated transferable improvements in real-word motor performance. It
has been hypothesized that the use of AR may provide an increase in
engagement and intrinsic motivation, leading to greater performance
improvement and learning. Therefore, the purpose of this study was to
compare performance improvement, intrinsic motivation and engagement
between AR and real-world groups practicing the same motor skill. It was
hypothesized that learning, motivation and engagement would be equal
when practicing a basketball shooting task with an AR defender compared
to practicing the same task with a live defender, while all parameters would
be lower for practicing with a static defender or no defender. Participants
(N = 100) were randomly assigned to one of ﬁve groups to practice a
basketball shooting task: control (no practice), solitary practice (no
defender), practice with a static practice defender, practice with a static
live defender (Live), and practice with an AR digital static defender (AR).
The digital AR basketball defender was deployed on a Micro Soft
HoloLens2 headset. All participants performed a 10-shot pre-test and
post-test against a live defender. All practice groups performed 150
practice shots over three days of acquisition within six days. The posttest was conducted 24 hours following the ﬁnal day of practice. A
multivariate test comparing performance improvement, engagement, and
intrinsic motivation showed signiﬁcant interactions for performance
(F = 4.1, p = .004), and engagement (F = 3.2, p = .015), but not for intrinsic
motivation (F = 2.1, p = .090). Post-hoc analysis revealed similar performance improvements for the Live, AR, and Static groups which were
signiﬁcantly higher than the Solitary and Control groups. Similarly,
intrinsic motivation was similar for all practice groups which were all
signiﬁcantly higher than the Control group. Interestingly, engagement
levels of the Live, Static and Solitary groups were similar, all were
signiﬁcantly higher than the AR and Control groups. While these results
did not fully support our hypotheses, they suggest that motor skill practice
in AR is equally effective as real-world practice at facilitating motor
learning as well as providing motivational practice, while contradicting
previous research concerning the level of engagement for practicing
with AR.
Navigating mental health: Help-seeking experiences of NCAA Division I international student-athletes
Emily F. Hayashi, Michigan State University; Matt D. Hoffmann, California State University, Fullerton; Julie E. Brice, California State University, Fullerton; Kathleen S. Wilson, California State University,
Fullerton
A National Collegiate Athletic Association (NCAA) study found that
although collegiate athletes experienced increased mental health challenges post-pandemic, the majority were reluctant to seek support.
While much research has focused on the mental health of studentathletes, there is comparatively less literature exploring the experiences
of international student-athletes, who comprise over 24,000 NCAA
athletes. This study qualitatively examined the unique mental health and
help-seeking experiences of international student-athletes through the
lens of Ryba et al.’s (2016) cultural transition model. Fifteen male
(n = 7) and female (n = 8) NCAA Division I international studentathletes from 13 different countries participated in one-on-one, semistructured interviews. Reﬂexive thematic analysis resulted in four
themes pertaining to athletes’: (a) early experiences with mental health
stigma,
(b)
pre-transition,
(c)
acute
cultural
adaptation,
and
(d) sociocultural adaptation. The ﬁrst theme described the presence of
mental health stigma within athletes’ home countries, cultural and
religious communities, and athletic settings. Participants discussed how
mental health issues were often dismissed or not socially acceptable,
ultimately creating a pervasive barrier to help-seeking. The second
theme summarized the range of emotions (e.g., sadness, anxiousness,
optimism) athletes felt directly before their transnational migration to
the United States. Though participants reported a mix of emotions, they
generally did not describe experiences of mental health struggles during
this period. The third theme epitomized the challenges of cultural
adaptation, highlighting the shifts in athletes’ mental health and support
systems, and the emergence of help-seeking barriers and facilitators.
The signiﬁcance of the coach-athlete relationship also became evident
as participants reported how the quality of their relationship positively
or negatively impacted their mental health. Finally, the fourth theme—
and ﬁnal phase of cultural transition—involved generally improved
athlete mental health and well-being as positive changes in help-seeking
attitudes and behaviors occurred. These ﬁndings expand upon Ryba
et al.’s (2016) work by highlighting the timing and progression of helpseeking barriers and facilitators during international student-athletes’
cultural transitions. The results have practical implications for fostering
supportive environments that prioritize the mental health and well-being
of international student-athletes, ensuring they can effectively navigate
their transitions and seek the support they need.
DE-PASS – Modiﬁable determinants of physical activity and sedentary behaviours in adolescents: Preliminary results of a systematic
meta-review of self-measured data
Simone Ciaccioni, Pegaso Telematic University, Italy; Livia Oddi, University of Rome “La Sapienza”, Italy; Athanasios Koloveloni, University
of Thessaly, Greece; Ioannis Syrmpas, University of Thessaly, Greece;
Anna Marcuzzi, University of Science and Technology, Norway; Mohammed Khudair, Cardiff University, UK; Kwok Ng, University of Turku,
Finland; Cristina Cortis, University of Cassino and Lazio Meridionale,
Italy; Laura Capranica, University of Rome “Foro Italico”, Italy; Ciaran
MacDonncha, University of Limerick, Ireland; Fiona Chun Man Ling,

Physical inactivity and sedentary behaviour (SB) during adolescence
signiﬁcantly impact long-term physical and mental health, yet only
20% of adolescents globally meet the international recommendations
for ≥60 minutes of daily moderate-to-vigorous physical activity (PA).
However, the potential associations between adolescents’ PA/SB and their
determinants within speciﬁc settings have been neglected. Following the
PRISMA Statement guidelines and analyzing the risk of bias through the
Cochrane RoB 2.0 and ROBINS-I tools, this systematic review and metaanalysis identiﬁed modiﬁable determinants of self-reported PA and SB in
adolescents aged 13–19 years across 14 studies (12 Randomized Control
Trials and 2 Control Trials) conducted in school, home, and home and
school settings. Across 67 unique determinants identiﬁed, most were
individual–psychological (e.g., self-efﬁcacy, motivation), while fewer
were interpersonal or individual–behavioural. Four determinants (planning, self-efﬁcacy, family support, school support) were meta-analyzed.
Planning showed anecdotal evidence for a positive effect, while family
support, school support, and self-efﬁcacy showed anecdotal evidence for
the absence of an effect. Inconclusive evidence for a positive effect
emerged for PA and SB. Overall, substantial heterogeneity was found
across studies. Risk of bias was high across studies, primarily due to issues
with randomization, outcome measurement, and deviations from intended
interventions. Interventions often incorporated theory-based approaches
during physical education classes, extracurricular activities, or home-based
programs, focusing on determinants such as self-efﬁcacy, motivation, and
social support. Findings emphasize the importance of targeted, theorybased interventions addressing modiﬁable determinants to enhance adolescent PA and reduce SB across various contexts. Funding source: COST
Action CA19101 Determinants of Physical Activities in Settings (DEPASS), supported by COST (European Cooperation in Science and
Technology)
Negative body-related self-conscious emotions inﬂuence visuomotor
adaptation in young adults
Madison F. Vani, University of Toronto; Ross M. Murray, University of
Toronto; Timothy N. Welsh, University of Toronto; Edina Bijvoet, University of Toronto; Catherine M. Sabiston, University of Toronto
Negative body-related self-conscious emotions (shame, guilt, envy, and
embarrassment) can deplete resources needed to perform cognitive and
motor tasks efﬁciently. Visuomotor adaptation is a crucial component of
cognitive and motor performance, however, researchers have not examined the extent to which body-related emotions impact visuomotor adaptation. The current study investigated the inﬂuence of negative bodyrelated self-conscious emotions on visuomotor adaptation in young adults.
Participants (N = 72; Mage = 22.47 ± 4.33 years; 69% women) completed
dispositional appearance-related self-conscious emotion questionnaires
and had appearance monitoring primed using body measurements (tape
measure and skin fold caliper measurements) and photographs of their
bodies prior to completing a visuomotor adaptation task. The adaptation
task involved participants holding a mouse in their hand and moved the
mouse to move a cursor to targets on a computer screen. In the key
conditions, the translation of the hand-to-mouse movements changed such
that the cursor moved in a direction that was 90° clockwise to the direction
of the hand movement. Movement time and movement error were indicators of motor performance while the participant adapted their hand
movements to accurately move the cursor to the targets. Linear mixed
effects models and linear slopes analyses were calculated. Participants who
reported higher scores on body guilt, embarrassment, and envy performed
worse on the task (longer movement times) and displayed slower task
learning (ps < .05) compared to those with lower scores. Higher body guilt
was associated with greater accuracy (less radial error; p < .05), but none of
the body self-conscious emotions were associated with changes in radial
error throughout the task (ps > .05). These results suggest that when those
who are prone to experiencing negative body emotions have their body
awareness primed, they become more careful and adapt slower on a
visuomotor adaptation task. The ﬁndings have important implications for
learning motor skills and having conﬁdence to perform and engage in
physical
tasks,
including
physical
activity
and
sport.
Funding
source: SSHRC.
Utilizing control-value theory to test relationships between cognitive
appraisals, boredom, and perceived success in competitive sport
practices
Nya G. Derkach, Thompson Rivers University; Patti C. Parker, Thompson
Rivers University
Boredom is a deactivating emotion signaling that a current situation may
lack importance or challenge—prompting a need for change. To date,
boredom has been overlooked in sport psychology research but is recently
attracting attention because of its potential impacts on learning and
performance. Drawing from Pekrun’s (2006) Control-Value Theory of
Achievement Emotions (CVTAE), the present study assesses whether
value and control impact boredom and if boredom in practices mediates
the relationship between perceived value and success. Years of competitive experience were tested as a moderator of this relationship. Early in
2024, a cross-sectional survey was administered to Canadian athletes
aged 18-35 participating in competitive sports, comprising demographic
variables and measures assessing cognitions (perceived value, control),
emotions, and perceived success. Employing secondary data analysis, we
conducted a moderated mediation regression analysis to evaluate: (1)
whether perceived value and control in practice are associated with
boredom; (2) if the relationship between perceived value and success
in practice is mediated by boredom; and (3) if the same relationship is
moderated by years of competitive experience, while controlling for
levels of perceived control. Consistent with CVTAE, perceived value
negatively predicted boredom, β = -0.18, p = .001, 95% CI [-0.32, -0.04].
Higher perceived value predicted greater levels of perceived success and
lower levels of boredom, but the relationship was not signiﬁcantly
mediated by boredom. We found a signiﬁcant Perceived Value x Years
of Competitive Experience interaction. Probing the interaction revealed
more years of competitive experience strengthened the relationship
between value and boredom, β = -0.35, p < .001, 95% CI [-0.52, -
0.18], for athletes with high levels of competitive experience (not low
or moderate levels). Finally, the value-boredom-success relationship was
not mediated at levels of the moderator. Although further research
designs are needed to test the longitudinal nature of these relationships,
our study contributes to the literature by considering how the CVTAE
framework supports our understanding of how competitive athletes
experience boredom in sports practice settings. Funding source: A
Scholarship of Teaching and Learning Grant (institutional)

JohnBosco Chukwuorji, University of Nigeria, Nsukka; Doris Tay,
University of Ghana; Vida Nyawornota, University of Ghana; Clement

Physical inactivity and mental distress were prevalent across African
populations during the COVID-19 pandemic. However, limited research
has explored the role of sedentary time in the relationship between
COVID-19 symptoms and mental health outcomes in these populations.
Understanding this relationship is crucial, as the pandemic’s spread, peak,
and public health responses varied across African countries, potentially
inﬂuencing physical activity and mental health differently across regions.
This study examined the moderating role of sedentary time (IPAQ-SF) on
the relationship among COVID-19 symptoms, depression (PHQ9), and
anxiety (GAD7) in Botswana, Ghana, Nigeria, and Tanzania. Crosssectional data were collected from 2,939 adults (male = 51.3%; Mage =
27.05; SD = 8.91). Four moderation models simultaneously speciﬁed in
Mplus using multi-group analysis revealed signiﬁcant associations
among COVID-19 symptoms, sedentary time, and mental health outcomes. No signiﬁcant moderation effects were observed in Botswana,
Nigeria, and Tanzania. However, a higher number of COVID-19 symptoms was linked to increased anxiety and depression, and sedentary time
was associated with greater depressive symptoms. In Ghana, COVID-19
symptoms (B = 0.97, p < .001) and sedentary time (B = 0.01, p = .001)
were positively associated with anxiety, with sex (B = 0.81, p = .008) as a
signiﬁcant covariate. For depression, a signiﬁcant interaction effect
(B = 0.01, p < .001) revealed that higher sedentary time strengthened the
relationship between COVID-19 symptoms and depression. Simple slope
analysis showed that the effect of COVID-19 symptoms on depression
was signiﬁcant at low (-1 SD, slope = 0.700, p = .003), moderate (0,
slope = 0.706, p = .003), and high (+1 SD, slope = 0.712, p = .03) levels of
sedentary time. This suggests that as sedentary time increased, the
relationship between COVID-19 symptoms and depressive symptoms
became stronger. Findings underscore the complex interplay among
COVID-19 symptoms, sedentary behavior, and mental health. Sedentary
time varies by country, emphasizing the importance of tailored strategies
to address mental health challenges during public health crises. This study
contributes valuable insights into regional differences in pandemicrelated mental health impacts, informing future research and interventions. Funding source: National Research Foundation of South Africa.
Exploring the nature of college students’ physical activity guideline
knowledge
Jenna A. Parsons, Louisiana State University; Jaclyn Hadﬁeld, Louisiana
State University; Ryan M. Hulteen, Louisiana State University
Physical activity guideline achievement remains low in young adults.
Level of knowledge can explain behavioral engagement with most health
behaviors. This study aims to explore the nature of college students’
knowledge of physical activity guidelines. A total of 12 individuals (mean
age 20 ± 1.8 yrs, n = 8 females) participated in semi-structured interviews.
Three constructs of focus (knowledge, experiences, behaviors) served as
predetermined themes. We found the thematic category of Individual
Factors to be associated with each construct and identiﬁed the following
subthemes: emotional responses, perceived appearance, and signiﬁcant
others. Participants reported various positive, negative and neutral emotions that they connected to their experiences related to physical activity
and the guidelines. The role of signiﬁcant others was primarily reported as
a positive inﬂuential factor related to their physical activity behaviors.
Self-perception of appearance seemed to create a complex relationship
with physical activity behaviors. Collectively, experiences and behaviors
were connected to the level of knowledge. Knowledge levels varied
among participants, with the majority being unable to describe the
physical activity guidelines accurately. Only two participants were able
to describe the aerobic component of the guidelines, in which both
reported learning the guidelines through their college coursework. No
participant described the muscle-strengthening component of the
guidelines. Efforts to educate young adults on the guidelines may be a
worthwhile effort to make them aware of what they should aim to achieve.
Physical activity guideline education should consider the individual
factors of this population to address the behavioral and experiential
aspects
inﬂuencing
knowledge
and
ultimately
physical
activity
engagement.
Children’s affective experiences from exercise: A new approach to
asking the right questions
Jona Kunz, University of Potsdam; Ralf Brand, University of Potsdam
Positive affective experiences from exercise appear to play a critical role in
shaping motivation to exercise regularly. They can be measured with the
recently published Affective Exercise Experiences Questionnaire (AFFEXX). Current research shows that the core affective experiences of
adolescents are shaped by a different set of antecedent appraisals than those
of adults. For example, perceptions of competence, as well as being
watched while exercising, seem to have a greater impact on adolescents’
experiences. Based on the hypothesis that both physical activity and
affective experiences of exercise are likely shaped in childhood, we
developed the AFFEXX-Kids, a validated version for children aged 812 years. The questionnaire format was adapted to include 11 unipolar item
pairs with colorful scales and child-friendly language. Parents provided
data on their child’s weekly participation in sports, exercise, and leisuretime physical activity. In an initial sample of 186 children (Mage = 9.5 ±
0.9 years, 49% female), “boredom vs. interest” emerged as the strongest
predictor of children’s core affective experiences from exercise (“displeasure vs. pleasure”), β = 0.51, 95% CI [0.42, 0.60], p < .001.Thus, it appears
that for younger children, the playful, engaging, and socially interactive
aspects of exercise may be the primary factors driving pleasurable
experiences. This ﬁnding further highlights the distinction in adolescence,
where exercise experiences are more strongly inﬂuenced by peer scrutiny
and perceived competence. Analyses also revealed a signiﬁcant correlation
between the attraction to exercise (hedonic wanting), which is critically
informed by antecedent appraisals and core affective experiences, and
leisure-time physical activity, ρ = 0.39, p = .002. These ﬁndings highlight
the relevance of the AFFEXX-Kids as a novel tool for understanding
children’s exercise experiences. In addition to ﬁlling a critical gap in the
literature, the instrument has signiﬁcant potential to inform early interventions in exercise psychology.
Psychological resilience during a 30 day ultra-endurance cycling
expedition
Amber Mosewich, University of Alberta; Danielle Cormier, University of
Alberta; Ben Sereda, University of Alberta; Timm Döbert, Wings of
Survival; Leanna Carriere, Wings of Survival
Psychological resilience is the ability to recover from adversity and react
adaptively to stressful situations (Fletcher & Sarkar, 2012). Examining
ultra-endurance athletes, who push physical and mental limits, offers
insights into developing and maintaining resilience (Morgan et al.,
2013). The purpose of our study was to explore factors and processes
related to psychological resilience in athletes when preparing for and
undertaking a 30 day ultra-endurance expedition, cycling approximately
150 km per day across Canada. Two ultra-endurance athletes (1 man, 1
woman) completed daily athlete self-report measures (ASRMs), audio
recordings (at relevant points while cycling), and one-on-one interviews
(pre-, during, and post-expedition). Kazdin’s (2021) single case study
design allowed the examination of each athlete over time, with ASRMs
subjected to visual inspection to identify trends. A qualitative content
analysis (Elo & Kyngäs, 2008) was adopted for the audio diaries and
interviews. Overall, the athletes demonstrated effective management of
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
FREE COMMUNICATIONS: VERBAL AND POSTERS
S110
Unauthenticated | Downloaded 06/07/26 07:03 AM UTC

--- [Página 114] ---
demands, evidenced across multiple data sources. Data from the ASRMs
suggest that while athlete perceptions of their physical and psychological
fatigue, recovery, and sleep quality varied throughout the expedition,
perceived stress, mood, task and coping self-efﬁcacy, physical and psychological well-being, and perceptions of physical and psychological
readiness remained relatively stable (at levels classiﬁed as adaptive by
participants), providing evidence of resilience. Five key themes from the
qualitative data emphasized processes related to resilience during the
expedition. Social Support and Collaboration emphasized mutual encouragement, clear communication, and collective decision-making to navigate
challenges and achieve successful outcomes. Goal-Oriented DecisionMaking supported purposeful actions by aligning decisions with core
values and long-term objectives, promoting clarity and forward momentum. Learning from Experience and Self-Awareness allowed the athletes to
adapt effectively by recognizing psychological and physical challenges
and employing proactive strategies like self-talk and mental reframing.
Consistency and Preparation reinforced resilience by cultivating trust in
one’s capacity through consistent preparation, persistence, and a growth
mindset. Acceptance and Adaptability enabled the athletes to manage
stressors with deliberate adjustments and a ﬂexible, integrated approach.
Findings from this study provide insight into the processes involved in
resilience, and an example of how to explore processes related to resilience
over time employing multiple methods. Funding source: This research was
funded by a grant from the University of Alberta Endowment Fund for the
Future Advancement of Scholarship.
Unpacking the factors related to ﬂourishing in high-performing
Australian athletes
Alyson J. Crozier, University of South Australia; Courtney De Barro,
University of South Australia; Maximillian J. Nelson, University of South
Australia; Amber D. Mosewich, University of Alberta
The critical role of positive mental health in enhancing sport performance,
career longevity, and overall satisfaction among athletes is increasingly
acknowledged. Flourishing, a comprehensive construct of mental health
encompassing emotional, psychological, and social well-being, offers a
valuable framework for understanding positive mental health in sport.
Despite its importance, limited research has examined the factors that
contribute to ﬂourishing in high-performing athletes, restricting sport
organizations’ capacity to effectively allocate resources for holistic athlete
development. This study aimed to identify the key factors related to
ﬂourishing in high-performing athletes. A sample of 114 athletes (53
males, 61 females) competing at state league level or higher across team
(n = 51) and individual (n = 63) sports completed an online survey. The
survey assessed ﬂourishing and several potential factors, including the
coach-athlete relationship, perceived support in sport, perceived motivational climate, team psychological safety, athlete self-efﬁcacy, emotional
intelligence, mental skills training, and sport-life balance. Eighteen variables (including subscales) were analyzed using Least Absolute Shrinkage
and Selection Operator (LASSO) regression to identify factors for inclusion in a multivariate linear regression model. Backward selection based
on the Bayesian Information Criterion (BIC) yielded a ﬁnal model with
four signiﬁcant factors, explaining 46.8% of the variance in athlete
ﬂourishing (F(4, 109) = 25.815, p <.001). The signiﬁcant factors included:
(1) perceived available informational support in sport (β = 1.01, p = .038);
(2) emotional intelligence (β = 0.41, p <.001); (3) interference of personal
life with sport (β = −0.75, p = .007); and (4) the mutual enhancement of
sport and personal life (β = 1.23, p =.006), all demonstrating large effect
sizes (η2 range = -0.19 – 0.34). These ﬁndings underscore the multidimensional nature of ﬂourishing in athletes and highlight actionable areas
for intervention. By prioritizing informational support, emotional intelligence development, and fostering a balance between sport and personal
life, sport organizations can cultivate environments that promote athlete
ﬂourishing, with the intention of enhancing both performance and
well-being.
Examining the feasibility and psychological impact of the ‘Just Run’
online educational program for recreational runners
Alyson J. Crozier, University of South Australia; Chloe T. Blacket,
University of South Australia; Hunter Bennett, University of South Australia; Joel T. Fuller, Macquarie University; John Arnold, University of
South Australia
Regular running elicits a myriad of physical and mental health beneﬁts, yet
discontinuation threatens the ability for individuals to achieve associated
positive health outcomes. Limited evidence explores the effect of interventions promoting running maintenance from a motivational and psychological perspective. Using a two-arm parallel group randomised trial
design, this study aimed to examine the feasibility of Just Run, a 12-week
online intervention that included basic training advice and education on
behaviour change techniques, versus a control program, which included
basic training advice only. Primary outcomes were feasibility based,
including recruitment, retention, and intervention engagement. Secondary
outcomes included motivation, basic psychological needs, self-efﬁcacy,
beneﬁts/barriers, wellbeing and running engagement metrics. Sixty adult
novice runners were recruited, of which 33 completed outcomes at the 12week follow-up for the intervention (n = 16) and control (n = 17) groups.
Despite low-to-moderate engagement with the educational content (ranging from 10.7% to 71.4%), participant feedback indicated an overall
positive experience, with all modules identiﬁed as important. Majority
regarded videos to be of high quality (53.8%) and a clear link between
videos and resources was identiﬁed by most (61.6%). Findings also
revealed large between group differences in change scores from baseline
to week 6 for amotivation (d = -0.81, 95% CI [-1.52, -0.09]) and from
baseline to week 12 for scheduling self-efﬁcacy (d = -0.89, 95% CI [-1.60,
-0.16]), each seeing decreases for the intervention group and increases for
the control group. No differences in basic psychological needs, exercise
beneﬁts/barriers, and wellbeing were observed. Running engagement was
similar between groups across the 12 weeks, with the proportion of each
group running weekly (29 to 50% vs. 14 to 54%), as well as average
duration ran (26 mins vs. 25 mins 34 sec) and average distance ran (2.98km
vs. 2.95km) similar for intervention vs. control groups, respectively. These
ﬁndings reveal that Just Run may be a feasible intervention, although
modiﬁcations are required to optimise engagement and retention, and a
larger sample size would assist in determining the effects of the online
running education program on psychological variables and running
parameters.
Coaches say lighter is better, but at what cost? A qualitative exploration of the lingering impact of body image on disordered eating in
retired elite women athletes
Delaney Thibodeau, University of Toronto; Sara Sutherland, University of
Toronto; David Brown, University of Toronto; Danika Quesnel, University of Toronto; Catherine Sabiston, University of Toronto
Women athletes often experience body dissatisfaction and disordered
eating, contributing to a heightened risk of developing an eating disorder
throughout their athletic career. Yet the experiences of body image and
disordered eating are not understood in retirement from sport. A qualitative description study with semi-structured interviews was used to
describe body image and disordered eating in eight retired elite women
athletes from various sports. Interviews were transcribed verbatim,
analyzed inductively, and organized into three themes. The ﬁrst theme
“Social inﬂuences and instigators of disordered eating while competing in
sport” reﬂects motivators of disordered eating with sub-themes:
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025
