(a) coaches are uncaring and unqualiﬁed, (b) parents set the ‘tone,’ for
better or for worse, and (c) peers are not always friends, though some
show support. The second theme “The pinnacle of disordered eating”
depicts behaviours and feelings experienced at the peak of career with
sub-themes: (a) losing weight by any means necessary and (b) the
physical and emotional tolls. The third theme “Post-retirement changes
and compensatory behaviours,” describes participants’ experiences after
ceasing competition with sub-themes: (a) changes in identity and
(b) compensatory eating and exercise. The results highlight diverse
social-cultural factors inﬂuencing body dissatisfaction and disordered
eating onset. Ultimately, results may help inform intervention strategies
that will improve the overall health and well-being of women athletes
both during sport and into retirement.
Ego-involving motivational climates trigger psychological and physiological stress responses in elite level collegiate athletes during a free
throw shooting clinic
Candace M. Hogue, University of Minnesota
According to achievement goal perspective theory (Nicholls, 1984, 1989),
the motivational climate fostered by coaches plays a key role in predicting
athlete responses. The climate literature has consistently linked egoinvolving climates (EICs) to less adaptive, often concerning responses in
athletes, compared to caring, task-involving climates (CTICs; Fry et al.,
2020). However, most research examining the impact of the motivational
climate in sport has been conducted with youth. More research with elite
athletes is needed if we are to understand how the coaching climate impacts
their sport experience and how they respond to performance stress. Prescreened Division I collegiate athletes (N = 39; Mage = 20 y.o.) took part in
a 45-min free throw shooting clinic with either a CTIC or an EIC. Saliva
was collected prior to (t = 0) and following (t = +45, +60, +75, +90 mins)
the clinic to assess salivary cortisol, as stress-responsive hormone with
implications for performance and well-being. Post-surveys assessed athletes’ experiences of more threatening forms of performance stress, shamerelated experiences, and motivational responses. Athletes were also asked
whether the type of coaching environment they were in during the free
throw clinic helps them perform their best and whether they feel it helps
athletes realize their full potential. ANOVAs were used to assess group
differences. Athletes reported experiencing more threatening forms of
stress in the EIC and responded with a signiﬁcant rise in cortisol. They also
reported more shame-related experiences, compared to the CTIC group. In
contrast, the cortisol levels of the CTIC group decreased from baseline and
they reported signiﬁcantly greater sport satisfaction (i.e., enjoyment)
during the free throw shooting clinic. The athletes in the CTIC group
also reported that the coaching environment they were in would help them
perform their best and would help athletes realize their full potential to a
greater degree than the EIC group did. This is the ﬁrst investigation to show
EICs can trigger a psychologically-induced rise in cortisol in elite athletes.
Results suggest if coaches consistently create EICs in sport, this may elicit
concerning physiological and psychological responses in their athletes.
Funding source: University of Minnesota, College of Education and
Human Development.
Acute exercise and pattern separation-dependent mnemonic discrimination in older adults: An fMRI investigation
Samantha L. DuBois, Appalachian State University; Alexis B. SlutskyGanesh, Emory University School of Medicine; Jennifer L. Etnier, University of North Carolina at Greensboro
As the older adult population grows, more individuals will experience
episodic memory declines that negatively impact their daily functioning.
Many of these episodic memory declines are attributed to a deﬁcit in
mnemonic discrimination (MD), deﬁned as the ability to differentiate
current events from memories of similar past events. MD is dependent on a
network of brain regions involved in memory, sensory processing, and
cognitive control that facilitate the neural process of pattern separation
(PS). However, these brain regions are highly susceptible to age-related
deterioration that impairs PS. Effective strategies to preserve PS and
reduce the severity of MD declines are therefore needed to protect older
adults’ everyday memory. Aerobic exercise can protect age-susceptible
brain regions and beneﬁt older adults’ cognitive function, but limited
research on this topic has implemented MD tasks sensitive to PS. Using a
within-subjects design and functional magnetic resonance imaging
(fMRI), we examined the effects of acute aerobic exercise on MD and
task-related brain activity in healthy, cognitively normal older adults.
Participants (N = 26, Mage = 65.5 ± 4.1 years) completed two separate visits
consisting of 20-minutes of moderate intensity cycling or seated rest prior
to performing an MD task during an fMRI. The MD task placed high
demands on PS by requiring participants to discriminate previously studied
objects from perceptually similar lure objects. A repeated measures
analysis of covariance revealed no signiﬁcant difference in MD following
acute exercise compared to seated rest (F(1, 22) = 2.36, p > .05), controlling for visit order, days between visits, and visual perception. However,
fMRI analyses using FMRIB Software Library (FSL) revealed that during
trials when lure objects were presented, brain activity in regions of the right
ventral visual cortex, speciﬁcally the fusiform gyrus, was signiﬁcantly
reduced following acute exercise compared to seated rest (voxels = 262,
zmax = 5.34, p < .01). These results suggest that while acute exercise may
not result in detectable changes in MD, it can induce short-term improvements in the neural efﬁciency of regions of the ventral visual cortex crucial
to PS. Future research is needed to explore how these acute exerciseinduced brain changes may relate to chronic exercise-induced beneﬁts to
older adults’ episodic memory. Funding source: NASPSPA Graduate
Student Research Grant (2023)
Giving arbitrary metrics meaning: Determining meaningful changes
on the Feeling Scale
Constantin Späth, University of Potsdam; Ralf Brand, University of
Potsdam
Researchers who use Likert-type scales to measure psychological variables
face the challenge that their measures are arbitrary, meaning that the
practical signiﬁcance of changes on these scales is unclear. One approach
to reducing this arbitrariness is to use Global Ratings of Change (GRoC).
This involves participants rating the variable at two points in time and
indicating on an external anchor (i.e. on the GRoC) whether they perceived
a change, as well as the magnitude of the change. This method helps to
identify the smallest change in the variable that is perceptible to an
individual, providing a meaningful threshold. In exercise psychology, the
Feeling Scale (FS) is a widely used tool to repeatedly measure core
affective responses to exercise, speciﬁcally affective valence. To assess
the relevance of interventions aimed at improving the affective experience
during exercise, we conducted a study to determine the Minimal Perceived
Change (MPC) in FS units. Data were collected from 225 participants
(Mage = 25.17 ± 8.48, 45% female) during two exercise sessions on a cycle
ergometer at different intensities: moderate (64-76% HRmax) and light
(57-63% HRmax). The second session was designed to improve affective
responses by reducing exercise intensity based on dual-mode theory,
which predicts more positive feelings at lower intensities. Participants
exercised for 10 minutes at each intensity, with FS responses measured
every two minutes. After the second session, participants provided their
GRoC. Ninety-one participants reported feeling “a little better” when
comparing the light to the moderate exercise session, corresponding to
a mean FS improvement of 0.77 units (95% CI [0.58, 0.96]). This value

represents an initial estimate of the MPC and provides a benchmark for
assessing the practical signiﬁcance of changes in FS scores. While further
research is needed, this estimate provides a Smallest Effect Size of Interest
(SESOI) that can guide a-priori power analyses in future studies and help
determine whether observed effects on FS are meaningful at the individual level.
Two-dimensional latent physical activity self-efﬁcacy and its relationships with physical activity: A secondary data analysis
Haeyong Chun, Michigan State University; Nicholas Myers, Michigan
State University; Issac Prilleltensky, University of Miami; Seungmin Lee,
Iowa State University; Leapetswe Malete, Michigan State University;
Christine Pacewicz, Miami University
The purpose of this study was to improve measurement of physical activity
self-efﬁcacy (PASE) in adults with obesity by evaluating relationships
between responses to a new battery of PASE Scales and physical activity
(PA). The pursuit of this purpose was guided by Standards for Educational
and Psychological Testing. These standards suggest that when the internal
structure of responses to an instrument (e.g., measurement model) is
revised, then evidence based on relationships between these revised
measures and other variables (i.e., external to the instrument) should be
evaluated. The internal structure of responses to the PASE Scales has
recently been revised from a unidimensional observed score approach to a
two-dimensional latent variable approach. However, the relationships
between these revised latent PASE measures and PA have yet to be
examined. To be consistent with the standards for psychological testing,
this study examined validity as a unitary concept and attempted to provide
a speciﬁc source of validity evidence (e.g., relationships to other variables)
versus providing evidence for a distinct type of validity (e.g., external
validity). Therefore, this study addresses this gap by examining relationships between a recently proposed two-dimensional (shorter and longer
durations) latent PASE conceptualization and PA across two intensities
(moderate and vigorous) and four domains (work, transport, domestic, and
leisure) measured by the PASE Scales. Longitudinal secondary data
(N = 461) from the Well-Being and Physical Activity (ClinicalTrials.gov,
identiﬁer: NCT03194854) study, which deployed the Fun For Wellness
(FFW) intervention, were analyzed. As a result, positive direct effects
(latent PASE→PA) were observed for shorter duration (up to 30/60
minutes for vigorous/moderate intensity), PASE across domains, but not
for longer durations (30/60 minutes or more for vigorous/moderate
intensity), reinforcing the importance of testing key measurement assumptions in self-efﬁcacy research. Indirect effects of FFW on PA through latent
PASE (FFW→latent PASE→PA) were signiﬁcant only for shorter durations at a moderate (but not vigorous) intensity, suggesting that interventions focusing on shorter-duration PASE may better promote PA in this
population. These results have implications for PA-promotion in this atrisk population like overweight or obese people.
Regular and non-regular exercisers’ engagement with person-adaptive exercise programming: Exploring ﬁdelity and social validity
Kelley Strohacker, University of Tennessee; Adam Ibrahim, University of
Tennessee; Jannika John, University of Tübingen; Cory Beaumont, Baldwin Wallace University; Jedediah Blanton, University of Tennessee; Dawn
Coe, University of Tennessee
Modiﬁed Flexible Nonlinear Periodization (mFNLP) is a multi-theoretical
model for person-adaptive exercise programming. mFNLP involves arranging goal-oriented exercise sessions from low- to and high-demand and
selecting sessions based on physical, cognitive, and emotional resources
(‘readiness’). No prior FNLP studies provided evidence that participantchosen demand corresponded with subjective readiness or documented
users’ perceptions of its goals, procedures, and effects. This mixed-method
study explored ﬁdelity and social validity of mFNLP based on a two-week
microcycle. Participants (N = 24, 34 ± 10y, 67% women, 79% white, 50%
regular exercisers) co-constructed nine exercise sessions with a researcher.
Participants completed pre-exercise surveys, documenting readiness
(Acute Readiness Monitoring Scale) and session choice. Session rating
of perceived exertion (sRPE; representing demand) was calculated using
post-exercise survey items (Category Ratio-10 RPE x duration). Fidelity
was explored using within-person correlations (readiness vs. sRPE;
small=.10; medium = .30; large = .50). Participants initiated 12 ± 2 sessions; many demonstrated positive associations between sRPE with
physical (58% rho = .32-.77; 25% rho = .10-.29), cognitive (54% rho =
.32-.82; 12% rho = .10-.25), and emotional readiness (54% rho = .31-.84;
21% rho = .10-.26). Reﬂexive thematic analysis of all participants’ interview data yielded three themes to understand perceptions of mFNLP. First,
“Good in theory, good in practice” demonstrated appreciation that the coconstruction process provided a customizable program that was simple to
follow with the menu of choices, providing beneﬁts such as increased
exercise frequency, feeling accomplished, and stress relief. Second, “We
may have been more strategic with it, potentially” offered points for
reﬁnement including less restrictive durations, adding rest day options, and
follow-ups to revise sessions. Finally, “I’m in a constant pursuit of what’s
right and what I’m supposed to be doing : : : that’s something I struggle
with” highlighted that brieﬂy engaging in mFNLP provided permission to
help dismantle ‘all-or-nothing’ thinking that previously contributed to
injury, exhaustion, and discouragement. Generally positive associations
between readiness and demand suggest participants’ feedback is based on
mFNLP enactment, and thusly relevant to guide reﬁnement. However, it is
critical to expand approaches for understanding ﬁdelity to mFNLP procedures. Longer implementation is warranted to evaluate psychophysiological effects of person-adaptive exercise programming using mFNLP.
Exploring barriers to help-seeking behaviour: Perspectives from
Canadian NextGen athletes and coaches
Lara Pomerleau-Fontaine, Université de Sherbrooke; Véronique Boudreault, Université de Sherbrooke; Juliette Maurin, Université de Sherbrooke; Sophie Brassard, Université de Sherbrooke
Research shows that elite athletes often face barriers when seeking mental
health services, suggesting stigma being the main obstacle. Coaches’
attitudes towards mental health can also impact athletes’ willingness to
consult as they often serve as a bridge between athletes’ and resources.
Understanding both athletes’ and coaches’ perceptions seems critical for
identifying and addressing barriers to help-seeking for creating quality and
supportive environments. However, there is a signiﬁcant gap in research
speciﬁcally understanding these challenges within the NextGen athletes’
environment, where they have access to fewer mental health support and
services compared to national athletes. This study aims to explore the
perceptions of NextGen athletes and coaches regarding the barriers to
mental health resources. Eleven focus groups were conducted: seven with
25 NextGen athletes and four with 15 coaches. Thematic analyses revealed
the presence of social, organizational, and personal barriers in NextGen
athletes’ environments. For social barriers, both coaches and athletes
emphasized challenges including limited access to mental health professionals, inadequate support from these professionals, and the negative
inﬂuence of a “mental toughness” culture. At the organizational level,
coaches and athletes pointed to several issues, including insufﬁcient
promotion of mental health, a lack of resources (e.g., inadequate funding,
performance-based funding models, regional funding disparities), and
limited support from sports federations. Personal barriers identiﬁed by
both groups included a lack of time, limited awareness and mental health
literacy, and insufﬁcient prioritization of mental health. From the coaches’

perspective, some also noted that NextGen athletes often demonstrate low
levels of autonomy, which could further contribute to the challenges faced
in addressing mental health needs effectively. Generally, results show that
NextGen athletes’ and coaches’ share similar perceptions regarding barriers to mental health resources, suggesting that these challenges are
prevalent in the participants’ environment. The data highlights the importance to invest into on-site services to improve scheduling, normalization,
visibility, and access to mental health resources. Considering that organizational barriers were identiﬁed, and funding was a key issue, future
research should explore the perceptions of sports directors and other
stakeholders on how to address these barriers and enhance support for
the development of NextGen athletes. Funding source: Social Sciences and

A daily diary study of women’s motivational self-compassion in
physical activity and its association with physical activity engagement
and distress
Katarina Huellemann, McGill University; Rachel Calogero, Western
University; Eva Pila, Western University
Although there are many documented beneﬁts of physical activity, women
continue to report lower levels of physical activity engagement and
enjoyment across the lifespan compared to men. Experiencing different
forms of distress in physical activity (e.g., body image-related, exerciserelated, gender-related) may be one reason that women participate less in
physical activity, especially since enjoyment is a key predictor of sustained
engagement. Self-compassion, a kind and supportive way of responding to
oneself in the face of distress, is positively associated with physical activity
engagement and enjoyment in women. Yet, most studies have been crosssectional and examined emotion regulation perspectives of self-compassion, rather than motivational perspectives, which limits understandings of
whether self-compassionate motivation and action are related to women’s
physical activity engagement and distress in their everyday lives. In the
present study, women (N = 163, aged 18-64) ﬁrst completed a novel
baseline measure of motivational self-compassion in physical activity. In
this measure, participants imagined themselves in ﬁve potentially distressing physical activity scenarios and reported how they would respond to
themselves using motivational self-compassion items. Next, participants
took part in a 14-day daily diary protocol where they reported whether they
engaged in purposeful physical activity and whether they experienced
distress in their physical activity each day. Multilevel modelling was used
to test hypotheses that participants with higher baseline self-compassion
would report a greater number of days engaged in physical activity, and
less days experiencing physical activity distress, across the study period.
Age and baseline physical activity were included in the models as
covariates. Contrary to expectations, baseline self-compassion was significantly associated with a lower number of days engaged in physical activity
(b = -2.03, SE = .05, p < .001) and was not signiﬁcantly associated with
number of days experiencing physical activity distress. Age and baseline
physical activity were signiﬁcantly associated with number of days
engaged in physical activity but not physical activity distress. The ﬁndings
provide insight into motivational perspectives of self-compassion in
physical activity and how the measurement of baseline self-compassion,
physical activity, and physical activity distress using a daily diary approach
may have impacted study ﬁndings. Funding source: SSHRC.
Examining proﬁles of athlete burnout, psychosocial outcomes, and
their associations with injury status and rehabilitation resources in
self-identiﬁed athletes during COVID-19
J.D. DeFreese, UNC-Chapel Hill; Shelby Baez, UNC-Chapel Hill; Hana
Marmura, UNC-Chapel Hill; Avinash Chandran, UNC-Chapel Hill;
Datalys Center for Sports Injury Research and Prevention, Inc.
Athlete burnout is a cognitive-affective syndrome characterized by emotional and physical exhaustion, reduced accomplishment, and sport devaluation with implications for injury and rehabilitation outcomes for athletes.
The COVID-19 pandemic represents an important period to examine the
interplay of athlete burnout, injury, and rehabilitation resources due to its
major disruption of sport participation at all levels. Informed by the
biopsychosocial model of athletic injury, we examined proﬁles of athlete
burnout dimensions (i.e., symptoms) and psychosocial perceptions
(e.g., perceived stress, social support, negative social interactions), and
their association with injury status and rehabilitation resources. We
hypothesized that a proﬁle exhibiting characteristic of heightened athlete
burnout combined with a maladaptive psychosocial proﬁle (high stress,
low social support, high negative social interactions) would be associated
with injury or participating in sport while injured and fewer rehabilitation
resources during the COVID-19 pandemic (i.e., Fall 2020-Spring 2021).
Participants were 192 self-identiﬁed athletes (M = 36.5 ± 16.8 years) who
completed valid and reliable self-report assessments of study variables
including athlete burnout, psychosocial perceptions, injury, and rehabilitation outcomes. Participants were majority male (n = 98, 51%) and White/
Caucasian (n = 175, 91%). The majority of the those sampled reported
being recreational athletes (n = 133, 69%) and not being currently injured
(n = 156, 81%). Following data screening and the calculation of descriptive
statistics, a cluster analysis (i.e., K-means, 2-cluster solution speciﬁed) was
conducted to probe the existence of athlete burnout dimensions and
psychosocial variable proﬁles. Resultant proﬁles were then compared on
injury status and rehabilitation resources via group difference analyses.
Participants reported levels of aggregate athlete burnout (M = 2.32 ± 0.68),
perceived stress (M = 2.66 ± 0.83), social support (M = 3.58 ± 0.84), and
negative social interactions (M = 1.68 ± 0.68). In partial support of study
hypotheses, a maladaptive proﬁle of psychosocial perceptions and heightened burnout levels was identiﬁed. However, membership in this proﬁle
was not associated with injury/participating while injured (χ2(2) = 3.22,
p = .200) or reporting fewer rehabilitation resources (F(1) = 3.38, p = .068).
Findings have implications for sport scientists seeking to delineate the
importance of athlete burnout to injury/rehabilitation outcomes during
disruptions to sport participation such as the COVID-19 pandemic. Future
work necessitates further theory-driven, prospective research efforts.
The effect viewing #whatieatinaday TikTok videos with and without
calories on body-related shame, guilt, envy, and intentions to change
diet and exercise in young women
Sarah Galway, Brock University; Kimberley Gammage, Brock University
Throughout daily life young adults are exposed to a variety of stimuli that
can impact their body image. Young adults may be exposed to food-related
content and idealized appearances when viewing TikTok content such as
what I eat in a day (WIEIAD) videos. The purpose of the present study was
to examine the effects of viewing WIEIAD TikTok videos (with and
without calories) on body-related shame, guilt and envy, and intentions to
change diet and exercise. We also aimed to examine indirect effects of
upward appearance comparisons and moderating effects of self-compassion. Participants were young women [N = 335, Mage = 25 years (SD =
3.24)] recruited for a study titled “social media use, self-perceptions and
mood” (cover story) through Cloud Research Connect. Participants selfreported demographic information including age, physical activity, height
and weight, ethnicity, socioeconomic status, time spent on social media,
time spent on TikTok, as well as internalization of the thin ideal.
Participants were randomly assigned to view a series of a) WIEIAD
videos with calories, b) WIEIAD videos without calories or c) travel
videos (control) and completed pre-post measures of body-related shame,
guilt and envy. Appearance related comparisons and intentinos to change
diet and exercise were assessed post-video exposure. Participants who
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

viewed WIEIAD videos (with or without calories) reported higher bodyrelated envy and intentions to change diet and exercise than participants
who viewed travel videos. No differences were reported between participants who viewed WIEIAD videos with or without calories. Appearance
comparisons mediated the relationship between WIEIAD videos and bodyrelated shame, guilt, envy and intentions to change diet and exercise,
however self-compassion did not moderate any relationships. Researchers
should continue to investigate ways to mitigate upward social comparisons
elicited by consuming social media content.
Stress resilience and self-esteem in athletic and non-athletic collegiate
populations
Tyler B Mack, Juniata College; Kyle J Jaquess, Juniata College
It is understood that self-esteem and stress resilience tend to co-occur;
groups with high self-esteem tend to also have high resilience. To extend
this observation, individuals who have greater opportunities to develop
their self-esteem may have greater opportunity to develop stress resilience. Student athletes are a unique population in that, amongst the
opportunities of ﬁnding and displaying competence in the classroom, they
also can display competence on the sports ﬁeld. This additional opportunity suggests that student athletes receive a greater degree of self-esteemrelevant information compared to non-athletes allowing for increased
self-esteem as previously observed in the literature. Further, this additional opportunity might allow for the relationship between self-esteem
and resilience to be more robust. We therefore hypothesize that student
athletes should have more resilience compared to non-athletes. We
further hypothesize that the relationship between self-esteem and resilience will be stronger in student athletes compared to non-athletes. Two
hundred ﬁfty-eight participants (108 athletes, 150 non-athletes; ages 1826; 164 female, 82 male, 4 other) completed the Nicholson-McBride
Resilience Questionnaire (NMRQ) and the Rosenberg Self-Esteem Scale
(RSES). T-tests were used to assess group differences and Pearson’s
correlation tests were completed to assess the relationship between
resilience and self-esteem among athletes and among non-athletes.
Counter to prior reports, there were no differences in self-esteem between
these two groups (t(252) = 1.51, p = 0.133, d = 0.191). However, as
expected, athletes displayed greater amounts of stress resilience compared to the non-athletes (t(250) = -2.56, p = .011, d = -0.327). Additionally, a strong positive correlation between resilience and self-esteem
among athletes (r = 0.616, p < .001) and non-athletes (r = 0.636, p < .001)
was found. Contrary to expectations, the correlation between resilience
and self-esteem among athletes was not stronger than among non-athletes
(z = 0.256, p = .399). Although athletes displayed greater stress resilience
than the non-athletic population, the relationship between self-esteem and
resilience appears to be consistent between the groups. Future work
should better evaluate the means through which non-student-athletes
might develop self-esteem or resilience which might replace the beneﬁcial effects of sport participation.
Physical effort approach tendencies are reﬂected in physical activity
motivation and participation in vigorous physical activity
Caleb Zigby, McMaster University; Emily Brown, McMaster University;
Sheereen Harris, The University of Waterloo; Steven Bray, McMaster
University
Engaging in moderate-vigorous intensity physical activity (MVPA) requires physical effort; however, people vary in their tendencies to approach
and avoid physical effort. People with greater approach tendencies (APT)
may be more motivated to engage in MVPA and engage in more MVPA
than those with more dominant avoidance tendencies (AVT). We examined relative approach tendency (RAPT) (approach minus avoidance) as a
correlate of MVPA and motivation to engage in physical activity (PA)
using an effort discounting paradigm. Higher RAPT scores were expected
to be associated with more MVPA and greater PA motivation. Undergraduate participants (N = 477) completed the Physical Effort Scale (PES) to
assess APT and AVT and self-reported MVPA for a typical week. A
subsample (n = 140) completed an effort discounting questionnaire in
which they made a series of hypothetical choices between engaging in
PA of three intensities [light, moderate, vigorous] crossed with six
durations [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60 minutes] for a ﬁxed reward ($20) or a
sedentary task for varying reward amounts ($2-$20). Motivation was
measured indirectly for each intensity-duration combination by calculating
a point of indifference (POI) score, representing the monetary value at
which the choice to complete the sedentary and PA options were equal.
RAPT predicted engagement in vigorous (R2 = .14, p < .001) but not
moderate PA (p = .30). Analysis of POI scores showed signiﬁcant interactions of RAPT with PA intensity (p = .01) and duration (p < .001). POI
scores were positively associated with RAPT across all intensities. Posthoc comparisons of slopes revealed that, compared to light PA, POI scores
increased at a greater rate for moderate (p = .003) and vigorous PA (p = .06)
as RAPT increased. People with higher RAPT had higher POI scores than
those with lower RAPT at each duration level, indicating greater PA
motivation. Findings support the validity of the PES and RAPT as a
correlate of PA motivation and behavior. Future research should examine
physical effort APT and AVT and, value of physical effort as longitudinal
predictors of MVPA and explore factors contributing to individual differences in effort motivation and value.
Athlete abuse in terms of antecedents, conceptualizations, operationalizations, and outcomes: A scoping review
Katherine N. Alexander, Utah State University; Kat V. Adams, Utah State
University; Travis E. Dorsch, Utah State University
The study of athlete abuse is an emerging area in sport psychology, as
scholars only began consistently examining its components in the late
1980s (Parent & Fortier, 2018). Despite a growing literature, an issue
facing the ﬁeld relates to the systematic conceptualization and operationalization of athlete abuse. To consolidate the known and available
literature, this historical scoping review aimed to frame the scholarship
in terms of deﬁnitions and measures utilized by researchers across time.
The authors followed PRISMA guidelines to complete the review,
utilizing PsychInfo, SportDiscus, and Google Scholar in an initial search
in November 2024. Three inclusion criteria were utilized in the article
screening process: (a) articles discussed athletes’ own experiences and
outcomes related to athlete abuse or violence and not the experiences or
perspectives of others; (b) the experiences primarily occurred in the
context of sport; and (c) articles were published in peer-reviewed journals
in English. No age or other sample constraints were utilized and no date
restrictions were placed on the article sample. After ﬁnalizing the article
sample, the gray literature was explored to compare the empirical articles
to theses, dissertations, and white papers on the topic. Results highlighted
diverse conceptualizations and operationalizations utilized by scholars,
with many relying on vague deﬁnitions of athlete abuse. Few empirical
measures existed across the literature, and the frequency and typology of
experiences of abuse were difﬁcult to glean due to varied, overlapping, or
competing operationalizations. In response to the scoping review, the
authors propose a guiding heuristic that distinguishes between antecedents, conceptualizations, operationalizations, interrelated phenomenon,
and outcomes of athlete abuse. This has the potential to more concisely
isolate and measure the more nuanced aspects of abuse, while providing
practitioners a basic framework for understanding antecedents, deﬁnitions, and outcomes that may serve as a foundation for future work in
the area.

“The beneﬁts are endless”: University athletes’ and coaches’ perspectives of outcomes of athlete leader fairness
Katherine Hirsch, New Mexico State University; Todd Loughead, University of Windsor
Athlete leader fairness is an individualized perception of an athlete
leaders’ action or statement as being appropriate according to team
standards and expectations for utilizing leadership power (Hirsch &
Loughead, 2024). University coaches and athletes have identiﬁed that
fairness is an essential characteristic of effective athlete leadership
(Hirsch & Loughead, 2024), thereby suggesting that the presence of
athlete leader fairness and unfairness can elicit critical beneﬁcial and
detrimental outcomes, respectively. The purpose of the present study
was to investigate athletes’ and coaches’ perspectives of consequences
that occur on sport teams when athlete leaders are fair and when they are
unfair. Participants included 11 intercollegiate varsity athletes and nine
coaches who completed a one-on-one semi-structured interview. An
inductive and deductive thematic analysis was conducted to identify
themes reﬂective of consequences that occur in sport environments with
fair athlete leaders and unfair athlete leaders. Themes were sorted into
consequences of fairness to the athlete leader (e.g., gaining trust, feeling
like a valued member of the team), individual teammates (e.g., enjoying
the sport experience, increasing motivation), and team as a whole
(e.g., developing cohesion, facilitating stronger positive norms) as well
as consequences of unfairness to athlete leader (e.g., developing resentment towards the leader, losing control of the group), individual
teammates (e.g., reducing conﬁdence, more attrition), and team as a
whole (e.g., poor team reputation, decrease in team performance). These
ﬁndings demonstrate the strong impact that fair and unfair athlete
leaders can have on their teams whereby teams with fair and unfair
athlete leaders can experience numerous positive and negative consequences, respectively.
Insights from staff and volunteers on facilitating a golf program for
people living with dementia
Khadija Ahmad, University of Calgary; Meghan McDonough, University
of Calgary; Pamela Roach, Cumming School of Medicine; Cindy Barha,
University of Calgary; Dallas Seitz, Cumming School of Medicine;
Chantelle Zimmer, University of Calgary
As the global population of older adults increases, there is a growing need
for recreational physical activity programs that promote physical activity,
social engagement, and overall well-being among older adults. These
programs support physical health and are important for fostering social
connections, reducing isolation, and enhancing quality of life; however,
participation levels remain low. Designing and implementing such
programs for older adults, especially those with diverse abilities and
cognitive capacities, requires considering accessibility, inclusivity, and
participant engagement. Despite the recognized beneﬁts of these programs for older adults, limited research explores program facilitators’
lived experiences and training needs. To address this gap, we conducted a
qualitative study to examine the perspectives of staff and volunteers in a
golf program tailored for older adults living with dementia and their care
partners. Data collection included semi-structured interviews (30-60
minutes) with ﬁve staff members, two volunteers, and observational ﬁeld
notes from ﬁve program sessions. The interviews explored facilitators’
strategies for addressing participant needs, overcoming logistical and
social barriers, and creating an inclusive and engaging environment.
Observational ﬁeld notes focused on dementia inclusivity, program
logistics (delivery and feasibility), social experiences, and staff and
volunteer interactions. Thematic analysis was conducted to identify
recurring patterns and meaningful insights across the data. We identiﬁed
four themes: (1) enhancing accessibility for participants with limited
mobility by focusing on modiﬁcations to ensure all individuals could
participate fully; (2) adapting communication to participants’ diverse
cognitive capacities by providing clear instructions and ﬂexible approaches; (3) fostering social connection and inclusion, emphasizing the
importance of creating a supportive and welcoming community; and (4)
providing patient, personalized support, highlighting the need to understand individual needs and tailor activities. Our ﬁndings highlight the
resourcefulness of facilitators in creating a safe and engaging environment to accommodate the physical, cognitive, and social challenges faced
by participants. Facilitators adapted communication, navigated barriers,
and provided personalized support to foster engagement. Findings suggest investing in facilitator training, resources, and program structure can
further improve future programming and prepare facilitators to be more
inclusive, ensuring recreational programs better address older adults’
physical, cognitive, and social needs while promoting inclusivity and
well-being.
Funding source: University of Calgary VPR Catalyst, University of
Calgary Faculty of Kinesiology-Joan Snyder Funds.
Investigating factors inﬂuencing career termination among professional women dual career ice hockey players
Christiana R. Colizza, McGill University; Claudia Zeppetelli, McGill
University; Gordon A. Bloom, McGill University; Lindsay R. Duncan,
McGill University
Until recently, most professional women ice hockey players were
required to maintain full-time non-sport jobs outside of their sport to
earn a livable wage. This dual workload often resulted in heightened
stress and, in some cases, early retirement from hockey. However, the
factors inﬂuencing their decision to retire remain largely underexplored.
This study aims to explore the factors that led professional women ice
hockey players to prioritize their full-time careers over continuing their
athletic pursuits. Ten professional women hockey players were recruited through purposeful criterion-based sampling. The eligibility
criteria included participants who: (a) were current professional women
ice hockey players at the time of the interview, (b) played in the
Professional Hockey Federation (PHF) for a minimum of two seasons,
(c) simultaneously held a full-time non-sport job, (d) were not full-time
students, and (e) were not nationally carded athletes receiving monthly
stipends from the government. Our ﬁnal sample included athletes from
six out of the seven PHF teams who averaged 26 years of age. Eight
participants held a full-time non-sport job, working upwards of 35 hours
per week, with two participants holding part-time jobs given their
status’ as part-time students. After the semi-structured interviews took
place, a reﬂexive thematic analysis identiﬁed key factors that prevented
and reinforced their athletic retirement. Every athlete expressed a
profound sense of purpose and identity tied to hockey, driven not only
by personal fulﬁllment but also by a moral responsibility to inspire and
create better opportunities for future generations of women in the sport.
This dual motivation served as a powerful anchor, compelling them to
continue playing despite signiﬁcant challenges. Conversely, societal
expectations, career demands, and the pursuit of a more stable life
beyond the rink created mounting pressure, ultimately reinforcing
thoughts of stepping away from the sport they deeply loved. The
ﬁndings highlight the complex emotional and practical challenges
women athletes face in balancing elite sport with vocational demands.
Implications from this work can empower athletes and sport psychology
practitioners by deepening awareness of these emotional and practical
challenges surrounding athletic retirement and the broader inequities
within women’s professional sport.
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025

The effects of strategic self-talk with built in attentional focus instruction on continuous motor task performance
Jack Sampson, University of Texas at Austin; Phillip Post, New Mexico
State University; Christopher Aiken, New Mexico State University
The self-talk matching hypothesis predicts that motivational self-talk is
more effective for tasks involving endurance, strength, and power and that
instructional self-talk is more effective for tasks involving precision and
accuracy (Theodorakis et al., 2000). However, only 60% of self-talk
interventions have supported these predictions (Hardy et al., 2018). One
possibility for the inconsistent ﬁndings may relate to the focus of attention
used within the self-talk manipulation. Research has shown that an external
focus improves performance over an internal focus across various motor
tasks (Chua et al., 2021). The present study aimed to examine if internal
and external focus cues within instructional self-talk, compared to motivational self-talk, would improve performance on a continuous motor task.
Twenty-two participants (M = 5, F = 17; M = 21.64 yrs.) performed a 10minute treadmill running task on four separate occasions, each under one
of four conditions: control, internal instructional self-talk (IIST), external
instructional self-talk (EIST), and motivational self-talk (MST). The
dependent variables to assess performance included total distance, total
work, and ratings of perceived exertion (RPE). Each dependent variable
was analyzed using a one-way ANOVA with repeated measures with
Sidak post-hoc tests. A signiﬁcant main effect for total distance and work
was observed (p’s < .001). Sidak post hoc tests revealed signiﬁcant
differences between control and all experimental conditions (p’s < .001).
A signiﬁcant main effect was found for RPE (p = .045). Sidak tests
revealed a signiﬁcant difference between control and EIST (p = .009) and
control and MST (p = .044). All other comparisons showed no signiﬁcant
differences. Results suggest that including either internal or external focus
into instructional self-talk can beneﬁt continuous motor task performance,
which is at odds with the matching hypothesis that suggests only motivational self-talk should improve the performance of continuous tasks, like
running. More research combining attentional focus and instructional selftalk is needed to examine the beneﬁts of instructional self-talk on various
motor tasks.
Mental toughness, sport anxiety, and principles for deep knowledge
among athletes in Taiwan
LIN-HSIANG KANG, The Ohio State University; Jacqueline Goodway,
Michigan State University; Ming Hung Lin, National Chung Husing
University; Blaize Shiebler, University of Maryland
Numerous studies have demonstrated that Mental Toughness (MT) and
Sport Anxiety (SA) are critical psychological factors inﬂuencing athletes’
performance. The Principles for Deep Knowledge (PDK) questionnaire
has shown potential as a tool for helping athletes reduce their SA. This
study examined the differences by gender and competitive level, and
relationships among MT, SA, and PDK in athletes from Taiwan. The study
included 280 athletes (Mage = 19.69 years, SD = 2.69; 159 boys), categorized into two competitive levels (CL; Regional/ University versus National/International). All participants completed the 48 item Mental
Toughness Questionnaire 48 (MTQ-48, 4 subscales), the 21 item Sport
Anxiety Scale (SAS, 3 subscales), and the 45 item Principles for Deep
Knowledge (PDK, 9 subscales) Questionnaires. Pearson correlation analysis revealed that the 9 subscales of the PDK were signiﬁcantly positively
correlated with the 4 subscales of the MTQ-48. Additionally, the 7
subscales of the PDK showed signiﬁcant negative correlations with 2-3
subscales of the SAS. Four linear regression analyses used the 9 subscales
of the PDK to predict each of the 4 subscales of the MTQ-48 (challenge,
commitment, control, conﬁdence). Speciﬁcally, the subscales of the PDK,
Revolving & Cycling, Changing, and Cracking & Rippling principles
explained 22% (p < .001) of the variance in Challenge. Deep & Down,
Alternating, Revolving & Cycling, and Inner Connection (p < .001) accounted for 50% of the variance in Commitment and Revolving & Cycling
and Changing predicted 27% (p < .001) of the variance in Control. Finally,
Revolving & Cycling, Changing, and Cracking & Rippling explained 30%
(p < .001) of the variance in Conﬁdence. Three (SAS, MTQ-48, PDK) 2
Competitive Level X 2 Gender MANOVAs investigating CL and gender
differences, revealed signiﬁcant main effects for the SAS and MTQ-48.
For the SAS, there was a signiﬁcant (p < .01) main effect for CL but not
Gender or the interaction. For the PDK, neither CL/Gender were signiﬁcant. For the MTQ-48 there were signiﬁcant main effects for CL (p < .05)
and Gender (p < .001). These data have implications to working with
athletes at all levels.
Navigating post sporting-career transitions: A systematic scoping
review of retirement programs for elite athletes
Nadia Siena Jurkovic, University of South Australia; Alyson J. Crozier,
University of South Australia; Amber D. Mosewich, University of Alberta;
Edward O’Connor, University of South Australia; Juliana M. Gleaves,
University of South Australia; Sarven S. McLinton, University of South
Australia
Retirement from elite sport presents signiﬁcant challenges, as athletes
struggle to adjust to life outside of sport. Factors such as involuntary
retirement, identity loss, and career/ﬁnancial uncertainty can contribute to
a difﬁcult transition and impact an athlete’s mental well-being. Understanding the supports and programs available to retiring athletes may
identify the factors that facilitate a successful adjustment post-sporting
career. The purpose of this scoping review was to examine the existing
evidence on elite athlete retirement programs available during the retirement process (before, during, and after), in order to identify (1) the most
prevalent program types available; (2) the type of athletes these programs
are targeted toward; and (3) the modes of delivery for these programs. Five
online databases and grey literature were searched to identify research
published up to November 2024, from which 21 studies met the inclusion
criteria. All research designs were considered, and studies were included if
they were empirical, written in English, had active and/or retired elite
athletes as their primary population, and discussed retirement-focused
programs. The 21 studies included 11 studies with retired athletes, seven
studies with actively competing athletes, one study with athletes who were
transitioning to retirement, and two studies with a sample of both active
and retired athletes. The most common participant age range was 2535 years old. Most studies (n = 16) included career assistance programs
delivered by sporting organisations and players’ associations, while only
ﬁve studies had psychological support programs, which were primarily
delivered by academic research teams and clinical psychologists. The
duration of programs varied from a 2-minute video to a 90-minute session,
with the number of sessions varying from 1 to 16. Only three studies
conducted efﬁcacy testing. These ﬁndings identiﬁed that there is a limited
number of psychological support programs available to athletes during the
retirement process, with none being delivered by sporting organisations.
There is a clear need for further empirical evaluation of these programs.
Incorporating athletes’experiences in the design of future programs could
enhance their effectiveness and better address the unique challenges faced
during the transition from elite sport to retirement.
Expertise and susceptibility to the Halo effect in assessment of talent
Daniel Fortin-Guichard, McGill University; Koen Grootnibbelink, Vrije
Universiteit Amsterdam ; Eef de Hoop, Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam ;
Abel Westra, Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam; Tom Bergkamp, Netherlands
Royal Football Association; David L. Mann, Vrije Universiteit Amsterdam
JSEP Vol.47, Supplement, 2025

The halo effect is the tendency of raters to rely on a salient aspect of the
performance of a ratee (e.g., technical skills) to impact judgments in
other unrelated aspects (e.g., physical or psychological skills). The
currently predominant actuarial approach in talent identiﬁcation in
sports requires scouts and coaches to separately assess different aspects
of performance to ultimately provide an overall assessment of potential.
However, the approach is likely to fail if observers are inﬂuenced by the
halo effect. Additionally, the role of expertise in mitigating the halo
effect is not well understood, with some evidence outside of sports
suggesting that more experienced evaluators may be less susceptible to
this bias. Therefore, the current study aims to investigate the inﬂuence
of the halo effect when individuals with varying levels of expertise make
judgements of talent. Trained soccer scouts (n = 30), soccer fans (n = 30)
and non-fans (n = 34) watched videos of soccer small-sided games, in
which an identiﬁed player’s shooting or passing was manipulated to be
either successful or unsuccessful. Participants were then asked to rate
the player on ﬁve different aspects on 7-point Likert scales (shooting,
passing, dribbling, tactical and physical). The manipulations worked for
all groups, as positive manipulations of shooting and passing yielded
higher ratings than negative manipulations. However, the manipulations
had a signiﬁcant impact on the ratings of other non-manipulated aspects
only among non-fans, with no difference observed for scouts and fans.
Results suggest that the halo effect can impact assessments of performance in sport, but that experience and/or interest in the sport may
prevent the effect. Importantly, the absence of a halo effect suggests that
scouts and fans are capable of assessing aspects of performance
independently as is required by the actuarial approach for talent
identiﬁcation.
JSEP Vol. 47, Supplement, 2025
